. Moreover, the lengths of the edges are contained in a compact interval
[a,b]
with
0<a<b<\infty
. In particular, there is a positive lower bound on the width of the simplices and an upper bound on the diameter.

2.3
Coarse homotopy groups

In order to define coarse homotopy groups, we need a coarse analogue of a basepoint. Let
[1,\infty)
be given the standard metric
d(t,s)=|t-s|
.

Definition 2.25
.

Let
X
be a bornological coarse space. A
base ray
of
X
is a coarse map
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow X
. If
Y
is another bornological coarse space with base ray
\tau
, then a coarse map
f:X\rightarrow Y
is called
base ray preserving
if
\tau=f\circ\omega
.

Definition 2.26
.

Let
X
be a bornological coarse space. We define the
0
-th coarse homotopy set

\pi_{0}^{c}(X)
to be the set of coarse homotopy classes of coarse maps from
[1,\infty)
to
X
.

The idea of the
0
-th coarse homotopy set is that it counts the "components at infinity".

Example 2.27
.

\pi_{0}^{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
has two elements if
n=1
and one element if
n\geq 2
. This is not trivial and followed from the main theorem of
[
29
]
.

If
X
has at least one base ray
\omega
, ie.
\pi_{0}^{c}(X)\neq\emptyset
, then we can consider the pointed set
\pi^{c}_{0}(X,\omega)
of coarse homotopy classes of coarse maps from
[1,\infty)
to
X
with base point
[\omega]
.

A
bornological coarse pair
is a pair of bornological coarse spaces
(X,A)
along with a bornologous, coarse map
k_{A}:A\rightarrow X
.

Definition 2.28
.

Let
(X,A)
and
(Y,B)
be bornological coarse pairs. A
coarse map of pairs

f:(X,A)\rightarrow(Y,B)
is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{|A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

As an example, let
X
and
Y
be bornological coarse spaces with base rays
\omega,\tau
.
f:(X,\omega)\rightarrow(Y,\tau)
is base ray preserving exactly when the diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,\infty)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,\infty)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Definition 2.29
.

Let
f,g:(X,A)\rightarrow(Y,B)
be coarse maps. A
coarse homotopy of pairs
between
f
and
g
is a coarse map of pairs
H:(I_{p}(X),I_{pk_{A}}(A))\rightarrow(Y,B)
so that
H\circ i_{0}=f
and
H\circ i_{1}=g
. A
relative coarse homotopy
between
f
and
g
is a coarse homotopy of pairs between
f
and
g
such that
H(a,t)=f(a)
for all
a\in A
and
t\leq pk_{A}(a)
.

Definition 2.30
.

Let
X
be a bornological coarse space with base ray
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow X
. For
n\geq 1
define the
n
-th coarse homotopy group

\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)
to be the set of relative coarse homotopy classes of maps

\displaystyle f:(c([0,1]^{n}),c(\partial[0,1]^{n}))\rightarrow(X,\omega[1,\infty))

such that
f|_{c(\partial[0,1]^{n})}=\omega\circ p
, where
p:c([0,1]^{n})\rightarrow[1,\infty),(hx,h)\mapsto h
denotes the height variable of the cone.

For a bornological coarse pair
k_{A}:A\rightarrow X
with base ray
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow A
we define the relative
n
-th coarse homotopy "group"
\pi_{n}^{c}(X,A,\omega)
to be the set of relative coarse homotopy classes of maps

\displaystyle f:(c([0,1]^{n}),c(\partial[0,1]^{n}),c(\partial_{+}[0,1]^{n}))\rightarrow(X,A,\omega[1,\infty))

such that
f|_{c(\partial_{+}[0,1]^{n})}=\omega\circ p
where
\partial_{+}[0,1]^{n}:=\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\partial[0,1]^{n}\,|\,x_{n}>0\}
.

The notion of relative here means relative to
c(\partial_{+}[0,1]^{n})
. The homotopy restricted to
c(\partial[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])
has image in
A
and is fixed on
c(\partial_{+}[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])
but does not need to be fixed on all of
c(\partial[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])
. We frequently refer to such homotopies as "relative and base ray preserving" and call two maps
f,g
"base ray preserving relatively coarsely homotopic" if there is a relative and base ray presreving coarse homotopy between them.

The reason why can fix
p
to be the standard height projection is because of the following:

Lemma 2.31
.

Let
X
be a compact metric space,
cX
the Euclidean cone of
X
, thought of as a bornological coarse space. Let
p_{0}:cX\rightarrow[1,\infty)
be the height projection. Let
q:cX\rightarrow[1,\infty)
be any controlled map. Then any coarse homotopy
H:I_{q}(cX)\rightarrow Y
between
f:cX\rightarrow Y
and
g:cX\rightarrow Y
gives rise to a coarse homotopy
\overline{H}:I_{p_{0}}(cX)\rightarrow Y
between
f
and
g
.

Proof.

Let
(h\mathbf{x},h)\in cX
. We have that
d_{cX}((h\mathbf{x},h),((h+1)\mathbf{x},h+1))=\|\mathbf{x}\|+1\leq\frac{5}{4}
. Let
L>0
be such that
|q(x)-q(y)|<L
for all
x,y\in cX
with
d_{cX}(x,y)<\frac{5}{4}
. Then

\displaystyle|q(h\mathbf{x},h)-q(\mathbf{x},1)|\leq|q(h\mathbf{x},h)-q(\left\lfloor{h}\right\rfloor\mathbf{x},\left\lfloor{h}\right\rfloor)|+\sum_{j=2}^{\left\lfloor{h}\right\rfloor}|q(j\mathbf{x},j)-q((j-1)\mathbf{x},j-1)|<Lh

Let
C=\sup_{x\in X}q(\mathbf{x},1)
.

\displaystyle|q(h\mathbf{x},h)|\leq|q(h\mathbf{x},h)-q(\mathbf{x},1)|+|q(\mathbf{x},1)|<Lh+C

Let
q^{\prime}=Lp_{0}+C
. We can extend the homotopy
H
to
H^{\prime}:I_{q^{\prime}}(cX)\rightarrow Y
by extending "constantly" for the additional time, ie.
H^{\prime}((h\mathbf{x},h),t)=g(h\mathbf{x},h)
if
((h\mathbf{x},h),t)\in I_{q^{\prime}}(X)\setminus I_{q}(X)
. Finally, there is a coarse equivalence
\Psi:I_{p_{0}}(cX)\rightarrow I_{q^{\prime}}(cX)
with

\Psi(x,t)=\begin{cases}(x,(C+L)t)&t\in[0,1]\\
(x,C+Lt)&t\in[1,p_{0}(x)]\\
\end{cases}

For coarseness, there is nothing to check except in the time coordinate, but
\Psi
is piecewise linear there, so it is controlled. Properness and the existence of an inverse are also easy to check.
∎

Remark
.

An analogous argument can be made for the mapping telescope
M_{X}
defined later on.

The advantage of Lemma
2.31
is we can always assume that the map
p
defining a coarse cylinder is the height projection. We can canonically identify

\displaystyle I_{p}(c[0,1]^{n})

\displaystyle=c[0,1]^{n+1}

\displaystyle(hx_{1},\dots,hx_{n},h,ht)

\displaystyle\mapsto(hx_{1},\dots,hx_{n},ht,h)

They are isometric if we choose the standard
L^{1}
-norm on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
and the product metric on
I_{p}(c[0,1]^{n})
, and bi-Lipschitz equivalent for any other sensible choice. Analogously,
I_{p}(cX)=c(X\times[0,1])
. Note that because of this identification, we will occasionally swap the last two variables throughout the rest of the thesis, writing parameters in the order most convenient for exposition, eg. putting the time variable last when we want to emphasise that a map is a homotopy.

The product of two elements is defined in the standard way. Let
n\geq 1
and
f,g
be representatives of
[f],[g]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)
. The element
[f]\cdot[g]
is represented by

f\cdot g(hx_{1},hx_{2},\dots,hx_{n},h)=\begin{cases}f(2hx_{1},hx_{2},\dots,hx_{n},h)&x_{1}\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]\\
g(h(2x_{1}-1),hx_{2},\dots,hx_{n},h)&x_{1}\in[\frac{1}{2},1]\\
\end{cases}

This operation turns
\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)
into a group, which is abelian if
n\geq 2
. For
n\geq 2
, the same formula defines a group structure for the relative homotopy groups, which are abelian if
n\geq 3
. The unit is represented by the map
\omega\circ p:c[0,1]^{n}\rightarrow X
.

Coarse homotopy groups are functorial under coarse maps:

Proposition 2.32
.

Let
(X,A)
and
(Y,B)
be bornological coarse pairs with base rays
\omega,\tau
and let
\mathfrak{a}:(X,A)\rightarrow(Y,B)
be a base ray preserving coarse map. Then there is an induced homomorphism

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{*}:\pi_{n}^{c}(X,A,\omega)\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{c}(Y,B,\tau)

defined by the formula
\mathfrak{a}_{*}[f]=[\mathfrak{a}\circ f]
. Furthermore, if
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}:(X,A,\omega)\rightarrow(Y,B,\tau)
are base ray preserving relatively coarsely homotopic, then the homomorphisms
\mathfrak{a}_{*}
and
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}_{*}
are equal.

Proof.

The map
\mathfrak{a}f
is coarse. If
f
is coarsely homotopic to
g
, then
\mathfrak{a}f
is coarsely homotopic to
\mathfrak{a}g
. By Proposition
2.4
, if
\mathfrak{a}
is coarsely homotopic to
\mathfrak{a^{\prime}}
then
\mathfrak{a}f
is coarsely homotopic to
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}f
, by a relative and base ray preserving homotopy. We have used here that the bornology on
c[0,1]^{n}
is induced by the metric, so the controlled map
f
is automatically bornologous.
∎

An analogous statement is true for pointed sets. If
\mathfrak{a}:(X,\omega)\rightarrow(Y,\tau)
is a base ray preserving coarse map between bornological coarse spaces with base rays, there is an induced map
\mathfrak{a}_{*}:\pi^{c}_{0}(X,\omega)\rightarrow\pi^{c}_{0}(Y,\tau)
by sending
[\beta]\in\pi^{c}_{0}(X,\omega)
to
[\mathfrak{a}\beta]
. Base ray preserving homotopic maps induce the same morphism.

Proposition 2.33
.

If
(X,A,\omega)
is a bornological coarse space with map
k:A\rightarrow X
, and base ray
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow A
, the analogue of the usual construction in topology defines a long exact sequence of coarse homotopy groups or pointed sets

\displaystyle\dots\rightarrow\pi_{2}^{c}(A,\omega)\xrightarrow{k_{*}}\pi_{2}^{c}(X,\omega)\xrightarrow{r}\pi_{2}^{c}(X,A,\omega)\xrightarrow{\partial}\pi_{1}^{c}(A,\omega)\xrightarrow{k_{*}}\pi_{1}^{c}(X,\omega)\xrightarrow{r}\pi_{1}^{c}(X,A,\omega)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\partial}\pi_{0}^{c}(A,\omega)\xrightarrow{k_{*}}\pi_{0}^{c}(X,\omega)

We only prove the proposition for
A\subset X
with the restricted bornological coarse structure. (The general statement is left as an exercise.) Consider
[0,1]^{n}
as a metric space equipped with a (any) norm-induced metric. Equip
[0,1]^{n}\times I
with the product metric.

Lemma 2.34
.

Let
G:[0,1]^{n}\times I\rightarrow[0,1]^{n}
be a Lipschitz homotopy with Lipschitz constant
C
. Define the cone
cG
as

\displaystyle cG:I_{p}(c[0,1]^{n})=c[0,1]^{n+1}

\displaystyle\rightarrow c[0,1]^{n}

\displaystyle(hx,h,ht)

\displaystyle\mapsto(hG(x,t),h)

This is a proper Lipschitz homotopy between
cG_{0}
and
cG_{1}
.

Proof.

A bounded set
B
in
c[0,1]^{n}
has a maximum height
h^{\prime}
, and by construction a point at height
h
stays at height
h
, so the preimage of
B
is contained in the set
c_{[1,h^{\prime}]}[0,1]^{n+1}
, which is bounded. To prove that
cG
is controlled, suppose we have two points
(hx,h,ht)
and
(sy,s,sl)
with distance less than
R
. Obviously then
\|hx-sy\|<R
,
|h-s|<R
,
|ht-sl|<R
. We compute:

\displaystyle d(cG(hx,h,ht),cG(sy,s,sl))=|h-s|+\|hG(x,t)-sG(y,l)\|

\displaystyle<R+\|hG(x,t)-hG(y,l)\|+\|hG(y,l)-sG(y,l)\|

\displaystyle<R+Ch(\|x-y\|+|t-l|)+RK_{n}

\displaystyle\leq R+RK_{n}+C(\|hx-sy\|+\|sy-hy\|+|ht-sl|+|sl-hl|)

\displaystyle<R+RK_{n}+C(R+RK_{n}+R+R)<\tilde{C}R

where
K_{n}
is a constant depending on the choice of metric on
[0,1]^{n}
and
\tilde{C}
is some other constant after absorbing everything, dependent only on
K_{n}
and
C
.
∎

Note that this proof works just as well for arbitrary compact metric spaces
X,Y
: a Lipschitz homotopy
G:X\times[0,1]\rightarrow Y
defines a proper Lipschitz homotopy
cG:c(X\times[0,1])\rightarrow cY
.

Lemma 2.35
.

(Compression criterion) Suppose that

\displaystyle f:(c([0,1]^{n}),c(\partial[0,1]^{n}),c(\partial_{+}[0,1]^{n}))\rightarrow(X,A,\omega[1,\infty))

is a coarse map. Then
[f]=0\in\pi_{n}^{c}(X,A,\omega)
if and only if it is coarsely homotopic relative to
c(\partial[0,1]^{n})
to a map with image contained in
A
.

Proof.

First, suppose that
[f]
is homotopic to a map
[g]
as in the lemma. Then
[f]=[g]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(X,A,\omega)
. Now, choose a Lipschitz homotopy
H:[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1]\rightarrow[0,1]^{n}
from the identity to a constant map with value
s=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1},1)
with the property that
H(\partial_{+}[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])\subset\partial_{+}[0,1]^{n}
(for example, deformation retract everything to level
1
and then deformation retract to
s
). Then
g\circ cH
is a coarse homotopy with image in
A
between
g
and the constant map
\omega\circ p
to the base ray . By construction
g\circ cH
is
\omega\circ p
when restricted to
c(\partial_{+}[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])
. Therefore, it is a homotopy of triples
(c([0,1]^{n}),c(\partial[0,1]^{n}),c(\partial_{+}[0,1]^{n}))\rightarrow(X,A,\omega[1,\infty))
. Thus
[g]=0
.

Conversely, suppose that
[f]=0\in\pi_{n}^{c}(X,A,\omega)
. This means that there is a homotopy

\displaystyle F:I_{p}(c[0,1]^{n})\rightarrow X

of triples between
f
and the constant map to the base ray. Observe that the "end" of the cylinder along with the cylinder restricted to
c(\partial[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])

\displaystyle\{(hx,h,h)\,|\,x\in[0,1]^{n}\}\cup\{(hx,h)\times[0,h]\,|\,x\in\partial[0,1]^{n}\}

is itself a copy of
c([0,1]^{n})
which is Lipschitz homotopic to
c([0,1]^{n}\times\{0\})
in
I_{p}(c[0,1]^{n})
, fixing
c(\partial[0,1]^{n}\times\{0\})
. This shows that
f
is homotopic rel
c(\partial[0,1]^{n})
to a map with image in
F(\{(hx,h,h)\,|\,x\in[0,1]^{n}\}\cup\{(hx,h)\times[0,h]\,|\,x\in\partial[0,1]^{n}\})\subset A
. Concretely, we have a Lipschitz deformation retract
D:[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1]\times[0,1]\rightarrow[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1]
relative to
\partial[0,1]^{n}\times\{0\}
such that
D_{0}=\id
and
D_{1}([0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])\subset([0,1]^{n}\times\{1\})\cup(\partial[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])
, define
q:I_{p}(c[0,1]^{n})\rightarrow[1,\infty)
as
q(hx,h,s)=h
and we have a homotopy of the homotopy cylinder

\displaystyle H:c([0,1]^{n}\times[0,1]\times[0,1])=I_{q}(I_{p}(c[0,1]^{n}))\rightarrow X

which is defined as
F\circ cD
. The restriction of
H
to
c([0,1]^{n}\times\{0\}\times[0,1])
is the desired coarse homotopy from
f
to a map with image in
A
.
∎

Now we prove the exactness of the long exact sequence in coarse homotopy groups:

Proof.

(of Proposition
2.33
) The first three points apply to
n\geq 1
.

1.

\image(\partial)=\Ker(k_{*})
: Suppose we have
[f]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(X,A,\omega)
, represented by

\displaystyle f:(c([0,1]^{n}),c(\partial[0,1]^{n}),c(\partial_{+}[0,1]^{n}))\rightarrow(X,A,\omega[1,\infty))

and denote by
g
the restriction of
f
to
c([0,1]^{n-1}\times\{0\})
, ie
\partial[f]=[g]
. Choose a Lipschitz deformation retract
H
of
[0,1]^{n}
to
s\in[0,1]^{n-1}\times\{1\}
. Then
f\circ cH
restricted to
c([0,1]^{n-1}\times\{0\}\times[0,1])
is a base ray preserving homotopy between
g
and the map
\omega\circ p
. Hence
[g]=0
in
\pi_{n-1}^{c}(X,\omega)
.

Conversely, suppose that
k_{*}([g])=0
, which means that there is a coarse homotopy
H:I_{p}(c[0,1]^{n-1})\rightarrow X
rel
c(\partial[0,1]^{n-1})
of
g
to the map
\omega\circ p
. Put
f(h(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1},x_{n}),h)=H(h(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}),h,hx_{n})
. This is a map
f
with
[f]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(X,A,\omega)
which restricts to
g
on
c([0,1]^{n-1})
, showing that
[g]=\partial[f]
.

2.

\image(k_{*})=\Ker{(r)}
: This is exactly the contents of Lemma
2.35
.

3.

\image{(r)}=\Ker(\partial)
: If
f
represents a class in
\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)
then thinking about it as an element of
\pi_{n}^{c}(X,A,\omega)
means immediately that its restriction to
c([0,1]^{n-1}\times\{0\})
is the constant map
\omega\circ p
to the base ray, showing that
\partial\circ r=0
.

Conversely, suppose the restriction of
f
to
c([0,1]^{n-1}\times\{0\})
is homotopic rel
c(\partial[0,1]^{n-1}\times\{0\})
with image in
A
to the map
\omega\circ p
. Then we can "stack" this homotopy onto the bottom of
f
to obtain a homotopy of triples between
f
and map
g
, where
g
restricted to
c(\partial[0,1]^{n})
is the constant map
\omega\circ p
to the base ray. Therefore
[f]=[g]\in\image(r)
.

4.

Exactness at
\pi^{c}_{0}(A,\omega)
: Let
[\beta]\in\pi^{c}_{0}(A,\omega)
. If there is a coarse homotopy
H:I_{p}([1,\infty))=c(\{\ast\}\times[0,1])\rightarrow X
between
\beta
and
\omega
, then
[H]\in\pi^{c}_{1}(X,A,\omega)
with
\partial[H]=[\beta]
. Conversely, for
[f]\in\pi^{c}_{1}(X,A,\omega)
, we have that
f
restricted to
c(\{0\})
is a base ray in
A
which is coarsely homotopic in
X
to
\omega
.

∎

We show that coarse homotopy groups commute with taking some colimits.

Definition 2.36
.

A
based bornological coarse space
is a quadruple
(X,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B},\omega)
consisting of a bornological coarse space
(X,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B})
and a coarse map
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow X
.

\textbf{BornCoarse}_{*}
is the category of based bornological coarse spaces. A morphism
f:(X,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B},\omega)\rightarrow(Y,\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\tau)
is a base ray preserving coarse map.

We have shown in Proposition
2.32
(take
A=\emptyset
) that coarse homotopy groups (resp. sets) are functors:
\textbf{BornCoarse}_{*}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
, where
\mathbf{C}
is the category of pointed sets for
n=0
, groups for
n=1
, or abelian groups for
n\geq 2
. We now introduce the idea of
u
-continuity, which is used in
[
9
]
to define the axioms of a local homology theory.

Definition 2.37
.

(Definition
3.11
in
[
9
]
) Let
E:\mathbf{UBC}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
be a functor. We say that
E
is
u
-continuous if for every uniform bornological space
X
the canonical morphism

\displaystyle\colim_{V\in\mathcal{C}}E(X_{V})\rightarrow E(X)

is an equivalence.

We use a variant of this definition.

Let
(X,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B},\omega)
be a bornological coarse space with base ray. Since
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow X
is coarse, we have that
(\omega\times\omega)D_{1}
is an entourage. Let
\mathcal{C}_{\omega}\subset\mathcal{C}
be the subset of
\mathcal{C}
consisting of entourages that contain
(\omega\times\omega)D_{1}
. Denote by
(X_{V},\mathcal{C}\langle V\rangle,\mathcal{B},\omega)
the based coarse bornological space with coarse structure generated by the entourage
V\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
. If
V\subset W
then the set-wise identity
i_{V,W}:X_{V}\rightarrow X_{W}
is coarse and base ray preserving. We have the equivalence

\displaystyle\colim_{V\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}}(X_{V},\omega)\simeq(X,\omega)

in
\textbf{BornCoarse}_{*}
.

Definition 2.38
.

Let
E:\textbf{BornCoarse}_{*}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
be a functor. We say that
E
is
u
-continous
if for every based bornological coarse space
(X,\omega)
, the canonical morphism

\displaystyle\colim_{V\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}}i_{V}:\colim_{V\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}}E(X_{V},\omega)\rightarrow E(X,\omega)

induced by the set-wise identity maps
i_{V}:(X_{V},\omega)\rightarrow(X,\omega)
is an equivalence.

Proposition 2.39
.

The functor
\pi^{c}_{n}:\textbf{BornCoarse}_{*}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
where
\mathbf{C}
is the category of pointed sets for
n=0
, groups for
n=1
, or abelian groups for
n\geq 2
, is
u
-continuous.

Proof.

We define the inverse to
\colim_{V\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}}i_{V}
. Let
[f]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)
. For
n\geq 1
,
[f]
is represented by a coarse map of pairs
f:(c[0,1]^{n},c\partial[0,1]^{n})\rightarrow(X,\omega)
. We have that
(f\times f)D_{1}\subset U
for some
U\in\mathcal{C}
. Since
(\omega\times\omega)D_{1}\subset(f\times f)D_{1}
(take points in
c\{(0,0)\}
) we have that
U\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
. Denote by
[f_{U}]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(X_{U},\omega)
the homotopy class represented by
f
. We send
[f]
to the image of
[f_{U}]
in the colimit.

\displaystyle j:\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\colim_{V\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}}\pi_{n}^{c}(X_{V},\omega)

\displaystyle[f]

\displaystyle\mapsto[f_{U}]

This is well defined: if
U\subset W
then
i_{U,W}[f_{U}]=[f_{W}]
because all maps are induced by the identity. If
[f]=[g]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)
then there exists a coarse homotopy
H:(c[0,1]^{n+1},c(\partial[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1]))\rightarrow(X,\omega)
such that
H_{|c([0,1]^{n}\times\{0\})}=f,H_{|c([0,1]^{n}\times\{1\})}=g
. There exists a
W\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
such that
(H\times H)D_{1}\subset W
. This gives us

\displaystyle j[f]=[f_{W}]=[g_{W}]=j[g]

Both compositions
j\circ\colim_{V\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}}i_{V},\colim_{V\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}}i_{V}\circ j
are the identity on representatives.

An analogous statement holds for
n=0
.
∎

2.4
Base ray considerations

We introduce in this section the change of base ray homomorphism
b
, and show that a coarse homotopy equivalence induces an isomorphism on coarse homotopy groups. Note that throughout this section, we will use
\|\cdot\|
to denote the
L^{1}
-norm
\|\cdot\|_{1}
on
[0,1]^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Definition 2.40
.

Identify the unit cube
[0,1]^{n}
with the set
[-1,1]^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
in the obvious way and take the Euclidean cone
c[-1,1]^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Let
f:(c[-1,1]^{n},c\partial[-1,1]^{n})\rightarrow(Z,\omega_{0})
be a coarse map representing a class
[f]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(Z,\omega_{0})
. Let
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow Z
be another base ray in
Z
such that
\omega_{0}
is coarsely homotopic to
\omega
: ie. there exists a coarse map
H:I_{p}[1,\infty)\rightarrow Z
restricting to
\omega_{0}
and
\omega
on
c\{0\}
and
c\{1\}
respectively. We define

\displaystyle b_{\omega_{0},\omega}^{H}:\pi_{n}^{c}(Z,\omega_{0})\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{c}(Z,\omega)

as follows:

Let
U=cW:=\{(hx_{1},\dots,hx_{n},h)\subset c[-1,1]^{n}\,|\,-\frac{1}{2}\leq x_{i}\leq\frac{1}{2}\,\forall 1\leq i\leq n\}

be a smaller cone inside
c[-1,1]^{n}
. Let
\mathfrak{r}:U\rightarrow c[-1,1]^{n}
be the height-wise radial multiplication by
2
,
(hx_{1},\dots,hx_{n},h)\mapsto(2hx_{1},\dots,2hx_{n},h)
. Define
b^{H}_{\omega_{0},\omega}[f]|_{U}
as the composition
f\circ\mathfrak{r}
. On
c[-1,1]^{n}\setminus U
we have

\displaystyle b^{H}_{\omega_{0},\omega}[f]|_{c[-1,1]^{n}\setminus U}:c[-1,1]^{n}\setminus U

\displaystyle\rightarrow Z

\displaystyle(h\mathbf{x},h)

\displaystyle\mapsto H(h,h(2\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}-1))

That is, on height
h
we travel radially outwards along the path between
\omega_{0}(h)
and
\omega(h)
as defined by the coarse homotopy
H
. See Figure
6
.

Figure 6:
A representative of
b^{H}_{\omega_{0},\omega}[f]
restricted to height
h
.

We call
b^{H}_{\omega_{0},\omega}
the
change of base ray homomorphism
between
\omega_{0}
and
\omega
. We omit the indices
\omega_{0},\omega,H
from the notation when it is obvious from context.

Definition 2.41
.

Let
H
be a coarse homotopy between
\omega_{0}
and
\omega_{1}
,
H^{\prime}
a coarse homotopy between
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
. We define the concatenation of homotopies
H\ast H^{\prime}:c[0,1]\rightarrow Z
as

H\ast H^{\prime}(h,ht)=\begin{cases}H(h,2ht)&t\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]\\
H^{\prime}(h,h(2t-1))&t\in[\frac{1}{2},1]\\
\end{cases}

This is just a rescaled version of running first
H
, then
H^{\prime}
. It is a coarse homotopy between the rays
\omega_{0}
and
\omega_{2}
. An analogous formula holds for homotopies
H,H^{\prime}:c([0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])\rightarrow Z
,
n\geq 1
.

Proposition 2.42
.

b^{H}_{\omega_{0},\omega}
is well defined:

1.

b^{H}_{\omega_{0},\omega}[f]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(Z,\omega)

2.

b^{H}_{\omega_{0},\omega}[f]
is independent of choice of representative
f

3.

b^{H}_{\omega_{0},\omega}
is a group homomorphism

4.

b^{H}_{\omega_{0},\omega}
is independent of homotopy class of
H

5.

b_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}^{H^{\prime}}b_{\omega_{0},\omega_{1}}^{H}=b_{\omega_{0},\omega_{2}}^{H\ast H^{\prime}}

6.

b_{\omega_{0},\omega_{0}}^{\id}=\id

7.

Let
\mathfrak{a}:X\rightarrow Y
be a coarse map between bornological coarse spaces. Then the diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{c}(Y,\mathfrak{a}\omega_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{c}(Y,\mathfrak{a}\omega)\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{\omega_{0},\omega}^{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{\mathfrak{a}\omega_{0},\mathfrak{a}\omega}^{\mathfrak{a}H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{*}}

Remark
.

Piecing statements
4,5,6
together we get that the change of base ray homomorphism is an isomorphism:
b_{\omega,\omega_{0}}^{H^{-1}}b_{\omega_{0},\omega}^{H}=b_{\omega_{0},\omega_{0}}^{H\ast H^{-1}}=b_{\omega_{0},\omega_{0}}^{\id}=\id
and
b_{\omega_{0},\omega}^{H}b_{\omega,\omega_{0}}^{H^{-1}}=b_{\omega,\omega}^{H^{-1}\ast H}=b_{\omega,\omega}^{\id}=\id
.

Proof.

1.

\mathfrak{r}
is clearly proper and has Lipschitz constant
2
.
b[f]|_{U}
is coarse, as a composition of proper Lipschitz and coarse maps. To show that
b[f]|_{c[-1,1]^{n}\setminus U}
is coarse, we just compute:

\displaystyle d_{c[-1,1]^{n}}(h,h(2\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}-1))(s,s(2\|\mathbf{y}\|_{\infty}-1))

\displaystyle\leq 2|\|h\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}-\|s\mathbf{y}\|_{\infty}|+2|h-s|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|h\mathbf{x}-s\mathbf{y}\|_{\infty}+2|h-s|

\displaystyle\leq 2d_{c[-1,1]^{n}}((h\mathbf{x},h,ht),(s\mathbf{y},s,sl))

b[f]|_{c[-1,1]^{n}\setminus U}
is a composition of proper Lipschitz and coarse maps, therefore coarse.

Clearly
b[f]
sends
c\partial[-1,1]^{n}
to the base ray
\omega
. Properness follows because the pre-image of a bounded set
K
is
b[f]^{-1}(K)=b[f]|_{U}^{-1}(K)\cup b[f]_{c[-1,1]^{n}\setminus U}^{-1}{K}=\mathfrak{s}(f^{-1}{K})\bigcup H^{-1}(K)\times\partial[-1,1]^{n}
, where
\mathfrak{s}:c[-1,1]^{n}\rightarrow U
is the height-wise radial multiplication by
\frac{1}{2}
and
H^{-1}(K)\times\partial[-1,1]^{n}
refers to the points
(hx_{1},\dots,hx_{n},h)
in
c[-1,1]^{n}\setminus U\cong c([0,1]\times\partial[-1,1]^{n})
which have appropriate norm to be in the preimage of
K
. Since taking products and forming unions of bounded sets is bounded,
b[f]^{-1}{(K)}
is bounded. Since the maps agree on their intersection and
c[-1,1]^{n}
is a path metric space,
b[f]
is globally coarse and therefore
b[f]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(Z,\omega)
.

2.

Let
g
be coarsely homotopic to
f
via
G^{\prime}:I_{p}(c[-1,1]^{n})=c([-1,1]^{n}\times[0,1])\rightarrow Z
which preserves the base ray. We can run
G^{\prime}
on the cylinder
c(W\times[0,1])
of
U
, and "fill in" with the constant homotopy which changes base ray on the remainder. Concretely, on
(U\times[0,\infty))\cap I_{p}(c[-1,1]^{n})
we define
G
as the composition
G^{\prime}\circ(\mathfrak{r}\times\id)
and on
(c[-1,1]^{n}\setminus U)\times[0,\infty)
we project onto
c[-1,1]^{n}\setminus U
and apply
b[f]|_{c[-1,1]^{n}\setminus U}=b[g]|_{c[-1,1]^{n}\setminus U}
. See Figure
7
.

Figure 7:
The coarse homotopy
G
between
b[f]
and
b[g]
on height
h
.

Again, the restriction to each component is clearly controlled and the maps agree on the intersection and so is globally controlled. To show properness, observe that for a bounded set
K
the preimage is
G^{-1}(K)=(\mathfrak{s}\times\id)(G^{\prime-1}(K))\cup((H^{-1}(K)\times\partial[-1,1]^{n})\times[0,\infty)\cap I_{p}(c[-1,1]^{n}))
. This set is bounded since
((H^{-1}(K)\times\partial[-1,1]^{n})\times[0,\infty)\cap I_{p}(c[-1,1]^{n}))
is contained within
(H^{-1}(K)\times\partial[-1,1]^{n})\times[0,h]
for some
h>0
. Hence
G
is proper.

3.

The proof is basically taking the cone of the proof for ordinary homotopy groups. First we show that for
[f]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(Z,\omega_{0})
, replacing half the cone with the constant map to the base ray gives us
f^{\prime}
which is coarsely homotopic to
f
. Let
V
be
c([-1,0]\times[-1,1]^{n-1})\subset c[-1,1]^{n}
and define:

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{|V}(hx_{1},\dots,hx_{n},h)

\displaystyle=f(2h(x_{1}+\frac{1}{2}),hx_{2},\dots,hx_{n},h)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{|V^{c}}(h\mathbf{x},h)

\displaystyle=\omega_{0}(h)

Let
Y:=\{(h\mathbf{x},h,ht)\in I_{p}(c[-1,1]^{n})\,|\,x_{1}\leq t\}
. For the homotopy
J
between
f^{\prime}
and
f
, we define

\displaystyle J_{|Y}(h\mathbf{x},h,ht)

\displaystyle=f(\frac{2h}{1+t}(x_{1}-\frac{t-1}{2}),hx_{2},\dots,hx_{n},h)

\displaystyle J_{|Y^{c}}(h\mathbf{x},h,ht)

\displaystyle=\omega_{0}(h)

See Figure
8
.

Figure 8:
The coarse homotopy
J
between
f^{\prime}
and
f
, restricted to height
h
.

To compute the Lipschitz constants, let
(h\mathbf{x},h,ht)
and
(s\mathbf{y},s,sl)
be two points in
Y
. We have the following estimates:

\displaystyle|\frac{2h}{1+t}(x_{1}-\frac{t-1}{2})-\frac{2s}{1+l}(y_{1}-\frac{l-1}{2})|

\displaystyle\leq|\frac{2hx_{1}}{1+t}-\frac{2sy_{1}}{1+l}|+|\frac{h(t-1)}{1+t}-\frac{s(l-1)}{1+l}|

\displaystyle\leq|\frac{2hx_{1}}{1+t}-\frac{2sy_{1}}{1+t}|+|\frac{2sy_{1}}{1+t}-\frac{2sy_{1}}{1+l}|+|\frac{h(t-1)}{1+t}-\frac{h(l-1)}{1+l}|+|\frac{h(l-1)}{1+l}-\frac{s(l-1)}{1+l}|

\displaystyle\leq 2|hx_{1}-sy_{1}|+2s|t-l|+2(|th-ls|+|h-s|)+|\frac{l-1}{1+l}||h-s|

\displaystyle\leq 2|hx_{1}-sy_{1}|+4(|th-ls|+|h-s|)+|h-s|

Therefore

\displaystyle|\frac{2h}{1+t}(x_{1}-\frac{t-1}{2})-\frac{2s}{1+l}(y_{1}-\frac{l-1}{2})|+\sum_{i=2}^{n}|hx_{i}-sy_{i}|+|h-s|

\displaystyle\leq 2|hx_{1}-sy_{1}|+4(|th-ls|+|h-s|)+2|h-s|+\sum_{i=2}^{n}|hx_{i}-sy_{i}|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|h\mathbf{x}-s\mathbf{y}\|+4|th-ls|+6|h-s|

This shows that
H
is a composition of controlled and Lipschitz maps, therefore controlled. Properness is clear.

Let
[f],[g]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(Z,\omega_{0})
. We denote by
f+0
the map
f^{\prime}
as defined above and by
0+g
the analogous construction with
V^{\prime}=c([0,1]\times[-1,1]^{n-1})\subset c[-1,1]^{n}
. We now construct a coarse homotopy
G
between
b[f+0]+b[0+g]
and
b[f+g]
. See Figure
9
.

G(h\mathbf{x},h,ht)=\begin{cases}b(f+0)(\frac{2h}{1+t}(x_{1}-\frac{t-1}{2}),hx_{2},\dots,hx_{n},h)&x_{1}\in[-1,0]\\
b(0+g)(\frac{2h}{1+t}(x_{1}+\frac{t-1}{2}),hx_{2},\dots,hx_{n},h)&x_{1}\in[0,1]\\
\end{cases}

Figure 9:
The coarse homotopy
G
between
b[f+0]+b[0+g]
and
b[f+g]
, restricted to height
h
.

The calculation of the Lipschitz constants is exactly the same as above, and properness is also clear. The composition
(b[(J_{f})^{-1}]+b[(J_{g})^{-1}])\ast G
(abusing notation) is a coarse homotopy between
b[f]+b[g]
and
b[f+g]
.

4.

Let
H^{\prime}
be homotopic to
H
via a coarse homotopy
G^{\prime}:I_{p}(c[0,1])=c([0,1]^{2})\rightarrow Z
restricting to
H
on
c([0,1]\times\{0\})
and
H^{\prime}
on
c([0,1]\times\{1\})
respectively. On the cylinder of
(U\times[0,\infty))\cap I_{p}(c[-1,1]^{n})
we define
G
by projecting onto
U
and applying the composition
f\circ\mathfrak{r}
. This is controlled, since projection has Lipschitz constant
1
. On its complement we define

\displaystyle G:((c[-1,1]^{n}\setminus U)\times[0,\infty))\cap I_{p}(c[-1,1]^{n})

\displaystyle\rightarrow Z

\displaystyle(h\mathbf{x},h,ht)

\displaystyle\mapsto G^{\prime}(h,h(2\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}-1),ht)

See Figure
10
. Since
G^{\prime}
is controlled, so is
G
restricted to
((c[-1,1]^{n}\setminus U)\times[0,\infty))\cap I_{p}(c[-1,1]^{n})
. For properness, for a bounded set
K
the preimage is
(\mathfrak{s}\times\id)(f^{-1}(K)\times[0,\infty)\cap I_{p}(c[-1,1]^{n}))\cup(G^{\prime-1}(K)\times\partial[-1,1]^{n})
which is bounded.

Figure 10:
The coarse homotopy
G
between
b^{H}_{\omega_{0},\omega}[f]
and
b^{H^{\prime}}_{\omega_{0},\omega}[g]
, restricted to height
h
.

5.

Let
[f]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(Z,\omega_{0})
. For a fixed height
h
on
c[-1,1]^{n}
the description of
b_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}^{H^{\prime}}b_{\omega_{0},\omega_{1}}^{H}([f])
is
f
on points with supremum norm less than
\frac{h}{4}
, the homotopy
H
between
\frac{h}{4}
and
\frac{h}{2}
, and the homotopy
H^{\prime}
between
\frac{h}{2}
and
h
. The description of
b_{\omega_{0},\omega_{2}}^{H\ast H^{\prime}}
is
f
on points with norm less than
\frac{h}{2}
,
H
between
\frac{h}{2}
and
\frac{3h}{4}
and
H^{\prime}
between
\frac{3h}{4}
and
h
. Let
\varphi_{1}:[-1,1]\rightarrow[-1,1]
be the function which takes values

\displaystyle(0,0),(\pm\frac{1}{4},\pm\frac{1}{2}),(\pm\frac{1}{2},\pm\frac{3}{4}),(\pm 1,\pm 1)

with linear interpolation in between. Let
\varphi_{t}(x)=(1-t)x+t\varphi_{1}(x)
be a homotopy between the identity and
\varphi_{1}
. We now write down a homotopy between
b_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}^{H^{\prime}}b_{\omega_{0},\omega_{1}}^{H}[f]
and
b_{\omega_{0},\omega_{2}}^{H\ast H^{\prime}}[f]
. Define

\displaystyle G^{\prime}:I_{p}(c[-1,1]^{n})=c([-1,1]^{n}\times[0,1])

\displaystyle\rightarrow c[-1,1]^{n}

\displaystyle(hx_{1},\dots,hx_{n},h,ht)

\displaystyle\mapsto(h\varphi_{t}(x_{1}),\dots,h\varphi_{t}(x_{n}),h)

for
t\in[0,1]
. Let
G
be the composition
b_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}^{H^{\prime}}b_{\omega_{0},\omega_{1}}^{H}[f]\circ G^{\prime}
, which clearly restricts to
b_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}^{H^{\prime}}b_{\omega_{0},\omega_{1}}^{H}[f]
on
c([-1,1]^{n}\times\{0\})
and
b_{\omega_{0},\omega_{2}}^{H\ast H^{\prime}}[f]
on
c([-1,1]^{n}\times\{1\})
. See Figure

11
.

Figure 11:
The coarse homotopy
G
between
b_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}^{H^{\prime}}b_{\omega_{0},\omega_{1}}^{H}[f]
and
b_{\omega_{0},\omega_{2}}^{H\ast H^{\prime}}[f]
, restricted to height
h
.

To show that this is controlled involves a straightforward calculation:

\displaystyle|\varphi_{t}(x)-\varphi_{l}(y)|

\displaystyle\leq|\varphi_{t}(x)-\varphi_{t}(y)|+|\varphi_{t}(y)-\varphi_{l}(y)|

\displaystyle|\varphi_{t}(x)-\varphi_{t}(y)|

\displaystyle=|(1-t)x+t\varphi_{1}(x)-(1-t)y-t\varphi_{1}(y)|

\displaystyle\leq|x-y|+|\varphi_{1}(x)-\varphi_{1}(y)|\leq 3|x-y|

\displaystyle|\varphi_{t}(y)-\varphi_{l}(y)|

\displaystyle=|(1-t)y+t\varphi_{1}(y)-(1-l)y-l\varphi_{1}(y)|\leq 2|t-l|

for
x,y\in[-1,1]
. For
(hx_{1},\dots,hx_{n},h,ht)
and
(sy_{1},\dots,sy_{n},s,sl)
we have the estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}|h\varphi_{t}(x_{i})-s\varphi_{l}(y_{i})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}|h\varphi_{t}(x_{i})-h\varphi_{l}(y_{i})|+|h\varphi_{l}(y_{i})-s\varphi_{l}(y_{i})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}3h(|t-l|+|x_{i}-y_{i}|)+|h-s|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}3(|ht-sl|+|hx_{i}-sy_{i}|)+7|h-s|

\displaystyle=3n|ht-sl|+\|h\mathbf{x}-s\mathbf{y}\|+7n|h-s|

Hence
G
is controlled. For a bounded set
K
the preimage is contained within
(b_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}^{H^{\prime}}b_{\omega_{0},\omega_{1}}^{H}[f]^{-1}(K)\times[0,\infty))\cap I_{p}(c[-1,1]^{n})
which is bounded.

6.

The homotopy is defined as follows: let
V\subset I_{p}(c[-1,1]^{n})
be the set

\displaystyle V:=\{(h\mathbf{x},h,ht)\in I_{p}(c[-1,1]^{n})\,|\,2\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}-1\leq t\}

Then we define:

\displaystyle G_{|V}:V

\displaystyle\rightarrow Z

\displaystyle(h\mathbf{x},h,ht)

\displaystyle\mapsto f(\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1},h)

and on the complement we define
G
by taking each height to
\omega_{0}(h)
. See Figure
12
.

Figure 12:
The coarse homotopy
G
between
b^{id_{\omega_{0}}}_{\omega_{0},\omega_{0}}[f]
and
[f]
, restricted to height
h
.

Let us compute the constants once again, for two points
(h\mathbf{x},h,ht)
and
(s\mathbf{y},s,sl)
in
V
.

\displaystyle\|\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1}-\frac{2s\mathbf{y}}{l+1}\|

\displaystyle=2\|\frac{h\mathbf{x}}{t+1}-\frac{s\mathbf{x}}{t+1}+\frac{s\mathbf{x}}{t+1}-\frac{s\mathbf{y}}{t+1}+\frac{s\mathbf{y}}{t+1}-\frac{s\mathbf{y}}{l+1}\|

\displaystyle\leq 2(|h-s|\|\mathbf{x}\|+s\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|+s\|\mathbf{y}\||\frac{1}{t+1}-\frac{1}{l+1}|)

\displaystyle\leq 2(n|h-s|+n|h-s|+\|h\mathbf{x}-s\mathbf{y}\|+ns|t-l|)

\displaystyle\leq 6n|h-s|+2\|h\mathbf{x}-s\mathbf{y}\|+2n|ht-sl|

Hence
G
is controlled. Properness is shown in a similar way to the previous points.

7.

The diagram already commutes on the level of maps: take any representative for
[f]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega_{0})
and the result is that on
U
we have
\mathfrak{a}\circ f\circ\mathfrak{r}
and on the complement of
U
we run along from
\mathfrak{a}\omega_{0}
to
\mathfrak{a}\omega
with the homotopy
\mathfrak{a}\circ H
.

∎

Lemma 2.43
.

Let
X,Y
be bornological coarse spaces and
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow X
a coarse base ray. Let
\mathfrak{a}:X\rightarrow Y
be a coarse homotopy equivalence. The induced map
\mathfrak{a}_{*}:\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{c}(Y,\mathfrak{a}\circ\omega)
is an isomorphism for all
n\geq 0
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{b}:Y\rightarrow X
be coarse homotopy inverse, that is, a coarse map such that
\mathfrak{b}\circ\mathfrak{a}:X\rightarrow X
and
\mathfrak{a}\circ\mathfrak{b}:Y\rightarrow Y
are coarsely homotopic to
\id_{X}
and
\id_{Y}
respectively. Call these homotopies
H:I_{p_{X}}(X)\rightarrow X
and
\tilde{H}:I_{p_{Y}}(Y)\rightarrow Y
. We show that the composition

\displaystyle\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{a}_{*}}\pi_{n}^{c}(Y,\mathfrak{a}\omega)\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{b}_{*}}\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}\omega)\xrightarrow{b_{\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}\omega,\omega}^{H}}\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)

is the identity, which gives us that
\mathfrak{a}_{*}
is injective. Let
n\geq 1
. The image of
[f]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)
under the composition is represented by the following map: on
U
we have
\mathfrak{b}\circ\mathfrak{a}\circ f\circ\mathfrak{r}
and on the complement of
U
we travel radially outwards along the path between
\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}\omega
and
\omega
as given by the homotopy
H
. Let us define a homotopy between this map and the identity. The idea is the same as the proof of point
6
in Proposition
2.42
. Let
V\subset I_{p}(c[-1,1]^{n})
be the set

\displaystyle V:=\{(h\mathbf{x},h,ht)\in I_{p}(c[-1,1]^{n})\,|\,2\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}-1\leq t\}

Then we define:

\displaystyle G_{|V}:V

\displaystyle\rightarrow X

\displaystyle(h\mathbf{x},h,ht)

\displaystyle\mapsto H(f(\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1},h),p_{X}(f(\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1},h))t)

That is, on each time slice
ht
we scale, apply
f
, then take the point along the homotopy
H
with appropriate rescaling with
p_{X}:X\rightarrow[1,\infty)
. On the complement of
V
we just "finish" the path between
\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}\omega
and
\omega
as given by the homotopy
H
. See Figure
13
. The concrete formula for
G|_{V^{c}}
is:

\displaystyle G|_{V^{c}}:I_{p}(c[-1,1]^{n})\setminus V

\displaystyle\rightarrow X

\displaystyle(h\mathbf{x},h,ht)

\displaystyle\mapsto H(\omega(h),p_{X}(\omega(h))(2\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}-1))

Figure 13:
The coarse homotopy
G
between
b_{\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}\omega,\omega}^{H}\mathfrak{b}_{*}\mathfrak{a}_{*}[f]
and
[f]
, restricted to height
h
.

Observe that the images agree on the intersection of
V
and the closure of its complement: on
V
for a fixed time
ht
and points satisfying
2\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}=t+1
we have that
\|\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1}\|_{\infty}=h
, and so these points get mapped to
H(\omega(h),p_{X}(\omega(h))t)
. The formula for
G|_{V^{c}}
also gives for points where
2\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}-1=t
the result
G|_{V^{c}}(h\mathbf{x},h,ht)=H(\omega(h),p_{X}(\omega(h))t)
, as claimed.

To show that this map is controlled, we again consider points first in
V
. Since
H
is coarse, it takes entourages to entourages. It therefore is sufficient to compute coarse estimates on
I_{p_{X}}(X)
. Let
(h\mathbf{x},h,ht)
and
(s\mathbf{y},s,sl)
be in
V
. We already know that:

\displaystyle\|\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1}-\frac{2s\mathbf{y}}{l+1}\|+|h-s|\leq(6n+1)|h-s|+2\|h\mathbf{x}-s\mathbf{y}\|+2n|ht-sl|

from point
6
of Proposition
2.42
. Since the composition of controlled maps is controlled and
p_{X}
is controlled, so is
p_{X}\circ f
. Let
D
be the large-scale Lipschitz constant for
p_{X}\circ f
.

\displaystyle|pf(

\displaystyle\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1},h)t-pf(\frac{2s\mathbf{y}}{l+1},s)l|

\displaystyle=|pf(\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1},h)t-pf(\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1},h)l+pf(\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1},h)l-pf(\frac{2s\mathbf{y}}{l+1},s)l|

\displaystyle\leq|t-l||pf(\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1},h)|+|pf(\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1},h)-pf(\frac{2s\mathbf{y}}{l+1},s)|

\displaystyle\leq|t-l||pf(\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1},h)|+D(\|\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1}-\frac{2s\mathbf{y}}{l+1}\|+|h-s|)+D

To bound the value of
|pf(\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1},h)|
with relation to
h
, we consider the point
(\frac{2\mathbf{x}}{t+1},1)
, the projection of
(\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1},h)
to the base of the cone. We have

\displaystyle\|(\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1},h)-(\frac{2\mathbf{x}}{t+1},1)\|=\frac{2(h-1)}{t+1}\|\mathbf{x}\|+(h-1)

\displaystyle|pf(\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1},h)|\leq|pf(\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1},h)-pf(\frac{2\mathbf{x}}{t+1},1)|+|pf(\frac{2\mathbf{x}}{t+1},1)|

Since
f(x_{0},1)=\omega(1)
for a point
x_{0}\in\partial[0,1]^{n}
and
(\frac{2\mathbf{x}}{t+1},1)
is distance at most
2n
from
(x_{0},1)
, there exists a constant
E
such that
|pf(\frac{2\mathbf{x}}{t+1},1)|\leq E
independent of
\mathbf{x}
and
t
.

\displaystyle|pf(\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1},h)|\leq D(\frac{2n}{t+1}(h-1)+(h-1))+D+E\leq(2n+1)Dh+D+E

Putting the computation together:

\displaystyle|pf(

\displaystyle\frac{2h\mathbf{x}}{t+1},h)t-pf(\frac{2s\mathbf{y}}{l+1},s)l|

\displaystyle\leq|t-l|((2n+1)Dh+D+E)+D((6n+1)|h-s|+2\|h\mathbf{x}-s\mathbf{y}\|+2n|ht-sl|)+D

\displaystyle\leq C(|h-s|+\|h\mathbf{x}-s\mathbf{y}\|+|ht-sl|)+C

For some
C>0
. These estimates are sufficient to show that
G
is controlled on
V
.

Let
B
be the large-scale Lipschitz constant for
\omega
,
B^{\prime}
for
p\circ\omega
. On the complement of
V
we that
|\omega(h)-\omega(s)|\leq B|h-s|+B
and

\displaystyle|p\omega(h)(2

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}-1)-p\omega(s)(2\|\mathbf{y}\|_{\infty}-1)|

\displaystyle\leq 2|p\omega(h)\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}-p\omega(s)\|\mathbf{y}\|_{\infty}|+|p\omega(h)-p\omega(s)|

\displaystyle\leq(1+2\|\mathbf{y}\|_{\infty})|p\omega(h)-p\omega(s)|+2p\omega(h)|\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}-\|\mathbf{y}\|_{\infty}|

\displaystyle\leq 3(B^{\prime}|h-s|+B^{\prime})+2p\omega(h)\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 3(B^{\prime}|h-s|+B^{\prime})+2(B^{\prime}h+B^{\prime}+p\omega(1))\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 3(B^{\prime}|h-s|+B^{\prime})+2B^{\prime}(\|h\mathbf{x}-s\mathbf{y}\|_{\infty}+|h-s|)+2(B^{\prime}+p\omega(1))

\displaystyle\leq C(\|h\mathbf{x}-s\mathbf{y}\|_{\infty}+|h-s|)+C

For some
C>0
. These estimates are sufficient to show that
G
is controlled on
V^{c}
.

To show that
G
is proper, let
K\subset X
be a bounded subset. Consider first
V^{c}
. For a fixed height
h
in
V^{c}
, its image under
G
has nonempty intersection with
K
if
H((\omega(h)\times[0,\infty))\cap I_{p}(X))\cap K\neq\emptyset
. Since
H
and
\omega
are proper, the
h^{\prime}s
for which this is true is contained within a bounded set
[1,l]
. Hence
V^{c}\cap G^{-1}(K)
is bounded. For
V
, a similar argument holds.
G|_{V}
is the composition of a rescaled version of the map
(h\mathbf{x},h,ht)\mapsto(f(h\mathbf{x},h),p_{X}f(h\mathbf{x},h)t)
and the homotopy
H
. Since
H
is proper,
H^{-1}(K)
is contained within
Z\times[0,\infty)\cap I_{p}(X)
for
Z
bounded. Now since
f
is proper,
G^{-1}(K)\cap V
is contained within a bounded set of heights
[1,l^{\prime}]
. Taking the maximum of
l,l^{\prime}
we see that
G^{-1}(K)
is bounded.

We now show that the composition

\displaystyle\pi_{n}^{c}(Y,\mathfrak{a}\omega)\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{b}_{*}}\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}\omega)\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{a}_{*}}\pi_{n}^{c}(Y,\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}\omega)\xrightarrow{b_{\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}\omega,\mathfrak{a}\omega}^{\tilde{H}}}\pi_{n}^{c}(Y,\mathfrak{a}\omega)

is the identity, which gives us that
\mathfrak{a}_{*}
is surjective. Let
[f]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(Y,\mathfrak{a}\omega)
. The image of
[f]
under the composition is represented by the following map: on
U
we have
\mathfrak{a}\circ\mathfrak{b}\circ f\circ\mathfrak{r}
and on the complement of
U
we travel radially outwards along the path between
\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}\omega
and
\mathfrak{a}\omega
given by image of the base ray
\mathfrak{a}\omega
under the homotopy
\tilde{H}
. The homotopy between this and the original
f
is exactly the same idea as the construction of
G
, and the proof of its coarseness is also identical. By point
6
of Proposition
2.42
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{c}(Y,\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{c}(Y,\mathfrak{a}\omega)\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}\omega,\omega}^{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}\omega,\mathfrak{a}\omega}^{\mathfrak{a}H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{*}}

commutes. Since the vertical arrows are isomorphisms,
\mathfrak{a}_{*}:\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{c}(Y,\mathfrak{a}\omega)
is also surjective. Combining the statements,
\mathfrak{a}_{*}
is an isomorphism.

For
n=0
, both compositions

\displaystyle\pi_{0}^{c}(X,\omega)\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{a}_{*}}\pi_{0}^{c}(Y,\mathfrak{a}\omega)\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{b}_{*}}\pi_{0}^{c}(X,\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}\omega)

\displaystyle\pi_{0}^{c}(Y,\mathfrak{a}\omega)\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{b}_{*}}\pi_{0}^{c}(X,\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}\omega)\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{a}_{*}}\pi_{0}^{c}(Y,\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}\omega)

are the identity. This is because for a base ray
[\tau]\in\pi^{c}_{0}(X,\omega)
, the map
\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}\tau
is coarsely homotopic to
\tau
. The other composition is analogous.

∎

This shows that coarse homotopy groups are invariant under coarse homotopy equivalence. This, combined with u-continuity, means we can make statements about the universal cone
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(X)
over a compact metric space
X
. This is the bornological coarse bornological space defined as

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(X):=\colim_{\phi}\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{\phi}(X)

for all
\mathcal{T}
-admissible functions
\phi:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(X\times X)
.

Corollary 2.44
.

The Euclidean cone
cX
has the same weak coarse homotopy type as
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(X)
.

Proof.

We know that
cX,c^{\prime}X
and
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{\phi_{1}}(X)
are coarsely homotopy equivalent. It is shown in the proof of Lemma
8.7
in
[
9
]
that if a
\mathcal{T}
-admissible function
\phi
takes values in
\mathcal{T}
(and not just
\mathcal{P}(X\times X)
), is monotone, and satisfies
\phi(0)=X\times X
, then for every
\mathcal{T}
-admissible function
\phi^{\prime}
such that
\phi(t)\subset\phi^{\prime}(t)
for all
t\in[0,\infty)
the canonical inclusion induced by the set-wise identity

\displaystyle k^{\phi^{\prime}}_{\phi}:\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{\phi}(X)\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{\phi^{\prime}}(X)

is a coarse homotopy equivalence. Furthermore, we have that

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(X)\simeq\colim_{\phi^{\prime}\geq\phi}\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{\phi}(X)

is an equivalence in
\mathbf{BornCoarse}
. The function
\phi=\phi_{1}
satisfies these conditions.

Let
\omega
be a coarse base ray in
cX
. Consider the based bornological coarse space
(cX,\omega)
. By invariance under coarse homotopy equivalence and u-continuity, we obtain

\displaystyle\pi^{c}_{n}(cX,\omega)\cong\pi^{c}_{n}(c^{\prime}X,\omega)\cong\pi^{c}_{n}(\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{\phi_{1}}(X),Log\omega)\cong\pi^{c}_{n}(\colim_{\phi^{\prime}\geq\phi_{1}}\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{\phi_{1}}(X),Log\omega)

\displaystyle=\pi^{c}_{n}(\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(X),Log\omega)

for all
n\geq 0
.
∎

3
Proof of
\varprojlim^{1}
sequence

The main theorem of this chapter is:

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
X
be a compact metric space,
x_{0}\in X
,
\omega
the standard parametrisation of
c\{x_{0}\}
,
n\geq 1
. There is a short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow{\varprojlim_{h}}^{1}\pi_{n+1}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,x_{0})\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)\rightarrow\check{\pi}_{n}(X,x_{0})\rightarrow 0

corresponding to a sequence of open covers
\mathcal{U}_{h}:=\cup_{z\in Z_{h}}B(z,\frac{1}{2^{h}})
of metric balls centered on a maximal
\frac{1}{2^{h+1}}
-separated subset
Z_{h}
of
X
.

In the following special case we have the stronger result:

Corollary 3.2
.

Let
X
be a compact metric space such that the open covers
\mathcal{U}_{h}
are good for sufficiently large
h
. Let
x_{0}\in X
,
\omega
the standard parametrisation of
c\{x_{0}\}
,
n\geq 1
. There is an isomorphism

\displaystyle\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)\cong\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})

3.1
Geometric setup

Before we prove the theorem, let us go through some preliminaries. Recall that
X
can be embedded isometrically into
C_{b}(X)
via the Kuratowski embedding
\Psi
(Definition
2.5
) such that
\|\Psi(x)\|<\frac{1}{4}
for all
x\in X
and
\Psi(x_{0})=0
. We identify
X
with its image in
C_{b}(X)
.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
(X,d)
be a metric space. A subset
Z\subset X
is
C
-separated if
d(x,y)>C
for all
x,y\in Z
.

Let
Z_{0}=\{x_{0}\}
. For
h\in\mathbb{N}
choose maximal
\frac{1}{2^{h+1}}
-separated subsets
Z_{h}
such that
Z_{h}\subset Z_{h+1}
for all
h
. Consider the open cover
\cup_{z\in Z_{h}}B(z,\frac{1}{2^{h}})=:\mathcal{U}_{h}
. The nerves of these open covers
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
correspond to the Rips complexes
R_{<}(Z_{h},\frac{1}{2^{h-1}})
. There are simplicial maps
\phi_{h+1}:|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
defined on vertices by the identity on
Z_{h}\subset Z_{h+1}
and by sending new points in
Z_{h+1}
to some point
z^{\prime}
in
Z_{h}
with distance
\leq\frac{1}{2^{h+1}}
away. These are well-defined since
\mathcal{U}_{h+1}
is a refinement of
\mathcal{U}_{h}
: a ball of radius
\frac{1}{2^{h+1}}
around
z\in Z_{h+1}
is completely contained in
B(z^{\prime},\frac{1}{2^{h}})
since for
d(z,y)<\frac{1}{2^{h+1}}
we have
d(y,z^{\prime})\leq d(y,z)+d(z,z^{\prime})<\frac{1}{2^{h+1}}+\frac{1}{2^{h+1}}=\frac{1}{2^{h}}
. These induce morphisms

\displaystyle\phi_{h+1}:\pi_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|,x_{0})\rightarrow\pi_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,x_{0})

and we define
\phi_{l,s}:\pi_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{l}|,x_{0})\rightarrow\pi_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{s}|,x_{0})
for
l>s
as
\phi_{s+1}\circ\dots\circ\phi_{l-1}\circ\phi_{l}
. The inverse limit
\varprojlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\pi_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,x_{0})
can be identified with the Čech (or shape) homotopy group
\check{\pi}_{n}(X,x_{0})=:\varprojlim_{\mathcal{U}}\pi_{n}(|\mathcal{U}|,x_{0})
(see Definition
4.1
). This is because
\{\mathcal{U}_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is cofinal with respect to all open covers of
X
.
X
is compact, so for any open cover
\mathcal{U}
there exists a finite subcover with some Lebesgue number
\varepsilon>0
. Choose
\frac{1}{2^{h}}<\varepsilon
and
\mathcal{U}_{h}
is a refinement of
\mathcal{U}
. We return to a more detailed discussion of the shape of
X
later.

Now we define the inverse mapping telescope, which is the main geometric object used in the proof. Consider the mapping cylinder of
\phi_{h}:|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|
.

\displaystyle(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times[2^{h},2^{h+1}])\cup_{|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times\{2^{h}\}}|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|

We glue these mapping cylinders together.

Definition 3.4
.

(The inverse mapping telescope
M
)

\displaystyle M:=|\mathcal{U}_{0}|\times[1,2]\cup_{\phi_{1}}|\mathcal{U}_{1}|\times[2,4]\cup_{\phi_{2}}\dots\cup_{\phi_{h}}|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times[2^{h},2^{h+1}]\cup_{\phi_{h+1}}\dots

Each simplex
\sigma
in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
is given the spherical metric (scaled such that the length of edges is
1
) and
M
is equipped with the largest path metric bounded above by the product metric on each
\sigma\times[2^{h},2^{h+1}]
: see Definition
13.1
.

As with the case of
cX
, we have canonical projections
p:M_{X}\rightarrow[1,\infty)
and
p^{\prime}:M_{X}\rightarrow\coprod_{h}|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
.

Definition 3.5
.

(Geometric simplices) Let
\{u_{0},\dots,u_{m}\}
be vertices in
M
. We say that
[u_{0},\dots,u_{m}]
is a
geometric simplex
if
\{u_{0},\dots,u_{m}\}
is a subset of the vertices in
\sigma\times I
for some simplex
\sigma
in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
. For
\tau,\sigma
geometric simplices, we say that
\tau
is a
geometric face
of
\sigma
if the vertices defining
\tau
are a subset of the vertices defining
\sigma
, ie.
vert(\tau)\subset vert(\sigma)
.

Definition 3.6
.

(Geometric cylinders) A
geometric cylinder
is a geometric simplex of the form
\sigma\times\{t\}
or
\sigma\times I
for some simplex
\sigma
in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
.

Definition 3.7
.

(Geometric stars) Let
u=(z,2^{h}+k)
be a vertex in
M
. We define the
open geometric star
of
u
in
M
, denoted by
\langle gst(u)\rangle
, as follows. If
1\leq k<2^{h}
(ie.
u
is not in a gluing component) then we let

\displaystyle\langle gst(u)\rangle=\langle st(z)\rangle\times(2^{h}+k-1,2^{h}+k+1)

where
\langle st(z)\rangle
denotes the open star of
z
in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
(see Figure
15
). If
u=(z^{\prime},2^{h})
then we define

\displaystyle\langle gst(u)\rangle=(\cup_{z\in\phi_{h}^{-1}(z^{\prime})}\langle st(z)\rangle\times[2^{h},2^{h}+1))\bigcup(\langle st(z^{\prime})\rangle\times(2^{h}-1,2^{h}])

where
\langle st(z)\rangle
denotes the open star in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
and
\langle st(z^{\prime})\rangle
the open star in
|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|
. (see Figure
15
). The
closed geometric star

|gst(u)|
is defined as the closure of
\langle gst(u)\rangle
.

Figure 14:
An open geometric star
\langle gst(z,t)\rangle
for
t=2^{h}+k
for
1\leq k<2^{h}
.

Figure 15:
An open geometric star
\langle gst(z,2^{h})\rangle
.

The idea of this definition is that the geometric star of
u
is the union of its stars over all possible choices of product CW-structure given the fixed vertex set and the geometric information of which vertices form a simplex in each
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
.

Remark
.

(Description of geometric simplices and stars in
M
) Let us restrict for a moment to heights in the interval
[2^{h},2^{h+1}]
. In
M
, this corresponds to the subset

\displaystyle(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times[2^{h},2^{h+1}])\cup_{\phi_{h}}|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|

A geometric simplex can be of the form
\sigma\times\{2^{h}+k\}
where
\sigma
is a simplex in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
and
1\leq k\leq 2^{h}
, or a simplex in
|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|
if
k=0
. Alternatively it is a subset of the vertices
\{u_{0},\dots,u_{j},u^{\prime}_{0},\dots,{u}^{\prime}_{j}\}
where the
u_{i}=(z_{i},2^{h}+k)
are vertices in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times\{2^{h}+k\}
for
1\leq k\leq 2^{h}
and
u^{\prime}_{i}=(z_{i},2^{h}+k-1)
refers to the same vertex in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times\{2^{h}+k-1\}
(which are identified with their images
(\phi_{h}(z_{i}),2^{h})\in|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|\times\{2^{h}\}
if
k=1
). Therefore, a set of vertices
\{u_{0},\dots,u_{m}\}
forms a geometric simplex in
(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times[2^{h},2^{h+1}])\cup_{\phi_{h}}|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|\subset M
exactly when their height variables differ by at most
1
and the following condition is satisfied: if
\{u_{0},\dots,u_{m}\}\subset|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times[2^{h}+1,2^{h+1}]
or
\{u_{0},\dots,u_{m}\}\subset|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|\times\{2^{h}\}
then their projections to
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
(resp.
|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|
) must span a simplex. Otherwise the vertices are of the form
\{u_{0},\dots,u_{k},w_{k+1},\dots,w_{m}\}\subset(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times[2^{h},2^{h}+1])\cup_{\phi_{h}}|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|
where
u_{i}=(z_{i},2^{h}+1)
for
0\leq i\leq k
and
w_{i}=(z^{\prime}_{i},2^{h})
for
k+1\leq i\leq m
. They define a geometric simplex exactly when there exist vertices
z_{k+1},\dots,z_{m}
such that
z_{i}\in\phi^{-1}_{h}(z^{\prime}_{i})
for
k+1\leq i\leq m
and
\{z_{0},\dots,z_{k},z_{k+1},\dots,z_{m}\}
spans a simplex
\sigma\subset|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
. In this case,
\{u_{0},\dots,u_{k},w_{k+1},\dots,w_{m}\}
is a subset of
\sigma\times I=sp\{z_{0},\dots,z_{k},z_{k+1},\dots,z_{m}\}\times[2^{h},2^{h}+1]
, where
sp(\cdot)
denotes "the simplex spanned by". See Figure
16
.

Notation
.

From now on, we denote by
M_{[a,b]}
the mapping telescope with heights in
[a,b]
. For example, we refer to the set
(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times[2^{h},2^{h+1}])\cup_{\phi_{h}}|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|\subset M
as
M_{[2^{h},2^{h+1}]}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

For a set of vertices
\{u_{0},\dots u_{m}\}
, a geometric simplex
[u_{0},\dots,u_{m}]
exists exactly when
\cap_{i=0}^{m}\langle gst(u_{i})\rangle\neq\emptyset

Proof.

Let
u_{i}=(z_{i},t_{i})
for
0\leq i\leq m
. Observe that
\langle gst(z_{i},t_{i})\rangle\cap\langle gst(z_{j},t_{j})\rangle\neq\emptyset
only if
|t_{i}-t_{j}|\in\{0,1\}
for all
0\leq i,j\leq m
. If
t_{i}=2^{h}+k
for some
1\leq k<2^{h}
and all
i
then this condition reduces to
\cap_{i=0}^{m}\langle st(z_{i})\rangle\neq\emptyset
, which is equivalent to the existence of a simplex
sp\{z_{0},\dots,z_{m}\}
in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
.

If
t_{i}=2^{h}
for all
i
and
\{z_{0},\dots,z_{m}\}
spans a simplex then clearly their geometric stars intersect. Conversely, if
\cap_{i=0}^{m}\langle gst(z_{i},t_{i})\rangle\neq\emptyset
that means that either
\cap_{i=0}^{m}\langle st(z_{i})\rangle\times(2^{h}-1,2^{h}]\neq\emptyset
or
\cap_{i=0}^{m}(\cup_{z\in\phi_{h}^{-1}(z_{i})}\langle st(z)\rangle\times[2^{h},2^{h}+1))\neq\emptyset
. In either case, we conclude that
\cap_{i=0}^{m}\langle st(z_{i})\rangle\neq\emptyset
and therefore
\{z_{0},\dots,z_{m}\}
spans a simplex in
|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|
.

If
t_{i}\in\{2^{h}+k,2^{h}+k+1\}
for some
1\leq k<2^{h}
and all
i
we are reduced again to the condition that
\cap_{i=0}^{m}\langle st(z_{i})\rangle\neq\emptyset
. Finally, if
t_{i}\in\{2^{h},2^{h}+1\}
and
\cap_{i=0}^{m}\langle gst(z_{i},t_{i})\rangle\neq\emptyset
it means that

\displaystyle\cap_{t_{i}=2^{h}+1}\langle st(z_{i})\rangle\times(2^{h},2^{h}+1]\bigcap\cap_{t_{j}=2^{h}}\cup_{y\in\phi_{h}^{-1}(z_{j})}\langle st(y)\rangle\times[2^{h},2^{h}+1)\neq\emptyset

Staring at this for a while, one concludes that the definition of a geometric simplex is satisfied. Namely, there exist vertices
y_{j}\in\phi_{h}^{-1}(z_{j})
for
t_{j}=2^{h}
such that the vertices
\cup_{t_{i}=2^{h}+1}z_{i}\bigcup\cup_{t_{j}=2^{h}}y_{j}
span a simplex in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
. The converse follows from the definition of geometric simplices. See Figure
18
.
∎

Definition 3.9
.

(Cores, spanning cylinders and neighbourhoods) Let
\Delta=[u_{0},\dots u_{m}]
be a geometric simplex.

•

The
geometric core
of
\Delta
, denoted by
\mathscr{C}_{\Delta}
, is defined as follows: if
\Delta
lies on one height, let
\mathscr{C}_{\Delta}=\Delta
. Otherwise,
\Delta=[x_{0},\dots,x_{k},w_{k+1},\dots,w_{m}]
, and we define

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{\Delta}=([x_{0},\dots,x_{k}]\times I)\cup_{[x_{0},\dots,x_{k}]\times\{0\}}sp\{q(x_{0}),\dots,q(x_{k}),w_{k+1},\dots,w_{m}\}

where
q
denotes the projection to the bottom of
\Delta
, ie.
q:|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times\{2^{h}+k\}\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times\{2^{h}+k-1\}
for some
h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
1\leq k\leq 2^{h}
. Note that
q
can be identified with the identity for
k>1
and the gluing map
\phi_{h}
for
k=1
.

•

A
geometric cylinder spanned by

\Delta
, denoted by
\eta_{\Delta}
, is defined as follows: if
\Delta
lies on one height, let
\eta_{\Delta}=\Delta
. For
\Delta=[x_{0},\dots,x_{k},w_{k+1},\dots,w_{m}]
we let

\displaystyle\eta_{\Delta}=([x_{0},\dots,x_{k},y_{k+1},\dots,y_{m}]\times I)\cup sp\{q(x_{0}),\dots,q(x_{k}),w_{k+1},\dots,w_{m}\}

for a choice of
y_{i}\in q^{-1}(w_{i})
for
k+1\leq i\leq m
. See Figure
17
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\Delta}
denote the set of geometric cylinders spanned by
\Delta
.

•

The
geometric neighbhourhood
of
\Delta
, denoted by
\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
, is defined as follows: if
\Delta=[(z_{0},2^{h}+k),\dots,(z_{m},2^{h}+k)]
lies on one height, we let

\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}:=\begin{cases}cl(\cap_{i=0}^{m}\langle st(z_{i})\rangle)\times(2^{h}+k-1,2^{h}+k+1)&1\leq k<2^{h}\\
\big(cl(\cap_{i=0}^{m}\langle st(z_{i})\rangle)\times(2^{h}-1,2^{h}]\big)\bigcup\\
\big(cl(\cap_{i=0}^{m}\cup_{y\in\phi_{h}^{-1}(z_{i})}\langle st(y)\rangle)\times[2^{h},2^{h}+1)\big)&k=0\\
\end{cases}

Note that in this case

\displaystyle\bigcap_{i=0}^{m}\langle gst(u_{i})\rangle\subset\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}\subset cl(\bigcap_{i=0}^{m}\langle gst(u_{i})\rangle)

If
\Delta
does not lie on one height, we define
\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
as the closure of the intersection of geometric stars

\displaystyle cl(\bigcap_{i=0}^{m}\langle gst(u_{i})\rangle)

See Figure
17
.

Figure 16:
K:=([u_{0},u_{1},u_{2}]\times I)\cup([u_{0},u_{1},u_{3}]\times I)\cup[u^{\prime}_{0},u^{\prime}_{1},u^{\prime}_{2}]
. Geometric simplices of
K
are all subsets of
\{u_{0},u_{1},u_{2},u^{\prime}_{0},u^{\prime}_{1},u^{\prime}_{2}\}
and all subsets of
\{u_{0},u_{1},u_{3},u^{\prime}_{0},u^{\prime}_{1},u^{\prime}_{2}\}
.

Figure 17:
The geometric core
\mathscr{C}_{\Delta}=([u_{0},u_{1}]\times I)\cup[u^{\prime}_{0},u^{\prime}_{1},u^{\prime}_{2}]
of
\Delta:=[u_{0},u_{1},u^{\prime}_{2}]
in
K
. Geometric cylinders
\eta_{\Delta}
are
([u_{0},u_{1},u_{2}]\times I)\cup[u^{\prime}_{0},u^{\prime}_{1},u^{\prime}_{2}]
and
([u_{0},u_{1},u_{3}]\times I)\cup[u^{\prime}_{0},u^{\prime}_{1},u^{\prime}_{2}]
.

Figure 18:
\sigma=[w,u_{0},u_{1},u_{2}],q\sigma=[w^{\prime},u^{\prime}_{0},u^{\prime}_{1},u^{\prime}_{2}]
. Let
K^{\prime}=K\cup(\sigma\times I\cup q\sigma)
.
w\in\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}

Notation
.

A geometric simplex can be written in the form
[\sigma,\tau]
, where
\sigma
is a simplex in some
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times\{2^{h}+k\}
for
1\leq k\leq 2^{h}
with
\tau
is a simplex at height
2^{h}+k-1
. If the geometric simplex lies on one height, then either
\sigma
or
\tau
is empty. If
\tau\neq\emptyset
, we can write
\mathscr{C}_{[\sigma,\tau]}=(\sigma\times I)\cup sp\{q\sigma,\tau\}
. Likewise
\eta_{[\sigma,\tau]}=(sp\{\sigma,\delta\}\times I)\cup sp\{q\sigma,\tau\}
for a choice of simplex
\delta\in q^{-1}\tau
. This is an abuse of notation, since we identify
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times\{2^{h}+k\}\times[0,1]
with
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times[2^{h}+k-1,2^{h}+k]
.

We say that a geometric simplex has type
1
if all its vertices lie on one height, and type
2
otherwise.

For a simplex
\Delta=[\sigma,\tau]
of type
2
, we denote by
\mathcal{I}_{0}\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
the subset of
\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
without the simplices at the height of
\tau
. Likewise, we denote by
\mathcal{I}_{1}\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
the subset of
\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
without the simplices at the height of
\sigma
.

The following are easy observations:

•

\bigcup_{\eta_{\Delta}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Delta}}\eta_{\Delta}\subset\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
.

•

\mathscr{C}_{\eta_{\Delta}}=\eta_{\Delta}
.

•

\mathscr{C}_{\Delta}\subset\bigcap_{\eta_{\Delta}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Delta}}\eta_{\Delta}
.

•

If
\Delta^{\prime}
is a face of
\Delta
, we have
\mathscr{C}_{\Delta^{\prime}}\subset\mathscr{C}_{\Delta}
.

•

If
\Delta^{\prime}
is a face of
\Delta
, we have
\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}\subset cl(\mathcal{N}_{\Delta^{\prime}})
. If
\Delta^{\prime},\Delta
are both type
1
or both type
2
we have
\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}\subset\mathcal{N}_{\Delta^{\prime}}
.

•

For a simplex
\Delta=[\sigma,\tau]
of type 2, a point
(x,t)\in(0,1]
lies in
\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
if and only if
x\in\mu
,
\mu
a simplex which contains
sp\{\sigma,\delta\}
as a face, for some simplex
\delta\in q^{-1}(\tau)
. A point
(x,0)
lies in
\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
if and only if
x\in\mu
,
\mu
a simplex which contains
sp\{q\sigma,\tau\}
as a face.

•

For a simplex
\Delta=[\sigma,\tau]
of type 2 with
\Delta^{\prime}=[\sigma^{\prime},\emptyset]
(resp.
[\emptyset,\tau^{\prime}]
) a face of
\Delta
, we have
\mathcal{I}_{0}\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}\subset\mathcal{N}_{\Delta^{\prime}}
(resp.
\mathcal{I}_{1}\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}\subset\mathcal{N}_{\Delta^{\prime}}
).

We now prove some useful properties about neighbourhoods and cores.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
\Delta=[\sigma,\tau]
be a geometric simplex and let
\Delta^{\prime}=[\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}]
be a face of
\Delta
, ie.
vert(\tau^{\prime})\subset vert(\tau),vert(\sigma^{\prime})\subset vert\sigma
.

1.

Let
\Delta
be equipped with the path metric induced from the standard (
L^{1}
) metric on
\sigma,\tau
, and the product metric on
\sigma\times I
. There is a
2
-Lipschitz deformation retract
H^{\mathscr{C}_{\Delta}}:\mathscr{C}_{\Delta}\times[0,1]\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{\Delta}
of
\mathscr{C}_{\Delta}
to a point.

2.

There is a deformation retract
H^{\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}}:\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}\times[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
of
\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
to a point.

3.

Suppose that
\Delta
is a geometric simplex with
\Delta^{\prime}
as a face. The deformation retract of
\mathcal{N}_{\Delta^{\prime}}
restricted to
\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\Delta^{\prime}}
has image in
\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
.

Proof.

1.

For a simplex of type
1
, we simply take the standard deformation retract of a simplex to its barycentre. Otherwise, we define the homotopies

\displaystyle H:\mathscr{C}_{\Delta}\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{\Delta}

\displaystyle([x,s],t)

\displaystyle\mapsto[x,(1-t)s]

\displaystyle([y,0],t)

\displaystyle\mapsto[y,0]

\displaystyle H^{\prime}:sp\{q\sigma,\tau\}\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\rightarrow sp\{q\sigma,\tau\}

\displaystyle(y,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto(1-t)y+tb_{sp\{q\sigma,\tau\}}

where
b_{sp\{q\sigma,\tau\}}
denotes the barycentre of
sp\{q\sigma,\tau\}
. The composition
H\ast(H^{\prime}\circ(H_{1}\times\id))
is a deformation retract of
\mathscr{C}_{\Delta}
to the barycentre of
sp\{q\sigma,\tau\}
. It is
2
-Lipschitz in the time variable and
1
-Lipschitz in the
\mathscr{C}_{\Delta}
-variable (since the bonding maps
\phi_{h}
are distance-decreasing).

2.

If
\Delta=[u_{0},\dots,u_{m}]
is type
1
, with
u_{i}=(z_{i},t)
for all
0\leq i\leq m
, then there is first an obvious deformation retract of
\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
to
cl(\cap_{i=0}^{m}\langle st(z_{i})\rangle)\times\{t\}
, which we denote by
G
. Then we can deformation retract to the barycentre of
[z_{0},\dots,z_{m}]\times\{t\}
by a linear homotopy
G^{\prime}
.

If
\Delta
is type
2
, then it has the form
[x_{0},\dots,x_{j},w_{j+1},\dots,w_{m}]
, where the
w
’s lie on a lower height. In this case
\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
deformation retracts to the bottom slice (which we denote by
G
), and then to the barycentre of the projection

\displaystyle[q(x_{0}),\dots,q(x_{j}),w_{j+1},\dots,w_{m}]

via a homotopy
G^{\prime}
, where
q
is the identity or
\phi
, depending on the height (see Figure
19
). To see that this works, let
(x,t)
be a point in
\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
for
t\in(0,1]
.
x
lies in a simplex
\tau
contained within the closure of

\displaystyle\cap_{i=0}^{j}\langle st(x_{i})\rangle\bigcap\cap_{i=j+1}^{m}\langle st(y_{i})\rangle

for some
y_{i}\in q^{-1}(w_{i})
for
j+1\leq i\leq m
. This is equivalent to
\tau
having
[x_{0},\dots,x_{j},y_{j+1},\dots,y_{m}]
as a face. Since the bonding maps
\phi
are simplicial, we have that image of
\tau
under the deformation retract to the bottom slice
t=0
always lies in the closure of

\displaystyle\cap_{i=0}^{j}\langle st(q(x_{i}))\rangle\bigcap\cap_{i=j+1}^{m}\langle st(w_{i})\rangle

So, the barycentre of
[q(x_{0}),\dots,q(x_{j}),w_{j+1},\dots,w_{m}]
can be joined to
q(x)
by a straight line within a simplex.

If
\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
is equipped with the path metric induced from the standard, product metric on each simplex
\sigma\times I
, then
H^{\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}}:=G\ast(G^{\prime}\circ G_{1})
is
2
-Lipschitz.

3.

If
\Delta=[u_{0},\dots,u_{m}]
is type
1
, then
u_{i}=(z_{i},t)
for all
0\leq i\leq m
and
vert\Delta^{\prime}\subset\{u_{0},\dots,u_{m}\}
. Let
H^{\mathcal{N}_{\Delta^{\prime}}}=G\ast(G^{\prime}\circ G_{1})
be the concatenation of two homotopies described in point
2.
above. It is clear that
G_{|\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}\times[0,1]}(\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}\times[0,1])\subset\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
. For
G^{\prime}
, the straight line between
x\in cl(\cap_{i=0}^{m}\langle st(z_{i})\rangle)\times\{t\}
and the barycentre of
\Delta^{\prime}
is contained within
cl(\cap_{i=0}^{m}\langle st(z_{i})\rangle)\times\{t\}
since
x
lies within a simplex
\tau
which contains
\Delta^{\prime}
as a face, and simplices are convex. The argument for
\Delta^{\prime}
being type
2
is analogous.

Suppose that
\Delta=[\sigma,\tau]
is type
2
and
\Delta^{\prime}=[\sigma^{\prime},\emptyset]
is type
1
. Then
\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\Delta^{\prime}}=\mathcal{I}_{0}\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\Delta^{\prime}}=\mathcal{I}_{0}\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
. It is easy to see that
G_{|\mathcal{I}_{0}\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}\times[0,1]}
has image in
\mathcal{I}_{0}\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
. For a point
(x,t)\in\mathcal{I}_{0}\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
, with
x\in\mu
,
\mu
a simplex containing
sp\{\sigma,\delta\}
for some simplex
\delta\in q^{-1}(\tau)
,
G_{1}(x,t)\in sp\{\sigma,\delta\}
. As before, the straight line between
G_{1}(x,t)
and
b_{\sigma^{\prime}}
lies in
\mu\subset\mathcal{I}_{0}\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
. The argument for
\Delta^{\prime}=[\emptyset,\tau^{\prime}]
is analogous.

∎

Remark
.

The reason why we had to define geometric neighbourhoods
\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}
differently depending on the type of
\Delta
is because if
\Delta
is type
1
at height
2^{h}+1
, the closure
cl(\mathcal{N}_{\Delta})
includes a gluing slice at height
2^{h}
, so does not deformation retract to height
2^{h}+1
.

Figure 19:
The image of the set
A\times[0,1]
under
H^{\mathcal{N}_{\Delta}}
.
\sigma=[w,u_{0},u_{1},u_{2}]
,

q\sigma=[w^{\prime},u^{\prime}_{0},u^{\prime}_{1},u^{\prime}_{2}]
,
\Delta=[u_{0},u_{1},u^{\prime}_{2}]

We now digress into a short discussion about shrinking maps, which will be useful in many parts of the rest of the arguments:

Definition 3.11
.

(Shrinking maps) Let
\gamma:[1,\infty)\rightarrow[1,\infty)
be a coarse map such that
\gamma(h)\leq h
. We define
s_{\gamma}:cX\rightarrow cX
as

\displaystyle s_{\gamma}:cX

\displaystyle\rightarrow cX

\displaystyle(hx,h)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\gamma(h)x,\gamma(h))

We call
s_{\gamma}
the
shrinking map associated to
\gamma
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
s_{\gamma},s_{\gamma^{\prime}}
be two shrinking maps. There is a linear coarse homotopy
G:I_{p}(cX)\rightarrow cX
between
s_{\gamma}
and
s_{\gamma^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Since
[1,\infty)
is a path metric space,
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
are large-scale Lipschitz. So there exist constants
C_{\gamma},C_{\gamma^{\prime}}>1
such that
|\gamma(t)-\gamma(s)|<C_{\gamma}|t-s|+C_{\gamma}
for all
t,s\in[1,\infty)
and similarly for
\gamma^{\prime}
. For simplicity’s sake, define
\eta(h,t)=t\gamma^{\prime}(h)+(1-t)\gamma(h)
. Consider the homotopy

\displaystyle G:I_{p}(cX)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow cX

\displaystyle(hx,ht,h)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\eta(h,t)x,\eta(h,t))

This is a homotopy between
s_{\gamma}
(when
t=0
) and
s_{\gamma^{\prime}}
(when
t=1
). We now show that
G
is a coarse map.

\displaystyle d_{cX}(G(hx,ht,h),G(sy,sl,s))

\displaystyle\leq\|\eta(h,t)x-\eta(h,t)y\|+|\eta(h,t)-\eta(s,l)|(\|y\|+1)

\displaystyle<\eta(h,t)\|x-y\|+2|\eta(h,t)-\eta(s,l)|

\displaystyle\eta(h,t)\|x-y\|

\displaystyle\leq h\|x-y\|\leq\|hx-sy\|+|h-s|\|y\|

\displaystyle<\|hx-sy\|+|h-s|

\displaystyle|\eta(h,t)-\eta(s,l)|

\displaystyle\leq|\eta(h,t)-\eta(s,t)|+|\eta(s,t)-\eta(s,l)|

\displaystyle|\eta(h,t)-\eta(s,t)|

\displaystyle\leq t|\gamma^{\prime}(h)-\gamma^{\prime}(s)|+(1-t)|\gamma(h)-\gamma(s)|

\displaystyle<(C_{\gamma^{\prime}}+C_{\gamma})|h-s|+(C_{\gamma^{\prime}}+C_{\gamma})

\displaystyle|\eta(s,t)-\eta(s,l)|

\displaystyle\leq|t-l|(\gamma^{\prime}(s)+\gamma(s))

\displaystyle<|t-l|(C_{\gamma^{\prime}}s+C_{\gamma^{\prime}}+C_{\gamma}s+C_{\gamma})

\displaystyle\leq C_{\gamma^{\prime}}|st-sl|+C_{\gamma}|st-sl|+(C_{\gamma^{\prime}}+C_{\gamma})

\displaystyle\leq(C_{\gamma^{\prime}}+C_{\gamma})(|ht-sl|+|h-s|)+(C_{\gamma^{\prime}}+C_{\gamma})

These estimates are sufficient to show that
G
is controlled.
G
is proper because
\eta:[1,\infty)\times[0,1]\rightarrow[1,\infty)
is proper: for a bounded interval
[1,h^{\prime}]
there exists a
l
such that
\gamma(h)>h^{\prime}
and
\gamma^{\prime}(h)>h^{\prime}
for all
h>l
. Therefore
\eta^{-1}[1,h^{\prime}]\subset[1,l]\times[0,1]
and
G^{-1}(c_{[1,h^{\prime}]}X)\subset c_{[1,l]}(X\times[0,1])
. Therefore
G
is coarse.
∎

In particular, it means that any shrinking map
s_{\gamma}
is coarsely homotopic to the identity. Observe that the proof also applies to any
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
coarse and not necessarily shrinking, since there exist constants
D_{\gamma}>C_{\gamma}
,
D_{\gamma^{\prime}}>C_{\gamma^{\prime}}
such that
\gamma(h)<D_{\gamma}h+D_{\gamma}
and
\gamma^{\prime}(h)<D_{\gamma^{\prime}}h+D_{\gamma^{\prime}}
for all
h\in[1,\infty)
. So we have

\displaystyle\eta(h,t)<t(D_{\gamma^{\prime}}h+D_{\gamma^{\prime}})+(1-t)(D_{\gamma}h+D_{\gamma})<Dh+D

where
D
is the maximum of the two constants. The coarseness of
G
follows. This concludes the digression.

We now define the maps
i:cX\rightarrow M_{X}
and
R:M_{X}\rightarrow cX
. Let
\rho_{h}=\{\rho_{U}\}_{U\in\mathcal{U}_{h}}
be a partition of unity subordinate to the cover
\mathcal{U}_{h}
. Define
\varphi_{h}:X\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
by sending a point
x\in X
to its image
\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}_{h}}\rho_{U}(x)[U]
in barycentric co-ordinates.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
i:cX\rightarrow M
be a map defined piecewise as follows:

\displaystyle i_{h}:X\times(2^{h},2^{h+1}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times(2^{h},2^{h+1}]

\displaystyle(tx,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\varphi_{h}(x),t)

Denote by
i
as the gluing of these maps.

Observe that
i
is not continuous. Nonetheless, it is useful for the following reason:

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
U\subset cX
be a set of diameter
<Q
. There exists a shrinking map
s
such that
i\circ s(U)
is contained within the geometric star of a vertex in
M
.

Proof.

Let
r\in\mathbb{N}
be large enough such that
\frac{Q}{2^{r}}<\frac{1}{4}
. Let
s_{r}:cX\rightarrow cX
be the shrinking map that divides everything by
2^{r}
:

s_{r}(tx,t)=\begin{cases}(\frac{t}{2^{r}}x,\frac{t}{2^{r}})&t\geq 2^{r}\\
(x,1)&t\in[1,2^{r}]\\
\end{cases}

This is obviously
\frac{1}{2^{r}}
-Lipschitz, so
diams_{r}(U)<\frac{Q}{2^{r}}
. From this we know that
s_{r}(U)
intersects at most one gluing component. Assume first that
s_{r}(U)\subset X\times[2^{h},2^{h+1}]
for some
h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Let
(tx,t),(sy,s)\in s_{r}(U)
. By a simple calculation

\displaystyle t\|x-y\|=\|tx-sy+sy-ty\|\leq\|tx-sy\|+|t-s|\|y\|

\displaystyle\|x-y\|\leq\frac{1}{t}(\|tx-sy\|+|t-s|\|y\|)<\frac{Q}{2^{h+r}}<\frac{1}{2^{h+2}}

Hence, the points projected onto
X
are distance less than the Lebesgue number of
\mathcal{U}_{h}
(which is
\frac{1}{2^{h+1}}
) and therefore
i\circ s_{r}(U)
is contained within the geometric star of a vertex in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times[2^{h},2^{h+1}]\subset M_{[2^{h},2^{h+1}]}
.

Suppose now that
s_{r}(U)\subset X\times(2^{h}-1,2^{h}+1)
with
(tx,t)\in s_{r}(U)\cap c_{[2^{h},2^{h+1})}X
. By the same calculation as before, we have that
\|x-y\|<\frac{1}{2^{h+2}}
for all
(sy,s)\in s_{r}(U)
. Therefore the projection of
s_{r}(U)
onto
X
has diameter
<\frac{1}{2^{h+1}}
. So there exists a
z\in Z_{h}
such that
y\in B(z,\frac{1}{2^{h}})
for all
(sy,s)\in s_{r}(U)
. We have that
i(sy,s)=i_{h}(sy,s)=(\varphi_{h}(y),s)
or
i(sy,s)=i_{h-1}(sy,s)=(\varphi_{h-1}(y),s)
depending on the height
s
. Let
z^{\prime}=\phi_{h}(z)
. It is now clear that
i(sy,s)\in\langle gst([B(z^{\prime},\frac{1}{2^{h-1}})],2^{h})\rangle
where
[B(z^{\prime},\frac{1}{2^{h-1}})]
denotes the vertex in
|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|
.
∎

Notation
.

From now on we identify points
z\in Z_{h}
with the vertices
[B(z,2^{h})]
in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
.

Definition 3.15
.

Let
R:M\rightarrow cX
be defined piecewise

\displaystyle R_{h}:|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times(2^{h},2^{h+1}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow X\times(2^{h},2^{h+1}]

by sending points
(z,t),z\in vert|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
to
(tz,t)
for
z\in Z_{h}\subset X
. For
x\in|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times\{t\}
,
x
in the interior of some simplex
\sigma\subset|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
, we send
(x,t)
to a point
(y,t)
where
y\in\cap_{z\in vert(\sigma)}B(z,2^{h})
.

The resulting map
R
is again not continuous, but it is coarse: for this, it suffices to prove the distance estimate for points lying in a common geometric cylinder, since
M
is a path metric space induced from the spherical metric on each
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times\{t\}
(for a discussion on why the spherical metric is necessary, see Subsection
13.4
in the Appendix). Let
\sigma\times I\subset|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times[2^{h}+1,2^{h+1}]
be a geometric cylinder. Vertices are sent to points whose projection to
X
are distance
<\frac{1}{2^{h-1}}
from each other. Interior points are sent to points whose projection are distance
<\frac{1}{2^{h}}
from the image of any vertex. Hence, the set
p^{\prime}R(\sigma\times I)
has diameter
<\frac{1}{2^{h-1}}
. Suppose that
(tx,t),(sy,s)
are two points in
R(\sigma\times I)
. We have

\displaystyle\|tx-sy\|+|t-s|\leq\|tx-sx\|+\|sx-sy\|+|t-s|=|t-s|\|x\|+s\|x-y\|+|t-s|

\displaystyle<\|x\|+2^{h+1}\frac{1}{2^{h-1}}+1<\frac{21}{4}

Now let
\sigma\times I=[z_{0},\dots,z_{m},\phi_{h}(z_{0}),\dots,\phi_{h}(z_{m})]\subset(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times[2^{h},2^{h}+1])\cup_{\phi_{h}}|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|
be a geometric cylinder. All points in
R(\sigma\times I)
, after projecting to
X
, are distance
\leq\frac{1}{2^{h-1}}
from some
\phi_{h}(z_{i})
. The vertices
\phi_{h}(z_{i})
are sent to distance
<\frac{1}{2^{h-2}}
from one another. Hence, the set
p^{\prime}R(\sigma\times I)
has diameter
<\frac{1}{2^{h-2}}+\frac{2}{2^{h-1}}=\frac{1}{2^{h-3}}
. Suppose that
(tx,t),(sy,s)
are two points in
R(\sigma)
. We have

\displaystyle\|tx-sy\|+|t-s|<\|x\|+2^{h+1}\frac{1}{2^{h-3}}+1<\frac{69}{4}

Hence,
R(\sigma\times I)
has diameter
<\frac{69}{4}
. This shows that
R
is controlled. It is height preserving, and therefore proper.

Let
(tx,t)\in X\times(2^{h},2^{h+1}]
. The composition
R\circ i
sends
(tx,t)
to a point
(tz,t)
which is at most distance
\frac{1}{2^{h-1}}\cdot 2^{h+1}=4
away. It is therefore close to the identity.

The composition
i\circ R:M\rightarrow M
sends vertices
(z,t)\in M
to something in the star
\langle st(z)\rangle\times\{t\}
, and it sends interiors of simplices
(x,t)
,
x\in\tau
into the intersection of open stars of its vertices
\cap_{z\in vert\tau}\langle st(z)\rangle\times\{t\}
. Therefore,
i\circ R
distance less the maximum diameter of a simplex at a single height in
M
, which is
1
, away from the identity.

3.2
Isomorphism to Lipschitz end homotopy groups

Let us now define the Lipschitz end homotopy groups.

Definition 3.16
.

(Coarse-Lipschitz homotopy) Let
X
be a proper metric space with Lipschitz projection map
p:X\rightarrow[1,\infty)
and a proper Lipschitz base ray
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow X
. We define the
coarse-Lipschitz cylinder

\displaystyle I_{p}(X):=\{(x,t)\in X\times[0,\infty)\,|\,t\leq p(x)\}

with inclusions
i_{0}:X\rightarrow I_{p}(X)
and
i_{1}:X\rightarrow I_{p}(X)
defined by
i_{0}(x)=(x,0)
and
i_{1}(x)=(x,p(x))
.

Let
X
and
Y
be proper metric spaces with projections. A
coarse-Lipschitz homotopy
between two proper Lipschitz maps
f_{0},f_{1}:X\rightarrow Y
is a proper Lipschitz map
H:I_{p}(X)\rightarrow Y
such that
f_{0}=H\circ i_{0}
and
f_{1}=H\circ i_{1}
.

As usual, for the cone
cX
of a compact metric space
X
, we can assume that
p
is the height projection.

Definition 3.17
.

(Lipschitz end homotopy groups) Let
X
be a proper metric space with proper Lipschitz base ray
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow X
. For
n\geq 1
we define the
n
-th Lipschitz end homotopy group

\pi_{n}^{L,e}(X,\omega)
to be the set of relative coarse-Lipschitz homotopy classes of proper Lipschitz maps

\displaystyle f:(c[0,1]^{n},c\partial[0,1]^{n})\rightarrow(X,\omega[1,\infty))

such that
f|_{c\partial[0,1]^{n}}=\omega\circ p
where
p:c[0,1]^{n}\rightarrow[1,\infty);(hx,h)\mapsto h
denotes the height variable of the cone.

We define the
0
-th coarse-Lipschitz homotopy set

\pi_{0}^{L,e}(X)
to be the set of coarse-Lipschitz homotopy classes of proper Lipschitz maps from
[1,\infty)
to
X
.

Many of the properties of coarse homotopy groups are directly applicable to Lipschitz end homotopy groups: the group multiplication is the same formula, they are functorial under proper Lipschitz maps with coarse-Lipschitz homotopic maps inducing the same morphism. There is a long exact sequence for pairs
(X,A)
with proper Lipschitz map
k:A\rightarrow X
and proper Lipschitz base ray
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow A
. The results of Proposition
2.42
apply verbatim if one exchanges "coarse map" with "proper Lipschitz map" and "coarse homotopy" with "coarse-Lipschitz homotopy", since all the other homotopies used in the proofs are coarse-Lipschitz. The details are left as an exercise to the reader. We will not be needing many of these properties as the coarse-Lipschitz homotopy groups themselves are only an intermediary construction in our proofs.

Consider now the base ray
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow cX
defined as
\omega(t)=(tx_{0},t)=(0,t)
. We call this the
standard parametrisation of the base ray
c\{x_{0}\}
. By construction,
x_{0}\in Z_{h}
