\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}\xleftarrow{\cdot 2}\mathbb{Z}\xleftarrow{\cdot 2}\mathbb{Z}\xleftarrow{\cdot 2}\mathbb{Z}\xleftarrow{\cdot 2}\dots

where
\cdot 2
denotes multiplication by
2
. Since there is no integer divisible by every power of
2
, we obtain
\varprojlim_{k}\pi_{1}(X_{k},1)=0
.

Since
S^{1}
is connected, we have
\varprojlim_{k}\pi_{0}(X_{k},1)=\{\ast\}
. However,
\pi_{0}^{c}(c\Lambda,\omega)\cong\varprojlim^{1}_{k}\pi_{1}(X_{k},1)\neq 0
. In fact,
\varprojlim^{1}_{k}\pi_{1}(X_{k},1)
has cardinality
\mathbb{R}
(Example
17.7.2
in
[
22
]
). Therefore

\pi_{n}^{c}(c\Lambda,\omega)\cong\begin{cases}0&n\geq 1\\
\mathbb{R}&n=0\\
\end{cases}

4.

Shrinking wedge of spaces
: If
X=\vee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}X_{j}
is a shrinking wedge of path-connected, compact, based spaces with wedge point
x_{0}
, we have that
(X,x_{0})=\varprojlim_{k}\{(X_{\leq k},x_{0}),\phi_{k+1,k};\mathbb{N}\}
in pointed topological spaces, where
X_{\leq k}=\vee_{j=1}^{k}X_{k}
and the bonding maps
\phi_{k+1,k}:X_{\leq k+1}\rightarrow X_{\leq k}
collapse of the last space
X_{k+1}
to
x_{0}
. The shape homotopy groups are

\displaystyle\check{\pi}_{n}(X,x_{0})\cong\varprojlim_{k}\{\pi_{n}(X_{\leq k},x_{0}),\phi_{k+1,k}\}

The inclusion map
\iota_{k,k+1}:X_{\leq k}\rightarrow X_{\leq k+1}
induces a canonical section of
\phi_{k+1,k}:\pi_{n}(X_{\leq k+1})\rightarrow\pi_{n}(X_{\leq k})
. Therefore
\varprojlim^{1}\{\pi_{n}(X_{\leq k},x_{0}),\phi_{k+1,k}\}
is trivial for
n\geq 1
. This means that there is an isomorphism

\displaystyle\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)\cong\check{\pi}_{n}(X,x_{0})

for
n\geq 0
.

5.

Hawaiian earring
: The Hawaiian earring
\mathbb{H}
is defined as the infinite wedge of shrinking circles embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with wedge point
x_{0}=(0,0)
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}:=\bigvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}S^{1}_{j}

By the previous example, we have that
\pi_{n}^{c}(c\mathbb{H},\omega)\cong\check{\pi}_{n}(\mathbb{H},x_{0})
for
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

\varprojlim_{k}\{\pi_{n}(S^{1}_{\leq k},x_{0}),\phi_{k+1,k}\}=0
for
n\geq 2
. For
n=1
it is the inverse limit of
\{F_{k},\phi_{k+1,k}\}
, with bonding maps

\displaystyle\phi_{k+1,k}:F_{k+1}

\displaystyle\rightarrow F_{k}

\displaystyle w(a_{1},\dots,a_{k},a_{k+1})

\displaystyle\mapsto w(a_{1},\dots,a_{k},1)

F_{k}
denotes the free group on the generating set
G_{k}=\{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\}
and
\phi_{k+1,k}
takes a word in
G_{k+1}\cup G_{k+1}^{-1}
and maps it to the same word with
a_{k+1}^{\pm 1}
replaced by the identity. We obtain

\pi_{n}^{c}(c\mathbb{H},\omega)\cong\begin{cases}0&n\geq 2\\
\varprojlim_{k}F_{k}&n=1\\
\{\ast\}&n=0\\
\end{cases}

6.

Shrinking wedge of spheres
: The infinite earring space
\mathbb{E}_{m}
is a shrinking wedge of
m
-spheres (
m\geq 2
) with wedge point
x_{0}
. The shape homotopy groups are

\displaystyle\check{\pi}_{n}(\mathbb{E}_{m},x_{0})\cong\bigoplus_{1\leq j\leq\frac{n-1}{m-1}}(\pi_{n}(S^{mj-j+1}))^{\mathbb{N}}

for
n\geq 2

[
8
]
.

Since
\mathbb{E}_{m}=\vee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}S^{m}_{j}
satisfies the conditions of Example
4
we have that the
\varprojlim^{1}
term vanishes and
\pi_{n}^{c}(c\mathbb{E}_{m},\omega)\cong\check{\pi}_{n}(\mathbb{E}_{m},x_{0})
for
n\geq 0
. Since
\pi_{1}(\vee_{j=1}^{k}S^{m}_{j})=0
and
\pi_{0}(\vee_{j=1}^{k}S^{m}_{j})=\{\ast\}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
we obtain

\pi_{n}^{c}(c\mathbb{E}_{m},\omega)\cong\begin{cases}\bigoplus_{i\leq j\leq\frac{n-1}{m-1}}(\pi_{n}(S^{mj-j+1}))^{\mathbb{N}}&n\geq 2\\
0&n=1\\
\{\ast\}&n=0\\
\end{cases}

7.

Strange wedge
:
This example is a warning that the coarse homotopy groups of
cX
can be different depending on the choice of
x_{0}\in X
.
Consider
X=\Lambda\vee S^{1}
, the wedge of the dyadic solenoid and a circle with wedge point
x_{0}
. Let
P=(S^{1},\ast)\vee(S^{1},\ast)
and consider the map
f
which takes the paths
\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3}
to the paths
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\omega_{1}\omega_{2},\omega_{3}
respectively. Similarly, let
g
be the map taking the paths
\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3}
to
\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3}
. See Figure
24
.

Figure 24:
[
27
]
The space
P=(S^{1},\ast)\vee(S^{1},\ast)
.

(X,x_{0})
is the inverse limit of the sequence

\displaystyle(P,\ast)\xleftarrow{f}(P,\ast)\xleftarrow{g}(P,\ast)\xleftarrow{f}(P,\ast)\xleftarrow{g}\dots

Analogously, the inverse limit of

\displaystyle(P,e)\xleftarrow{f}(P,e)\xleftarrow{g}(P,e)\xleftarrow{f}(P,e)\xleftarrow{g}\dots

is homeomorphic to
(X,x_{1})
for a suitable
x_{1}\in X
. We have that
\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,c\{x_{0}\})=\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,c\{x_{1}\})=0
for
n\geq 2
. By definition
\pi_{0}^{c}(cX,c\{x_{0}\})\cong\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,c\{x_{1}\})
since it is only a pointed set. However, it can be shown that

\displaystyle\pi_{1}^{c}(cX,c\{x_{0}\})\cong\check{\pi}_{1}(X,x_{0})\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\pi_{1}^{c}(cX,c\{x_{1}\})\cong\check{\pi}_{1}(X,x_{1})=0

See Example 4 of Ch. II §3.3 in
[
27
]
for the details of this computation.

8.

Random groups
: Let
F_{n}
be the free group on
n\geq 2
generators
s_{1},\dots,s_{n}
. For any integer
L
let
R_{L}\subset F_{n}
be the set of reduced words of length
L
in these generators.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
0<d<1
. A random set of relators at density
d
, at length
L
is a
\left\lfloor{(2n-1)^{dL}}\right\rfloor
-tuple of elements of
R_{L}
, randomly picked among all elements of
R_{L}
. A random group at density
d
, at length
L
is the group
G
presented by
\langle S|R\rangle
, where
S=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\}
and
R
is a random set of relators at density
d
, at length
L
.

We say that a property occurs with overwhelming probability at density
d
if for fixed
d
, the probability of occurence tends to
1
as
L\rightarrow\infty
.

Theorem 4.3
.

(Section 9.B in
[
24
]
) A random group is with overwhelming probability

(a)

Trivial or
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
at density greater than
\frac{1}{2}
.

(b)

Word-hyperbolic, with aspherical presentation complex, at density less than
\frac{1}{2}
.

Theorem 4.4
.

(Theorem
1.4
in
[
13
]
) Let
0<d<\frac{1}{2}
. Then with overwhelming probability the boundary of a random group at density
d
is the Menger sponge.

Combining this with Theorem
1.1
of
[
20
]
, we obtain that the generic (finitely presented, infinite) group is coarsely homotopy equivalent to the cone of the Menger sponge
c\mathbb{M}
. The Menger sponge
\mathbb{M}
is a topologically
1
-dimensional fractal constructed from the unit cube by subdividing it into
27
smaller cubes, removing the central cube and the six cubes centred on each face; and then inductively repeating the same procedure with the
20
remaining smaller cubes (see Figure
25
).

Figure 25:
[
37
]
The first four iterations of the construction of the Menger sponge.

We now follow the paper
[
19
]
.
(\mathbb{M},x_{0})
can be alternatively constructed as the inverse limit of a sequence of
4
-valent graphs
\{X_{h},x_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}}
, where each
X_{h}
has
2\cdot 4^{h}
vertices and
x_{h}
is the top-most vertex. See Figure
26
.

Figure 26:
[
19
]
The graphs
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
.

We have that
\pi_{1}(X_{h+1},x_{h+1})=\pi_{1}(X_{h},x_{h})\ast F_{3}\ast\dots\ast F_{3}
with one factor of
F_{3}
for each vertex of
X_{h}
. The bonding morphisms
\pi_{1}(X_{h+1},x_{h+1})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(X_{h},x_{h})
trivialise these additional factors. The surjectivity of the bonding morphisms imply the vanishing of
\varprojlim^{1}\pi_{1}
, and all higher homotopy groups vanish. Therefore,

\pi_{n}^{c}(G_{ran},\omega)\cong\begin{cases}0&n\geq 2\\
{\varprojlim}_{h}F_{c_{h}}&n=1\\
\{\ast\}&n=0\\
\end{cases}

where
c_{h}=2\cdot 4^{h}+1
, and the bonding maps
\psi_{h+1,h}:F_{c_{h+1}}\rightarrow F_{c_{h}}
are the canonical projection.

4.2
Relation to Spanier groups

We relate the coarse homotopy groups
\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)
the kernel of the group homomorphism
\Psi_{n}:\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})\rightarrow\check{\pi}_{n}(X,x_{0})
, which consists of elements of
\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})
which cannot be detected by polyhedral approximations to
X
. We will show that there exists a coning map
\mathfrak{c}
such that the composition

\displaystyle\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{c}}\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)\rightarrow\check{\pi}_{n}(X,x_{0})

where the second arrow comes from the
\varprojlim^{1}
sequence, coincides with
\Psi_{n}
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
X
be a compact metric space,
x_{0}\in X
,
n\geq 1
. There is a canonical group homomorphism
\mathfrak{c}:\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)
obtained by coning a representative of
[f]\in\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})
.

Proof.

This is subtler than may first appear: it is not true in general that any continuous map
f:([0,1]^{n},\partial[0,1]^{n})\rightarrow(X,x_{0})
, where
X
is a compact metric space, can be continuously homotoped to a Lipschitz map, so it may not even be possible to find a representative
f
of
[f]\in\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})
such that the cone
cf:(c[0,1]^{n},c\partial[0,1]^{n})\rightarrow(cX,\omega)
is coarse.

However, the fact that any continuous map between compact metric spaces is uniformly continuous rescues us. That means there is a function
\beta:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with the following properties:

•

\beta(0)=0
.

•

\beta
is monotonically increasing.

•

If
x,y\in[0,1]^{n}
with
d_{[0,1]^{n}}(x,y)\leq r
then
d_{X}(f(x),f(y))\leq\beta(r)
.

•

\beta
is continuous.

We let

\displaystyle\beta(r):=\max_{d_{[0,1]^{n}}(x,y)\leq r}d_{X}(f(x),f(y))

The first three points are obvious. Assume that
\beta
is not left continuous. Then there exists an
r_{0}
and
\varepsilon>0
such that
\beta(r)<\beta(r_{0})-\varepsilon
for all
r<r_{0}
. The function

\displaystyle d_{X}(f\times f):[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1]^{n}

\displaystyle\rightarrow[0,\infty)

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto d_{X}(f(x),f(y))

is continuous and
\{(x,y)\in[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1]^{n}\,|\,d_{[0,1]^{n}}(x,y)\leq r_{0}\}
is a compact set, so it assumes its maximum. This maximum must be for points
x_{0},y_{0}
with distance equal to
r_{0}
, or it would contradict the assumption. Since
[0,1]^{n}
is a geodesic path metric space, there exists a sequence
\{y_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
d_{[0,1]^{n}}(x_{0},y_{n})<d_{[0,1]^{n}}(x_{0},y_{0})
for all
n
, such that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}d_{[0,1]^{n}}(x_{0},y_{n})=r_{0}
and
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}d_{X}(f(x_{0}),f(y_{n}))=d_{X}(f(x_{0}),f(y_{0}))=\beta(r_{0})
, contradiction.

Right continuity: the sets
K_{r}:=\{(x,y)\in[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1]^{n}\,|\,d(x,y)\leq r\}
for
r\geq r_{0}
are compact and
\cap_{r\geq r_{0}}K_{r}=K_{r_{0}}
. The function
d_{X}(f\times f)
attains its maximum on each
K_{r}
: there exist
(x_{r},y_{r})\in K_{r}
with
\beta(r)=d_{X}(f(x_{r}),f(y_{r}))
. By passing to a subsequence, we can assume that
\lim_{r\rightarrow r_{0}}(x_{r},y_{r})=(x_{0},y_{0})\in\cap_{r\geq r_{0}}K_{r}=K_{r_{0}}
. We obtain that
\lim_{r\rightarrow r_{0}}\beta(r)=\beta(r_{0})
.

For ease of notation, define

\displaystyle\gamma(t):=[\beta(\frac{1}{\log(t)+1})]^{-1}+1

Observe that
\gamma:[1,\infty)\rightarrow[1,\infty)
is a cotinuous, monotonically increasing function and that
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\gamma(t)=\infty
.
\gamma
might not be coarse.

Lemma 4.6
.

There exists a shrinking map
sh_{\gamma}:[1,\infty)\rightarrow[1,\infty)
such that
\gamma\circ sh_{\gamma}
is still continuous, monotonically increasing, and is coarse.

Proof.

Let
h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Consider the restriction
\gamma_{|[2^{h},2^{h+1}]}:[2^{h},2^{h+1}]\rightarrow[1,\infty)
, which we denote by
\gamma_{h}
. Since
\gamma_{h}
is uniformly continuous, there exists a constant
d_{h}
such that
|\gamma_{h}(t)-\gamma_{h}(s)|<1
for
t,s\in[2^{h},2^{h+1}]
and
|t-s|<d_{h}
. Let
N_{h}
denote the open
d_{h}
-neighbourhood of the diagonal. The function

\displaystyle([2^{h},2^{h+1}]\times[2^{h},2^{h+1}])\setminus N_{h}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow[0,\infty)

\displaystyle(t,s)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\frac{|\gamma(t)-\gamma(s)|}{|t-s|}

is continuous on a compact set, so is bounded by some constant
m_{h}
. Choose a constant
l_{h}
such that
2^{l_{h}}>m_{h}
. Without loss of generality we can assume that
\{l_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is an increasing sequence and
l_{0}\geq 1
.

We take
sh_{\gamma}
to be the linear extension of a function which scales by
\frac{1}{2^{l_{h}}}
on the set
[2^{h+l_{h}},2^{h+l_{h}+1}]
. This is a continuous shrinking function, coarse-Lipschitz homotopic to the identity. For any two points
2^{l_{h}}t,2^{l_{h}}s\in[2^{h+l_{h}},2^{h+l_{h}+1}]
, the composition
\gamma\circ sh_{\gamma}
takes them to
\gamma(t),\gamma(s)
. If
|t-s|<d_{h}
then
|\gamma sh_{\gamma}(2^{l_{h}}t)-\gamma sh_{\gamma}(2^{l_{h}}s)|<1
. Otherwise, we have

\displaystyle|\gamma sh_{\gamma}(2^{l_{h}}t)-\gamma sh_{\gamma}(2^{l_{h}}s)|=|\gamma(t)-\gamma(s)|<2^{l_{h}}|t-s|

For two points in the interval
[2^{{h}+l_{h-1}},2^{h+l_{h}}]
, the composition
\gamma\circ sh_{\gamma}
sends them to the same point,
\gamma(2^{h})
. Now suppose we have any two
t,s\in[1,\infty)
with
t\geq s
. We have

\displaystyle|\gamma sh_{\gamma}(t)-\gamma sh_{\gamma}(s)|

\displaystyle\leq|\gamma sh_{\gamma}(t)-\gamma sh_{\gamma}(\lfloor{t}\rfloor)|+|\gamma sh_{\gamma}(\lfloor{t}\rfloor)-\gamma sh_{\gamma}(\lceil s\rceil)|+|\gamma sh_{\gamma}(\lceil s\rceil)-\gamma sh_{\gamma}(s)|

\displaystyle<1+\max\{|t-s|,1\}+1

\displaystyle\leq|t-s|+3

This shows that
\gamma\circ sh_{\gamma}
is controlled. It is clear that
\gamma\circ sh_{\gamma}
is continuous, montonically increasing, and proper. Additionally we have that

\displaystyle\gamma sh_{\gamma}(t)=\gamma sh_{\gamma}(t)-\gamma sh_{\gamma}(1)+\beta(1)^{-1}+1<t+C

for all
t\in[1,\infty)
, where
C=1+\beta(1)^{-1}
.

∎

Now we have a series of technical claims:

Claim
.

The map

\displaystyle cf\circ a_{f}:c[0,1]^{n}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow cX

\displaystyle(hx,h)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\gamma sh_{\gamma}(h)f(x),\gamma sh_{\gamma}(h))

is coarse.

We just compute. Let
(tx,t),(sy,s)\in c[0,1]^{n}
with distance less than
Q>0
and
t\geq s
. This means
|t-s|<Q
and
\|x-y\|<\frac{AQ}{t}
for
A=1+diam([0,1]^{n})
.

\displaystyle d_{cX}(cfa_{f}(tx,t),cfa_{f}(sy,s))

\displaystyle\leq\|\gamma sh_{\gamma}(t)f(x)-\gamma sh_{\gamma}(t)f(y)\|+|\gamma sh_{\gamma}(t)-\gamma sh_{\gamma}(s)|(\|f(y)\|+1)

\displaystyle<\gamma(t)\beta(\frac{AQ}{t})+(Q+3)(diam(X)+1)

\displaystyle=([\beta(\frac{1}{\log(t)+1})]^{-1}+1)\beta(\frac{AQ}{t})+(Q+3)(diam(X)+1)

Since
\frac{(\log(t)+1)AQ}{t}\rightarrow 0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
, there is a
t_{0}
such that for all
t\geq t_{0}
,
\frac{1}{\log(t)+1}>\frac{AQ}{t}
. Additionally choose
t_{0}
large enough so that
\beta(\frac{AQ}{t})<1
for all
t\geq t_{0}
. Since
\beta
is monotonically increasing, we obtain

\displaystyle([\beta(\frac{1}{\log(t)+1})]^{-1}+1)\beta(\frac{AQ}{t})<1+1=2

Hence for two points
(tx,t),(sy,s)
with
t\geq s,t_{0}
we have a coarse estimate. If both
t,s\leq t_{0}
, then they lie in the set
B^{c[0,1]^{n}}_{t_{0}}:=\{(tx,t)\in c[0,1]^{n}\,|\,t\leq t_{0}\}
, which is bounded. We have

\displaystyle(cfa_{f}\times cfa_{f})(B^{c[0,1]^{n}}_{t_{0}}\times B^{c[0,1]^{n}}_{t_{0}})\subset B^{cX}_{\gamma sh_{\gamma}(t_{0})}\times B^{cX}_{\gamma sh_{\gamma}(t_{0})}

We simply add
diam(B^{cX}_{\gamma sh_{\gamma}(t_{0})})
to the distance estimate for these points. So
cf\circ a_{f}
is controlled. It is obviously proper since
\gamma sh_{\gamma}
is proper. Therefore,
cf\circ a_{f}
is coarse. ∎

Claim
.

The coarse homotopy class of
cf\circ a_{f}
is independent of the choices of
\beta
and
sh_{\gamma}
.

Proof.

Suppose we now have a
\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}
. Suppose further that
sh_{\gamma^{\prime}}:[1,\infty)\rightarrow[1,\infty)
is a shrinking map so that
\gamma^{\prime}\circ sh_{\gamma^{\prime}}
is still continuous, monotonically increasing and is coarse, ie. for every
Q>0
there exists a constant
C_{\gamma^{\prime},Q}
such that

\displaystyle|t-s|<Q\implies|\gamma^{\prime}sh_{\gamma^{\prime}}(t)-\gamma^{\prime}sh_{\gamma^{\prime}}(s)|<C_{\gamma^{\prime},Q}

Additionally, because
[1,\infty)
is a path metric space,
\gamma^{\prime}sh_{\gamma^{\prime}}
is large-scale Lipschitz. So there exists a constant
D_{\gamma^{\prime}}
such that
|\gamma^{\prime}sh_{\gamma^{\prime}}(t)|<D_{\gamma^{\prime}}t+D_{\gamma^{\prime}}
for all
t\in[1,\infty)
.

Let
\eta(h,t)=t\gamma sh_{\gamma}(h)+(1-t)\gamma^{\prime}sh_{\gamma^{\prime}}(h)
. Consider the homotopy

\displaystyle G:c([0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])

\displaystyle\longrightarrow cX

\displaystyle(hx,ht,h)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\eta(h,t)f(x),\eta(h,t))

This is a homotopy between
cf\circ a_{f}
(when
t=1
) and
cf\circ a^{\prime}_{f}
(when
t=0
). We now show that
G
is a coarse map. Suppose we have two points
(hx,ht,h),(ly,ls,l)
with distance
<Q
. Assume that
h\geq l
. As before, this means that
|h-l|<Q
and
|t-s|,\|x-y\|<\frac{AQ}{h}
for some constant
A\geq 1
.

\displaystyle d_{cX}(G(hx,ht,h),G(ly,ls,l))

\displaystyle\leq\|\eta(h,t)f(x)-\eta(h,t)f(y)\|+|\eta(h,t)-\eta(l,s)|(\|f(y)\|+1)

\displaystyle\leq\eta(h,t)\|f(x)-f(y)\|+|\eta(h,t)-\eta(l,s)|(diam(X)+1)

\displaystyle\eta(h,t)\|f(x)-f(y)\|

\displaystyle=t\gamma sh_{\gamma}(h)\|f(x)-f(y)\|+(1-t)\gamma^{\prime}sh_{\gamma^{\prime}}(h)\|f(x)-f(y)\|

\displaystyle<t\gamma sh_{\gamma}(h)\beta(\frac{AQ}{h})+(1-t)\gamma^{\prime}sh_{\gamma^{\prime}}(h)\beta^{\prime}(\frac{AQ}{h})

\displaystyle<2t+2(1-t)=2

for all
h
greater than some
h_{0}
.

\displaystyle|\eta(h,t)-\eta(l,s)|

\displaystyle\leq|\eta(h,t)-\eta(l,t)|+|\eta(l,t)-\eta(l,s)|

\displaystyle|\eta(h,t)-\eta(l,t)|

\displaystyle\leq t|\gamma sh_{\gamma}(h)-\gamma sh_{\gamma}(l)|+(1-t)|\gamma^{\prime}sh_{\gamma^{\prime}}(h)-\gamma^{\prime}sh_{\gamma^{\prime}}(l)|

\displaystyle<(Q+3)+C_{\gamma^{\prime},Q}

\displaystyle|\eta(l,t)-\eta(l,s)|

\displaystyle\leq|t-s|(\gamma sh_{\gamma}(l)+\gamma^{\prime}sh_{\gamma^{\prime}}(l))

\displaystyle<\frac{AQ}{h}(l+C+D_{\gamma^{\prime}}l+D_{\gamma^{\prime}})

\displaystyle<AQ(1+C+2D_{\gamma^{\prime}})

If both
h,l\leq h_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle(G\times G)(B^{c[0,1]^{n+1}}_{h_{0}}\times B^{c[0,1]^{n+1}}_{h_{0}})\subset B^{cX}_{\max\{\gamma sh_{\gamma}(h_{0}),\gamma^{\prime}sh_{\gamma^{\prime}}(h_{0})\}}\times B^{cX}_{\max\{\gamma sh_{\gamma}(h_{0}),\gamma^{\prime}sh_{\gamma^{\prime}}(h_{0})\}}

This is sufficient to show that
G
is controlled.
G
is obviously proper.
∎

Claim
.

The coarse homotopy class of
cf\circ a_{f}
is independent of the choice of representative
f
.

Proof.

Let
[f]=[g]
in
\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})
. This means there is a homotopy

\displaystyle H:([0,1]^{n}\times[0,1],\partial[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])\rightarrow(X,x_{0})

which restricts to
f
on
[0,1]^{n}\times\{0\}
and to
g
on
[0,1]^{n}\times\{1\}
. Let
\beta_{H}
and
\gamma_{H}
be the functions associated with
H
and consider the composition

\displaystyle cH\circ a_{H}:c([0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])

\displaystyle\rightarrow cX

\displaystyle(h(x,t),h)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\gamma_{H}sh_{\gamma_{H}}(h)H(x,t),\gamma_{H}sh_{\gamma_{H}}(h))

By the same computation as above, the map
cH\circ a_{H}
is coarse, and
cH\circ a_{H}
provides a coarse homotopy between
cf\circ a_{f}
and
cg\circ a_{g}
, since
\beta_{H}
is also an appropriate function for
f
and
g
(by the previous claim,
cf\circ a_{f}
and
cg\circ a_{g}
are independent of the choice of
\beta
). Additionally, the homotopy restricted to
c(\partial[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])
has image in
\omega[1,\infty)
. So we are done.
∎

Definition 4.7
.

The
coning homomorphism

\mathfrak{c}:\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)
is defined by taking a representative
f
of
[f]\in\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})
, and sending it to
b_{\gamma sh_{\gamma}\omega,\omega}[cf\circ a_{f}]
.

This is a group homomorphism. Let
[f],[g]\in\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})
. We can choose the same
\beta=\max\{\beta_{f},\beta_{g}\}
and shrinking map
a
for both
f
and
g
. It follows that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}[f]\cdot\mathfrak{c}[g]=b_{\gamma sh_{\gamma}\omega,\omega}[cf\circ a]\cdot b_{\gamma sh_{\gamma}\omega,\omega}[cg\circ a]=b_{\gamma sh_{\gamma}\omega,\omega}([cf\circ a]\cdot[cg\circ a])

\displaystyle=b_{\gamma sh_{\gamma}\omega,\omega}[c(f\cdot g)\circ a]=\mathfrak{c}([f]\cdot[g])

Remark
.

There are easier ways to prove the existence of a shrinking map
a_{f}
such that
cf\circ a_{f}
is coarse, by using the fact that
cf
is uniformly continous on compact subsets and that the coarse structure of
c[0,1]^{n}
is generated by one entourage (eg. see the proof of Theorem
11.1
). However, the reason we have included the proof above is because it works for all compact metric spaces
K
and continuous maps
f:K\rightarrow X
even if
cK
is not a path metric space. This is because we can replace
\beta(r):=\max_{d_{K}(x,y)\leq r}d_{X}(f(x),f(y))
with

\displaystyle\beta_{1}(r):=\frac{1}{r}\int^{2r}_{r}\beta(s)ds

which is a continous, monotonically increasing function with
\beta_{1}(0)=0
.

The rest of the proof of Proposition
4.5
follows through with
\beta_{1}
. For some intuition, going up the cone expands linearly along the modulus of continuity
\beta_{1}
, but the shrinking map is logarithmic, so for any distance scale, the shrunken map is eventually coarse. This uses the property that
cf
is the cone of a single map, rather than some arbitrary continuous map between cones. We obtain the following corollary:

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
f:K\rightarrow X
be a continous map between compact metric spaces. There exists a shrinking map
a_{f}:cK\rightarrow cK
such that
cf\circ a_{f}:cK\rightarrow cX
is coarse.

To define the homomorphism
\Psi_{n}:\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})\rightarrow\check{\pi}_{n}(X,x_{0})
, we require a small modification to the definition of
\check{\pi}_{n}(X,x_{0})
.

Let
X
be a paracompact Hausdorff space. Let
\Lambda
be the subset of
O(X,x_{0})
consisting of pairs
(\mathcal{U},U_{0})
where
\mathcal{U}
is a normal open cover of
X
and such that there is a partition of unity
\{\rho_{U}\}_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
subordinate to
\mathcal{U}
with
\rho_{U_{0}}(x_{0})=1
. It is known that every open cover of a paracompact Hausdorff space
X
is normal. Additionally, if
(\mathcal{U},U_{0})\in O(X,x_{0})
, we can refine
(\mathcal{U},U_{0})
to a cover
(\mathcal{V},V_{0})
such that
V_{0}
is the only element of
\mathcal{V}
containing
x_{0}
. Therefore,
\Lambda
is cofinal in
O(X,x_{0})
and we can write

\displaystyle\check{\pi}_{n}(X,x_{0})\cong\varprojlim_{\mathcal{U}\in\Lambda}\{\pi_{n}(|N(\mathcal{U})|,U_{0}),\phi_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{U}}\}

To explain what this means, consider our fixed sequence of open covers
(\mathcal{U}_{h},x_{0})
. For each
h\in\mathbb{N}
, we can modify
\mathcal{U}_{h}
as follows: if
B(z,\frac{1}{2^{h}})
contains
x_{0}
, and
z\neq x_{0}
, we replace it by
B^{\prime}(z,\frac{1}{2^{h}}):=B(z,\frac{1}{2^{h}})\setminus\{x_{0}\}
. Otherwise,
B^{\prime}(z,\frac{1}{2^{h}})=B(z,\frac{1}{2^{h}})
. The open cover

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{h}:=\bigcup_{z\in Z_{h}}B^{\prime}(z,\frac{1}{2^{h}})

has the same nerve
|\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{h}|
as
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
. This is because the intersection of finitely many open sets is open. Assuming that the singleton
\{x_{0}\}
is not open (otherwise all the homotopy groups
\pi_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,x_{0})
would be trivial for large
h
), removing the point
\{x_{0}\}
does not affect whether intersections are empty or not. The bonding maps
\phi_{h+1,h}:|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
are also unaffected.

The map
i:cX\rightarrow M_{X}
can be modified to
\hat{i}:cX\rightarrow M_{X}
by taking partitions of unity
\{\rho_{h}^{\prime}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}}
subordinate to
\{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}}
instead, and this also has no effect on the homomorphism
\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{L,e}(M_{X},\omega)
since we can always choose the geometric simplicial approximation
\widetilde{is_{r}f}
to be the same as
\widetilde{\hat{i}s_{r}f}
for any
[f]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)
and
r\in\mathbb{N}
. This is because the only case to consider is if, for a vertex
v
in
c[0,1]^{n}
,
p^{\prime}s_{r}f|st(v)|
has non-trivial intersection with
\{x_{0}\}
. In that case, we can always define
u_{v}
to be
(hx_{0},h)
where
h
is the closest integer to
ps_{r}f(v)
. Therefore,
is_{r}f
and
\hat{i}s_{r}f
satisfy the same geometric star condition.

The purpose of this is purely for the convenience of avoiding change of base ray homomorphisms. Observe that the image of
c\partial[0,1]^{n}
under
\hat{i}s_{r}f
is
s_{r}\omega
, since
\rho^{\prime}_{h}(x_{0})=x_{0}
for all
h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Therefore
\widetilde{\hat{i}s_{r}f}
is homotopic to
\hat{i}s_{r}f
by a homotopy whose image when restricted to
c\partial[0,1]^{n}
is contained within the image of the base ray
\omega
.

For the rest of this subsection, we assume that
X
is path-connected and locally path-connected.

Definition 4.9
.

Let
X
be a paracompact Hausdorff space and
n\geq 1
. Let
\{\rho_{U}\}_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
be a locally finite partition of unity subordinate to
\mathcal{U}
such that
\rho_{U_{0}}(x_{0})=1
. Let

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathcal{U}}:(X,x_{0})

\displaystyle\rightarrow(|N(\mathcal{U})|,U_{0})

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\rho_{U}(x)[U]

be the map which sends a point to its barycentric co-ordinates in
|N(\mathcal{U})|
. We define
\Psi_{n}
as the group homomorphism

\displaystyle\Psi_{n}:\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\check{\pi}_{n}(X,x_{0})\cong\varprojlim_{\mathcal{U}\in\Lambda}\{\pi_{n}(|N(\mathcal{U})|,U_{0}),\phi_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{U}}\}

\displaystyle[f]

\displaystyle\mapsto\{[\varphi_{\mathcal{U}}\circ f]\}_{\mathcal{U}\in\Lambda}

This is well-defined: the sum in
\varphi_{\mathcal{U}}(x)
is finite since the covers are locally finite; since
\mathcal{U}\in\Lambda
we have that
\varphi_{\mathcal{U}}(x_{0})=U_{0}
. Additionally, if
(\mathcal{V},V_{0})\succeq(\mathcal{U},U_{0})
then
\phi_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{U}}\circ\varphi_{\mathcal{V}}
is homotopic to
\varphi_{\mathcal{U}}
by a base point preserving homotopy, so
\Psi_{n}
is a map into the direct limit.

Roughly speaking, the kernel of
\Psi_{n}
is a measure of how much homotopical information is lost by approximating
X
by polyhedra:
[f]\in\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})\setminus\ker{\Psi_{n}}
if and only if there exist a polyhedron
K
and a map
q:(X,x_{0})\rightarrow(K,k_{0})
such that
q_{*}[f]\neq 0
in
\pi_{n}(K,k_{0})
.

Proposition 4.10
.

The composition

\displaystyle\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{c}}\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi_{n}^{L,e}(M_{X},\omega)\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi_{n}^{e}(M_{X},\omega)\rightarrow\varprojlim_{h}\pi_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,x_{0})

coincides with
\Psi_{n}
.

Proof.

Let
[f]\in\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})
, where
f:([0,1]^{n},\partial[0,1]^{n})\rightarrow(X,x_{0})
is a representative of
[f]
. Ignoring change of base rays for now, the map
\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi_{n}^{L,e}(M_{X},\omega)
sends
[f]
to the cone
[cf\circ a_{f}]
of
f
, and then to
[\widetilde{\hat{i}s_{r}cfa_{f}}]
, for sufficiently large
r
. Observe that
(\hat{i}\circ s_{r}\circ cf\circ a_{f})_{|[0,1]^{n}\times(\gamma sh_{\gamma})^{-1}[2^{h}+2^{r},2^{h+1}]}
is just, by following the definitions,

\displaystyle(\varphi^{\prime}_{h-r}\times\id_{[2^{h-r}+1,2^{h-r+1}]})\circ(\id_{X}\times\frac{1}{2^{r}}\cdot)\circ(f\times\id_{[2^{h}+2^{r},2^{h+1}]})\circ(\id_{[0,1]^{n}}\times\gamma sh_{\gamma})

\displaystyle=(\varphi^{\prime}_{h-r}\circ f)\times(\frac{1}{2^{r}}\cdot\circ\gamma sh_{\gamma})

where
\varphi^{\prime}_{h-r}:X\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h-r}|
is the canonical map induced by a partition of unity subordinate to
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{h-r}
. By the same argument as in the proof of independence of choice of (geometric) simplicial approximation,
(\hat{i}s_{r}cfa_{f})_{|[0,1]^{n}\times(\gamma sh_{\gamma})^{-1}\{2^{h}+2^{r}\}}
and its simplicial approximation are continuously homotopic, with image above height
2^{h-r}+1
in
M_{X}
. Additionally, this homotopy has image in
\omega
when restricted to
\partial[0,1]^{n}\times(\gamma sh_{\gamma})^{-1}\{2^{h}+2^{r}\}
. Therefore the last map
\pi_{n}^{e}(M_{X},\omega)\rightarrow\varprojlim_{h}\pi_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,x_{0})
takes us to the element

\displaystyle\{[\widetilde{\hat{i}s_{r}cfa_{f}}_{|[0,1]^{n}\times(\gamma sh_{\gamma})^{-1}\{2^{h}+2^{r}\}}]\}_{h>r}=\{[\varphi^{\prime}_{h-r}\circ f]\}_{h>r}\in\{\pi_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h-r}|,x_{0})\}_{h>r}

in
\varprojlim_{h}\pi_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,x_{0})
, as claimed. The changes of base ray homomorphisms we have omitted for the purpose of clarity do not affect the result.
∎

With this identification, we can say things about when the coning map
\mathfrak{c}:\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)
is an isomorphism, and develop some intuition about what the coarse homotopy groups actually measure. Recall that when
\varprojlim_{h}^{1}\pi_{n+1}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,x_{0})
vanishes, we have
\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)\cong\check{\pi}_{n}(X,x_{0})
. In this case, the kernel of
\mathfrak{c}
coincides exactly with
\ker(\Psi_{n})
.

Definition 4.11
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
\alpha:([0,1],0)\rightarrow(X,x_{0})
be a path and
U
be an open neighbourhood of
\alpha(1)
in
X
. Define

\displaystyle[\alpha]\ast\pi_{n}(U)=\{b^{\alpha^{-1}}_{\alpha(1),x_{0}}[f]\in\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})\,|\,f([0,1]^{n})\subset U,f(\partial[0,1]^{n})=\alpha(1)\}

where
b^{\alpha^{-1}}_{\alpha(1),x_{0}}:\pi_{n}(X,\alpha(1))\rightarrow\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})
is the change of base point homomorphism via the path
\alpha^{-1}
.

The set
[\alpha]\ast\pi_{n}(U)
is a subgroup of
\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})
.

Definition 4.12
.

(Spanier groups) Let
n\geq 1
,
\mathcal{U}
be an open cover of
X
, and
x_{0}\in X
. The
n-th Spanier group of
(X,x_{0})
with respect to

\mathcal{U}
is the subgroup
\pi_{n}^{Sp}(\mathcal{U},x_{0})
of
\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})
generated by the subgroups
[\alpha]\ast\pi_{n}(U)
for all pairs
(\alpha,U)
with
\alpha(1)\in U
and
U\in\mathcal{U}
:

\displaystyle\pi_{n}^{Sp}(\mathcal{U},x_{0})=\langle[\alpha]\ast\pi_{n}(U)\,|\,U\in\mathcal{U},\alpha(1)\in U\rangle

The
n-th Spanier group of
(X,x_{0})
is the intersection

\displaystyle\pi_{n}^{Sp}(X,x_{0})=\bigcap_{\mathcal{U}\in O(X)}\pi_{n}^{Sp}(\mathcal{U},x_{0})

where
O(X)
is the directed system of open covers of
X
.

Definition 4.13
.

•

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. A space
X
is
LC^{n}
if for every neighbourhood
U
of any point
x\in X
, there exists a neighbourhood
V
of
x
in
U
such that every map
f:S^{k}\rightarrow V,0\leq k\leq n
is nullhomotopic in
U
.

•

A space
X
is
semi-locally
\pi_{n}
-trivial
if for every
x\in X
there exists an open neighbourhood
U
of
x
such that every map
S^{n}\rightarrow U
is null-homotopic in
X
.

Observe that
LC^{n}\implies LC^{n-1}
and semilocally
\pi_{n}
-trivial.

Theorem 4.14
.

(Thm
1.1
in
[
1
]
) Let
n\geq 1
and
x_{0}\in X
. If
X
is paracompact, Hausdorff, and
LC^{n-1}
then
\pi_{n}^{Sp}(X,x_{0})=\ker{\Psi_{n}}
.

Theorem 4.15
.

(Thm
1.2
in
[
1
]
) Let
n\geq 1
and
x_{0}\in X
. If
X
is paracompact, Hausdorff,
LC^{n-1}
and semi-locally
\pi_{n}
-trivial, then
\Psi_{n}
is an isomorphism.

Theorem 4.16
.

(Corollary
3.11
in
[
1
]
) Let
n\geq 1
. For any based space
(X,x_{0})
we have
\pi_{n}^{Sp}(X,x_{0})\subset\ker(\Psi_{n})
.

In summary: for compact metric spaces
X
, the kernel of
\Psi_{n}
exactly corresponds to those elements
[f]
of
\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})
such that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}[f]\in{\varprojlim_{h}}^{1}\pi_{n+1}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,x_{0})\leq\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)

If
X
is path-connected, locally path-connected and
LC^{n-1}
, then this coincides with the Spanier group. If
X
is additionally semi-locally
\pi_{n}
-trivial, then the coning homomorphism
\mathfrak{c}
is injective, and
\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})
can be identified with a subgroup of
\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)
.

If we assume that
\varprojlim_{h}^{1}\pi_{n+1}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,x_{0})
vanishes, the coning map induces a group homomorphism
\overline{\mathfrak{c}}:\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})/\pi_{n}^{Sp}(X,x_{0})\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)\cong\check{\pi}_{n}(X,x_{0})
. If
X
is additionally
LC^{n-1}
, then
\overline{\mathfrak{c}}
is injective. If
X
is additionally semi-locally
\pi_{n}
-trivial, then
\mathfrak{c}:\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)
is an isomorphism.

Note that if there exists a
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathcal{U}_{h}
is good for all
h>N
, then
X
is
LC^{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. To see this, take any point
x\in X
and
U
a neighbourhood of
x
. There exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
B(x,\varepsilon)\subset U
. Choose
h>N
large enough so that
\frac{1}{2^{h}}+\frac{1}{2^{h+1}}<\varepsilon
. Then there is an
z\in Z_{h}
such that
B(z,\frac{1}{2^{h}})
is contractible and
x\in B(z,\frac{1}{2^{h}})\subset U
.

Now for some interesting examples:

1.

Hawaiian earring
: Recall that
\pi_{n}^{c}(c\mathbb{H},\omega)\cong\check{\pi}_{n}(\mathbb{H},x_{0})
for all
n\geq 0
. The conditions of Theorem
4.14
apply to the Hawaiian earring, but the conditions of Theorem
4.15
do not, since it is famously not semi-locally simply connected. Nonetheless,
\Psi_{1}
is injective (which is not easy to prove), and so
\pi_{1}^{Sp}(\mathbb{H},x_{0})=0
. To see why
\Psi_{1}
is not surjective, observe than an element of
\check{\pi}_{1}(\mathbb{H},x_{0})=\varprojlim_{h}F_{h}
can be represented by a sequence
(w_{h})_{h\in\mathbb{N}}
where

•

w_{h}\in F_{h}
is a reduced word in the free group on the generators
a_{1},\dots,a_{h}
.

•

Removing the letter
a_{h}^{\pm}
from
w_{h}
gives the word
w_{h-1}
.

Suppose we had a word
(w_{h})_{h\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for some
k
, the number of appearances of
a_{k}
in
w_{h}
grows without bound as
h\rightarrow\infty
. This cannot be represented by a continuous loop
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{H}
since for any such
\gamma
, the length of each appearance of
a_{k}
, by uniform continuity, has length bounded below by a strictly positive constant; contradiction. This suggests that the fundamental group of the Hawaiian earring is exactly those sequences with the additional property that for each
k\geq 1
, the number of times the letter
a_{k}
appears in
w_{h}
is eventually constant as
h\rightarrow\infty
. Denote this subgroup of
\varprojlim_{h}F_{h}
as
\#_{\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{Z}
.

Theorem 4.17
.

(Theorem 4.1 in
[
30
]
)
\Psi_{1}:\pi_{1}(\mathbb{H},x_{0})\rightarrow\varprojlim_{h}F_{h}
embeds
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{H},x_{0})
isomorphically into
\#_{\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{Z}
.

The intuitive reason why every element of
\varprojlim_{h}F_{h}
has a representative as a coarse homotopy class of
\pi_{1}^{c}(c\mathbb{H},\omega)
is because the additional height variable allows for "infinitely" long paths in
\mathbb{H}
.

2.

Griffiths twin cone
: The Griffiths twin cone
\mathbb{G}
is obtained by taking two copies of the cone of the Hawaiian earring and gluing them together at the "bad" point on the base. It is a
2
-dimensional, path-connected, locally path-connected, compact metric space. See Figure
27
.

Figure 27:
[
6
]
The Griffiths twin cone
\mathbb{G}
.

It can be embedded into
\mathbb{R}^{3}
via the alternative construction: for each integer
n\geq 1
, let
S^{1}_{n}
be the circle of radius
\frac{1}{n}
centred at
(\frac{1}{n},0)
with basepoint
x_{0}=(0,0)
. Let
\mathbb{H}_{o}=\vee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}S^{1}_{2j-1}
be the odd circles and
\mathbb{H}_{e}=\vee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}S^{1}_{2j}
be the even circles so that
\mathbb{H}=\mathbb{H}_{o}\vee\mathbb{H}_{e}
and
\mathbb{H}_{o}\cap\mathbb{H}_{e}=\{x_{0}\}
. Embed
\mathbb{H}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
in the
xy
-plane. Let
C\mathbb{H}_{o}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be the cone over the odd circles with vertex
(0,0,1)
and
C\mathbb{H}_{e}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be the cone over the even circles with vertex
(0,0,-1)
. Then
\mathbb{G}\cong C\mathbb{H}_{o}\vee_{\{x_{0}\}}C\mathbb{H}_{e}
. See Figure
28
.

Figure 28:
[
6
]
An alternate embedding of
\mathbb{G}
into
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

The inclusions
\iota_{o}:\mathbb{H}_{o}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}
and
\iota_{e}:\mathbb{H}_{e}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}
induce isomorphisms on
\pi_{1}
, since
\mathbb{H}_{o}
and
\mathbb{H}_{e}
are homeomorphic copies of
\mathbb{H}
. Let
H_{o}
and
H_{e}
be these images in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{H},x_{0})
respectively. By the van Kampen Theorem

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(\mathbb{G},x_{0})\cong\pi_{1}(\mathbb{H},x_{0})/N

where
N
is the normal closure of
H_{o}\cup H_{e}
. The higher homotopy groups of
\mathbb{G}
vanish.

To compute the shape homotopy groups of
\mathbb{G}
, observe that
(\mathbb{G},x_{0})
is the inverse limit of the sequence

\displaystyle\{C(\bigvee_{j=1}^{k}S^{1}_{2j-1})\vee_{\{x_{0}\}}C(\bigvee_{j=1}^{k}S^{1}_{2j}),\phi_{k+1,k};\mathbb{N}\}

where the maps
\phi_{k+1,k}:C(\vee_{j=1}^{k+1}S^{1}_{2j-1})\vee_{\{x_{0}\}}C(\vee_{j=1}^{k+1}S^{1}_{2j})\rightarrow C(\vee_{j=1}^{k}S^{1}_{2j-1})\vee_{\{x_{0}\}}C(\vee_{j=1}^{k}S^{1}_{2j})
are induced by the collapse of the last wedge of cones. Since all these spaces are contractible, we obtain

\displaystyle\pi_{n}^{c}(c\mathbb{G},\omega)\cong\check{\pi}_{n}(\mathbb{G},x_{0})=0

for all
n
.

In this example,
\Psi_{n}:\pi_{n}(\mathbb{G},x_{0})\rightarrow\check{\pi}_{n}(\mathbb{G},x_{0})
makes every homotopy class trivial, ie.
no
class can be detectable by polyhedra. We consider now the coarse homotopy groups of
c\mathbb{G}
. Intuitively speaking, the reason that there are any non-trivial homotopy classes in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{G},x_{0})
at all, despite its being the wedge sum of two contractible spaces, lies in the fact that there are two cone points, and so if the path is complicated enough, the obvious null-homotopy takes too long to fit into an interval. This, however, is not a problem when looking at its image in the coarse homotopy groups under
\mathfrak{c}
, because the infinite height direction gives us the space we need.

3.

Shrinking wedge of spheres
: Recall that
\pi_{n}^{c}(c\mathbb{E}_{m},\omega)\cong\check{\pi}_{n}(\mathbb{E}_{m},x_{0})
for all
n\geq 0
, and that

\displaystyle\check{\pi}_{n}(\mathbb{E}_{m},x_{0})\cong\bigoplus_{1\leq j\leq\frac{n-1}{m-1}}(\pi_{n}(S^{mj-j+1}))^{\mathbb{N}}

for
n\geq 2
.

\mathbb{E}_{m}
is
LC^{m-1}
but is not semi-locally
\pi_{m}
-trivial. However,
\Psi_{m}:\pi_{m}(\mathbb{E}_{m},x_{0})\rightarrow\check{\pi}_{m}(\mathbb{E}_{m},x_{0})
is an isomorphism where both groups are canonically isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}
. It is shown in
[
8
]
that
\Psi_{n}
is split surjective for
m\leq 2n-1
.

Part II
Relation to Shape Theory

Shape theory is designed to extend homotopy-theoretic methods to compact metric spaces which are not homotopy equivalent to CW complexes. It replaces homotopy equivalence with shape equivalence, a weaker relation that captures topological information invariant under approximation by polyhedra.

Every compact metric space
X
admits a shape expansion: that is, there exists an inverse system
\mathcal{X}:=\{X_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha};\mathbb{N}\}
of finite polyhedra associated to
X
which is unique up to pro-isomorphism in the category pro-hCW. This allows us to define the shape of
X
as the pro-homotopy type of a system. Shape pro-homotopy groups arise naturally from this construction, and coincide with the classical homotopy groups when
X
is a CW complex. Shape-equivalent spaces have isomorphic pro-homotopy groups.

In this thesis, we also adopt the framework of strong shape theory developed via
Z
-sets and proper homotopy. In this setting, the end homotopy groups (also known as strong or Steenrod homotopy groups) provide invariants of the strong shape of a space.

5
Proper homotopy theory

This section introduces some basic definitions from proper homotopy theory and proves a version of the Whitehead theorem for continuous, proper maps inducing isomorphisms on end homotopy groups.

5.1
Pro-completion of a category

We begin with a small digression into pro-completions. Loosely speaking, the pro-completion of a category
\mathcal{C}
, denoted by pro-
\mathcal{C}
, is a way of formally adding inverse limits to
\mathcal{C}
. The objects of pro-
\mathcal{C}
are cofiltered inverse systems of objects in
\mathcal{C}
indexed over a small cofiltered category
\mathcal{A}
, and there is a canonical, fully faithful embedding

\displaystyle\iota:\mathcal{C}\hookrightarrow\text{pro-}\mathcal{C}

which sends an object to the constant inverse system (over any small cofiltered category
\mathcal{A}
). pro-
\mathcal{C}
admits all small cofiltered limits, and satisfies the following universal property: for any category
\mathcal{D}
with small cofiltered limits, the composition with
\iota
induces an equivalence of categories

\displaystyle\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathrm{cofilt}}(\text{pro-}\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\simeq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})

where
\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathrm{cofilt}}
denotes the category of functors that preserve small cofiltered limits.

We will mostly set aside the categorical properties of pro-completions in favour of a hands-on approach. Let
\mathcal{C}
be the category of groups, pointed sets, or hTop (where the objects are topological spaces and morphisms are homotopy classes of continuous maps).

Definition 5.1
.

A
directed set

(\mathcal{A},\leq)
is a nonempty set together with a reflexive and transitive binary relation, with the additional property that every pair of elements has an upper bound.

(\mathcal{A},\leq)
forms a small cofiltered category by letting objects be the elements of
\mathcal{A}
and letting there be a unique morphism
a\rightarrow b
between two objects if
a\geq b
. Every small cofiltered category admits a cofinal functor from a directed set: one can replace a cofiltered indexing category by a directed set without changing the pro-object (p.
71
of
[
17
]
). This is why we will define pro-objects using directed sets instead of arbitrary cofiltered categories.

Definition 5.2
.

(inv-
\mathcal{C}
) Let
\mathcal{A}
be a directed set. The objects of inv-
\mathcal{C}
are inverse systems
\{X_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha};\mathcal{A}\}
in
\mathcal{C}
, where
\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}:X_{\alpha^{\prime}}\rightarrow X_{\alpha}
are morphisms whenever
\alpha\leq\alpha^{\prime}
. A morphism of inv-
\mathcal{C}
from
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha};\mathcal{A}\}
to
\mathcal{Y}=\{Y_{\beta},\psi_{\beta^{\prime},\beta};\mathcal{B}\}
consists of a function
\rho:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
and for each
\beta\in\mathcal{B}
, a morphism of
\mathcal{C}
,
q_{\beta}:X_{\rho(\beta)}\rightarrow Y_{\beta}
, such that whenever
\beta\leq\beta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
there exists
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
with
\rho(\beta)\leq\alpha
and
\rho(\beta^{\prime})\leq\alpha
making the following diagram commute in
\mathcal{C}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho(\beta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho(\beta^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{\beta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{\beta^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\alpha,\rho(\beta)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\alpha,\rho(\beta^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\beta^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}}

There is an obvious definition of composition in inv-
\mathcal{C}
, and the identity morphism of
\{X_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha};\mathcal{A}\}
consists of
\id_{\mathcal{A}}
, with each
q_{\alpha}=\id_{X_{\alpha}}
.

Definition 5.3
.

(pro-
\mathcal{C}
) The category pro-
\mathcal{C}
is the quotient category of inv-
\mathcal{C}
which has the same objects. If
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are objects in inv-
\mathcal{C}
, define an equivalence relation on the set of morphisms of inv-
\mathcal{C}
from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{Y}
by:
(\rho,\{q_{\beta}\})\sim(\rho^{\prime},\{q^{\prime}_{\beta}\})
if for each
\beta\in\mathcal{B}
there exists a
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
with
\alpha\geq\rho(\beta)
and
\alpha\geq\rho^{\prime}(\beta)
such that the following diagram commutes in
\mathcal{C}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho(\beta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho^{\prime}(\beta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\alpha,\rho(\beta)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\alpha,\rho^{\prime}(\beta)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{\beta}}

The equivalence classes so defined are the morphisms of pro-
\mathcal{C}
from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{Y}
. Taking equivalence classes is compatible with composition of morphisms so this is a well-defined category. Two objects are called
pro-isomorphic
if there is an isomorphism in pro-
\mathcal{C}
from one to the other.

The definition of pro-
\mathcal{C}
gives the flexibility we need to change the index set
\mathcal{A}
. For example, if
\mathcal{A^{\prime}}
is cofinal in
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{\alpha},\phi_{\beta,\alpha};\mathcal{A}\}
gives rise to a cofinal subsystem
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\{X_{\alpha^{\prime}},\phi_{\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}};\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\}
in which one retains only the objects and bonds indexed by elements of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. There is a restriction morphism
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
in inv-
\mathcal{C}
defined by
\mathcal{A^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}
and
\{\id_{X_{\alpha^{\prime}}}\,|\,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

The restriction morphism induces an isomorphism in pro-
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Let us construct a candidate for an inverse
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
. We define
\rho:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
via

\rho(\alpha)=\begin{cases}\alpha&if $\alpha\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}$\\[4.30554pt]
\tilde{\alpha}>\alpha&else\end{cases}

for some
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. This exists since
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{A}
is cofinal. As morphisms we choose the bonding morphisms of
\mathcal{X}
, ie.
\phi_{\rho(\alpha),\alpha}:X_{\rho(\alpha)}\rightarrow X_{\alpha}
. To check that this defines a morphism in inv-
\mathcal{C}
we have to check the commutativity of the pentagon diagram. Assume that
\alpha\leq\alpha^{\prime}
. Since
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is a directed set, there exists a
\beta\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
such that
\beta\geq\rho(\alpha),\rho(\alpha^{\prime})
. The diagram becomes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\beta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho(\alpha)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho(\alpha^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\alpha^{\prime}}}

All the maps appearing are the bonding maps of
\mathcal{X}
and thus the diagram commutes.

Now we check that this is an inverse in pro-
\mathcal{C}
. The composition
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
in inv-
\mathcal{C}
is already the identity, so it is the identity in pro-
\mathcal{C}
. We first calculate the composition
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
in inv-
\mathcal{C}
. The map of directed sets
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
is the composition of
\rho
and the inclusion
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{A}
. The maps
X_{\rho(\alpha)}\rightarrow X_{\alpha}
are the bonding maps of
\mathcal{X}
. We check that morphism is equivalent to the identity. Let
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
,
\beta=\rho(\alpha)
. Since
\rho(\alpha)\geq\alpha
by construction we have
\beta\geq\rho(\alpha),\id(\alpha)
. The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\beta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho(\alpha)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\id(\alpha)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{X_{\beta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\beta,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\beta,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{X_{\alpha}}}

commutes. This shows that the composition is equivalent to the identity.

∎

Let
\mathcal{C}=
Gp, the category of groups. We use the symbol
0
to denote both the trivial group and the trivial group homomorphism.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a directed set. The constant system
0:=\{0_{\zeta},0_{\zeta^{\prime},\zeta};\mathcal{Z}\}
is a
0
-object in pro-Gp.

Proof.

We check that
0
is initial and terminal. Let
\mathcal{X}:=\{X_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha};\mathcal{A}\}
and
\mathcal{Y}:=\{Y_{\beta},\varphi_{\beta^{\prime},\beta};\mathcal{B}\}
be two objects. We will show that there is a unique morphism
0\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
(showing that
0
is initial) and a unique morphism
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow 0
(showing that
0
is terminal).

•

0
is initial:

For
0\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
choose some map
\rho:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
. Then the maps
q_{\beta}:0_{\rho(\beta)}\rightarrow Y_{\beta}
have to be the
0
-morphism. Given
\beta\leq\beta^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{B}
choose a
\zeta\in\mathcal{Z}
such that
\zeta\geq\rho(\beta),\rho(\beta^{\prime})
which exists since
\mathcal{Z}
is a directed set. Then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{\zeta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{\rho(\beta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{\rho(\beta^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{\beta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{\beta^{\prime}}}

obviously commutes since both paths are
0
. This shows that there is a morphism
0\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
. To show uniqueness, let
\rho^{\prime}:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
be another map. Again the group homomorphisms
q^{\prime}_{\beta}:0_{\rho^{\prime}(\beta)}\rightarrow Y_{\beta}
have to be the
0
-morphism. We now check that
(\rho,\{q_{\beta}\})\sim(\rho^{\prime},\{q^{\prime}_{\beta}\})
. Let
\beta\in\mathcal{B}
and choose a
\zeta\in\mathcal{Z}
with
\zeta\geq\rho(\beta),\rho^{\prime}(\beta)
. Then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{\zeta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{\rho(\beta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{\rho^{\prime}(\beta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{\beta}}

commutes because both paths are
0
.

•

0
is terminal:

This is basically the same. To define
(\rho,\{q_{\zeta}\}):\mathcal{X}\rightarrow 0
choose any map
\rho:\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
. All the group homomorphisms
q_{\zeta}
must be
0
and the pentagon commutes since
0
is the target (ie. in the bottom left corner), so both paths need to be the
0
-morphism. Given another
(\rho^{\prime},\{q^{\prime}_{\zeta}\})
, the diamond commutes since we have
0
again as the target (ie. at the bottom).

∎

For a concrete example, we consider objects in pro-Gp indexed over the natural numbers. What does it mean for a pro-group
\mathcal{X}:=\{X_{i},\phi_{j,i};\mathbb{N}\}
to be isomorphic to
0:=\{0_{i},0_{j,i};\mathbb{N}\}
? It means that there are morphisms
\mathcal{X}\xrightarrow{\rho}0\xrightarrow{\sigma}\mathcal{X}
so that
\id_{\mathcal{X}}\sim\sigma\rho
.
\sigma\rho
can be represented by the morphism
(\id_{\mathbb{N}},0_{X_{i}})
. The identity morphism
\id_{\mathcal{X}}
can be represented by
(\id_{\mathbb{N}},\id_{X_{i}})
. The condition that
(\id_{\mathbb{N}},\id_{X_{i}})\sim(\id_{\mathbb{N}},0_{X_{i}})
is: for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a
j\geq i
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j,i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j,i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

commutes
\iff
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a
j\geq i
such that the bonding map
\phi_{j,i}=0
.

Example 5.6
.

Consider the object
\{\pi_{n}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\omega(i)),\psi_{i+1};\mathbb{N}\}
(see Theorem
5.12
) in pro-Gp, where the bonding maps
\psi_{i+1}:\pi_{n}(L_{i+1}^{c},(L_{i+1}\cap A)^{c},\omega(i+1))\rightarrow\pi_{n}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\omega(i))
are the composition of the inclusion and a change of base point
b_{\omega(i+1),\omega(i)}(\iota_{i+1,i})_{\ast}
. We see that
\{\pi_{n}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\omega(i)),\psi_{i+1};\mathbb{N}\}=0

\iff
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a
j\geq i
such that the morphism

\displaystyle\psi_{j,i}:\pi_{n}(L_{j}^{c},(L_{j}\cap A)^{c},\omega(j))\xrightarrow{(\iota_{j,i})_{\ast}}\pi_{n}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\omega(j))\xrightarrow{b_{\omega(j),\omega(i)}}\pi_{n}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\omega(i))

where
(\iota_{j,i})_{\ast}
denotes the morphism induced by inclusion, and
b_{\omega(j),\omega(i)}
denotes the change of base point homomorphism using the path in
\omega
between
\omega(j)
and
\omega(i)
, is
0
. Since the change of base point homomorphism is an isomorphism, this means that
(\iota_{j,i})_{\ast}=0
.

We now relate the condition of
\mathcal{X}
being pro-isomorphic to
0
to the vanishing of its
\varprojlim
and
\varprojlim^{1}
.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{i},\phi_{j,i};\mathbb{N}\}
be an object in pro-Gp. Assume that
X_{i}
is countable for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the following are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{X}=\{X_{i},\phi_{j,i};\mathbb{N}\}
is pro-isomorphic to
0
.

2.

\varprojlim_{i}^{1}\{X_{i}\}
and
\varprojlim_{i}\{X_{i}\}
are trivial.

The implication
1\implies 2
comes from the fact that
\varprojlim
and
\varprojlim^{1}
are functors pro-Gp
\rightarrow
Gp.
2\implies 1
is a combination of the following two lemmas:

Lemma 5.8
.

(Theorem 11.3.2 in
[
22
]
) If
\varprojlim_{i}^{1}\{X_{i}\}
is trivial and each group
X_{i}
is countable then
\{X_{i}\}
is Mittag-Leffler, ie. for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a
j\geq i
such that for all
k\geq j
,
\image\phi_{j,i}=\image\phi_{k,i}
.

Lemma 5.9
.

(Theorem 3.4 in
[
28
]
) Let
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{i},\phi_{j,i};\mathbb{N}\}
be an inverse sequence of pointed sets. If
\{X_{i}\}
is Mittag-Leffler and
\varprojlim_{i}\{X_{i}\}
is trivial, then for each
i
there exists a
j>i
such that
\phi_{j,i}:X_{j}\rightarrow X_{i}
is trivial.

Remark
.

The groups in Example
5.6
are countable: by Proposition
11.4.3
of
[
22
]
, a path-connected CW complex having locally finite type is countable. By Theorem C. in
[
43
]
the homotopy groups of a countable CW complex are countable. Hence the absolute homotopy groups of
L_{i}^{c}
and
(L_{i}\cap A)^{c}
are countable. By the long exact sequence, so are the relative homotopy groups
\pi_{n}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\omega(i))
.

5.2
Proof of
\varprojlim^{1}
sequence for
\pi^{e}_{n}

Theorem 5.10
.

Let
Y
be a strongly locally finite CW complex which is path-connected. If
\mathcal{L}=\{L_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a finite filtration of
Y
and
\omega
is well-parametrised wrt
\mathcal{L}
, then there is a natural short exact sequence of groups

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow{\varprojlim}^{1}\pi_{n+1}(Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i},\omega(i))\xrightarrow{\overline{a}}\pi^{e}_{n}(Y,\omega)\xrightarrow{\overline{b}}\varprojlim\pi_{n}(Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i},\omega(i))\rightarrow 0

for
n\geq 1
.

We let
L_{i}^{c}:=Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i}
.

Proof.

Recall that
\{\pi_{n}(L^{c}_{i},\omega(i)),\psi_{i+1};\mathbb{N}\}
is a system in pro-Gp, where the bonding maps
\psi_{i+1}:\pi_{n}(L^{c}_{i+1},\omega(i+1))\rightarrow\pi_{n}(L^{c}_{i},\omega(i))
are the composition of the inclusion and a change of base point
b_{\omega(i+1),\omega(i)}(\iota_{i+1,i})_{\ast}
.

Let
[f]\in\pi_{n}^{e}(Y,\omega)
. A representative
f
is a continuous, proper map

\displaystyle f:([0,1]^{n}\times[1,\infty),\partial[0,1]^{n}\times[1,\infty))\rightarrow(Y,\omega)

For every
i\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a
j_{i}\geq i
such that
f_{|[0,1]^{n}\times[j_{i},\infty)}
has image in
L^{c}_{i}
. We can assume that
\{j_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a strictly increasing sequence. Let
f_{j_{i}}:=f_{|[0,1]^{n}\times\{j_{i}\}}
and
b_{j_{i},i}:=b_{\omega(j_{i}),\omega(i)}
. We define
\overline{b}:\pi^{e}_{n}(Y,\omega)\rightarrow\varprojlim\pi_{n}(Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i},\omega(i))
as follows:

\displaystyle\overline{b}:\pi^{e}_{n}(Y,\omega)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\varprojlim\pi_{n}(L^{c}_{i},\omega(i))

\displaystyle[f]

\displaystyle\mapsto\{b_{j_{i},i}[f_{j_{i}}]\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

The restriction of
f
to
[0,1]^{n}\times[j_{i},j_{i+1}]
gives us a homotopy between
f_{j_{i+1}}
and
f_{j_{i}}
which is
b_{j_{i+1},j_{i}}
on the boundary. We obtain

\displaystyle b_{i+1,i}b_{j_{i+1},i+1}[f_{j_{i+1}}]=b_{i+1,i}b_{j_{i},i+1}b_{j_{i+1},j_{i}}[f_{j_{i+1}}]=b_{i+1,i}b_{j_{i},i+1}[f_{j_{i}}]=b_{j_{i},i}[f_{j_{i}}]

where the inclusion maps
\iota
have been omitted. Therefore
\{b_{j_{i},i}[f_{j_{i}}]\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\varprojlim\pi_{n}(L^{c}_{i},\omega(i))
.

Suppose we have another choice of
\{j^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
. For a fixed
i
, assume that
j^{\prime}_{i}\geq j_{i}
. We have that

\displaystyle b_{j^{\prime}_{i},i}[f_{j^{\prime}_{i}}]=b_{j_{i},i}b_{j^{\prime}_{i},j_{i}}[f_{j^{\prime}_{i}}]=b_{j_{i},i}[f_{j_{i}}]

Now if
[f]=[g]
there exists a continous, proper homotopy

\displaystyle H:[0,1]^{n}\times[1,\infty)\times[0,1]\rightarrow Y

such that
H_{|[0,1]^{n}\times[1,\infty)\times\{0\}}=f
,
H_{|[0,1]^{n}\times[1,\infty)\times\{1\}}=g
and
H_{|\partial[0,1]^{n}\times[1,\infty)\times[0,1]}=\omega
. There exists a strictly increasing sequence
\{j_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, such that
j_{i}\geq i
and
H_{|[0,1]^{n}\times[j_{i},\infty)\times[0,1]}
has image in
L^{c}_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We choose the sequence
\{j_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
for both
f
and
g
. Then
b_{j_{i},i}[f_{j_{i}}]=b_{j_{i},i}[g_{j_{i}}]
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
which gives us
\overline{b}[f]=\overline{b}[g]
. This shows that
\overline{b}
is well-defined.
\overline{b}
is obviously a group homomorphism.

\overline{b}
is surjective: let
\{[f_{i}]\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\varprojlim\pi_{n}(L^{c}_{i},\omega(i))
. There exist homotopies
H^{i,i+1}:[0,1]^{n}\times[i,i+1]\rightarrow L^{c}_{i}
such that

\displaystyle H^{i,i+1}_{|[0,1]^{n}\times\{i\}}=f_{i}

\displaystyle H^{i,i+1}_{|[0,1]^{n}\times\{i+1\}}=f_{i+1}

\displaystyle H^{i,i+1}_{|\partial[0,1]^{n}\times[i,i+1]}=b_{i,i+1}:=b_{\omega(i),\omega(i+1)}=(b_{i+1,i})^{-1}

We glue these homotopies together to a map
H:([0,1]^{n}\times[1,\infty),\partial[0,1]^{n}\times[1,\infty))\rightarrow(Y,\omega)
. We have that
\overline{b}[H]=\{[f_{i}]\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
.

We now define
\overline{a}:{\varprojlim}^{1}\pi_{n+1}(Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i},\omega(i))\rightarrow\pi^{e}_{n}(Y,\omega)
. Let
([f_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\pi_{n+1}(L^{c}_{i},\omega(i))
. We can identify
[0,1]^{n+1}
with
[0,1]^{n}\times[0,\frac{1}{2}]=[0,1]^{n}\times[i-\frac{1}{4},i+\frac{1}{4}]
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We define the map
G
piecewise as follows:

\displaystyle G:[0,1]^{n}\times[i-\frac{1}{4},i+\frac{1}{4}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow Y

\displaystyle(x,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto f_{i}(x,t)\quad i\geq 2

\displaystyle[0,1]^{n}\times[i+\frac{1}{4},i+\frac{3}{4}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow Y

\displaystyle(x,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto\omega(2t-i-\frac{1}{2})\quad i\geq 1

\displaystyle[0,1]^{n}\times[1,1+\frac{1}{4}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow Y

\displaystyle(x,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto f_{1}(x,t)

This glues together to a continous, proper map. See Figure
29
. The base ray
\omega^{\prime}=G_{|\partial[0,1]^{n}\times[1,\infty)}
is a reparametrisation of (and therefore properly homotopic to)
\omega
. We define
a([f_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}=b_{\omega^{\prime},\omega}[G]
. It is clear that if
([f_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}=([g_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
then
a([f_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}=a([g_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and that
a:\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\pi_{n+1}(L^{c}_{i},\omega(i))\rightarrow\pi^{e}_{n}(Y,\omega)
is a group homomorphism.

Figure 29:
The map
G

We show that the kernel of
a=\image\varphi
, where
\varphi
is the group homomorphism

\displaystyle\varphi:\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\pi_{n+1}(L^{c}_{i},\omega(i))

\displaystyle\rightarrow\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\pi_{n+1}(L^{c}_{i},\omega(i))

\displaystyle([f_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

\displaystyle\mapsto([f_{i}]-\psi_{i+1}[f_{i+1}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

Suppose that
([g_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\image\varphi
, ie. there exists
([f_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
[g_{i}]=[f_{i}]-\psi_{i+1}[f_{i+1}]
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We prove that
[G]
is properly homotopic to the identity by picture. Consider the set
[0,1]^{n}\times[i,i+\frac{3}{4}]
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
, labelled red in Figure
30
.

Figure 30:
A representative of
[G]
, where
a([g_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}=b_{\omega^{\prime},\omega}[G]
.

There is a homotopy
Q:[0,1]^{n}\times[i,i+\frac{3}{4}]\times[0,1]
defined by Figure
31
. Observe that
Q_{1}=G_{|[0,1]^{n}\times[i,i+\frac{3}{4}]}
and that
Q
is constant on the boundary
[0,1]^{n}\times\partial[i,i+\frac{3}{4}]
, and therefore can be constantly extended to all of
[0,1]^{n}\times[1,\infty)
, which we denote also by
Q
.

Figure 31:
Diagram of the homotopy
Q
.

It is clear that
Q_{0}
restricted to the set
[0,1]^{n}\times[i,i+1]
is homotopic to the change of base point
b_{i,i+1}
, via a homotopy
Q^{\prime}
that can be extended constantly to
[0,1]^{n}\times[1,\infty)
. We denote the concatenation of
Q^{-1}\ast Q^{\prime}
as
J_{i}
. Let
J:[0,1]^{n}\times[1,\infty)\times[0,1]
be the infinite concatenation of homotopies
J_{1}\ast J_{2}\ast\dots
, which is well-defined since each compact set is affected by only finitely many of the
J_{i}
.
J
is a proper homotopy between
G
and the constant map to the base ray
\omega
. The homotopy is not base ray preserving, but
J_{|\partial[0,1]^{n}\times[1,\infty)\times[0,1]}
always has image in
\omega
, and any two changes of base ray obtained from reparametrisation are homotopic. Therefore
b_{\omega^{\prime},\omega}G
is homotopic to the constant map to
\omega
. This shows that
\image\varphi\subset\ker a
.

For the other inclusion, we prove an auxilliary lemma about
\varprojlim^{1}
. Let
\{G_{i},\psi_{i+1};\mathbb{N}\}
be an inverse system of (not necessarily abelian) groups.
\varprojlim^{1}G_{i}
is defined a quotient of the product
\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}G_{i}
under the equivalence relation
\sim
.

\displaystyle(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\sim(f^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\iff\exists(g_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}:f_{i}=g_{i}f^{\prime}_{i}\psi_{i+1}(g_{i+1})^{-1}\quad\forall i\in\mathbb{N}

If the groups
G_{i}
are all abelian, this agrees with the definition of
\varprojlim^{1}G_{i}
as the cokernel of the group homomorphism
\varphi
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
\{n_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a strictly increasing sequence such that
n_{i}>i
for all
i
. Let
(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}G_{i}
. The element
(f^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, defined as

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{i}=\prod_{j\in[n_{i},n_{i+1}-1]}\psi_{j,i}f_{j}

is equivalent to
(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

This is just a computation. Let

\displaystyle g_{i}=\prod_{j\in[i,n_{i}-1]}\psi_{j,i}f_{j}

Then we have

\displaystyle g_{i}f^{\prime}_{i}\psi_{i+1,i}(g_{i+1})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j\in[i,n_{i}-1]}\psi_{j,i}f_{j}\prod_{j\in[n_{i},n_{i+1}-1]}\psi_{j,i}f_{j}(\psi_{i+1,i}(\prod_{j\in[i+1,n_{i+1}-1]}\psi_{j,i+1}f_{j})^{-1})

\displaystyle=\prod_{j\in[i,n_{i+1}-1]}\psi_{j,i}f_{j}\prod_{j\in[n_{i+1}-1,i+1]}\psi_{j,i}(f_{j})^{-1}

\displaystyle=f_{i}

∎

We now show that
\ker a\subset\image\varphi
. Suppose we have
([f_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\ker a
, ie. the map
G
is properly homotopic to the constant map to
\omega^{\prime}
, via a base ray preserving homotopy
H:[0,1]^{n}\times[1,\infty)\times[0,1]\rightarrow Y
. For every
i
, choose a
n_{i}>i
such that
H_{|[0,1]^{n}\times[n_{i}-\frac{1}{4},\infty)\times[0,1]}
has image in
L^{c}_{i}
. We can assume
\{n_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is strictly increasing. By Lemma
5.11
, the element

\displaystyle([g_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}=(\sum_{j\in[n_{i},n_{i+1}-1]}\psi_{j,i}[f_{j}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

is equivalent to
([f_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Figure 32:
The two images are homotopic relative to the boundary, via the homotopy
H
restricted to the set
[0,1]^{n}\times[3-\frac{1}{4},5+\frac{1}{4}]\times[0,1]
.

Figure 33:
The homotopy between
\psi_{3,1}[f_{3}]+\psi_{4,1}[f_{4}]+\psi_{5,1}[f_{5}]
and
\psi_{3,1}[L_{3}]+\psi_{5,1}[R_{5}]
The region outlined in green represents the homotopy in Figure
32
.

Figure 34:
Diagram showing the homotopy
[R_{n_{i+1}-1}]=-\psi_{n_{i+1},n_{i+1}-1}[L_{n_{i+1}}]
for
i=1,n_{2}=6
.

For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that
[g_{i}]=\psi_{n_{i},i}[L_{n_{i}}]+\psi_{n_{i+1}-1,i}[R_{n_{i+1}-1}]
, where
[L_{n_{i}}]\in\pi_{n+1}(L^{c}_{i},\omega(n_{i}))
is the homotopy class of
H_{|[0,1]^{n}\times\{n_{i}-\frac{1}{4}\}\times[0,1]}
. Similarly,
[R_{n_{i+1}-1}]\in\pi_{n+1}(L^{c}_{i},\omega(n_{i+1}-1))
is the homotopy class of
H_{|[0,1]^{n}\times\{n_{i+1}-1+\frac{1}{4}\}\times[0,1]}
. See Figure
32
and Figure
33
for a diagram of the proof for the example
[g_{1}]
, where
n_{1}=3,n_{2}=6
. By Figure
34
we have that
[R_{n_{i+1}-1}]=-\psi_{n_{i+1},n_{i+1}-1}[L_{n_{i+1}}]
. Define
[h_{i}]=\psi_{n_{i},i}[L_{n_{i}}]
. We have that

\displaystyle[h_{i}]-\psi_{i+1,i}[h_{i+1}]

\displaystyle=\psi_{n_{i},i}[L_{n_{i}}]-\psi_{i+1,i}\psi_{n_{i+1},i+1}[L_{n_{i+1}}]

\displaystyle=\psi_{n_{i},i}[L_{n_{i}}]-\psi_{i+1,i}\psi_{n_{i+1}-1,i+1}\psi_{n_{i+1},n_{i+1}-1}[L_{n_{i+1}}]

\displaystyle=\psi_{n_{i},i}[L_{n_{i}}]+\psi_{i+1,i}\psi_{n_{i+1}-1,i+1}[R_{n_{i+1}-1}]

\displaystyle=\psi_{n_{i},i}[L_{n_{i}}]+\psi_{n_{i+1}-1,i}[R_{n_{i+1}-1}]

\displaystyle=[g_{i}]

This shows that
([g_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\image\varphi
. Therefore,
\ker a=\image\varphi
, so
a
descends to an injective group homomorphism
\overline{a}:{\varprojlim}^{1}\pi_{n+1}(Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i},\omega(i))\rightarrow\pi^{e}_{n}(Y,\omega)
.

To see the sequence is exact, observe that
\image\overline{a}\subset\ker\overline{b}
comes from Figure
29
: the blue lines indicate the image of
([f_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
under
\overline{b}\circ\overline{a}
. Each
{f_{i}}_{|[0,1]^{n}\times\{i\}}
is nullhomotopic in
L^{c}_{i}
via
{f_{i}}_{|[0,1]^{n}\times[i,i+\frac{1}{4}]}
.

Suppose now that
[G^{\prime}]\in\pi^{e}_{n}(Y,\omega)
lies in
\ker\overline{b}
. There exists a strictly increasing sequence
\{j_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
G^{\prime}_{|[0,1]^{n}\times\{j_{i}\}}
is nullhomotopic in
L^{c}_{i}
. Let
\gamma
be the linear extension of the function
i\mapsto j_{i}
. By pre- and post-composing with
\id_{[0,1]^{n}}\times\gamma
and a change of base ray, we obtain
[G^{\prime}]=[b_{\gamma,\omega}G^{\prime}(\id_{[0,1]^{n}}\times\gamma)]=:[G]\in\pi^{e}_{n}(Y,\omega)
with the property that
[G_{|[0,1]^{n}\times\{i\}}]\in\pi_{n}(L^{c}_{i},\omega(i))
is nullhomotopic via a homotopy
H^{i}:[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1]\rightarrow L^{c}_{i}
. Consider the restriction
G_{|[0,1]^{n}\times[i,i+1]}
, which we denote by
G_{i}
. We can add some change of base point "wings" to the boundary
\partial[0,1]^{n}\times[i,i+1]
to turn this into an element which we denote by
g_{i}
(see Figure
35
) with the property that
{g_{i}}_{|\partial[0,1]^{n}\times[i,i+1]}=\omega(i)
. We can stack the sequence
-H^{i}+g_{i}+\psi_{i+1,i}H^{i+1}+B_{i+1,i}
together, where
B_{i+1,i}
is the change of base point
b_{i+1,i}
with wings attached, and
\psi_{i+1,i}
is written suggestively to denote the wings attached to
H^{i+1}
. We have that

\displaystyle[f_{i}]:=[-H^{i}+g_{i}+\psi_{i+1,i}H^{i+1}+B_{i+1,i}]\in\pi_{n}(L^{c}_{i},\omega(i))

for
i\in\mathbb{N}
. The element
\overline{a}([f_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is represented by Figure
36
. This element is homotopic to
G
by iteratively performing the homotopy in Figure
36
. (For
i=1
, we can rescale
[f_{1}]
so that
-[H_{1}]
takes up exactly half the interval). Note that since the boundary
\partial[0,1]^{n}\times[1,\infty)
always has image in
\omega
during the homotopies, we can freely re-parametrise the base ray via
b,b^{\prime}
. Therefore
\ker\overline{b}\subset\image\overline{a}
. This completes the proof of the short exact sequence. We omit the proof for naturality, since we only need the easier statement for the inclusion of a subcomplex.

Figure 35:
The segments
H^{i},H^{i+1}
(blue),
g_{i}
(red), and
B_{i+1,i}
(green).

Figure 36:
The homotopy between
\overline{a}([f_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and
[G]
when restricted to heights in
[i-\frac{1}{4},(i+1)+\frac{1}{4}]
. It is relative to
[0,1]^{n}\times\partial[i-\frac{1}{4},(i+1)+\frac{1}{4}]
.
b,b^{\prime}
denote changes of base ray with image in
\omega
.

∎

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
Y
be a strongly locally finite CW complex which is path-connected,
A
a path-connected subcomplex of
Y
. Let
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow A
be a base ray. If
\mathcal{L}=\{L_{i}\}
is a finite filtration of
Y
and
\omega
is well-parametrised wrt
\mathcal{L}
, then there is a natural short exact sequence of groups

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow{\varprojlim}^{1}\pi_{n+1}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\omega(i))\xrightarrow{\overline{a}}\pi_{n}^{e}(Y,A,\omega)\xrightarrow{\overline{b}}\varprojlim\pi_{n}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\omega(i))\rightarrow 0

for
n\geq 2
, where
L_{i}^{c}:=Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i}
and
(L_{i}\cap A)^{c}:=A\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}(L_{i}\cap A)=A\cap(Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i})
. For
n=1
, there is a natural short exact sequence of pointed sets

\displaystyle{\varprojlim}^{1}\pi_{2}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\omega(i))\xhookrightarrow{\overline{a}}\pi^{e}_{1}(Y,A,\omega){\mathrel{\to}^{\overline{b}}}\varprojlim\pi_{1}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\omega(i))

where
\overline{a}
is injective and
\overline{b}
is surjective.

Proof.

The situation for
n\geq 2
is analagous to Theorem
5.10
: we can define a change of base ray homomorphism
b_{\omega^{\prime},\omega}:\pi_{n}^{e}(Y,A,\omega^{\prime})\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{e}(Y,A,\omega)
by coning the construction for ordinary relative homotopy groups, and
\overline{a},\overline{b}
are defined in the obvious way. All arguments from the proof of Theorem
5.10
work with slightly different diagrams.

For
n=1
,
{\varprojlim}^{1}\pi_{n+1}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\omega(i))
is not a group, only a pointed set. This is because relative
\pi_{2}
is not necessarily abelian, so the map
\varphi
is not a group homomorphism. However, observe that the proof of Theorem
5.10
never actually uses the fact that higher homotopy groups are abelian: for example, Lemma
5.11
applies to arbitrary inverse systems of groups, and all the homotopies constructed apply to sequential elements. Therefore, it suffices to replace all the sums by products, and minus signs by inverses. The details are left as an exercise to the reader.
∎

5.3
Proper Whitehead theorem

Definition 5.13
.

Let
Y
be a finite-dimensional, strongly locally finite, path-connected CW complex,
A
a subcomplex,
\mathcal{L}
a finite filtration of
Y
. Let
n\geq 0
. We say
(Y,A)
is
n
-connected at infinity
if for every
0\leq k\leq n
and for every
i
there exists
j\geq i
such that every map
(D^{k},S^{k-1})\rightarrow(Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{j},A\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}(L_{j}\cap A))
is homotopic rel
S^{k-1}
in
Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i}
to a map whose image lies in
A
.
(Y,A)
is called
properly
n
-connected
if
(Y,A)
is
n
-connected and
n
-connected at infinity.

For example,
(Y,A)
is
0
-connected at infinity if the inclusion morphism on the set of ends
\iota_{*}:\varprojlim\pi_{0}((L_{i}\cap A)^{c})\rightarrow\varprojlim\pi_{0}(L_{i}^{c})
is surjective.

Proposition 5.14
.

(Prop
17.1.1
in
[
22
]
) The following are equivalent:

1.

A
is a proper strong deformation retract of
Y
.

2.

\iota:A\rightarrow Y
is a proper homotopy equivalence.

3.

(Y,A)
is properly
n
-connected for all
n
such that
Y-A
contains an
n
-cell.

Proof.

For our purposes, we only need the implication
3\implies 1
. For this, we consider the identity map
\id_{Y}
. The pair
(Y,A)
is properly
0
-connected, so
\id_{|Y^{0}\cup A}
can be homotoped to have image in
A
. Call this homotopy
H^{0}:(Y^{0}\cup A)\times I\rightarrow Y
.

Assume that the homotopy
H^{k-1}
has already defined on
Y^{k-1}\cup A
with
(H^{k-1})_{0}=\id_{Y^{k-1}\cup A}
and
(H^{k-1})_{1}(Y^{k-1}\cup A)\subset A
. Let
e^{k}
be a
k
-cell in
Y-A
with characteristic map
\varphi:(D^{k},S^{k-1})\rightarrow(Y^{k}\cup A,Y^{k-1}\cup A)
. Let
\psi
be the composite

\displaystyle\psi:(D^{k}\times\{0\}\cup S^{k-1}\times I,S^{k-1}\times\{1\})\xrightarrow{\varphi\cup(\varphi_{|S^{k-1}}\times\id)}

\displaystyle((Y^{k}\cup A)\times\{0\}\cup(Y^{k-1}\cup A)\times I,(Y^{k-1}\cup A)\times\{1\})\xrightarrow{\id\cup H_{k-1}}(Y,A)

Observe that there is a homotopy equivalence
(D^{k}\times\{0\}\cup S^{k-1}\times I,S^{k-1}\times\{1\})\simeq(D^{k},S^{k-1})
. Since
(Y,A)
is
0
-connected we can use the homotopy rel
S^{k-1}
to "fill in" the map
\psi
, ie. there is an extension of
\psi
to

\displaystyle\overline{\psi}:(D^{k}\times I,S^{k-1}\times I)\rightarrow(Y,A)

such that the image of
D^{k}\times\{1\}
lies in
A
. Doing this for all
k
-cells of
Y-A
simultaneously we obtain an extension of
H^{k-1}
to a homotopy
H^{k}

\displaystyle H^{k}:((Y^{k}\cup A)\times I,(Y^{k}\cup A)\times\{1\})\rightarrow(Y,A)

Repeating this process we obtain the desired homotopy

\displaystyle H:Y\times I

\displaystyle\rightarrow Y

\displaystyle H_{|Y\times\{0\}}=\id_{Y}\quad

\displaystyle H(Y\times\{1\})\subset A

To show that we can choose
H
to be proper, we need that
Y
is finite-dimensional. In defining
H
we always choose the extension of
\psi
to lie in
Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{j}
for the largest possible
j
. Let
l
be the maximal dimension of cells in
Y-A
and let
i\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed. Let
i_{l}
to be the number such that all maps
(D^{l},S^{l-1})\rightarrow(Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i_{l}},A\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}(L_{i_{l}}\cap A))
are homotopic rel
S^{l-1}
in
Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i}
to a map whose image lies in
A
. Let
i_{l-1}
be the number such that all maps
(D^{l-1},S^{l-2})\rightarrow(Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i_{l-1}},A\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}(L_{i_{l-1}}\cap A))
are homotopic rel
S^{l-1}
in
Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i_{l}}
to a map whose image lies in
A
. Repeating this process we obtain
L_{i}\subset L_{i_{l}}\subset L_{i_{l-1}}\subset\dots\subset L_{i_{1}}\subset L_{i_{0}}
.

Claim: any cell
e\in Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i_{0}}
has the property that
H(e\times I)\subset Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i}
. To show this, observe that the
0
-skeleton’s homotopy lies in
Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i_{1}}
, the
1
-skeleton’s homotopy in
Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i_{2}}
, and so on, until we get that the
l
-skeleton’s homotopy has image in
Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i}
. Therefore
H^{-1}(L_{i})\subset L_{i_{0}}\times I
which shows that
H
can be chosen to be proper.
∎

We now relate proper
n
-connectedness of
(Y,A)
to some group-theoretic properties.

Theorem 5.15
.

Let
Y
be a finite-dimensional, strongly locally finite, path-connected CW complex,
A
a subcomplex with base ray
\tau_{1}
,
\{L_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
a finite filtration of of
Y
. Assume that
\varprojlim\pi_{0}((L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau_{1}(i))
is a finite set with well-parametrised representatives
\{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{t}\}
in
\pi_{0}^{e}(A,\tau_{1})
and that
\iota_{*}:\varprojlim\pi_{0}((L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau_{1}(i))\rightarrow\varprojlim\pi_{0}(L_{i}^{c},\tau_{1}(i))
is bijective. Suppose that
\iota:A\rightarrow Y
induces an isomorphism
\iota_{*}:\pi_{n}^{e}(A,\tau)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\pi_{n}^{e}(Y,\tau)
for all
n\geq 0
and all
\tau\in\{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{t}\}
. Then there exists a
m\in\mathbb{N}
and a proper homotopy

\displaystyle H:Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{m}\times[0,1]\rightarrow Y

from the identity to a map whose image lies in
A
.

Proof.

For
n\geq 1
we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n+1}^{e}(Y,A,\tau)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varprojlim^{1}\pi_{n+1}((L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{e}(A,\tau)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varprojlim\pi_{n}((L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varprojlim^{1}\pi_{n+1}(L_{i}^{c},\tau(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{e}(Y,\tau)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varprojlim\pi_{n}(L_{i}^{c},\tau(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varprojlim^{1}\pi_{n+1}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{e}(Y,A,\tau)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varprojlim\pi_{n}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n-1}^{e}(A,\tau)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

where the horizontal arrows come from the
\varprojlim^{1}
sequence of end homotopy groups (Theorems
5.10
and
5.12
), and the middle vertical arrows come from the long exact sequence of relative end homotopy groups. All the vertical arrows are induced by inclusion maps and are the literal identity on representatives; hence the diagram commutes strictly. (Note that for
n=1
we have to replace the bottom horizontal sequence with a short exact sequence of pointed sets.) Since
\pi_{n}^{e}(A,\tau)\simeq\pi_{n}^{e}(Y,\tau)
for all
n\geq 0
and
\tau\in\{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{t}\}
we obtain

\displaystyle\pi_{n}^{e}(Y,A,\tau)=0

\displaystyle\varprojlim\pi_{n}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau(i))=0

\displaystyle{\varprojlim}^{1}\pi_{n+1}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau(i))=0

for all
n\geq 1
and
\tau\in\{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{t}\}
. By Proposition
5.7
we have that
\{\pi_{n}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau(i)),\psi_{i+1,i};\mathbb{N}\}
is pro-isomorphic to
0
for
n\geq 2
. We also have the commutative diagram (Theorem
3.36
) of pointed sets

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varprojlim^{1}\pi_{1}((L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}^{e}(A,\tau)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varprojlim\pi_{0}((L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varprojlim^{1}\pi_{1}(L_{i}^{c},\tau(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}^{e}(Y,\tau)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varprojlim\pi_{0}(L_{i}^{c},\tau(i))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the horizontal sequences are exact. Hence, we obtain an isomorphism

\displaystyle{\varprojlim}^{1}\pi_{1}((L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau(i))\simeq{\varprojlim}^{1}\pi_{1}(L_{i}^{c},\tau(i))

The next point is surprisingly subtle. We cannot directly apply Proposition
5.7
to
\{\pi_{1}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau(i))\}
since relative fundamental groups are only pointed sets, not groups. However, we have the following lemma:

Lemma 5.16
.

(Lemma
3.7
in
[
28
]
) Let
G_{i}
be an inverse sequence of groups and
H_{i}
an inverse sequence of their subgroups.

1.

If the inverse sequence of the pointed sets
G_{i}/H_{i}
of right cosets satisfies the Mittag-Leffler condition, then
{\varprojlim}^{1}H_{i}\rightarrow{\varprojlim}^{1}G_{i}
is surjective.

2.

If all the
G_{i}
are countable, the converse holds.

We can consider
H_{i}:=\pi_{1}((L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau(i))
as a subgroup of
G_{i}:=\pi_{1}(L_{i}^{c},\tau(i))
by identifying it with its image under the inclusion morphism. Since
