{\varprojlim}^{1}H_{i}\rightarrow{\varprojlim}^{1}G_{i}
is surjective, the inverse sequence of pointed sets
\{G_{i}/H_{i},\psi_{i+1};\mathbb{N}\}
satisfies the Mittag-Leffler condition. The short exact sequence of groups
0\rightarrow H_{i}\rightarrow G_{i}\rightarrow G_{i}/H_{i}\rightarrow 0
commutes with the bonding maps. By Theorem 3.1
d^{\prime})
of
[
28
]
, there is a
5
-term exact sequence of pointed sets

\displaystyle\{\ast\}\rightarrow\varprojlim H_{i}\rightarrow\varprojlim G_{i}\rightarrow\varprojlim(G_{i}/H_{i})\rightarrow{\varprojlim}^{1}H_{i}\rightarrow{\varprojlim}^{1}G_{i}

This gives us
\varprojlim(G_{i}/H_{i})=\{\ast\}
. By Lemma
5.9
we obtain that for every
i
there exists a
j>i
such that
\psi_{j,i}:G_{j}/H_{j}\rightarrow G_{i}/H_{i}
is the trivial morphism.

We now show that
(Y,A)
is
n
-connected at infinity for all
n\geq 0
. For this we need the assumption that
Y
has finitely many ends.

Proposition 5.17
.

(Prop. 13.4.8. and Addendum 13.4.9. in
[
22
]
) Let
Y
have
t
ends. There exists a
q\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
j\geq q
there exists a path-connected finite subcomplex
K_{j}
of
Y
containing
L_{j}
such that
Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}K_{j}
has exactly
t
path components.

Since
K_{j}
is compact, there is a
k\geq j
such that
K_{j}\subset L_{k}
. Therefore for any point
y
in
Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{k}
there is a path from
y
to
\tau(k)
in
Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{j}
for some
\tau\in\{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{t}\}
. We can concatenate this path with the segment
\tau_{[j,k]}^{-1}
to obtain a path in
Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{j}
from
y
to
\tau(j)
.

Remark
.

We require that
Y
has finitely many ends, because if it has infinitely many, even though a
K_{j}
can still be chosen so that
Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}K_{j}
has only unbounded path components,
K_{j}
is not necessarily compact, so the properness of the later constructed homotopy
H
fails.

Now let
n\geq 1
be fixed and
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
i\geq q
. For every
\tau\in\{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{t}\}
there exists a
j_{\tau}\geq i
such that
\iota_{*}:\pi_{n}(L_{j_{\tau}}^{c},(L_{j_{\tau}}\cap A)^{c},\tau(j_{\tau}))\rightarrow\pi_{n}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau(j_{\tau}))
is the
0
morphism. We let
j:=\max_{\tau}j_{\tau}
. The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}(L_{j}^{c},(L_{j}\cap A)^{c},\tau(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}(L_{j_{\tau}}^{c},(L_{j_{\tau}}\cap A)^{c},\tau(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}(L_{j_{\tau}}^{c},(L_{j_{\tau}}\cap A)^{c},\tau(j_{\tau}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau(j_{\tau}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota=0}

commutes because all the maps are inclusions
\iota
or changes of base point
b
using the path segment
\tau_{[j_{\tau},j]}^{-1}
. Since the
b
’s are isomorphisms, we obtain that

\displaystyle\iota_{*}:\pi_{n}(L_{j}^{c},(L_{j}\cap A)^{c},\tau(j))\rightarrow\pi_{n}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau(j))

is the
0
morphism for all
\tau\in\{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{t}\}
.

Now let
k
,
k\geq j
be the number which satisfies the condition obtained from Proposition
5.17
, ie. every point in
L_{k}^{c}
is connected to some
\tau(j)
by a path in
L_{j}^{c}
. Suppose we now have a map:

\displaystyle f:(D^{n},S^{n-1},s)\rightarrow(L_{k}^{c},(L_{k}\cap A)^{c},f(s))

This defines a homotopy class
[f]\in\pi_{n}(L_{k}^{c},(L_{k}\cap A)^{c},f(s))
. Now we have the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}(L_{k}^{c},(L_{k}\cap A)^{c},f(s))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}(L_{j}^{c},(L_{j}\cap A)^{c},f(s))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},f(s))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}(L_{j}^{c},(L_{j}\cap A)^{c},\tau(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\tau(j))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota=0}

which commutes since all the maps are inclusions
\iota
or changes of base point
b
using the path from
f(s)
to
\tau(j)
constructed above. Therefore,
\iota_{*}:\pi_{n}(L_{k}^{c},(L_{k}\cap A)^{c},f(s))\rightarrow\pi_{n}(L_{i}^{c},(L_{i}\cap A)^{c},f(s))
is the
0
-map. This shows that every map
(D^{n},S^{n-1})\rightarrow(L_{k}^{c},(L_{k}\cap A)^{c})
can be homotoped rel
S^{n-1}
in
L_{i}^{c}
to a map whose image lies in
A
.

This shows that
(Y,A)
is
n
-connected at infinity for all
n\geq 1
.
(Y,A)
is
0
-connected at infinity because
\iota:A\rightarrow Y
is surjective on the set of ends. If additionally
(Y,A)
is
n
-connected for all
n\geq 0
, (eg. if both
A
and
Y
are contractible), then by Proposition
5.14
we obtain a proper strong deformation retract of
Y
to
A
. Without this assumption, there still exists a proper deformation retract outside a compact subcomplex, defined in the following way:

Let
l
be the maximal dimension of cells in
Y-A
. Let
q_{l}
be the number such that all maps
(D^{l},S^{l-1})\rightarrow(Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{q_{l}},A\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}(L_{q_{l}}\cap A))
are homotopic rel
S^{l-1}
to a map whose image lies in
A
. Let
q_{l-1}
be the number such that all maps
(D^{l-1},S^{l-2})\rightarrow(Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{q_{l-1}},A\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}(L_{q_{l-1}}\cap A))
are homotopic rel
S^{l-2}
in
Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{q_{l}}
to a map whose image lies in
A
. Repeating this process we obtain
L_{q_{l}}\subset L_{q_{l-1}}\subset\dots\subset L_{q_{1}}\subset L_{q_{0}}
. Let
m:=q_{0}
.

There is a proper homotopy
H:Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{m}\times[0,1]\rightarrow Y
from the identity map to a map whose image lies in
A
. To see this, we simply follow the proof of Proposition
5.14
, this time only considering cells in
(Y-A)\cap(Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{m})
. Our choice of
m
means that
H
can be extended skeleta by skeleta to be defined on all of
Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{m}
. Furthermore, as in the proof of Proposition
5.14
, we can choose
H
to be proper by picking for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
i\geq m
a sequence of natural numbers
i\leq i_{l}\leq i_{l-1}\leq\dots\leq i_{0}
such that any cell
e\in Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i_{0}}
has the property that
H(e\times[0,1])\subset Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i}
.

This concludes the proof of Theorem
5.15
.
∎

Now we extend to the case where
\psi:X\rightarrow Y
is a proper cellular map which induces an isomorphism on end homotopy groups and the set of ends by a standard trick in topology involving the mapping cylinder
M_{\psi}
.

Corollary 5.18
.

Let
X
and
Y
be finite-dimensional, strongly locally finite, path-connected CW complexes with finitely many ends. Let
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{t}\in\pi_{0}^{e}(X,\omega_{1})
be proper rays which represent the ends of
X
. Let
\psi:X\rightarrow Y
be a proper cellular map which induces an isomorphism
\psi:\pi_{n}^{e}(X,\omega)\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{e}(Y,\psi\omega)
for all
n\geq 0
and all
\omega\in\{\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{t}\}
, and a bijection on the set of ends. Then there exist finite subcomplexes
Q\subset Y
and
L\subset X
and a map

\displaystyle\xi:(Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}Q)\rightarrow X

such that

\displaystyle\xi\circ\psi_{|X\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L}\simeq\id_{|X\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L}

\displaystyle\psi\circ\xi\simeq\id_{|Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}Q}

If
\psi
additionally induces an isomorphism on ordinary homotopy groups,
Q=\emptyset
,
L=\emptyset
, and
\xi
is a proper homotopy inverse to
\psi
.

Proof.

We consider the mapping cylinder

\displaystyle M_{\psi}:=X\times[0,1]\cup_{\psi}Y

There are canonical maps

\displaystyle i:X\rightarrow M_{\psi}

\displaystyle j:Y\rightarrow M_{\psi}

\displaystyle j^{-1}:M_{\psi}\rightarrow Y

where
i
denotes inclusion into
X\times\{0\}
,
j
is also the inclusion, and
j^{-1}
is the deformation retract of
M_{\psi}
to
Y
.
i
and
j
are obviously proper, and
j^{-1}
is proper since the preimage of a compact set
K\subset Y
is
\psi^{-1}(K)\times[0,1]\cup_{\psi}K
. By standard arguments,
j^{-1}
is a proper homotopy inverse to
j
and therefore induces an isomorphism on all end homotopy groups. Let
\omega
be a base ray in
X
. Observe that the composition

\displaystyle(X,\omega)\xrightarrow{i}(M_{\psi},\omega\times\{0\})\xrightarrow{j^{-1}}(Y,\psi\omega)

is exactly the map
\psi
, which by assumption induces an isomorphism on end homotopy groups. Therefore

\displaystyle i_{*}:\pi_{n}^{e}(X,\omega)\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{e}(M_{\psi},\omega\times\{0\})

is an isomorphism for all
n\geq 0
.

We can choose a finite filtration on the mapping cylinder as follows. Choose finite filtrations
\{L_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}},\{Q_{l}\}_{l\in\mathbb{N}}
on
X
and
Y
respectively so that
\omega
is well-parametrised with respect to
\{L_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. Then for every
k
there exists a
l_{k}
such that
\psi(L_{k})\subset Q_{l_{k}}
. Take
\{l_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
to be increasing. We define

\displaystyle M_{k}:=L_{k}\times I\cup Q_{l_{k}}

\mathcal{M}:=\{M_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a finite filtration for
M_{\psi}
, and
\omega\times\{0\}
is well-parametrised with respect to
\mathcal{M}
.

By Theorem
5.15
there is a
m\in\mathbb{N}
and a homotopy
H:(M_{\psi}\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}M_{m})\times[0,1]\rightarrow M_{\psi}
such that
H_{1}(M_{\psi}\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}M_{m})\subset X\times\{0\}
. Let
r=H_{1}
. Since
\psi
is proper, there exists a
k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
,
k_{m}>m
such that
\psi(X\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{k_{m}})\subset Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}Q_{l_{m}}
. We have that the homotopy
G:M_{\psi}\times[0,1]\rightarrow M_{\psi}
from
j\circ j^{-1}
to
\id_{M_{\psi}}
, when restricted to
(M_{\psi}\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}M_{k_{m}})\times[0,1]
, has image in
M_{\psi}\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}M_{m}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\psi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

Let
K=M_{m},K^{\prime}=M_{k_{m}}
. Let
Q,L
be finite subcomplexes chosen such that
j(Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}Q)\subset(M_{\psi}\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}K^{\prime})
,
i(X\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L)\subset(M_{\psi}\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}K^{\prime})
, and
\psi(X\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L)\subset(Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}Q)
. We define a homotopy inverse to
\psi
as
\xi:=r\circ j_{|Q^{c}}:(Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}Q)\rightarrow X
. To check that this is actually what we want, we compute

\displaystyle\psi\circ r\circ j_{|Q^{c}}=j^{-1}\circ i\circ r\circ j_{|Q^{c}}\simeq j^{-1}\circ\id_{|K^{\prime c}}\circ j_{|Q^{c}}\simeq j^{-1}\circ j_{|Q^{c}}=\id_{|Q^{c}}

\displaystyle r\circ j_{|Q^{c}}\circ\psi_{|L^{c}}=r\circ j\circ j^{-1}\circ i_{|L^{c}}\simeq r\circ\id_{K^{\prime c}}\circ i_{|L^{c}}=r\circ i_{|L^{c}}=\id_{|L^{c}}

All the homotopies above are proper, as they are a composition of proper maps and proper homotopies. This completes the proof. Note that if additionally
\psi
induces an isomorphism on ordinary homotopy groups, we can let
K=\emptyset
,
Q=\emptyset
,
L=\emptyset
and obtain a proper homotopy inverse
\xi:Y\rightarrow X
.
∎

Remark
.

It should be noted that the homotopies

\displaystyle\xi\circ\psi_{|X\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L}\simeq\id_{|X\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L}

\displaystyle\psi\circ\xi\simeq\id_{|Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}Q}

do not have to remain in
X\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L
and
Y\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}Q
respectively.

Corollary 5.19
.

Let
X
and
Y
be finite-dimensional, strongly locally finite, path-connected CW complexes with finitely many ends. Let
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{t}\in\pi_{0}^{e}(X,\omega_{1})
be proper rays which represent the ends of
X
. Let
\psi:X\rightarrow Y
be a proper cellular map which induces an isomorphism
\psi:\pi_{n}^{e}(X,\omega)\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{e}(Y,\psi\omega)
for all
n\geq 0
and all
\omega\in\{\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{t}\}
, and a bijection on the set of ends. Assume further that
\psi
also induces an isomorphism on ordinary homotopy groups. Then there is a proper homotopy inverse
\xi
to
\psi
.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
5.14
, Theorem
5.15
and Corollary
5.18
. The combination of
\psi:\pi_{n}^{e}(X,\omega)\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{e}(Y,\psi\omega)
being an isomorphism for all
n\geq 0
, being an isomorphism on
\pi_{n}
for all
n\geq 0
and inducing a bijection on the finite set of ends, imply that
i:X\rightarrow M_{\psi}
is properly
n
-connected for all
n\geq 0
.
∎

Remark
.

It may seem strange that we only require isomorphisms
\psi:\pi_{n}^{e}(X,\omega)\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{e}(Y,\psi\omega)
for a fixed set of representatives
\omega\in\{\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{t}\}
for the ends of
X
, rather than all proper homotopy classes of proper rays
\pi_{0}^{e}(X)
, as this set can be much larger. This has to do with the relationship between shape theory and strong shape theory (Subsection
6.3
).

6
Shape Theory

This chapter introduces some basic notions about shape theory and proves some further results about coarse homotopy groups. There are two main constructions of shape theory. The first, originally introduced by Borsuk, uses systems of neighbourhoods and fundamental sequences, and only applies to compact metrisable spaces. The second, attributed to Mardes̆ić, Segal and Morita, uses inverse systems, and is applicable to arbitrary topological spaces. We will work with the inverse system contruction.

6.1
Inverse system construction

Let hTop be the homotopy category of topological spaces, where the objects are topological spaces and the morphisms between them are homotopy classes of continous functions. The homotopy category hCW of spaces homotopy equivalent to CW complexes, is a subcategory of hTop. We can construct the categories inv-hTop and pro-hTop as discussed in Subsection
5.1
. Then inv-hCW (resp. pro-hCW) is a subcategory of inv-hTop (resp. pro-hTop).

Definition 6.1
.

•

A metrisable topological space
Y
is an
absolute neighbourhood retract
(ANR) if for any embedding
Y\subset Z
as a closed subspace in a metrisable topological space
Z
,
Y
is a neighbourhood retract of
Z
.

•

A metrisable topological space
Y
is an
absolute retract
(AR) if for any embedding
Y\subset Z
as a closed subspace in a metrisable topological space
Z
,
Y
is a retract of
Z
.

Proposition 6.2
.

(Lemma 1.1 in
[
33
]
) For a topological space
X
, the following conditions are equivalent:

1.

X
has the homotopy type of a CW complex.

2.

X
has the homotopy type of a simplicial complex with the metric topology.

3.

X
has the homotopy type of an ANR.

We call such an
X
an ANR-space. Taking this into account, we can say that hCW is the homotopy category of spaces having the homotopy type of an ANR. In shape theory, only the properties of ANRs are used, so we refer to inverse systems in inv-hCW as ANR-systems.

Proposition 6.3
.

(Proposition II.7.2 in
[
42
]
) A metrisable topological space is an absolute retract exactly when it is a contractible topological space and an absolute neighbourhood retract.

Definition 6.4
.

An inverse ANR-system
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{\alpha},[\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}];\mathcal{A}\}\in
inv-hCW is said to be
associated with a topological space

X
if there are homotopy classes of continuous maps
[p_{\alpha}]:X\rightarrow X_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
, satisfying the following conditions:

1.

[p_{\alpha}]=[\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}][p_{\alpha^{\prime}}]
for all
\alpha\leq\alpha^{\prime}
.

2.

For any ANR-space
P
and any homotopy class
[f]:X\rightarrow P
, there exist an
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
and a homotopy class
[h_{\alpha}]:X_{\alpha}\rightarrow P
such that
[h_{\alpha}][p_{\alpha}]=[f]
.

3.

If
[h][p_{\alpha}]=[h^{\prime}][p_{\alpha}]
for two homotopy classes
[h],[h^{\prime}]:X_{\alpha}\rightarrow P
, then there exists an index
\alpha^{\prime}\geq\alpha
such that
[h][\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}]=[h^{\prime}][\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}]
.

We all such a system
\mathcal{X}
a
shape expansion
of
X
.

Analagously, we say that an inverse ANR-system
\mathcal{X}=\{(X_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}),[\phi_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}];\mathcal{A}\}\in
inv-hCW
∗
is associated to the pointed topological space
(X,x_{0})
, if all the maps and homotopies in the above definition are pointed. We call
\mathcal{X}
a
pointed shape expansion
of
X
.

Proposition 6.5
.

For any topological space
X
, there exists an associated inverse ANR-system. Moreover, any two ANR-systems associated to
X
are isomorphic in pro-hCW by a canonical isomorphism.

Proof.

Let
\{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\,|\,\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\}
be the set of normal, locally finite open covers of
X
. If a cover
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
is a refinement of
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
, then we write
\alpha^{\prime}\geq\alpha
. Let
X_{\alpha}
be the geometric realisation of the nerve of
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
; this is a simplicial complex with the metric topology. For each
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
, there exists a continous map
p_{\alpha}:X\rightarrow X_{\alpha}
(which can be defined via a partition of unity subordinate to
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
) satisfying the condition

\displaystyle p_{\alpha}^{-1}\langle st(u,X_{\alpha})\rangle\subset U

where
u
is the vertex of
X_{\alpha}
corresponding to the element
U
of the cover
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
. A map satisfying this condition is said to be a
canonical projection
. All canonical projections are homotopic to one another. If
\alpha^{\prime}\geq\alpha
then there exists a simplicial map
\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}:X_{\alpha^{\prime}}\rightarrow X_{\alpha}
(coming from the refinement of open covers) such that
\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}(u)=v
implies
U\subset V
, where
u
and
v
are the vertices of the simplicial complex
X_{\alpha^{\prime}}
and
X_{\alpha}
corresponding to the elements
U
and
V
of the covers
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
respectively. The map
\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}
is called a
canonical bonding map
. Any two canonical bonding maps are homotopic to one another. Moreover

\displaystyle[\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}][p_{\alpha^{\prime}}]=[p_{\alpha}]

for all
\alpha^{\prime}\geq\alpha
. By Theorem 4.3 of
[
32
]
,
\{X_{\alpha},[\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}];\mathcal{A}\}
is associated to X.

To show uniqueness, suppose that
\mathcal{Y}:=\{Y_{\beta},\varphi_{\beta^{\prime},\beta};\mathcal{B}\}
is another inverse ANR-system associated to
X
, with projections
[q_{\beta}]:X\rightarrow Y_{\beta}
. For
\beta\in\mathcal{B}
there exist
\rho(\beta)\in\mathcal{A}
and
h_{\beta}:X_{\rho(\beta)}\rightarrow Y_{\beta}
such that
[h_{\beta}][p_{\rho(\beta)}]=[q_{\beta}]
. Analogously, for
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
there exist
\eta(\alpha)\in\mathcal{B}
and
k_{\alpha}:Y_{\eta(\alpha)}\rightarrow X_{\alpha}
such that
[k_{\alpha}][q_{\eta(\alpha)}]=[p_{\alpha}]
. The pair
(\rho,\{h_{\beta}\}):\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is a morphism in inv-hCW. This is because for any
\beta^{\prime}\geq\beta
there exists an
\alpha^{\prime}\geq\rho(\beta),\rho(\beta^{\prime})
such that
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\alpha^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\alpha^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho(\beta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho(\beta^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{\beta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{\beta^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\rho(\beta)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\rho(\beta^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\beta^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}}

commutes up to homotopy, where the unlabelled arrows are canonical projections. We obtain

\displaystyle[\varphi_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}][h_{\beta^{\prime}}][\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\rho(\beta^{\prime})}][p_{\alpha^{\prime}}]=[h_{\beta}][\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\rho(\beta)}][p_{\alpha^{\prime}}]

Since
\mathcal{Y}
is associated to
X
, there exists
\alpha^{\prime\prime}\geq\alpha
such that

\displaystyle[\varphi_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}][h_{\beta^{\prime}}][\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\rho(\beta^{\prime})}][\phi_{\alpha^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}]=[h_{\beta}][\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\rho(\beta)}][\phi_{\alpha^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}]

This shows that
(\rho,\{h_{\beta}\})
is a morphism in inv-hCW. Analogously,
(\eta,\{k_{\alpha}\}):\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
is a morphism in inv-hCW.

We show that the composition
(\eta,\{k_{\alpha}\})\circ(\rho,\{h_{\beta}\})=(\rho\circ\eta,\{k_{\alpha}\circ h_{\eta(\alpha)}\})
is equivalent to the identity. The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho\eta(\alpha)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho\eta(\alpha)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{\eta(\alpha)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\rho\eta(\alpha)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\eta(\alpha)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\rho\eta(\alpha)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\eta(\alpha)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\rho\eta(\alpha),\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\alpha}}

commutes up to homotopy. We have that
[k_{\alpha}][h_{\eta(\alpha)}][p_{\rho\eta(\alpha)}]=[\phi_{\rho\eta(\alpha),\alpha}][p_{\rho\eta(\alpha)}]
. Therefore, there exists
\alpha^{\prime}\geq\rho\eta(\alpha)
such that

\displaystyle[k_{\alpha}][h_{\eta(\alpha)}][\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\rho\eta(\alpha)}]=[\phi_{\rho\eta(\alpha),\alpha}][\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\rho\eta(\alpha)}]=[\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}]

This shows that
(\rho\circ\eta,\{k_{\alpha}\circ h_{\eta(\alpha)}\})
is equivalent to the identity. The composition
(\rho,\{h_{\beta}\})\circ(\eta,\{k_{\alpha}\})
is also equivalent to the identity, by the same argument. Therefore, the systems
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
are pro-isomorphic.
∎

Analagously, for every pointed topological space
(X,x_{0})
, there exists an associated pointed inverse ANR-system, by taking normal, locally finite open covers of
X
with distinguished element
U_{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
where
x_{0}\in U_{0}
. The maps
p_{\alpha}
are defined via partitions of unity subordinate to
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{\alpha}
, where
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{\alpha}
denotes the modified open cover as discussed in the proof of Lemma
4.10
. Any two pointed ANR-systems associated to
(X,x_{0})
are isomorphic in pro-hCW
∗
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{\alpha},[\phi_{\beta,\alpha}];\mathcal{A}\}
be an inverse ANR-system associated to
X
and suppose
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\{X_{\alpha^{\prime}},[\phi_{\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}];\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\}
is cofinal in
\mathcal{X}
. Then
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
is also associated to
X
.

Proof.

For any homotopy class
[f]:X\rightarrow P
there exists
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
and
[h_{\alpha}]:X_{\alpha}\rightarrow P
such that
[h_{\alpha}][p_{\alpha}]=[f]
. By cofinality, there is an
\alpha^{\prime}\geq\alpha
in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. We let
h^{\prime}_{\alpha^{\prime}}:=h_{\alpha}\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}
. Then
[h^{\prime}_{\alpha^{\prime}}][p_{\alpha^{\prime}}]=[h_{\alpha}][\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}][p_{\alpha^{\prime}}]=[h_{\alpha}][p_{\alpha}]=[f]
, as required.

Suppose we have two homotopy classes
[h],[k]:X_{\alpha^{\prime}}\rightarrow P
with
[h][p_{\alpha^{\prime}}]=[k][p_{\alpha^{\prime}}]
. There exists an index
\beta\in\mathcal{A}
such that
[h][\phi_{\beta,\alpha^{\prime}}]=[k][\phi_{\beta,\alpha^{\prime}}]
. By cofinality there is a
\beta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
with
\beta^{\prime}\geq\beta
. We obtain

\displaystyle[h][\phi_{\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}]=[h][\phi_{\beta,\alpha^{\prime}}][\phi_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}]=[k][\phi_{\beta,\alpha^{\prime}}][\phi_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}]=[k][\phi_{\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}]

∎

The analagous statement is true for pointed inverse ANR-systems.

Let us restrict to the case where
X
is a compact metric space. The ANR-system
\{|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,[\phi_{h+1}];\mathbb{N}\}
with projections
\varphi_{h}:X\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
is cofinal in
\{X_{\alpha},[\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}];\mathcal{A}\}
. Therefore, it is an inverse system associated to
X
. Additionally, it is pointed (with modified projections
\varphi^{\prime}_{h}
), and cofinal in the pointed inverse ANR-system of normal, locally finite open covers of
X
with distinguished element. So, it is a pointed shape expansion of
(X,x_{0})
.

Notation
.

From now on we omit the brackets to indicate homotopy class, eg. it is understood that
\phi_{h+1}:|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
denotes the homotopy class of
\phi_{h+1}
.

There are two other helpful ways to obtain an ANR-system associated to
X
.

1.

We can embed the compact metric space
X
as a closed subset of an AR
P
(eg. via the Kuratowski embedding into the Banach space
C_{b}(X)
). Consider the set
\{N\}
of all open neighbourhoods
N
of
X
in
P
. The inverse system
\{N,\iota_{N^{\prime},N}\}
of ANRs with inclusions
\iota_{N^{\prime},N}
as bonding maps, denoted by
U(X,P)
, is called the
complete neighbourhood system
of
X
in
P
.
\{N,\iota_{N^{\prime},N}\}
is an inverse system associated to
X
with inclusion maps
\iota_{N}:X\rightarrow N
as the canonical projections. If we equip
P
with a metric, then the inverse sequence
\{N_{k},\iota_{k+1,k};\mathbb{N}\}
of
\frac{1}{k}
-neighbourhoods of
X
is cofinal in
\{N,\iota_{N^{\prime},N}\}
.

2.

Let
X=\varprojlim_{k}X_{k}
be the limit (in Top) of an inverse sequence
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{k},\phi_{k+1,k};\mathbb{N}\}
, where
X_{k}
have the homotopy type of ANRs. The canonical projections
p_{k}:X\rightarrow X_{k}
, in this case, come from the definition of
X
as a limit.

By Theorems
1.4
and
1.9
of
[
33
]
, these two inverse ANR-systems are associated to
X
. If
x_{0}\in X
, then
\{(N_{k},x_{0}),\iota_{k+1,k};\mathbb{N}\}
is a pointed shape expansion of
(X,x_{0})
. Analagously, if
(X,x_{0})=\varprojlim_{k}(X_{k},x_{k})
is the limit (in Top
∗
) of an inverse sequence
\mathcal{X}=\{(X_{k},x_{k}),\phi_{k+1,k};\mathbb{N}\}
with canonical projections
p_{k}:(X,x_{0})\rightarrow(X_{k},x_{k})
, then
\mathcal{X}
is a pointed shape expansion of
(X,x_{0})
.

Consider now a continous map
f:X\rightarrow Y
between topological spaces. Let
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha};\mathcal{A}\},\mathcal{Y}=\{Y_{\beta},\varphi_{\beta^{\prime},\beta},\mathcal{B}\}
be shape expansions of
X
and
Y
respectively, with canonical projections
p_{\alpha},q_{\beta}
. We construct a morphism
S(f):\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
as follows. For every
\beta\in\mathcal{B}
, there exists a
\rho(\beta)\in\mathcal{A}
and
h_{\beta}:X_{\rho(\beta)}\rightarrow Y_{\beta}
such that
q_{\beta}\circ f=h_{\beta}\circ p_{\rho(\beta)}
up to homotopy. The pair
(\rho,\{h_{\beta}\}):\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is a morphism in inv-hCW. It can be checked that any other choice of
(\rho^{\prime},\{h^{\prime}_{\beta}\})
is equivalent to
(\rho,\{h_{\beta}\})
in pro-hCW. Moreover, homotopic maps
f,g
induce (by construction) equivalent morphisms in pro-hCW. The morphism
(\rho,\{h_{\beta}\}):\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
constructed in Proposition
6.5
between two shape expansions of
X
satisfies
S(\id_{X})=(\rho,\{h_{\beta}\})
.

Definition 6.7
.

(The category Shape) Let Shape be the category whose objects are pairs
(\mathcal{X},X)
, where
\mathcal{X}
is a shape expansion of
X
. A morphism
(\mathcal{X},X)\rightarrow(\mathcal{Y},Y)
in Shape is a morphism
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
in pro-hCW.

Definition 6.8
.

(The shape functor) Let
X
be an object in hTop. The
shape functor

S
sends
X
to
(\mathcal{X},X)
, where
\mathcal{X}
is a shape expansion of
X
. To define
S
on morphisms, let
X,Y\in
hTop,
f:X\rightarrow Y
a homotopy class of continuous maps and let
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
be ANR-systems associated to
X,Y
. We define
S(f):=(\rho,\{h_{\beta}\}):\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
. We obtain a functor

\displaystyle S:\text{hTop}\rightarrow\text{Shape}

We say that two spaces
X,Y
are
shape equivalent
if
S(X),S(Y)
are equivalent objects in Shape.

Analagously, there is a pointed shape category Shape
∗
and a pointed shape functor
S:
hTop
∗

\rightarrow
Shape
∗
.

Remark
.

S
is dependent on a choice of shape expansion
\mathcal{X}
for each
X\in
hTop. A different set of choices results in a functor
S^{\prime}
which is naturally isomorphic to
S
.

Proposition 6.9
.

(Page 49 of
[
27
]
) The shape functor
S:
hTop
\rightarrow
Shape restricted to the subcategory hCW of hTop is full and faithful.

This means that while the shape classification of spaces is in general weaker than the homotopy classification, on the class hCW, both classifications coincide.

Definition 6.10
.

Let
(X,x_{0})
be a pointed topological space and
\mathcal{X}=\{(X_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}),\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha};\mathcal{A}\}
a pointed shape expansion of
(X,x_{0})
. Applying the functors
\pi_{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
to this system, we obtain the homotopy pro-groups (or pointed sets) of the pointed space
(X,x_{0})

\displaystyle\text{pro-}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{X},x_{0})=\{\pi_{n}(X_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}),\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha};\mathcal{A}\}

Let
F=(\rho,\{q_{\beta}\}):\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be a pointed shape morphism between pointed shape expansions
\{(X_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}),\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha};\mathcal{A}\},\{(X_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}),\psi_{\beta^{\prime},\beta};\mathcal{B}\}
of
(X,x_{0}),(Y,y_{0})
. By applying the functors
\pi_{n}
to
\{q_{\beta}\}
, we obtain the induced morphism of pro-groups (or pointed sets)

\displaystyle\text{pro-}\pi_{n}(F):\text{pro-}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{X},x_{0})\rightarrow\text{pro-}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{Y},y_{0})

This gives us a functor

\displaystyle\text{pro-}\pi_{n}:\text{Shape}_{\ast}\rightarrow\text{pro-}\mathcal{C}

where
\mathcal{C}
is the category of groups for
n\geq 1
and pointed sets for
n=0
.

We write the pro-homotopy groups of
X
as
\text{pro-}\pi_{n}(X,x_{0}):=\text{pro-}\pi_{n}(S(X),x_{0})
. This is well-defined up to canonical natural isomorphism. Post-composing with the functor
\varprojlim:
pro-
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
yields the shape homotopy groups of
(X,x_{0})
. (Definition
4.1
).

6.2
Proper homotopy theory and Shape

An important theorem in shape theory is the shape Whitehead theorem:

Theorem 6.11
.

(Theorem C in
[
31
]
) A pointed shape morphism
F:S(X,x_{0})\rightarrow S(Y,y_{0})
of finite shape-dimensional, connected topological spaces is a pointed shape equivalence if and only if it induces an isomorphism pro-
\pi_{n}(F)
:pro-
\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})\rightarrow
pro-
\pi_{n}(Y,y_{0})
of homotopy pro-groups for all
n\geq 0
.

In this subsection, we will present a proof of this theorem (Corollary
6.22
) when
X,Y
are compact metric spaces. In doing so, we connect the proper homotopy theory of the inverse mapping telescopes
M_{\mathcal{X}},M_{\mathcal{Y}}
associated to pointed shape expansions of
(X,x_{0})
and
(Y,y_{0})
to the shapes
S(X,x_{0}),S(Y,y_{0})
. To do this, we have to assume that each space
X_{h}
in a pointed shape expansion
\mathcal{X}=\{(X_{h},x_{h}),\phi_{h+1,h};\mathbb{N}\}
is an actual simplicial complex,
x_{0}
is a vertex, and each
\phi_{h+1,h}
is simplicial.

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{(X_{h},x_{h}),\phi_{h+1,h};\mathbb{N}\}
be a pointed ANR-expansion of
(X,x_{0})
. Let
(X^{\prime}_{h},x^{\prime}_{h})
be a finite simplicial complex and
f_{h}:(X^{\prime}_{h},x^{\prime}_{h})\rightarrow(X_{h},x_{h})
a pointed homotopy equivalence with inverse
f_{h}^{-1}
. Then the inverse system

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}=\{(X^{\prime}_{h},x^{\prime}_{h}),f_{h}^{-1}\phi_{h+1,h}f_{h+1};\mathbb{N}\}

is associated to
X
. The analogous statement for the unpointed case holds.

The proof of the lemma is left as an exercise in following the definitions. We can always assume that
x^{\prime}_{h}
is a vertex of
X^{\prime}_{h}
, by stellar subdivision of the unique simplex whose interior contains
x^{\prime}_{h}
. By simplicial approximation, we can assume that
f_{h}^{-1}\phi_{h+1,h}f_{h+1}
is base point preserving and simplicial.

Recall that we have a fixed pointed shape expansion
\{(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,x_{0}),\phi_{h+1};\mathbb{N}\}
of
(X,x_{0})
, with inverse mapping telescope
M_{X}
. Since we are now only interested in proper homotopy, the scaling on the height variable of
M_{X}
does not matter. We can therefore identify it topologically with

\displaystyle M_{X}:=|\mathcal{U}_{0}|\cup_{{\phi}_{1,0}}|\mathcal{U}_{1}|\times[1,2]\cup_{{\phi}_{2,1}}|\mathcal{U}_{2}|\times[2,3]\cup_{{\phi}_{3,2}}\dots

Indeed, for any pointed shape expansion
\mathcal{X}=\{(X_{h},x_{h}),\phi_{h+1,h};\mathbb{N}\}
of
(X,x_{0})
, we can define

\displaystyle M_{\mathcal{X}}:=\{\ast\}\cup_{\phi_{1,0}}X_{1}\times[1,2]\cup_{\phi_{2,1}}X_{2}\times[2,3]\cup_{\phi_{3,2}}\dots

where
\phi_{1,0}:X_{1}\times\{1\}\rightarrow\{\ast\}=:X_{0}
is the unique map to a point. There is a base ray
\omega:[1,\infty)
associated to
x_{0}
which takes the value
\omega(t)=(x_{t},t)
. Assuming that all
X_{h}
are finite simplicial complexes,
M_{\mathcal{X}}
is a strongly locally finite, path-connected CW complex.

Let
(X,x_{0}),(Y,y_{0})
be pointed compact metric spaces with pointed shape expansions
\mathcal{X}=\{(X_{h},x_{h}),\phi_{h+1,h};\mathbb{N}\},\mathcal{Y}=\{(Y_{h},y_{h}),\varphi_{h+1,h};\mathbb{N}\}
. Let
\omega
and
\tau
be the base rays associated to
x_{0}
and
y_{0}
respectively. Suppose that
\psi:M_{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow M_{\mathcal{Y}}
is a continuous, proper map.

Lemma 6.13
.

\psi:M_{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow M_{\mathcal{Y}}
induces a morphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\psi):=(\rho,\{q_{h}\}):\{X_{h},\phi_{h+1,h};\mathbb{N}\}\rightarrow\{Y_{h},\varphi_{h+1,h};\mathbb{N}\}

in pro-hCW. If
\psi:(M_{\mathcal{X}},\omega)\rightarrow(M_{\mathcal{Y}},\tau)
is a map of pairs (ie.
\psi(\omega)\subset\tau
), then this morphism is pointed.

Proof.

Define a finite filtration
\{L_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}}
of
M_{\mathcal{X}}
by letting
L_{h}:=(M_{\mathcal{X}})_{[1,h]}
be everything with height in the interval
[1,h]
. Analogously, let
Q_{h}:=(M_{\mathcal{Y}})_{[1,h]}
. As before, the CW complement of
L_{h}
, denoted by
L_{h}^{c}:=M_{\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{h}
, consists of all points with height
\geq h+1
, and deformation retracts onto the slice
X_{h}\times\{h+1\}
. Analogously,
Q^{c}_{h}
deformation retracts to the slice
Y_{h}\times\{h+1\}
.

Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
be given. Since
\psi
is proper, there exists a
\rho(i)
such that
\psi(L_{\rho(i)}^{c})\subset Q_{i}^{c}
. We define
q_{i}
as the homotopy class of

\displaystyle q_{i}:X_{\rho(i)}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow Y_{i}\quad q_{i}:=\mathfrak{q}^{Y}_{i}\circ\psi_{|X_{{\rho(i)}}\times\{\rho(i)+1\}}

where
\mathfrak{q}^{Y}_{i}
refers to the deformation retract of
Q^{c}_{i}\subset M_{\mathcal{Y}}
to height
i+1
. We can always choose
\rho
to be an increasing sequence.

We check that
(\rho,\{q_{h}\})
actually defines a morphism in pro-hCW. Let
i\leq j
. We choose
\alpha=\rho(j)
and obtain the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\rho(j),\rho(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{j,i}}

To show commutativity, observe that
\varphi_{ji}q_{j}
is obtained from the map
\mathfrak{q}^{Y}_{i}\mathfrak{q}^{Y}_{j}\psi_{|X_{\rho(j)}\times\{\rho(j)+1\}}
.
q_{i}\phi_{\rho(j),\rho(i)}
is obtained by the map
\mathfrak{q}^{Y}_{i}\psi_{|X_{\rho(i)}\times\{\rho(i)+1\}}\mathfrak{q}^{X}_{\rho(i)}
, where
\mathfrak{q}^{X}_{\rho(i)}
refers to the deformation retract of
L^{c}_{\rho(j)}\subset M_{\mathcal{X}}
to the height slice
\{\rho(i)+1\}
. Since
\psi\mathfrak{q}^{X}_{\rho(i)}
is homotopic to
\psi
we have that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}^{Y}_{i}\psi\mathfrak{q}^{X}_{\rho(i)}\simeq\mathfrak{q}^{Y}_{i}\psi=\mathfrak{q}^{Y}_{i}\mathfrak{q}^{Y}_{j}\psi

as required. By a similar calculation, one can show that a different choice of
\rho
results in an equivalent morphism in pro-hCW.

If
\psi(\omega)\subset\tau
, then we have
q_{i}(x_{\rho(i)},\rho(i)+1)=\mathfrak{q}^{Y}_{i}\circ\psi(x_{\rho(i)},\rho(i)+1)=(y_{i},i+1)
. Therefore
(\rho,\{q_{h}\})
is a pointed morphism.
∎

Assume that
\psi
has a partially defined proper homotopy inverse
\xi:(M_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}Q)\rightarrow M_{\mathcal{X}}
in the sense of Corollary
5.18
. Then we have the following:

Lemma 6.14
.

The map
\mathcal{P}(\xi):\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
induced by
\xi:(M_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}Q)\rightarrow M_{\mathcal{X}}
has the property that
\mathcal{P}(\xi)\circ\mathcal{P}(\psi)=\id_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\mathcal{P}(\psi)\circ\mathcal{P}(\xi)=\id_{\mathcal{Y}}
in pro-hCW.

If
\psi:(M_{\mathcal{X}},\omega)\rightarrow(M_{\mathcal{Y}},\tau)
and
\xi:(M_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}Q,(M_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}Q)\cap\tau)\rightarrow(M_{\mathcal{X}},\omega)
are maps of pairs, such that
\xi\circ\psi\simeq\id_{|L^{c}}
and
\psi\circ\xi\simeq\id_{|Q^{c}}
as maps of pairs, then
\mathcal{P}(\xi)\circ\mathcal{P}(\psi)=\id_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\mathcal{P}(\psi)\circ\mathcal{P}(\xi)=\id_{\mathcal{Y}}
in pro-hCW
∗
.

From the lemma we conclude that
X
and
Y
are shape equivalent (resp. pointed shape equivalent). We write
X\simeq_{se}Y
(resp.
(X,x_{0})\simeq_{se}(Y,y_{0})
).

Proof.

\xi
induces a morphism in pro-hCW
\mathcal{P}(\xi):\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
in the same way as
\psi
. We show that both compositions are equivalent to the identity.

•

\mathcal{X}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\xi)\mathcal{P}(\psi)}\mathcal{X}
:

The composition
\mathcal{P}(\xi)\mathcal{P}(\psi)
is defined as follows. Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
be given. Since
\xi
is proper there exists a
j
such that
\xi(Q_{j}^{c})\subset L_{i}^{c}
. We can assume that
Q_{j}^{c}\subset Q^{c}
. Since
\psi
is proper there exists a
k\geq i
such that
\psi(L_{k}^{c})\subset Q_{j}^{c}
. So we have

\displaystyle\xi\psi(L_{k}^{c})\subset\psi(Q_{j}^{c})\subset L_{i}^{c}

Choose additionally
k
large enough so that
L^{c}_{k}\subset L^{c}
and the homotopy
H
from
\xi\psi_{|L_{k}^{c}}
to
\id_{|L_{k}^{c}}
has
H(L_{k}^{c}\times[0,1])\subset L_{i}^{c}
. We let
\rho(i)=k
and define
q_{i}
by taking the bottom slice of
L_{k}^{c}
, applying
\xi\psi
, then retracting to the bottom of
L_{i}^{c}
, ie.

\displaystyle q_{i}:X_{k}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{X}_{i}\quad q_{i}:=\mathfrak{q}^{X}_{i}\xi\psi_{|X_{k}\times\{k+1\}}

The composition
\mathcal{P}(\xi)\mathcal{P}(\psi):\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
is represented by the morphism
(\rho,\{q_{h}\})
.

Consider
\mathfrak{q}^{X}_{i}H
restricted to
{X}_{k}\times\{k+1\}
. We have that
\mathfrak{q}^{X}_{i}\xi\psi_{|{X}_{k}\times\{k+1\}}
is homotopic to
\mathfrak{q}^{X}_{i}
. Thus the diagram (under appropriate identifications)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{k,i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}\simeq\mathfrak{q}^{X}_{i}=\phi_{k,i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}

commutes. This shows that
\mathcal{P}(\xi)\mathcal{P}(\psi)
is equivalent to
\id_{\mathcal{X}}
in pro-hCW.

•

\mathcal{Y}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\psi)\mathcal{P}(\xi)}\mathcal{Y}
:

The argument for
\mathcal{P}(\psi)\mathcal{P}(\xi)=\id_{\mathcal{Y}}
follows analogously.

If
\psi,\xi
are maps of pairs, then
\mathcal{P}(\psi),\mathcal{P}(\xi)
are morphisms in pro-hCW
∗
by the same formulas. If the homotopies
H,G
realising
\xi\circ\psi\simeq\id_{|L^{c}}
and
\psi\circ\xi\simeq\id_{|Q^{c}}
satisfy
H(\omega\times[0,1])\subset\omega
and
G(\tau\times[0,1])\subset\tau
, then all morphisms and homotopies in the proof are pointed.
∎

Remark
.

Clearly if
\xi
is an actual proper homotopy inverse then Lemma
6.14
also holds. However, a partially-defined inverse is sufficient, since we have the freedom of going up the telescope in our choice of indices.

In summary, we have shown that a proper map
M_{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow M_{\mathcal{Y}}
induces a shape morphism
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
, with a proper homotopy equivalence inducing a shape equivalence. We now construct the correspondence in the other direction.

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{h},\phi_{h+1,h};\mathbb{N}\}
and
\mathcal{Y}=\{Y_{h},\varphi_{h+1,h};\mathbb{N}\}
be objects in pro-hCW. A morphism
(\rho,\{q_{h}\}):\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is equal in pro-hCW to a morphism
(\rho^{\prime},\{q^{\prime}_{h}\})
with the properties

•

\rho^{\prime}:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
is a strictly increasing function.

•

The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho^{\prime}(h)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho^{\prime}(h+1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{h+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{h+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\rho^{\prime}(h+1)\rho^{\prime}(h)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{h+1,h}}

commutes up to homotopy for all
h\in\mathbb{N}
.

The analogous statement is true for objects and morphisms in pro-hCW
∗
.

Proof.

By the definitions, there exists a
j\geq\rho(1),\rho(2)
such that

\displaystyle\varphi_{2,1}q_{2}\phi_{j,\rho(2)}\simeq q_{1}\phi_{j,\rho(1)}

Define
\rho^{\prime}(1)=\rho(1)
,
q^{\prime}_{1}=q_{1}
, and
\rho^{\prime}(2)=j
and
q^{\prime}_{2}:X_{\rho^{\prime}(2)}\rightarrow Y_{2}
as

\displaystyle q^{\prime}_{2}:=q_{2}\phi_{j,\rho(2)}.

Then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho(1)}=X_{\rho^{\prime}(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho^{\prime}(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{2}=q_{2}\phi_{\rho^{\prime}(2)\rho(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\rho^{\prime}(2)\rho^{\prime}(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2,1}}

commutes up to homotopy by construction. Suppose for the induction step that
\rho^{\prime}(h)
and
q^{\prime}_{h}:X_{\rho^{\prime}(h)}\rightarrow Y_{h}
has been defined and satisfes the properties in the lemma. Consider
\rho(h+1)
and
q_{h+1}:X_{\rho(h+1)}\rightarrow Y_{h+1}
. From the definitions there exists
j\geq\rho(h),\rho(h+1)
such that

\displaystyle\varphi_{h+1,h}q_{h+1}\phi_{j,\rho(h+1)}\simeq q_{h}\phi_{j,\rho(h)}

If we choose any
j^{\prime}\geq j
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{j^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho(h)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho(h+1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{h+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j\rho(h)}\phi_{j^{\prime}j}\simeq\phi_{j^{\prime}\rho(h)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j^{\prime}\rho(h+1)}\simeq\phi_{j\rho(h+1)}\phi_{j^{\prime}j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{h+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{h+1,h}}

also commutes up to homotopy since the bonding maps
\phi
are compatible. Therefore we can assume that
j^{\prime}>\rho^{\prime}(h)
and we let
\rho^{\prime}(h+1):=j^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime}_{h+1}=q_{h+1}\phi_{j^{\prime}\rho(h+1)}
. The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho^{\prime}(h)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho^{\prime}(h+1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{h+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{h+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\rho^{\prime}(h+1)\rho^{\prime}(h)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{h+1,h}}

commutes up to homotopy since

\displaystyle q^{\prime}_{h}\phi_{\rho^{\prime}(h+1)\rho^{\prime}(h)}\simeq q_{h}\phi_{\rho^{\prime}(h)\rho(h)}\phi_{\rho^{\prime}(h+1)\rho^{\prime}(h)}\simeq q_{h}\phi_{\rho^{\prime}(h+1)\rho(h)}\simeq\varphi_{h+1,h}q_{h+1}\phi_{j^{\prime}\rho(h+1)}

\displaystyle\simeq\varphi_{h+1,h}q^{\prime}_{h+1}

Therefore
(\rho^{\prime},\{q^{\prime}_{h}\})
is defined and satisfies the desired properties. To check that it is actually a morphism in pro-hCW requires that for all
i\leq j
there exists a
k\geq\rho^{\prime}(i),\rho^{\prime}(j)
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho^{\prime}(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho^{\prime}(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{k\rho^{\prime}(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{k\rho^{\prime}(j)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{ji}}

is homotopy commutative. To this end, choose
k=\rho^{\prime}(j)
. Since
\rho^{\prime}(j)\geq\rho^{\prime}(l),\rho(l)
for all
i\leq l\leq j
we have the following homotopy commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho^{\prime}(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho(j-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{j-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{j-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{j}}

where all the unlabelled arrows are bonding morphisms. This, combined with the definition of
q^{\prime}_{h}
as
q_{h}\phi_{\rho^{\prime}(h)\rho(h)}
for all
h\in\mathbb{N}
gives us the desired result.

It remains to show that
(\rho,\{q_{h}\})=(\rho^{\prime},\{q^{\prime}_{h}\})
in pro-hCW. To do this, we need that for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a
j\geq\rho(i),\rho^{\prime}(i)
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\rho^{\prime}(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j\rho(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j\rho^{\prime}(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{i}}

commutes up to homotopy. We simply pick
j=\rho^{\prime}(i)
and the diagram commutes by construction.

If
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\in
pro-hCW
∗
the same proof works by letting all maps and homotopies be pointed.
∎

We say that an ANR-system
\mathcal{X}=\{(X_{h},x_{0}),\phi_{h+1,h};\mathbb{N}\}
, where all
X_{h}
are simplicial complexes, has dimension
\leq n
if all
X_{h}
have dimension
\leq n
.

Theorem 6.16
.

Let
\mathcal{X}:=\{(X_{h},x_{0}),\phi_{h+1,h};\mathbb{N}\},\mathcal{Y}:=\{(Y_{h},y_{0}),\varphi_{h+1,h};\mathbb{N}\}
be inverse systems associated with the compact, based metric spaces
(X,x_{0})
and
(Y,y_{0})
. A pointed shape morphism
(\rho,\{q_{h}\}):\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
induces a continuous, proper, cellular map of pairs
\psi:(M_{\mathcal{X}},\omega)\rightarrow(M_{\mathcal{Y}},\tau)
such that
\mathcal{P}(\psi)=(\rho,\{q_{h}\})
.

Assume that
X,Y
are connected and that
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
are finite-dimensional expansions.
\psi
has a proper homotopy inverse of pairs
\xi
if and only if the induced map on end homotopy groups
\psi:\pi_{n}^{e}(M_{\mathcal{X}},\omega)\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{e}(M_{\mathcal{Y}},\psi\omega)
is an isomorphism for all
n\geq 0
. This occurs exactly when the induced map pro-
\pi_{n}(\rho,\{q_{h}\}):
pro-
\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})\rightarrow
pro-
\pi_{n}(Y,y_{0})
on pro-homotopy groups is an isomorphism for all
n\geq 0
.

Proof.

We can assume that
(\rho,\{q_{h}\})
has the form as in Lemma
6.15
. Since
\rho
is a strictly increasing function the object
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}:=\{X_{\rho(h)},\phi_{\rho(h+1)\rho(h)};\mathbb{N}\}
is pro-isomorphic to
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{h},\phi_{h+1,h};\mathbb{N}\}
and the morphism
(\rho,\{q_{h}\})
restricts to
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
. We define the inverse mapping telescopes
M_{\mathcal{X}},M_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}},M_{\mathcal{Y}}
as follows:

\displaystyle M_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle:=\{\ast\}\cup X_{1}\times[0,1]\cup_{\phi_{2,1}}X_{2}\times[0,1]\cup_{\phi_{3,2}}\dots

\displaystyle M_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:=\{\ast\}\cup X_{\rho(1)}\times[0,1]\cup_{\phi_{\rho(2)\rho(1)}}X_{\rho(2)}\times[0,1]\cup_{\phi_{\rho(3)\rho(2)}}\dots

\displaystyle M_{\mathcal{Y}}

\displaystyle:=\{\ast\}\cup Y_{1}\times[0,1]\cup_{\varphi_{2,1}}Y_{2}\times[0,1]\cup_{\varphi_{3,2}}\dots

There is a proper homotopy equivalence
r:M_{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow M_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
, obtained by collapsing all intermediate cylinders, with inverse
\iota:M_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\rightarrow M_{\mathcal{X}}
. Therefore it suffices to construct a map
\psi^{\prime}:M_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\rightarrow M_{\mathcal{Y}}
with the desired properties.
\psi^{\prime}
is defined piecewise for
h\in\mathbb{N}
by

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}_{h}:X_{\rho(h)}\times[0,1]\rightarrow Y_{h}\times[0,1]\cup_{\phi_{h,h-1}}Y_{h-1}\times\{1\}

\psi^{\prime}_{h}(x,t):=\begin{cases}[(q_{h}(x),t)]&t\in[\frac{1}{2},1]\\
[(q_{h}(x),2t-\frac{1}{2})]&t\in[\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}]\\
[(H^{h}(x,4t),1)]&t\in[0,\frac{1}{4}]\\
\end{cases}

where
H^{h}:X_{\rho(h)}\times[0,1]\rightarrow Y_{h-1}
is a homotopy between
H^{h}_{0}=q_{h-1}\phi_{\rho(h)\rho(h-1)}
and
H^{h}_{1}=\varphi_{h,h-1}q_{h}
. This glues to a continuous, proper map
\psi^{\prime}
. Let
\psi:=\psi^{\prime}\circ r
. Since all maps and homotopies are pointed,
\psi:(M_{\mathcal{X}},\omega)\rightarrow(M_{\mathcal{Y}},\tau)
is a map of pairs. By construction we have
\mathcal{P}(\psi)=(\rho,\{q_{h}\})
. Additionally, by the relative simplicial approximation theorem, we can assume that all
q_{h},H^{h}
are simplicial. Then
\psi^{\prime}
is cellular map.

For the second half of the theorem, we require the following claim:

Claim
.

Let
X
be a compact metric space.
X
is connected exactly when the inverse mapping telescope
M_{X}
has one end.

Suppose
X
is connected. Then every
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
is path-connected. Therefore the inverse mapping telescope
M_{X}
has the property that
M_{X}\mathrel{\mathop{-}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-1.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\vss}}}L_{i}
is path-connected for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, which implies that
M_{X}
has one end. Conversely, if
X
not connected there exist
U,V
open and disjoint such that
X=U\cup V
. Since the sequence of open covers
\mathcal{U}_{h}
is cofinal, there exists a
h^{\prime}
large enough such that
\mathcal{U}_{h}
is refinement of
U\cup V
for all
h\geq h^{\prime}
. This shows that
M_{X}
has at least
2
ends.

The number of ends of
M_{X}
is a shape invariant of
X
. Therefore for any shape expansion
\mathcal{X}
of
X
, the inverse mapping telescope
M_{\mathcal{X}}
has one end.

Let
\omega^{\prime}=r\omega
. Consider the mapping cylinder of
\psi^{\prime}
, which we denote by

\displaystyle M_{\psi^{\prime}}:=M_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\times[0,1]\cup_{\psi^{\prime}}M_{\mathcal{Y}}

This is a finite-dimensional, strongly locally finite (this uses the properness of
\psi^{\prime}
), path-connected CW complex, with
M_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\times\{0\}
as a subcomplex. The commutative diagrams in the proof of
5.15
with
Y=M_{\psi^{\prime}}
,
A=M_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\times\{0\}
and
\{L_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
a finite filtration of
M_{\psi^{\prime}}
, show that
\psi^{\prime}:(M_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}},\omega)\rightarrow(M_{\mathcal{Y}},\tau)
induces an isomorphism on end homotopy groups for all
n\geq 0
exactly when the induced morphisms

\displaystyle{\varprojlim}^{1}\pi_{n+1}((L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\omega^{\prime}(i))

\displaystyle\rightarrow{\varprojlim}^{1}\pi_{n+1}(L^{c}_{i},\omega^{\prime}(i))

\displaystyle\varprojlim\pi_{n}((L_{i}\cap A)^{c},\omega^{\prime}(i))

\displaystyle\rightarrow\varprojlim\pi_{n}(L^{c}_{i},\omega^{\prime}(i))

are isomorphisms for all
n\geq 0
.

By post-composing with the deformation retract
M_{\psi^{\prime}}\rightarrow M_{\mathcal{Y}}
, this is equivalent (after suitable identifications) to the condition that

\displaystyle{\varprojlim}^{1}\text{pro-}\pi_{n+1}(\rho,\{q_{h}\})

\displaystyle:{\varprojlim}^{1}\text{pro-}\pi_{n+1}(X,x_{0})\rightarrow{\varprojlim}^{1}\text{pro-}\pi_{n+1}(Y,y_{0})

\displaystyle\varprojlim\text{pro-}\pi_{n}(\rho,\{q_{h}\})

\displaystyle:\varprojlim\text{pro-}\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})\rightarrow\varprojlim\text{pro-}\pi_{n}(Y,y_{0})

are isomorphisms for all
n\geq 0
. This occurs exactly when pro-
\pi_{n}(\rho,\{q_{h}\}):
pro-
\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})\rightarrow
pro-
\pi_{n}(Y,y_{0})
is an isomorphism for all
n\geq 0
, by applying the following lemma:

Lemma 6.17
.

(Lemma 3.8 in
[
28
]
) A collection of homomorphisms
f_{i}:G_{i}\rightarrow H_{i}
between inverse sequences of countable groups commuting with the bonding maps is a pro-isomorphism if and only if
\varprojlim f_{i}:\varprojlim G_{i}\rightarrow\varprojlim H_{i}
and
{\varprojlim}^{1}f_{i}:{\varprojlim}^{1}G_{i}\rightarrow{\varprojlim}^{1}H_{i}
are bijections.

and using the fact that connectedness of
X,Y
implies pro-
\pi_{0}
is the trivial system.

By Corollary
5.18
and Corollary
5.19
, there is a proper homotopy
G:M_{\psi^{\prime}}\times[0,1]\rightarrow M_{\psi^{\prime}}
such that
G_{0}=\id
and
G_{1}(M_{\psi^{\prime}})\subset M_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\times\{0\}
. Let
K:=((\omega^{\prime}\times[0,1])\cup\tau)
. We can assume
G
satisfies
G(K\times[0,1])\subset K
by the following argument: since
\psi^{\prime}_{|\omega^{\prime}}:\omega^{\prime}\rightarrow\tau
is a proper homotopy equivalence, there exists a homotopy
G^{\omega^{\prime}}:K\times[0,1]\rightarrow K
such that
G^{\omega^{\prime}}_{0}=\id
and
G^{\omega^{\prime}}_{1}(K)\subset\omega^{\prime}\times\{0\}
. We can extend
G^{\omega^{\prime}}
to
G
now by inducting over cells not in
K\times[0,1]
in the usual fashion.

This gives us a continuous proper map of pairs
\xi^{\prime}:(M_{\mathcal{Y}},\tau)\rightarrow(M_{\mathcal{X^{\prime}}},\omega^{\prime})
which is a proper homotopy inverse of pairs to
\psi^{\prime}
. The composition
\xi:=\iota\circ\xi^{\prime}:(M_{\mathcal{Y}},\tau)\rightarrow(M_{\mathcal{X}},\omega)
is a proper homotopy inverse of pairs to
\psi
.
∎

Before we prove the shape Whitehead theorem, we need to discuss dimension.

Definition 6.18
.

(Lebesgue covering dimension) (Definition
2.1
in
[
14
]
) The
multiplicity
of an open cover
\mathcal{V}
is the maximal number of elements of
\mathcal{V}
having a common point. The
Lebesgue covering dimension
of a topological space
X
does not exceed
n
(
\dim X\leq n
) if for every open cover
\mathcal{U}
of
X
there is an open refinement
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{U}
with multiplicity
\leq n+1
. Define
\dim X
as the minimal number such that
\dim X\leq n
and
\infty
otherwise.

Definition 6.19
.

(Shape dimension) (Definition
11
in
[
23
]
) A topological space
X
has
shape dimension

sdX\leq n
if there exists an associated ANR-system
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha};\mathcal{A}\}
such that each
X_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
is
homotopy dominated
by an ANR of dimension
\leq n
, ie. there exists an ANR
Y_{\alpha}
of dimension
\dim Y_{\alpha}\leq n
and maps
f_{\alpha}:X_{\alpha}\rightarrow Y_{\alpha}
and
g_{\alpha}:Y_{\alpha}\rightarrow X_{\alpha}
such that

\displaystyle g_{\alpha}f_{\alpha}\simeq\id_{X_{\alpha}}

Define
sdX
as the minimal number such that
sdX\leq n
and
\infty
otherwise.

Theorem 6.20
.

(Theorem
2.2
of
[
14
]
) For a compact metric space
X
, its shape dimension and Lebesgue covering dimension coincide.

From this we obtain the following proposition:

Proposition 6.21
.

Let
(X,x_{0})
be a compact metric space with pointed shape expansion
\mathcal{X}:=\{(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,x_{0}),\phi_{h+1,h};\mathbb{N}\}
. Let
X
have finite shape dimension
sdX=n
. Then there exists a pointed shape expansion
\mathcal{X^{\prime}}:=\{(\mathcal{V}_{h},V_{0h}),\tilde{\phi}_{h+1,h};\mathbb{N}\}
of dimension
\leq n
obtained from a cofinal sequence of open covers with distinguished element containing
x_{0}
. There is a proper homotopy equivalence of pairs
\zeta_{\mathcal{X^{\prime}}\mathcal{X}}:(M_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}},\tau)\rightarrow(M_{X},\omega)
.

Proof.

Let
\{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}}
be our fixed sequence of (modified) open covers of
X
. For ease of notation in this proof, we drop the primes on
\mathcal{U}_{h}
entirely. It is understood that
\mathcal{U}_{h}
is the modified sequence of open covers (ie.
B(x_{0},\frac{1}{2^{h}})
is the only set in
\mathcal{U}_{h}
containing
x_{0}
). We also stop writing
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
and just use
\mathcal{U}_{h}
even when we mean the geometric realisation of the nerve.

Let
\V_{0}=\{\ast\}
. Let
\psi_{0}:\U_{h_{1}}:=\U_{1}\rightarrow\V_{0}
be the obvious map to a point. Choose
\V_{1}
to be a refinement of
\U_{1}
with multiplicity
\leq n+1
. By a further modification if necessary, we can assume that
\V_{1}
has a distinguished element
V_{01}
as the unique
V\in\V_{1}
containing
x_{0}
. There is a simplicial refinement map
\varphi_{1}:\V_{1}\rightarrow\U_{1}
which sends the vertex
V_{01}
to
x_{0}
. We define
\tilde{\phi}_{1,0}:\V_{1}\rightarrow\V_{0}
to be
\psi_{0}\circ\varphi_{1}
. Since
\{\U_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}}
is cofinal, there exists
h_{2}>1
such that
\U_{h_{2}}
refines
\V_{1}
with refinement map
\psi_{1}:\U_{h_{2}}\rightarrow\V_{1}
. We have that
\psi_{1}(x_{0})=V_{01}
. We can choose
\V_{2}
with multiplicity
\leq n+1
and distinguished element
V_{02}
so that it refines
\U_{h_{2}}
with pointed refinement map
\varphi_{2}:\V_{2}\rightarrow\U_{h_{2}}
. We define
\tilde{\phi}_{2,1}:\V_{2}\rightarrow\V_{1}
as
\psi_{1}\circ\varphi_{2}
. Continuing iteratively we have

\displaystyle\psi_{j-1}\circ\varphi_{j}=:\tilde{\phi}_{j,j-1}:\V_{j}\rightarrow\V_{j-1}

for all
j\geq 1
where

\displaystyle\varphi_{j}

\displaystyle:\V_{j}\rightarrow\U_{h_{j}}

\displaystyle\psi_{j-1}

\displaystyle:\U_{h_{j}}\rightarrow\V_{j-1}

are pointed, simplicial refinement maps.

Since
\{(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,x_{0}),\phi_{h+1,h};\mathbb{N}\}
is cofinal in the system of locally finite, normal covers of
X
with distinguished element, so is
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\{(\mathcal{V}_{h},V_{0h}),\tilde{\phi}_{h+1,h};\mathbb{N}\}
. Therefore, by Lemma
6.6
,
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
is a pointed shape expansion of
(X,x_{0})
.

We construct the inverse mapping telescope
M_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
as

\displaystyle M_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}:=\V_{0}\cup_{\tilde{\phi}_{1,0}}\V_{1}\times[0,1]\cup_{\tilde{\phi}_{2,1}}\V_{2}\times[0,1]\cup_{\tilde{\phi}_{3,2}}\dots

Let
\tau
be the base ray of
M_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
corresponding to the distinguished points
V_{0h}
in the simplicial complex
\mathcal{V}_{h}
.

Likewise, we can identify
M_{\mathcal{X}}=M_{X}
topologically with

\displaystyle M_{X}:=\U_{0}\cup_{{\phi}_{1,0}}\U_{1}\times[0,1]\cup_{{\phi}_{2,1}}\U_{2}\times[0,1]\cup_{{\phi}_{3,2}}\dots

with base ray
\omega
corresponding to
x_{0}
. Observe that
\varphi_{j}\circ\psi_{j}:\U_{h_{j+1}}\rightarrow\U_{h_{j}}
is pointed homotopic to
\phi_{h_{j+1},h_{j}}
for all
j\geq 1
.

Our goal now is to extend the algebraic data
\varphi_{j}
and
\psi_{j}
to a proper homotopy equivalence
\zeta_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\mathcal{X}}:(M_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}},\tau)\rightarrow(M_{X},\omega)
of pairs, defined on the whole telescope by "filling in" the gaps. To do this, we define
\zeta_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\mathcal{X}}
piecewise:

\displaystyle\zeta^{j}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\mathcal{X}}:\V_{j+1}\times[0,\frac{1}{2}]\cup_{\tilde{\phi}}\V_{j}\times[\frac{1}{2},1]\longrightarrow\U_{h_{j+1}}\times[0,\frac{1}{2}]\cup_{\phi}(\bigcup_{h_{j}<h<h_{j+1}}\U_{h}\times[0,1])\cup_{\phi}\U_{h_{j}}\times[\frac{1}{2},1]

The definition is best explained with a picture (Figure
37
), but it does the following on specific slices:

\displaystyle\zeta^{j}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\mathcal{X}}:

\displaystyle\V_{j+1}\times\{\frac{1}{2}\}\rightarrow\U_{h_{j+1}}\times\{\frac{1}{2}\}\quad

\displaystyle:=\varphi_{j+1}

\displaystyle\V_{j+1}\times\{\frac{3}{8}\}\rightarrow\U_{h_{j+1}-1}\times\{1\}

\displaystyle:=\phi_{h_{j+1},h_{j+1}-1}\varphi_{j+1}

\displaystyle\V_{j+1}\times\{\frac{1}{4}\}\rightarrow\U_{h_{j}}\times\{1\}

\displaystyle:=\phi_{h_{j+1},h_{j}}\varphi_{j+1}

\displaystyle\V_{j+1}\times\{0\}\rightarrow\U_{h_{j}}\times\{\frac{1}{2}\}

\displaystyle:=\varphi_{j}\psi_{j}\varphi_{j+1}

\displaystyle\V_{j}\times[\frac{1}{2},1]\rightarrow\U_{h_{j}}\times\{\frac{1}{2}\}

\displaystyle:=\varphi_{j}

On the segments
\V_{j+1}\times[\frac{3}{8},\frac{1}{2}]
and
\V_{j+1}\times[\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{8}]
we fill in with the obvious deformation retract on
M_{X}
. On
\V_{j+1}\times[0,\frac{1}{4}]
we fill in with a fixed choice of linear homotopy
\phi_{h_{j+1},h_{j}}\simeq\varphi_{j}\psi_{j}
. One can check that all this together is a well-defined map compatible with gluing. The
\zeta^{j}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\mathcal{X}}
are also compatible with each other on boundary components. On the segment
\V_{1}\times[0,\frac{1}{2}]
we take the cone of the map
\varphi_{1}
. We therefore obtain a globally defined map
\zeta_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\mathcal{X}}
, which is continuous and proper.
