be a proper, cellular map which induces an isomorphism on end homotopy groups. Then there exists a proper homotopy inverse
\xi:M_{Y}\rightarrow M_{X}
.

Proof.

The composition

\displaystyle M_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\zeta_{\mathcal{X^{\prime}}\mathcal{X}}}M_{X}\xrightarrow{\psi}M_{Y}\xrightarrow{\zeta_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y^{\prime}}}}M_{\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}}

is a proper, cellular map between contractible, finite-dimensional, strongly locally finite, path-connected CW complexes, which induces an isomorphism on all end homotopy groups and the set of ends. Therefore Corollary
5.19
applies, and there exists a proper homotopy inverse
\overline{\xi}:M_{\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}}\rightarrow M_{\mathcal{X^{\prime}}}
. We define
\xi=\zeta_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\mathcal{X}}\circ\overline{\xi}\circ\zeta_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y^{\prime}}}
.

\displaystyle\xi\psi=\zeta_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\mathcal{X}}\overline{\xi}\zeta_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y^{\prime}}}\psi\simeq\zeta_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\mathcal{X}}(\overline{\xi}\zeta_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y^{\prime}}}\psi\zeta_{\mathcal{X^{\prime}}\mathcal{X}})\zeta_{\mathcal{X}\mathcal{X^{\prime}}}\simeq\zeta_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\mathcal{X}}\id_{M_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}}\zeta_{\mathcal{X}\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\simeq\id_{M_{X}}

\displaystyle\psi\xi=\psi\zeta_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\mathcal{X}}\overline{\xi}\zeta_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y^{\prime}}}\simeq\zeta_{\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\mathcal{Y}}(\zeta_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}}\psi\zeta_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\mathcal{X}}\overline{\xi})\zeta_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}}\simeq\zeta_{\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\mathcal{Y}}\id_{M_{\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}}}\zeta_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}}\simeq\id_{M_{Y}}

where the
\simeq
denote proper homotopy. Hence,
\xi
is a proper homotopy inverse to
\psi
.
∎

7.2
Constructing a coarse inverse

The goal of this subsection is to prove:

Theorem 7.9
.

Let
(X,x_{0})
and
(Y,y_{0})
be connected, compact metric spaces with finite shape dimension. Let
\omega
be the standard parametrisation of the base ray
c\{x_{0}\}
. Let
\mathfrak{a}:(cX,\omega)\rightarrow(cY,\mathfrak{a}\omega)
be a coarse map, which induces an isomorphism on coarse homotopy groups
\mathfrak{a}:\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{c}(cY,\mathfrak{a}\omega)
for all
n\geq 0
. Then
\mathfrak{a}
is a coarse homotopy equivalence, ie. there exists
\mathfrak{b}:cY\rightarrow cX
such that
\mathfrak{b}\circ\mathfrak{a}\simeq id_{cX}
and
\mathfrak{a}\circ\mathfrak{b}\simeq id_{cY}
.

We now take the proper homotopy inverse
\xi:M_{Y}\rightarrow M_{X}
and use it to construct a coarse homotopy inverse to
\mathfrak{a}:cX\rightarrow cY
. The technical issue to overcome is that
i:cY\rightarrow M_{Y}
is not coarse, so we cannot define the coarse inverse to
\mathfrak{a}
as simply
R\circ\xi\circ i
. The trick is to precompose with a shrinking map
s:cY\rightarrow cY
.

Let
X
be a metric space and
\mathcal{U}
a locally finite cover of
X
. We choose a specific partition of unity subordinate to
\mathcal{U}
as follows. Let
U\in\mathcal{U}
. We define
\varphi_{U}:U\rightarrow[0,1]
by the formula

\displaystyle\varphi_{U}(x)=\frac{d(x,X\setminus U)}{\sum_{V\in\mathcal{U}}d(x,X\setminus V)}

Recall that the map
\varphi:X\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}|
induced by the partition of unity
\{\varphi_{U}\}_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
is defined as
\varphi(x)=\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\varphi_{U}(x)[U]
.

Lemma 7.10
.

(Lemma
4.3.5
in
[
36
]
) Let
\mathcal{U}
be a uniformly bounded cover of
X
with multiplicity
\kappa
and Lebesgue number
2r
. Then for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
the function
\varphi_{U}
is
(\frac{1}{r}+\frac{2\kappa}{r^{2}})
-Lipschitz.

For
x,y\in X
we have that
d_{std}(\varphi(x),\varphi(y))=\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}|\varphi_{U}(x)-\varphi_{U}(y)|\leq 2\kappa(\frac{1}{r}+\frac{2\kappa}{r^{2}})d(x,y)
. Therfore,
\varphi
is
2\kappa(\frac{1}{r}+\frac{2\kappa}{r^{2}})
-Lipschitz with respect to the standard metric on
|\mathcal{U}|
. Changing from the standard metric to the spherical metric
d^{s}
has a Lipschitz constant dependent on
\kappa=\dim|\mathcal{U}|+1
.

Let
X
be a compact metric space. The open covers
\mathcal{U}_{h}
are finite, since they are constructed from a
\frac{1}{2^{h+1}}
-separated set. Therefore each
i_{h}:X\rightarrow(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,d^{s})
has a Lipschitz constant, say
\alpha_{h}
, which only depends on the multiplicity of the cover and Lebesgue number
\frac{1}{2^{h}}
of
\mathcal{U}_{h}
.

Lemma 7.11
.

The map
i
can be made coarse, by precomposing with a shrinking map
s
on
cX
.

Proof.

We know that the map

\displaystyle\varphi_{h}:X\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h}|

is
\alpha_{h}
-Lipschitz. Choose constants
m_{h}
such that
2^{m_{h}}>\alpha_{h}
and
m_{h}\leq m_{h+1}
for all
h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. We can assume that
m_{0}\geq 1
for later purposes. Consider now the shrinking map
s
, which is defined piecewise as follows:

\displaystyle s^{h}:X\times 2^{m_{h}}(2^{h},2^{h+1}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow X\times(2^{h},2^{h+1}]

\displaystyle(tx,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\frac{t}{2^{m_{h}}}x,\frac{t}{2^{m_{h}}})

and we send points with heights in the interval
(2^{h+m_{h-1}},2^{h+m_{h}}]
to height
2^{h}
and all points below height
2^{m_{0}}
to height
1
. This is a coarse shrinking map, and it is coarsely homotopic to the identity by a linear homotopy.

We now check that the composition
i\circ s
is coarse. Let us first restrict to
X\times 2^{m_{h}}(2^{h},2^{h+1}]
. For two points
(tx,t),(ly,l)
in
X\times 2^{m_{h}}(2^{h},2^{h+1}]
with distance
\leq Q
we have that
\|x-y\|\leq\frac{Q}{2^{h+m_{h}}}
and
|t-l|\leq Q
.

\displaystyle d_{M}(is^{h}(tx,t),is^{h}(ly,l))

\displaystyle=d_{M}((\varphi_{h}(x),\frac{t}{2^{m_{h}}}),(\varphi_{h}(y),\frac{l}{2^{m_{h}}}))

\displaystyle\leq d_{|\mathcal{U}_{h}|}(\varphi_{h}(x),\varphi_{h}(y))+\frac{1}{2^{m_{h}}}|t-l|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{m_{h}}\|x-y\|+\frac{1}{2^{m_{h}}}|t-l|<\frac{Q}{2^{h}}+\frac{Q}{2}<2Q

Therefore
i\circ s
is
2
-Lipschitz when restricted to any
X\times 2^{m_{h}}(2^{h},2^{h+1}]
for any
h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
(actually
1
-Lipschitz for
h\geq 1
). For two points with heights in the interval
(2^{h+m_{h-1}},2^{h+m_{h}}]
with distance
\leq Q
we just get

\displaystyle d_{M}(is(tx,t),is(ly,l))

\displaystyle=d_{M}((\varphi_{h-1}(x),2^{h}),(\varphi_{h-1}(y),2^{h}))

\displaystyle\leq 2^{m_{h-1}}\|x-y\|<\frac{Q}{2^{h}}<Q

The map
i\circ s
is not continuous at heights
2^{h+m_{h}}
but since
\phi_{h}\varphi_{h}(x)
and
\varphi_{h-1}(x)
lie in a common simplex for any
x
, the discontinuity is uniformly bounded by
1
, the diameter of a simplex in
|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|
.

To see that
i\circ s
is globally coarse, let
Q>0
. Choose
h\geq 1
large enough such that
Q<2^{h-1}
, ie. so that a set of diameter
Q
above height
2^{h-1}
intersects at most one gluing component. Consider two points
(tx,t),(ly,l)
with
t\geq l
and
\|tx-ly\|+|t-l|<Q
. Observe that for any
l^{\prime}\in[l,t]
we have

\displaystyle\|tx-l^{\prime}y\|+|t-l^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq\|tx-ly\|+|l^{\prime}-l|+|t-l^{\prime}|=\|tx-ly\|+|t-l|<Q

\displaystyle\|l^{\prime}y-ly\|+|l^{\prime}-l|

\displaystyle<2|l^{\prime}-l|<2Q

If both
t,l>2^{h}
then by possibly choosing
l^{\prime}
at a height where
i\circ s
is discontinuous, we get

\displaystyle d_{M}(is(tx,t),is(ly,l))

\displaystyle\leq d_{M}(is(tx,t),is(l^{\prime}y,l^{\prime}))+d_{M}(is(l^{\prime}y,l^{\prime}),is(ly,l))+1

\displaystyle<Q+2Q+1=3Q+1

where the two images of
is(l^{\prime}y,l^{\prime})
are different, to reflect the discontinuity. If only
l\leq 2^{h}
then
l>2^{h-1}
and we can choose
l^{\prime}=2^{h}
.
i\circ s
sends
X\times[1,2^{h}]
to a bounded set in
M
, say of diameter
<T_{Q}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle d_{M}(is(tx,t),is(ly,l))

\displaystyle\leq d_{M}(is(tx,t),is(l^{\prime}y,l^{\prime}))+d_{M}(is(l^{\prime}y,l^{\prime}),is(ly,l))+1

\displaystyle<Q+T_{Q}+1

Obviously if both
t,l\leq 2^{h}
then
d_{M}(is(tx,t),is(ly,l))<T_{Q}
. Therefore

\displaystyle d_{cX}((tx,t),(ly,l))<Q\implies d_{M}(is(tx,t),is(ly,l))<\max\{3Q+1,Q+T_{Q}+1\}

This shows that the map
i\circ s
is controlled. It is obviously proper.
∎

Lemma 7.12
.

i\circ s
is a coarse homotopy inverse to
R
.

Proof.

We simply compute both compositions.

R\circ i\circ s
: Let
G:c(X\times[0,1])\rightarrow cX
be the coarse homotopy with
G_{c(X\times\{0\})}=\id_{cX}
and
G_{c(X\times\{1\})}=s
. We let
H:c(X\times[0,1])\rightarrow cX
be defined as
R\circ i\circ G
. Since
R\circ i
is coarse,
H
is a coarse homotopy between
R\circ i\circ s
and
R\circ i
.
R\circ i
is close and therefore coarsely homotopic to
\id_{cX}
.

i\circ s\circ R
: Let
\omega^{\prime}
be the linear extension of the function
2^{h+m_{h}}\mapsto 2^{h},2^{h+1+m_{h}}\mapsto 2^{h+1}
to
[1,\infty)\rightarrow[1,\infty)
, so that
s
is the shrinking map associated to
\omega^{\prime}
. We show that
i\circ s\circ R
is close to
\mathfrak{q}\circ i\circ R
where
\mathfrak{q}
is a deformation retract on the mapping telescope that takes the height slice
t
to
\omega^{\prime}(t)
, for example:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}:|\mathcal{U}_{h+m_{h}}|\times 2^{m_{h}}(2^{h},2^{h+1}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times(2^{h},2^{h+1}]

\displaystyle(x,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\phi_{h+m_{h},h}(x),\omega^{\prime}(t))

Let
t\in 2^{m_{h}}(2^{h},2^{h+1}]
.

\displaystyle isR(x,t)

\displaystyle=is(tR_{h+m_{h}}(x),t)=i(\omega^{\prime}(t)R_{h+m_{h}}(x),\omega^{\prime}(t))=(i_{h}R_{h+m_{h}}(x),\omega^{\prime}(t))

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}iR(x,t)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}i(tR_{h+m_{h}}(x),t)=\mathfrak{q}(i_{h+m_{h}}R_{h+m_{h}}(x),t)=(\phi_{h+m_{h},h}i_{h+m_{h}}R_{h+m_{h}}(x),\omega^{\prime}(t))

The points
i_{h}R_{h+m_{h}}(x)
and
\phi_{h+m_{h},h}i_{h+m_{h}}R_{h+m_{h}}(x)
lie in a common simplex, so differ by a distance
\leq 1
. For a height
t\in(2^{h+m_{h-1}},2^{h+m_{h}}]
we have

\displaystyle isR(x,t)

\displaystyle=is(tR_{c(t)}(x),t)=i(2^{h}R_{c(t)}(x),2^{h})=(i_{h-1}R_{c(t)}(x),2^{h})

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}iR(x,t)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}i(tR_{c(t)}(x),t)=\mathfrak{q}(i_{c(t)}R_{c(t)}(x),t)=(\phi_{c(t),h-1}i_{c(t)}R_{c(t)}(x),2^{h})

where
c(t)
is the largest number such that
2^{c(t)}<t
. As before, the images differ by at most the diameter of a simplex. Therefore
i\circ s\circ R
is close to
\mathfrak{q}\circ i\circ R
. Since
i\circ R
is close to the identity and
\mathfrak{q}
is Lipschitz,
\mathfrak{q}\circ i\circ R
is close to
\mathfrak{q}
.
\mathfrak{q}
is coarse-Lipschitz homotopic to the identity, so we are done.

∎

Before we proceed with the technical details, we outline the rest of the proof in steps.

1.

Find a locally Lipschitz representative
\xi^{\prime}
of
\xi
in its proper homotopy class, such that
\psi\xi^{\prime}
and
\xi^{\prime}\psi
are locally Lipschitz homotopic to
\id_{M_{Y}}
and
\id_{M_{X}}
respectively via homotopies
F
and
G
. (Lemmas
7.13
and
7.15
)

2.

Define coarse shrinking maps
S_{\xi^{\prime}},S_{\psi\xi^{\prime}},S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
on
M_{Y},I_{p}(M_{Y})
and
I_{p}(M_{X})
respectively such that
\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}},FS_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}
and
GS_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
are coarse. (Lemma
7.16
)

3.

Prove that
\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}
is a coarse homotopy inverse to
is_{l}\mathfrak{a}R
. (Lemma
7.17
)

4.

Show that
\mathfrak{b}:=R\circ\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}\circ is
is a coarse homotopy inverse to
\mathfrak{a}
. (Lemma
7.18
)

Lemma 7.13
.

\xi
has a locally Lipschitz representative
\xi^{\prime}
in its proper homotopy class.

Proof.

This proof combines the ideas of Proposition
3.25
and Proposition
7.6
. Recall that
M_{Y}=\pi(\coprod_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times[2^{h},2^{h+1}])=\pi(\coprod_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\tilde{M}^{h})
. Fix the CW structure on
M_{Y}
as defined in Proposition
7.6
. We inductively define a simplicial map on
\coprod_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\tilde{M}^{h}
with respect to a progressively subdivided simplicial structure, which is locally Lipschitz with respect to
\{\tilde{M}^{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
and decends to the quotient.

Since
\tilde{M}^{0}
is a finite simplicial complex, there exists a barycentric subdivision of
\tilde{M}^{0}
such that
\xi_{0}:=\xi\circ\pi_{|\tilde{M}^{0}}
satisfies the geometric star condition. Therefore there is a simplicial map
\overline{\xi}^{\prime}_{0}
, and a homotopy
\overline{H}_{0}:\tilde{M}^{0}\times[0,1]\rightarrow M_{X}
between
\xi_{0}
and
\overline{\xi}^{\prime}_{0}
. Extend this homotopy to a homotopy
H_{0}:\tilde{M}_{Y}\times[0,1]\rightarrow M_{X}
from
\xi\circ\pi
to a map
\xi^{\prime}_{0}
such that
H_{0}
descends to
M_{Y}\times[0,1]
and is constant above height
3
.

Assume for induction that
H_{0}\ast H_{1}\ast\dots\ast H_{k}
has already been defined, descends to the quotient, and the endpoint of the homotopy
\xi^{\prime}_{k}:\tilde{M}_{Y}\rightarrow M_{X}
is simplicial when restricted to
\coprod_{h\leq k}\tilde{M}^{h}
. Consider now
\tilde{M}^{k+1}=|\mathcal{U}_{k+1}|\times[2^{k+1},2^{k+2}]
. We define
\xi^{\prime}_{k+1}
to be
\xi^{\prime}_{k}\circ\phi_{k+1}
when restricted to the subcomplex
|\mathcal{U}_{k+1}|\times\{2^{k+1}\}
.
\phi_{k+1}
is simplicial with respect to iterated barycentric subdivision, so
\xi^{\prime}_{k}\circ\phi_{k+1}
is simplicial with respect to the subdivided structure on
\tilde{M}^{k+1}
. By geometric simplicial approximation relative to the subcomplex
|\mathcal{U}_{k+1}|\times\{2^{k+1}\}
(Theorem
3.30
), we have a homotopy
\overline{H}_{k+1}:\tilde{M}^{k+1}\times[0,1]\rightarrow M_{X}
and a simplicial
\overline{\xi}_{k+1}^{\prime}
. As before, we extend
\overline{H}_{k+1}
to a homotopy
H_{k+1}:\tilde{M}_{Y}\rightarrow M_{X}
which descends to the quotient and is constant for heights
\geq 2^{k+2}+1
and heights
\leq 2^{k+1}
. Call the endpoint of this homotopy
{\xi}_{k+1}^{\prime}
.

The infinite concatenation of homotopies
H:=H_{0}\ast\dots\ast H_{k}\ast H_{k+1}\ast\dots
is well-defined since any point in
\tilde{M}_{Y}
is affected by at most
2
of the
H_{k}
. Call
\xi^{\prime}
the endpoint of the homotopy
H
. By construction, it is locally Lipschitz with respect to the filtration
\{\tilde{M}^{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
of
\tilde{M}_{Y}
. Since the metric on
M_{Y}
is induced from paths in
\tilde{M}_{Y}
, we obtain a locally Lipschitz map
\xi^{\prime}:M_{Y}\rightarrow M_{X}
with respect to the filtration
\{M_{[1,2^{h}]}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
after descending to the quotient.
\xi^{\prime}
is homotopic to
\xi
. (Note that we use the same symbols
\xi^{\prime},H
to denote the maps and homotopies on the quotient.)

The homotopy
H
is proper: by the properness of
\xi
for every
h\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a
l_{h}
such that
\xi(M_{[l_{h},\infty)})\subset M_{[h,\infty)}
. We have that
H(M_{[l_{h},\infty)}\times[0,1])\subset M_{[h,\infty)}
.
∎

By carefully following the proof above we have:

Lemma 7.14
.

Assume that
f:M_{Y}\rightarrow M_{X}
is already locally Lipschitz with respect to
\{M_{[1,2^{h}]}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. Then the simplicial approximation
f^{\prime}:M_{Y}\rightarrow M_{X}
is locally Lipschitz homotopic to
f
with respect to the filtration
\{M_{[1,2^{h}]}\times[0,1]\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
.

Proof.

It is clear that
f\circ\pi:\tilde{M}_{Y}\rightarrow M_{X}
is locally Lipschitz with respect to
\{\tilde{M}^{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. We first show that the extension
H_{k-1}
of
\overline{H}_{k-1}
is Lipschitz when restricted to
\tilde{M}^{k}=|\mathcal{U}_{k}|\times[2^{k},2^{k}+1]
. Consider the subcomplex

\displaystyle L_{1}\cup L_{1}:=(|\mathcal{U}_{k}|\times\{2^{k}\})\cup(|\mathcal{U}_{k}|\times\{2^{k}+1\})

The general procedure which extends homotopies from subcomplexes of CW complexes actually gives us a Lipschitz homotopy
|\mathcal{U}_{k}|\times[2^{k},2^{k}+1]\times[0,1]\rightarrow M_{X}
. This is because there is a retract

\displaystyle D:|\mathcal{U}_{k}|\times[2^{k},2^{k}+1]\times[0,1]\rightarrow(|\mathcal{U}_{k}|\times[2^{k},2^{k}+1]\times\{0\})\cup(L_{1}\times[0,1])\cup(L_{2}\times[0,1])

The homotopy extension we want is then the composition
(f\pi\cup\overline{H}_{k-1}\phi_{k}\cup\id_{f\pi})\circ D
. It is clear that this homotopy will be Lipschitz as long as
D
and
\overline{H}_{k-1}
are Lipschitz.
\overline{H}_{k-1}
is a homotopy between a Lipschitz map
f\pi_{||\mathcal{U}_{k-1}|\times\{2^{k}\}}
from a finite simplicial complex and its geometric simplicial approximation. This is Lipschitz.

Consider the unit square
[0,1]^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the point
(\frac{1}{2},2)
. For a point
x\in[0,1]^{2}
,
r(x)
is defined as the unique point of intersection between the ray from
(\frac{1}{2},2)
passing through
x
, and the set
([0,1]\times\{0\})\cup(\partial[0,1]\times[0,1])
.
r:[0,1]^{2}\rightarrow([0,1]\times\{0\})\cup(\partial[0,1]\times[0,1])
is Lipschitz, say with constant
A
. We can let
D
be defined as
\id_{|\mathcal{U}_{h}|}\times r
after identifying
[2^{k},2^{k+1}]\cong[0,1]
. Its Lipschitz constant is
A+1
.

The homotopy
\overline{H}_{k}:|\mathcal{U}_{k}|\times[2^{k},2^{k+1}]
is Lipschitz by Theorem
3.30
. Therefore, the homotopy
H:\tilde{M}_{Y}\times[0,1]\rightarrow M_{X}
, obtained as the concatenation of
2
Lipschitz homotopies when restricted to any
\tilde{M}^{h}\times[0,1]
, is locally Lipschitz with respect to this filtration.

Now consider
M_{Y}\times[0,1]
, equipped with the product metric. This is a path metric space, where a length-realising geodesic between
(x,t),(y,s)
is the concatenation
(\gamma_{xy},t)\ast(y,\eta_{ts})
, where
\gamma_{xy}
is a geodesic in
M_{Y}
and
\eta_{ts}
is the straight line segment in
[0,1]
between
t
and
s
. This can be broken up into geodesic segments each completely contained in one
M_{[2^{h},2^{h+1}]}\times[0,1]
for some
h
, whose lifts realise the product metric on
\tilde{M}^{h}\times[0,1]
, for which we know that
H
is Lipschitz. Therefore
H
is locally Lipschitz with respect to
\{M_{[1,2^{h}]}\times[0,1]\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
.
∎

Lemma 7.15
.

\psi\xi^{\prime}
is locally Lipschitz homotopic to
\id_{M_{Y}}
and
\xi^{\prime}\psi
is locally Lipschitz homotopic to
\id_{M_{X}}
.

Proof.

We know that
\psi\xi^{\prime}
is properly homotopic to
\id_{M_{Y}}
. Give
M_{Y}\times[0,1]
the CW structure as defined in Proposition
7.6
. By the same construction as Lemma
7.13
there exists a proper, locally Lipschitz homotopy
\overline{F}:M_{Y}\times[0,1]\rightarrow M_{Y}
from
\widetilde{\psi\xi^{\prime}}:=\overline{F}_{|M_{Y}\times\{0\}}
to
\widetilde{\id_{M_{X}}}:=\overline{F}_{|M_{Y}\times\{1\}}
. By Lemma
7.14
,
\widetilde{\psi\xi^{\prime}}
and
\widetilde{\id_{M_{X}}}
are proper, locally Lipschitz homotopic to
\psi\xi^{\prime}
and
\id_{M_{Y}}
respectively. This gives us a proper, locally Lipschitz homotopy
F:M_{Y}\times[0,1]\rightarrow M_{Y}
from
\psi\xi^{\prime}
to
\id_{M_{Y}}
. Similarly, there exists a proper, locally Lipschitz homotopy
G:M_{X}\times[0,1]\rightarrow M_{X}
from
\xi^{\prime}\psi
to
\id_{M_{X}}
.
∎

By stretching out the interval we obtain proper, locally Lipschitz homotopies

\displaystyle F:I_{p}(M_{Y})\rightarrow M_{Y}

\displaystyle G:I_{p}(M_{X})\rightarrow M_{X}

from
\psi\xi^{\prime}
to
\id_{M_{Y}}
and from
\xi^{\prime}\psi
to
\id_{M_{X}}
respectively.

Lemma 7.16
.

1.

There exists a coarse shrinking map
S_{\xi^{\prime}}
on
M_{Y}
, defined as the composition
S_{\xi^{\prime}}:=i^{\prime}\circ s_{\xi^{\prime}}\circ R
, such that
\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}
is coarse.

2.

There exists a coarse shrinking map
S_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}
on
I_{p}(M_{Y})
, defined as the composition
S_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}:=(i^{\prime}\times\id)\circ s_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}\circ(R\times\id)
, where
s_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}:I_{p}(cY)\rightarrow I_{p}(cY)
is a shrinking map on
I_{p}(cY)
, such that
FS_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}
is coarse.

3.

There exists a coarse shrinking map
S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
on
I_{p}(M_{X})
, defined as the composition
S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}:=(i^{\prime}\times\id)\circ s_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}\circ(R\times\id)
, where
s_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}:I_{p}(cX)\rightarrow I_{p}(cX)
is a shrinking map on
I_{p}(cY)
, such that
GS_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
is coarse.

4.

S_{\xi^{\prime}},S_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}
and
S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
are coarsely homotopic to the identity.

Proof.

1.

We can modify the map
i
to a close map
i^{\prime}
which is continuous. To do this, observe that
\phi_{h}\circ\varphi_{h}
is homotopic to
\varphi_{h-1}
by the linear (in particular, Lipschitz) homotopy
\Psi_{h}:Y\times[2^{h}-1,2^{h}]\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|\times[2^{h}-1,2^{h}]
within a simplex. We have that
\Psi_{h}(Y\times\{2^{h}\})=\phi_{h}\circ\varphi_{h}
and
\Psi_{h}(Y\times\{2^{h}-1\})=\varphi_{h-1}
. We simply replace
i
when restricted to height intervals
[2^{h}-1,2^{h}]
by
\Psi_{h}
and observe that this glues to a continuous map
i^{\prime}:cY\rightarrow M_{Y}
which is locally Lipschitz and distance at most the diameter of a simplex away from
i
. Let
a_{h}
be an increasing sequence of Lipschitz constants of
i^{\prime}
restricted to heights
(2^{h},2^{h+1}]
, ie

\displaystyle i^{\prime}_{|c_{(2^{h},2^{h+1}]}Y}:c_{(2^{h},2^{h+1}]}Y\rightarrow\tilde{M}^{h}

After descending to the quotient, the same constants
a_{h}
are appropriate as Lipschitz constants for

\displaystyle i^{\prime}_{|c_{[2^{h},2^{h+1}]}Y}:c_{[2^{h},2^{h+1}]}Y\rightarrow\pi(\tilde{M}^{h})

where
\pi(\tilde{M}^{h})
is equipped with the path metric induced by the product metric on
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times[2^{h},2^{h+1}]\cup|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|\times\{2^{h}\}
. Since the inclusion
\pi(\tilde{M}^{h})\rightarrow M_{Y}
is
1
-Lipschitz,
i^{\prime}:cY\rightarrow M_{Y}
is also locally Lipschitz with respect to
\{c_{[2^{h},2^{h+1}]}Y\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
with the same sequence of constants.

Let
b_{h}
be the Lipschitz constant of
\xi^{\prime}
(thought of as a map
\tilde{M}_{Y}\rightarrow M_{X}
) restricted to the set
\coprod_{k\leq h}\tilde{M}^{h}
. By previous arguments, after descending to the quotient,
b_{h}
is an approxiate Lipschitz constant for
M_{[1,2^{h+1}]}\subset M_{Y}
with the subspace metric as well as for
\pi(\tilde{M}^{h})
equipped with the path metric. Since
i^{\prime}
is height-preserving, the composition
\xi^{\prime}i^{\prime}
has Lipschitz constant
b_{h}a_{h}
. Choose constants
m_{h}
such that
2^{m_{h}}>b_{h}a_{h}
and
m_{h}\leq m_{h+1}
for all
h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. By the same construction as in Lemma
7.11
we can define a shrinking map
s_{\xi^{\prime}}
such that the composition
\xi^{\prime}i^{\prime}s_{\xi^{\prime}}
is proper and uniformly locally
1
-Lipschitz with respect to
\{c_{[2^{h},2^{h+1}]}Y\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. Note that we had to make the map
i^{\prime}
continuous rather than just coarse, because even a uniformly bounded discontinuity can become arbitrarily large under post-composition with
\xi^{\prime}
if the Lipschitz constants
b_{h}
are unbounded. Precomposing with the coarse map
R
gives us the desired result.

2.

Let
c_{(2^{h},2^{h+1}]}(Y\times[0,1])=\{(nx,n,nt)\,|\,x\in Y,n\in(2^{h},2^{h+1}],t\in[0,1]\}
be the portion of
I_{p}(cY)
with heights in
(2^{h},2^{h+1}]
. Let
a_{h}
be the Lipschitz constants for
i^{\prime}
as before, and let
b_{h}
be the Lipschitz constants for
F
when restricted to heights
[2^{h},2^{h+1}]
. Choose constants
m_{h}
such that
2^{m_{h}}>b_{h}a_{h}
and
m_{h}\leq m_{h+1}
for all
h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. We define the shrinking map as follows:

\displaystyle s^{h}_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}:c_{(2^{h+m_{h}},2^{h+1+m_{h}}]}(Y\times[0,1])

\displaystyle\longrightarrow c_{(2^{h},2^{h+1}]}(Y\times[0,1])

\displaystyle(nx,n,nt)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\frac{n}{2^{m_{h}}}x,\frac{n}{2^{m_{h}}},\frac{n}{2^{m_{h}}}t)

and we send points with heights in the interval
(2^{h+m_{h-1}},2^{h+m_{h}}]
to height
2^{h}
. By the same computation as in Lemma
7.11
the composition
F\circ(i^{\prime}\times\id)\circ s_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}
is proper and uniformly locally
1
-Lipschitz with respect to
\{c_{[2^{h},2^{h+1}]}(Y\times[0,1])\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. Therefore it is globally coarse, by a similar calculation to Lemma
7.11
. The map
R\times\id
is defined as

\displaystyle R\times\id:I_{p}(M_{Y})

\displaystyle\rightarrow c(Y\times[0,1])

\displaystyle(x,n,nt)

\displaystyle\mapsto(R(x,n),nt)

Since
R\times\id
is coarse, we get that
FS_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}
is coarse.

3.

This is the same proof as the previous point, except with technical caveats required for Lemma
7.18
. First, let
a_{h}
be the Lipschitz constants of
i^{\prime}
as before. Let
(L_{\psi})_{h}
be the Lipschitz constant of
\psi
restricted to
M_{[1,2^{h+1}]}\subset M_{X}
. This has bounded image in
M_{[1,2^{l^{\prime}+1}]}
for some
l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
(L_{\xi^{\prime}})_{l^{\prime}}
be the Lipschitz constant of
\xi^{\prime}
restricted to
M_{[1,2^{l^{\prime}+1}]}\subset M_{Y}
. Note that
(L_{\xi^{\prime}})_{l^{\prime}}(L_{\psi})_{h}
may be strictly larger than just the Lipschitz constant of
\xi^{\prime}\psi
restricted to
M_{[1,2^{h+1}]}
.

Let
c_{l^{\prime}}d_{l^{\prime}}
be the Lipschitz constant obtained from going between the path metric on
|\mathcal{U}_{l^{\prime}}|
induced from the spherical metric, to the standard metric, back to the path metric.

\displaystyle(|\mathcal{U}_{l^{\prime}}|,d^{s})\xrightarrow{c_{l^{\prime}}}(|\mathcal{U}_{l^{\prime}}|,d_{std})\xrightarrow{d_{l^{\prime}}}(|\mathcal{U}_{l^{\prime}}|,d^{s})

The reason why these metrics are bi-Lipschitz equivalent is because each
|\mathcal{U}_{l^{\prime}}|
is a finite simplicial complex. We can assume that all the sequences
\{c_{l^{\prime}}\}_{l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}},\{d_{l^{\prime}}\}_{l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
\{(L_{\xi^{\prime}})_{l^{\prime}}\}_{l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, as well as
\{a_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
\{(L_{\psi})_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
are increasing and
\geq 2
. Choose an increasing sequence
\{m^{\prime}_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
such that

\displaystyle 2^{m^{\prime}_{h}}\geq a_{h}(L_{\psi})_{h}(L_{\xi^{\prime}})_{l^{\prime}}c_{l^{\prime}}d_{l^{\prime}}

Let
b_{h}
be the Lipschitz constants for
G
when restricted to heights
[2^{h},2^{h+1}]
. We can assume that
2^{m^{\prime}_{h}}\geq a_{h}b_{h}
. The construction of the shrinking map
s_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
and the rest of the proof is identical to point
2
. of this lemma.

4.

We can assume that the
s
from Lemma
7.12
is the same as
s_{\xi^{\prime}}
. So
S_{\xi^{\prime}}=i^{\prime}s_{\xi^{\prime}}R
is close to
isR
, which by Lemma
7.12
is coarsely homotopic to the identity. Similarly, one can show that
S_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}=(i^{\prime}\times\id)\circ s_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}\circ(R\times\id)
is close to
(i\times\id)\circ s_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}\circ(R\times\id)
, which is close to
\mathfrak{q}\circ(iR\times\id)
, where
\mathfrak{q}:I_{p}(M_{X})\rightarrow I_{p}(M_{X})
is the projection to the height determined by
s_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}
. The same calculation works for
S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
.

∎

Lemma 7.17
.

\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}
is a coarse homotopy inverse to
is_{l}\mathfrak{a}R:M_{X}\rightarrow M_{Y}
.

Proof.

We compute both compositions. Recall that
\psi=\widetilde{is_{l}\mathfrak{a}R}
for sufficiently large
l
. By Lemma
7.16
,
FS_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}
gives us a coarse homotopy between
\psi\xi^{\prime}S_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}
and
\id_{M_{Y}}S_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}
. (Note that since the shrinking map
S_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}
is uniform in time we have used the same symbol for its restriction to
t=0,1
.) We wish to show the homotopy
\psi\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}\simeq\psi\xi^{\prime}S_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}
. This is a similar calculation to Lemma
3.12
. Let
\gamma:[1,\infty)\rightarrow[1,\infty)
be the shrinking map associated to
s_{\xi^{\prime}}
and let
\gamma^{\prime}
be associated to
s_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}
. We can assume that
\gamma^{\prime}(t)\leq\gamma(t)
for all
t\in[1,\infty)
. Let

\displaystyle H:I_{p}(cY)

\displaystyle\rightarrow cY

\displaystyle(nx,n,nt)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\eta(n,t)x,\eta(n,t))

be the coarse homotopy from
s_{\xi^{\prime}}
to
s_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}
, where
\eta(n,t)=(1-t)\gamma(n)+t\gamma^{\prime}(n)
. We show that
\xi^{\prime}i^{\prime}H
is uniformly locally Lipschitz. Let
\{m_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be the sequence of constants associated to
\gamma
. Consider two points
(nx,n,nt),(sy,s,sl)
in
c_{2^{m_{h}}(2^{h},2^{h+1}]}(Y\times[0,1])
.

\displaystyle d_{cY}(H(nx,n,

\displaystyle nt),H(sy,s,sl))\leq\|\eta(n,t)x-\eta(n,t)y\|+2|\eta(n,t)-\eta(s,l)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n}{2^{m_{h}}}\|x-y\|+2(|\eta(n,t)-\eta(s,t)|+|\eta(s,t)-\eta(s,l)|)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{m_{h}}}\|nx-ny\|+2(\frac{1}{2^{m_{h}}}|n-s|+|t-l||\gamma(s)+\gamma^{\prime}(s)|)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{m_{h}}}(\|nx-sy\|+|n-s|)+2(\frac{1}{2^{m_{h}}}|n-s|+\frac{2}{2^{m_{h}}}|st-sl|)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{m_{h}}}(\|nx-sy\|+|n-s|)+2(\frac{1}{2^{m_{h}}}|n-s|+\frac{2}{2^{m_{h}}}(|nt-sl|+|n-s|))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{m_{h}}}(\|nx-sy\|+7|n-s|+4|nt-sl|)

The images of the points have heights in
[2^{h},2^{h+1}]
. Therefore the restriction of
\xi^{\prime}i^{\prime}H
to
c_{2^{m_{h}}(2^{h},2^{h+1}]}(Y\times[0,1])
is
7
-Lipschitz. For points in the height interval
(2^{h+m_{h-1}},2^{h+m_{h}}]
the calculation is analagous. We have

\displaystyle d_{cY}(H(nx,n,nt),H(sy,s,sl))\leq\frac{1}{2^{m_{h-1}}}(\|nx-sy\|+7|n-s|+4|nt-sl|)

with image at height
2^{h}
. Therefore
\xi^{\prime}i^{\prime}H
restricted to
c_{(2^{h+m_{h-1}},2^{h+m_{h}}]}(Y\times[0,1])
is also
7
-Lipschitz. This shows that
\xi^{\prime}i^{\prime}H
is uniformly locally
7
-Lipschitz, and therefore coarse. Hence the composition
\psi\circ(\xi^{\prime}i^{\prime}H)\circ(R\times\id)
is a coarse homotopy between
\psi\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}
and
\psi\xi^{\prime}S_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}
. We have the following sequence of coarse homotopies:

\displaystyle(is_{l}\mathfrak{a}R)\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}\simeq\psi\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}\simeq\psi\xi^{\prime}S_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}\simeq\id_{M_{Y}}S_{\psi\xi^{\prime}}\simeq\id_{M_{Y}}

where in the first equivalence we have used that
\psi
is close to
is_{l}\mathfrak{a}R
.

To show that
\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}(is_{l}\mathfrak{a}R)\simeq\id_{M_{X}}
is more complicated.
We have a coarse homotopy
GS_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
between
\xi^{\prime}\psi S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
and
\id_{M_{X}}S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
. We would like to have the following sequence of homotopies:

\displaystyle\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}(is_{l}\mathfrak{a}R)\simeq\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}\psi\simeq\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}\psi S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}\simeq\xi^{\prime}\psi S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}\simeq\id_{M_{X}}S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}\simeq\id_{M_{X}}

The trouble appears in the homotopy
\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}\psi S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}\simeq\xi^{\prime}\psi S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
. Since
\xi^{\prime}
is only locally Lipschitz, we cannot simply run the homotopy
\xi^{\prime}H\psi S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
where
H
is a coarse homotopy between
S_{\xi^{\prime}}
and
\id
without thinking: any bounded distance can become arbitrarily large under
\xi^{\prime}
. We therefore run the homotopy in two steps:

Let
\gamma
be the shrinking map corresponding to
s_{\xi^{\prime}}
,
\gamma^{\prime}
the shrinking map corresponding to
s_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
. We let
\mathbf{D}
be the deformation retract

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}:I_{p}(M_{Y})

\displaystyle\rightarrow M_{Y}

\displaystyle(x,n,nt)

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathfrak{q}_{(1-t)n+t\gamma(n)}(x,n)

where
\mathfrak{q}
refers to the height projection.
\mathbf{D}_{1}:M_{Y}\rightarrow M_{Y}
is the map which shrinks heights via
\gamma
. We first compute the Lipschitz constant of
\mathbf{D}
. Let
(x,n,nt),(y,s,sl)
be two points in
I_{p}(M_{Y})
. Assume that
n\geq s
.

\displaystyle d(\mathbf{D}(x,n,nt),\mathbf{D}(y,s,sl))\leq d(\mathbf{D}(x,n,nt),\mathbf{D}(\mathfrak{q}_{s}(x,n),sl))+d(\mathbf{D}(\mathfrak{q}_{s}(x,n),sl),\mathbf{D}(y,s,sl))

\displaystyle=d(\mathfrak{q}_{(1-t)n+t\gamma(n)}(x,n),\mathfrak{q}_{(1-l)s+l\gamma(s)}\mathfrak{q}_{s}(x,n))+d(\mathfrak{q}_{(1-l)s+l\gamma(s)}\mathfrak{q}_{s}(x,n),\mathfrak{q}_{(1-l)s+l\gamma(s)}(y,s))

The first distance is just the straight line of length

\displaystyle|(1-t)n+t\gamma(n)-(1-l)s-l\gamma(s)|

\displaystyle\leq 2|nt-sl|+3|n-s|

\displaystyle\leq 2|nt-sl|+3d((x,n),(y,s))

The distance between the second two points is the distance between the projections of
x
and
y
to the height
(1-l)s+l\gamma(s)
, measured within the appropriate nerve
|\mathcal{U}|
. Since the bonding maps are
1
-Lipschitz, we have

\displaystyle d(\mathfrak{q}_{(1-l)s+l\gamma(s)}\mathfrak{q}_{s}(x,n),\mathfrak{q}_{(1-l)s+l\gamma(s)}(y,s))\leq d(\mathfrak{q}_{s}(x,n),(y,s))\leq d((x,n),(y,s))

Therefore the homotopy
\mathbf{D}
is
4
-Lipschitz. Consider the composition

\displaystyle\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{D}\circ H:I_{p}(cX)

\displaystyle\rightarrow M_{X}

\displaystyle(nx,n,nt)

\displaystyle\mapsto\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{D}(\psi i^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}(n)x,\gamma^{\prime}(n)),p\psi i^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}(n)x,\gamma^{\prime}(n))t)

where
H:I_{p}(cX)\rightarrow I_{p}(M_{Y})
is the rescaled version of
\psi i^{\prime}s_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}\times\id
.
\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{D}\circ H
is a homotopy from
\xi^{\prime}\psi i^{\prime}s_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
to
\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{D}_{1}\psi i^{\prime}s_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
. We now show that
H
is Lipschitz. Recall that
(L_{\psi})_{h}
is the Lipschitz constant of
\psi
restricted to heights
[1,2^{h+1}]
and that
\{m^{\prime}_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is the sequence of constants associated to
\gamma^{\prime}
. We can assume that
\psi=\widetilde{is_{l}\mathfrak{a}R}
is height-decreasing by choosing
l
sufficiently large. Let
(nx,n,nt),(sy,s,sl)
be two points in
c_{2^{m^{\prime}_{h}}[2^{h},2^{h+1}]}(X\times[0,1])
. We have that

\displaystyle d(\psi i^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}(n)x,\gamma^{\prime}(n)),\psi i^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}(s)y,\gamma^{\prime}(s)))\leq a_{h}(L_{\psi})_{h}d((\gamma^{\prime}(n)x,\gamma^{\prime}(n)),(\gamma^{\prime}(s)y,\gamma^{\prime}(s)))

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{h}(L_{\psi})_{h}}{2^{m^{\prime}_{h}}}d((nx,n),(sy,s))

\displaystyle|p\psi i^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}(n)x,\gamma^{\prime}(n))t-p\psi i^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}(s)y,\gamma^{\prime}(s))l)|

\displaystyle\leq|p\psi i^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}(n)x,\gamma^{\prime}(n))t-p\psi i^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}(n)x,\gamma^{\prime}(n))l|+|p\psi i^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}(n)x,\gamma^{\prime}(n))l-p\psi i^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}(s)y,\gamma^{\prime}(s))l|

\displaystyle=|t-l||p\psi i^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}(n)x,\gamma^{\prime}(n))|+|p\psi i^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}(n)x,\gamma^{\prime}(n))-p\psi i^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}(s)y,\gamma^{\prime}(s))|

\displaystyle\leq|t-l|\frac{n}{2^{m^{\prime}_{h}}}+\frac{(L_{\psi})_{h}a_{h}}{2^{m^{\prime}_{h}}}d((nx,n),(sy,s))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{m^{\prime}_{h}}}(|nt-sl|+|n-s|)+\frac{(L_{\psi})_{h}a_{h}}{2^{m^{\prime}_{h}}}d((nx,n),(sy,s))

A similar computation works for heights in the interval in
[2^{h+m^{\prime}_{h}+1},2^{h+m^{\prime}_{h+1}+1}]
. This shows that
H
restricted to
c_{[2^{h+m^{\prime}_{h}},2^{h+m^{\prime}_{h+1}+1}]}(X\times[0,1])
is
\frac{3a_{h}(L_{\psi})_{h}}{2^{m^{\prime}_{h}}}
-Lipschitz.
The computation for heights in the interval
[2^{h-1+m^{\prime}_{h-1}},2^{h+m^{\prime}_{h}}]
is analagous, replacing
h
with
h-1
.

Since in the construction of
S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
, we have maximised over the Lipschitz constants of each successive bounded set, and
\mathbf{D}
is height-decreasing, the homotopy
\xi\mathbf{D}H
is uniformly locally
12
-Lipschitz with respect to
\{c_{[2^{h+m^{\prime}_{h}},2^{h+m^{\prime}_{h+1}+1}]}(X\times[0,1])\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. Hence it is globally coarse. Precomposing with the coarse map
R\times\id
, we obtain a coarse homotopy between
\xi^{\prime}\psi S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
and
\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{D}_{1}\psi S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
. This was step 1.

For the second step, we construct a coarse homotopy between
\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{D}_{1}\psi S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
and
\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}\psi S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
as the composition
\xi^{\prime}\circ\mathbf{G}\circ\mathbf{F}\circ(R\times\id)
.
\mathbf{G}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}:M_{Y}\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\rightarrow M_{Y}

\displaystyle(x,n,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto(1-t)\mathbf{D}_{1}(x,n)+ti^{\prime}s_{\xi^{\prime}}R(x,n)

Why does this make sense? Consider a point
(x,n)
with
x\in int(\sigma)
in some appropriate nerve
|\mathcal{U}|
.
\mathbf{D}_{1}(\sigma\times\{n\})=\mathfrak{q}_{\gamma(n)}(\sigma\times\{n\})=\phi(\sigma)\times\{\gamma(n)\}
is a simplex at height
\gamma(n)
for the appropriate bonding map
\phi
. The image of
i^{\prime}s_{\xi^{\prime}}R(int(\sigma)\times\{n\})
lies in the set

\displaystyle\bigcap_{v\in\phi(\sigma)}\langle st(v)\rangle\times\{\gamma(n)\}

Therefore, the straight line between
\mathbf{D}_{1}(x,n)
and
i^{\prime}s_{\xi^{\prime}}R(x,n)
makes sense. Note that this homotopy is very much non-continuous. We define
\mathbf{F}
as the map

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}:I_{p}(cX)

\displaystyle\rightarrow M_{Y}\times[0,1]

\displaystyle(nx,n,nt)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\psi i^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}(n)x,\gamma^{\prime}(n)),t)

Let
(nx,n,nt),(sy,s,sl)
be two points heights in the interval
[2^{h+m^{\prime}_{h}},2^{h+m^{\prime}_{h+1}+1}]
. Recall that we have

\displaystyle d(\psi i^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}(n)x,\gamma^{\prime}(n)),\psi i^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}(s)y,\gamma^{\prime}(s)))\leq\frac{a_{h}(L_{\psi})_{h}}{2^{m^{\prime}_{h}}}d((nx,n),(sy,s))

\displaystyle|t-l|=\frac{1}{n}|nt-nl|\leq\frac{1}{2^{m^{\prime}_{h}+h}}(|nt-sl|+|n-s|)

Now we have to show that for a set sufficiently small in
M_{Y}\times[0,1]
, the image under
\mathbf{G}
is actually sufficiently small. Let
\varepsilon,\delta
be very small constants, to be determined later. Suppose we have two points
(x,n,t),(y,s,l)\in M_{Y}\times[0,1]
below height
2^{l^{\prime}}
, contained within
U_{c_{l^{\prime}}\varepsilon}\times I_{\varepsilon}\times I_{\delta}
, where
I_{\varepsilon}
only intersects at most one a gluing slice on the boundary and
U_{c_{l^{\prime}}\varepsilon}
is a set of diameter
<c_{l^{\prime}}\varepsilon
(with respect to the standard metric) contained within a simplex. The reason why we can restrict to this case is that for an arbitrary set
V
of diameter
<\varepsilon^{\prime}
(with respect to the path metric) in
M_{Y}
where
c_{l^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{\prime}<\frac{1}{2}
, any two points
(u,n),(w,s)
in
V
can be split into at most
5
segments of the form we have assumed.

To see this, let
n\geq s
. First we assume that the shortest path between
(u,n),(w,s)
consists of
\omega_{(u,n)}\ast\eta_{s}
where
\omega_{(u,n)}
is the straight line downards and
\eta_{s}\subset(U_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\times\{s\})
is a geodesic at height
s
between
\mathfrak{q}_{s}(u)
and
w
. Split
\omega_{(u,n)}
into two paths which intersect at most one gluing slice. Now for two points
x,y
in
U_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
, we consider their image in
(|\mathcal{U}|,d_{std})
, which have distance
<c_{l^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{\prime}<\frac{1}{2}
. Let
x=\sum_{v\in vert|\mathcal{U}|}x_{v}[v]
and
y=\sum_{v\in vert|\mathcal{U}|}y_{v}[v]
. Define

\displaystyle Z=\{v\in vert|\mathcal{U}|\,|\,x_{v}\neq 0,y_{v}\neq 0\}

and
a=\sum_{v\in Z}x_{v}
and
b=\sum_{v\in Z}y_{v}
. The distance between
x
and
y
is

\displaystyle d_{std}(x,y)=(1-a)+(1-b)+\sum_{v\in Z}|x_{v}-y_{v}|<c_{l^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{\prime}

Define
z_{x}=\frac{1}{a}\sum_{v\in Z}x_{v}[v]
and
z_{y}=\frac{1}{b}\sum_{v\in Z}y_{v}[v]
. We compute

\displaystyle d_{std}(x,z_{x})

\displaystyle=(1-a)+\sum_{v\in Z}(\frac{1}{a}-1)x_{v}=2(1-a)<2c_{l^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{\prime}

\displaystyle d_{std}(y,z_{y})

\displaystyle<2c_{l^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{\prime}

\displaystyle ad_{std}(z_{x},z_{y})

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in Z}|x_{v}-\frac{a}{b}y_{v}|\leq\sum_{v\in Z}|x_{v}-y_{v}|+|\frac{a}{b}-1|y_{v}

\displaystyle=(\sum_{v\in Z}|x_{v}-y_{v}|)+|a-b|\leq(\sum_{v\in Z}|x_{v}-y_{v}|)+|1-a|+|1-b|<c_{l^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{\prime}

\displaystyle d_{std}(z_{x},z_{y})

\displaystyle<\frac{c_{l^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{\prime}}{a}<\frac{c_{l^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{\prime}}{1-c_{l^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{\prime}}<2c_{l^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{\prime}

Therefore
\eta_{s}
can be split into three straight paths of length less than
2c_{l^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{\prime}
, each contained within a simplex.

The only other case is if
n,s\in(2^{h},2^{h}+1]
and the shortest path between
(u,n),(w,s)
is a concatenation
\omega_{(u,n)}\ast\eta_{2^{h}}\ast\omega_{(w,s)}^{-1}
, where
\eta_{2^{h}}
is a geodesic at height
2^{h}
. As before, we can split
\eta_{2^{h}}
into three paths of the desired form.

Let us return to the proof. Recall we have two points
(x,n,t),(y,s,l)\in M_{Y}\times[0,1]
below height
2^{l^{\prime}}
, contained within
U_{c_{l^{\prime}}\varepsilon}\times I_{\varepsilon}\times I_{\delta}
. Assume that
n\geq s
. We consider the points
(x,n,t),(x,s,t),(x,s,l),(y,s,l)
under the image of
\mathbf{G}
with respect to the standard metric
d_{std}
:

1.

d_{std}(\mathbf{G}(x,s,t),\mathbf{G}(x,s,l))
: Since the homotopy is the straight line between two points that lie within a simplex, it is
1
-Lipschitz in the time variable. Therefore
d_{std}(\mathbf{G}(x,s,t),\mathbf{G}(x,s,l))\leq|t-l|<\delta
.

2.

d_{std}(\mathbf{G}(x,s,l),\mathbf{G}(y,s,l))
: We know that
d_{std}(\mathbf{D}_{1}(x,s),\mathbf{D}_{1}(y,s))\leq d_{std}(x,y)<c_{l}^{\prime}\varepsilon
. Let
\sigma
be the simplex that contains
x,y
. We know from previous calculations that
R(\sigma)
has diameter less than
\frac{69}{4}=C
(a conservative upper bound). Recall that
i^{\prime}s_{\xi^{\prime}}
is
\frac{1}{2^{b_{h^{\prime}}}}
-Lipschitz for some
h^{\prime}<l^{\prime}
where
2^{b_{h^{\prime}}}
is sufficiently large so that
\xi^{\prime}i^{\prime}s_{\xi^{\prime}}
is uniformly locally
1
-Lipschitz. We can assume that
2^{b_{h^{\prime}}}
is also greater than the local Lipschitz constant
(L_{\xi^{\prime}})_{h^{\prime}}
multipled by the Lipschitz constant
c_{h^{\prime}}d_{h^{\prime}}
which we obtain from changing between metrics. Therefore
d(i^{\prime}s_{\xi^{\prime}}R(x,s),i^{\prime}s_{\xi^{\prime}}R(y,s))\leq\frac{C}{2^{b_{h^{\prime}}}}
. In standard metric,
d_{std}(\mathbf{G}(x,s,l),\mathbf{G}(y,s,l))\leq\max\{\frac{Cc_{h^{\prime}}}{2^{b_{h^{\prime}}}},c_{l}^{\prime}\varepsilon\}
. To see this, observe that for four points
v_{1},v_{2},w_{1},w_{2}
in Euclidean space,

\displaystyle\|(1-t)v_{1}+tw_{1}-(1-t)v_{2}+tw_{2}\|

\displaystyle\leq(1-t)\|v_{1}-v_{2}\|+t\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|\leq\max\{\|v_{1}-v_{2}\|,\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|\}

3.

d_{std}(\mathbf{G}(x,n,t),\mathbf{G}(x,s,t))
: Ignore first the possible discontinuity related to
R
at the boundary. We have that
R(x,n)
and
R(x,s)
get sent to the same
x
-coordinate, so their distance in
cY
is less than
2|n-s|
. Remember that by construction,
i^{\prime}s_{\xi^{\prime}}
is actually
\frac{1}{2^{b_{h^{\prime}}}}
-Lipschitz with respect to the product metric on each
\tilde{M}^{h^{\prime}}
before gluing. Because the shrinking maps are constructed using powers of
2
, the image of
s_{\xi^{\prime}}(x,n)
and
s_{\xi^{\prime}}(x,s)
do not intersect a gluing component except possibly at the boundary - we are allowed to compute distances in one such
\tilde{M}^{h^{\prime}}
. Therefore, the
|\mathcal{U}_{h^{\prime}}|
-component of
i^{\prime}s_{\xi^{\prime}}R(x,n)
and
i^{\prime}s_{\xi^{\prime}}R(x,s)
have distance less than than
2\cdot\frac{1}{2^{b_{h^{\prime}}}}|n-s|
. Since
\mathbf{D}_{1}(x,n)
and
\mathbf{D}_{1}(x,s)
have the same
x
-coordinate, by the previous case we conclude that

\displaystyle d_{std}(\mathbf{G}(x,n,t),\mathbf{G}(x,s,t))\leq(2\cdot\frac{c_{h^{\prime}}}{2^{b_{h^{\prime}}}}+1)|n-s|

If we do not intersect a gluing slice, this finishes the estimate. If
n
or
s
lie on a gluing slice there is a discontinuity to consider. By the previous case, the discontinuity is bounded by
\frac{Cc_{h^{\prime}}}{2^{b_{h^{\prime}}}}
, so in total we get

\displaystyle d_{std}(\mathbf{G}(x,n,t),\mathbf{G}(x,s,t))<\frac{Cc_{h^{\prime}}}{2^{b_{h^{\prime}}}}+(2\cdot\frac{c_{h^{\prime}}}{2^{b_{h^{\prime}}}}+1)\varepsilon<\frac{Cc_{h^{\prime}}}{2^{b_{h^{\prime}}}}+3\varepsilon

in both cases.

Therefore,
d_{std}(\mathbf{G}(x,n,t),\mathbf{G}(y,s,l))<\delta+\max\{\frac{Cc_{h^{\prime}}}{2^{b_{h^{\prime}}}},c_{l}^{\prime}\varepsilon\}+\frac{Cc_{h^{\prime}}}{2^{b_{h^{\prime}}}}+3\varepsilon
. We now let
\varepsilon=\frac{a_{h}(L_{\psi})_{h}}{2^{m^{\prime}_{h}}}
and
\delta=\frac{1}{2^{m^{\prime}_{h}+h}}<\varepsilon
, which gives us
d_{std}(\mathbf{G}(x,n,t),\mathbf{G}(y,s,l))<(5c_{l^{\prime}}\varepsilon+2\frac{Cc_{h^{\prime}}}{2^{b_{h^{\prime}}}})
. Recall that
2^{m_{h}^{\prime}}>a_{h}(L_{\psi})_{h}(L_{\xi^{\prime}})_{l^{\prime}}c_{l^{\prime}}d_{l^{\prime}}
and that
2^{b_{h^{\prime}}}>c_{h^{\prime}}d_{h^{\prime}}(L_{\xi^{\prime}})_{h^{\prime}}
. Postcomposing with the locally Lipschitz map
\xi^{\prime}
, we obtain

\displaystyle d_{path}(\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{G}(x,n,t),\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{G}(y,s,l))

\displaystyle\leq(L_{\xi^{\prime}})_{h^{\prime}}d_{h^{\prime}}(5c_{l^{\prime}}\varepsilon+2\frac{Cc_{h^{\prime}}}{2^{b_{h^{\prime}}}})

\displaystyle\leq(L_{\xi^{\prime}})_{l^{\prime}}d_{l^{\prime}}\frac{5c_{l^{\prime}}a_{h}(L_{\psi})_{h}}{2^{m^{\prime}_{h}}}+2(L_{\xi^{\prime}})_{h^{\prime}}d_{h^{\prime}}\frac{Cc_{h^{\prime}}}{2^{b_{h^{\prime}}}}

\displaystyle<5+2C

Now we finally show that
\xi\circ\mathbf{G}\circ\mathbf{F}
is a coarse homotopy. Consider
(nx,n,nt),(sy,s,sl)
, two points in
I_{p}(cX)
with heights in the interval
[2^{h+m^{\prime}_{h}},2^{h+m^{\prime}_{h+1}+1}]
and distance less than
Q\in\mathbb{N}
.
\mathbf{F}(nx,n,nt),\mathbf{F}(sy,s,sl)
live below height
2^{l^{\prime}}
and are contained in
V_{Q\varepsilon}\times I_{Q\delta}
. Therefore, the shortest path between them can be split up into
2Q
segments each lying within
V_{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}\times I_{\delta}
. By previous discussion we can split this further to obtain
10Q
path segments each lying within a set of the form
U_{c_{l^{\prime}}\varepsilon}\times I_{\varepsilon}\times I_{\delta}
with respect to the standard metric. The endpoints of each of these segments, after composing with
\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{G}
, are distance
<5+2C
apart. We obtain

\displaystyle d_{path}(\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{GF}(nx,n,nt),\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{GF}(sy,s,sl))

\displaystyle\leq 10Q(5+2C)

Therefore, a set of diameter
Q\in\mathbb{N}
in
c_{[2^{h+m^{\prime}_{h}},2^{h+m^{\prime}_{h+1}+1}]}(X\times[0,1])
gets sent to a set of diameter
(50+20C)Q
under the map
\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{GF}
. By a similar argument to Lemma
7.11
, for arbitrary points
(nx,n,nt),(sy,s,sl)
in
I_{p}(cX)
and
Q>0
, there exists a constant
T_{Q}
such that

\displaystyle d((nx,n,nt),(sy,s,sl))<Q

\displaystyle\implies d(\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{GF}(nx,n,nt),\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{GF}(sy,s,sl))<\max\{3(50+20C)Q,(50+20C)Q+T_{Q}\}

This shows that
\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{GF}
is coarse. By precomposing with the coarse map
R\times\id
, we obtain that
\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{D}_{1}\psi S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
and
\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}\psi S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
are coarsely homotopic.

The two steps show that
\xi^{\prime}\psi S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}\simeq\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{D}_{1}\psi S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}\simeq\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}\psi S_{\xi^{\prime}\psi}
which was the required homotopy to conclude

\displaystyle\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}(is_{l}\mathfrak{a}R)\simeq\id_{M_{X}}

Therefore,
\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}
is a coarse homotopy inverse to
is_{l}\mathfrak{a}R
.
∎

Lemma 7.18
.

The composition
\mathfrak{b}:=(R\circ\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}\circ is)
is a coarse homotopy inverse to
\mathfrak{a}
.

Proof.

We compute

\displaystyle R\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}is\mathfrak{a}\simeq R(\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}})(is_{l}\mathfrak{a}R)is\simeq R\id_{M_{X}}is\simeq\id_{cX}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}R\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}}is\simeq R(is_{l}\mathfrak{a}R)(\xi^{\prime}S_{\xi^{\prime}})is\simeq R\id_{M_{Y}}is\simeq\id_{cY}

∎

This concludes the proof of Theorem
7.9
.

There is also a based version of coarse Whitehead, which can be obtained from observing that all our constructions can be chosen to be maps of pairs, if
\mathfrak{a}
is a map of pairs.

Corollary 7.19
.

Let
(X,x_{0})
and
(Y,y_{0})
be connected, compact metric spaces with finite shape dimension. Let
\omega,\tau
be the standard parametrisations of the base rays
c\{x_{0}\},c\{y_{0}\}
respectively. Let
\mathfrak{a}:(cX,\omega)\rightarrow(cY,\tau)
be a coarse map of pairs, which induces an isomorphism on coarse homotopy groups
\mathfrak{a}:\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\omega)\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{c}(cY,\mathfrak{a}\omega)
for all
n\geq 0
. Then
\mathfrak{a}
is a coarse homotopy equivalence of pairs, ie. there a coarse map of pairs
\mathfrak{b}:(cY,\tau)\rightarrow(cX,\omega)
such that
\mathfrak{b}\circ\mathfrak{a}\sim id_{cX}
and
\mathfrak{a}\circ\mathfrak{b}\sim id_{cY}
as coarse homotopies of pairs.

8
The functor
\mathcal{F}

Recall that
\mathbf{BornCoarse}
has objects bornological coarse spaces and morphisms proper and controlled maps. Consider now a set
X
equipped with a coarse structure
\mathcal{C}
. We define a bornology
\mathcal{B}
on
X
by letting
B\in\mathcal{B}
if there exists
x\in X
such that
B\times\{x\}\in\mathcal{C}
. We say that
\mathcal{B}
is
induced from the coarse structure
. Recall that a controlled map
f:X\rightarrow Y
, where
Y
is an arbitrary bornological coarse space, is automatically bornologous. We denote by
Coarse
the subcategory of
BornCoarse
consisting of coarse spaces with the induced bornology from the coarse structure, while retaining the morphisms from
BornCoarse
.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
\mathbf{hCoarse}
be the category with the same objects as
\mathbf{Coarse}
but where morphisms are coarse homotopy classes of coarse bornologous maps.

The reason why we could not define
\mathbf{hBornCoarse}
directly is because composition of coarse homotopy classes is only well-defined for coarse bornologous maps: see Proposition
2.4
.

Theorem 8.2
.

There is a full functor

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}:\mathbf{StrongShape}\rightarrow\mathbf{hCoarse}

Conjecture 8.3
.

The functor
\mathcal{F}
from Theorem
8.2
is faithful.

Definition 8.4
.

(The functor
\mathcal{F}
)
Let
(W,X)
be an object in
\mathbf{StrongShape}
. We define

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(W,X):=cX

the Euclidean cone over the compact metric space
X
.

Let
[\overline{\psi}]:(W_{X},X)\rightarrow(W_{Y},Y)
be a morphism in
\mathbf{StrongShape}
, ie. a proper homotopy class of proper maps
W_{X}\setminus X\rightarrow W_{Y}\setminus Y
. By Theorem
6.28
, we know that
W_{X}\setminus X\simeq M_{X}
and
W_{Y}\setminus Y\simeq M_{Y}
. By pre- and postcomposing with the proper homotopy equivalences
\zeta_{X}:M_{X}\rightarrow W_{X}\setminus X
and
\zeta^{-1}_{Y}:W_{Y}\setminus Y\rightarrow M_{Y}
, we obtain a proper homotopy class of proper maps
[\zeta^{-1}_{Y}\overline{\psi}\zeta_{X}]=:[\psi]:M_{X}\rightarrow M_{Y}
. We define

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}([\overline{\psi}]):=[R_{Y}\psi^{\prime}S_{\psi^{\prime}}i_{X}s_{X}]

where
\psi^{\prime}
is a locally Lipschitz approximation of
\psi
,
S_{\psi^{\prime}}
is a coarse shrinking map on
M_{X}
, and
i_{X}s_{X}:cX\rightarrow M_{X}
,
R_{Y}:M_{Y}\rightarrow cY
are the coarse homotopy equivalences defined previously.

We dedicate the rest of this section to showing that
F
is well-defined, and that it is full. We will outline a proof attempt that it is faithful.

F
is well-defined on morphisms
: Let
\overline{\eta}
be another representative of
[\overline{\psi}]
. Then
\eta
is properly homotopic to
\psi
. We have to show that there is a coarse homotopy

\displaystyle R_{Y}\psi^{\prime}S_{\psi^{\prime}}i_{X}s_{X}\simeq R_{Y}\eta^{\prime}S_{\eta^{\prime}}i_{X}s_{X}

By Lemma
7.13
,
\psi
and
\eta
have locally Lipschitz representatives
\psi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}
. By Lemma
7.16
there are coarse shrinking maps
S_{\psi^{\prime}}
and
S_{\eta^{\prime}}
such that
\psi^{\prime}S_{\psi^{\prime}}
and
\eta^{\prime}S_{\eta^{\prime}}
are coarse. By Lemma
7.15
there exists a locally Lipschitz homotopy
F:I_{p}(M_{X})\rightarrow M_{Y}
from
\psi^{\prime}
to
\eta^{\prime}
. There exists a coarse shrinking map
S_{F}:I_{p}(M_{X})\rightarrow I_{p}(M_{X})
such that
FS_{F}
is a coarse homotopy between
\psi^{\prime}S_{F}
and
\eta^{\prime}S_{F}
. By the same argument as in Lemma
7.17
, we have that
\psi^{\prime}S_{\psi^{\prime}}\simeq\psi^{\prime}S_{F}
and
\eta^{\prime}S_{F}\simeq\eta^{\prime}S_{\eta^{\prime}}
. Therefore, we obtain:

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}S_{\psi^{\prime}}\simeq\psi^{\prime}S_{F}\simeq\eta^{\prime}S_{F}\simeq\eta^{\prime}S_{\eta^{\prime}}

and the claim follows.

Remark
.

This proof also tells us that the coarse homotopy class of
R_{Y}\psi^{\prime}S_{\psi^{\prime}}i_{X}s_{X}
is independent of the choice of simplicial approximation
\psi^{\prime}
since any two choices of locally Lipschitz simplicial approximation are properly homotopic.

\mathcal{F}
is a functor
: To prove that
\mathcal{F}
is functorial, we have to show that
\mathcal{F}
respects composition of morphisms and that
\mathcal{F}([\id_{M_{X}}])=[\id_{cX}]
. Suppose that
X,Y,Z
are compact metric spaces and let

\displaystyle W_{X}\setminus X\xrightarrow{\overline{\psi}}W_{Y}\setminus Y\xrightarrow{\overline{\eta}}W_{Z}\setminus Z

be a composition of morphisms. We have that
\zeta^{-1}_{Z}\overline{\eta}\overline{\psi}\zeta_{X}\simeq\eta\psi
. We show that
\mathcal{F}([\eta\circ\psi])=\mathcal{F}([\eta])\circ\mathcal{F}([\psi])
ie. there is a coarse homotopy

\displaystyle R_{Z}(\eta\psi)^{\prime}S_{(\eta\psi)^{\prime}}i_{X}s_{X}\simeq(R_{Z}\eta^{\prime}S_{\eta^{\prime}}i_{Y}s_{Y})\circ(R_{Y}\psi^{\prime}S_{\psi^{\prime}}i_{X}s_{X})

Since
\eta^{\prime}\psi^{\prime}
is a locally Lipschitz map which is properly homotopic to
(\eta\psi)^{\prime}
, it is also locally Lipschitz homotopic to
(\eta\psi)^{\prime}
via a homotopy
F:I_{p}(M_{X})\rightarrow M_{Z}
. As before, there exists a shrinking map
S_{F}
such that
FS_{F}
is coarse. Therefore we obtain

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}S_{\eta^{\prime}}\psi^{\prime}S_{\psi^{\prime}}\simeq\eta^{\prime}S_{\eta^{\prime}}\psi^{\prime}S_{F}\simeq(\eta^{\prime}\psi^{\prime})S_{F}\simeq(\eta\psi)^{\prime}S_{F}\simeq(\eta\psi)^{\prime}S_{(\eta\psi)^{\prime}}

where the argument from Lemma
7.17
is required for the homotopy
\eta^{\prime}S_{\eta^{\prime}}\psi^{\prime}S_{F}\simeq(\eta^{\prime}\psi^{\prime})S_{F}
. The claim follows.

It is clear that
\mathcal{F}([\id_{M_{X}}])=[\id_{cX}]
since
R_{X}i_{X}s_{X}
is coarsely homotopic to the identity.

\mathcal{F}
is full:
To prove that
\mathcal{F}
is full, we need to show that for a coarse map
\mathfrak{a}:cX\rightarrow cY
, there exists a
\psi:M_{X}\rightarrow M_{Y}
such that
[\mathfrak{a}]=[R_{Y}\psi^{\prime}S_{\psi^{\prime}}i_{X}s_{X}]
. To that end, choose
\psi
to be the geometric cellular approximation
\widetilde{i_{Y}s_{l}\mathfrak{a}R_{X}}
for
l
sufficiently large. It is clear that
\psi^{\prime}S_{\psi^{\prime}}=\psi
and that

\displaystyle R_{Y}\circ(\widetilde{i_{Y}s_{l}\mathfrak{a}R_{X}})\circ i_{X}s_{X}\simeq(R_{Y}i_{Y})\circ s_{l}\mathfrak{a}\circ(R_{X}i_{X}s_{X})\simeq s_{l}\mathfrak{a}\simeq\mathfrak{a}

F
is faithful:
To prove that
F
is faithful, we need to show that if
[R_{Y}\psi^{\prime}S_{\psi^{\prime}}i_{X}s_{X}]=[R_{Y}\eta^{\prime}S_{\eta^{\prime}}i_{X}s_{X}]
then
\psi
and
\eta
are properly homotopic. Unfortunately this appears to be difficult. The trouble lies in the fact that even though
S_{\psi^{\prime}}
is coarsely homotopic to the identity, it does not follow trivially that
\widetilde{\psi^{\prime}S_{\psi^{\prime}}}
is properly homotopic to
\psi^{\prime}
. What would need to be proven is the following

\displaystyle\widetilde{\psi^{\prime}S_{\psi^{\prime}}}\simeq\widetilde{\psi^{\prime}\widetilde{S_{\psi^{\prime}}}}\simeq\psi^{\prime}\widetilde{S_{\psi^{\prime}}}

for some suitable definition of simplicial approximation
\widetilde{S_{\psi^{\prime}}}
. This is technically tricky: recall that by construction
\psi^{\prime}
is already cellular, but with respect to a CW-structure on
M_{X}
that becomes more and more subdivided as we go up the telescope. So we would have to redefine simplicial approximation again for target space this iteratively-subdivided CW complex. We leave this statement for future research.

Even without faithfulness, the functor
\mathcal{F}
respects equivalences. To prove this, we need some auxiliary lemmas.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
H:I_{p}(cX)\rightarrow cY
be a coarse homotopy between
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}
. Then there exists a proper homotopy
M_{X}\times[0,1]\rightarrow M_{Y}
from
\widetilde{i_{Y}s_{l}\mathfrak{a}R_{X}}
to
\widetilde{i_{Y}s_{l}\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}R_{X}}
for sufficiently large
l
.

Proof.

Recall that
I_{p}(cX)=c(X\times[0,1])
. We take distances on
X\times[0,1]
to be measured by the maximum of their distances in
X
and
[0,1]
where
[0,1]
carries the standard metric.

\displaystyle d_{\infty}((x,t),(y,s))=\max\{d_{X}(x,y),|t-s|\}

Recall that we have a fixed sequence of
\frac{1}{2^{h+1}}
-separated sets
\{Z_{h}\}_{\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
in
X
. We can construct a maximal
\frac{1}{2^{h+1}}
-separated set
Z^{\prime}_{h}
on
X\times[0,1]
by simply letting it be copies of
Z_{h}
at points separated by
\frac{1}{2^{h}}
in
[0,1]
:

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}_{h}:=\bigcup_{0\leq k\leq 2^{h}}Z_{h}\times\{\frac{k}{2^{h}}\}=:Z_{h}\times A_{h}

It is clear that
Z^{\prime}_{h}
is a maximal
\frac{1}{2^{h+1}}
-separated set with respect to the metric
d_{\infty}
, and that
Z^{\prime}_{h}\subset Z^{\prime}_{h+1}
for all
h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Take the open cover
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{h}
of balls of radius
\frac{1}{2^{h}}
with respect to
d_{\infty}
and now we can construct the mapping telescope of
M_{X\times[0,1]}
exactly as before. There are analogous constructions of
i_{X\times[0,1]}
and
R_{X\times[0,1]}
for
M_{X\times[0,1]}
. It is clear that
c(X\times[0,1])
and
M_{X\times[0,1]}
are coarsely homotopy equivalent.

Given a coarse homotopy
H:c(X\times[0,1])\rightarrow cY
, there exists a
l
large enough such that the geometric cellular approximation
(i_{Y}s_{l}HR_{X\times[0,1]})^{\sim}
exists. This gives us a proper, locally Lipschitz map:

\displaystyle(i_{Y}s_{l}HR_{X\times[0,1]})^{\sim}:M_{X\times[0,1]}\rightarrow M_{Y}

We define a CW complex
\mathbf{M}_{X\times[0,1]}
, which we will show is a subcomplex of
M_{X\times[0,1]}
. Consider the simplicial complex

\displaystyle K_{h}=|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times[0,2^{h}]

where
[0,2^{h}]
is the locally ordered simplicial complex with vertices at
\mathbb{N}_{0}
and total order inherited from
\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Recall that there is a fixed total order on the vertices of
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
.
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times[0,2^{h}]
is given the product simplicial structure. There are simplicial maps

\displaystyle\psi_{h+1,h}:|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|\times[0,2^{h+1}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times[0,2^{h}]

defined as the linear extension of the vertex map
(z,n)\mapsto(\phi_{h+1}(z),\left\lfloor{\frac{n}{2}}\right\rfloor)
. Let the linear extension of
n\mapsto\left\lfloor{\frac{n}{2}}\right\rfloor
be the function denoted by
\phi^{\prime}_{h+1}
.
\psi_{h+1,h}
can be expressed as
\phi_{h+1}\times\phi^{\prime}_{h+1}
.

Let
\mathbf{M}_{X\times[0,1]}
be the inverse mapping telescope

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{X\times[0,1]}:=K_{0}\times[1,2]\cup_{\psi_{1,0}}K_{1}\times[2,4]\cup_{\psi_{2,1}}\dots

A coninuous, proper homotopy
\mathbf{M}_{X\times[0,1]}\rightarrow M_{Y}
gives us a homotopy
M_{X}\times[0,1]\rightarrow M_{Y}
. This is because in the time component of
\mathbf{M}_{X\times[0,1]}
what we have is actually the mapping telescope

\displaystyle M_{[0,1]}:=[0,1]\times[1,2]\cup_{\phi^{\prime}_{1}}[0,2]\times[2,4]\cup_{\phi^{\prime}_{2}}\dots\cup_{\phi^{\prime}_{h}}[0,2^{h}]\times[2^{h},2^{h+1}]\cup_{\phi^{\prime}_{h+1}}\dots

of a shape expansion of the interval: the simplicial complexes
[0,2^{h}]
correspond to the nerves of the open cover

\displaystyle\cup_{z\in A_{h}}B(z,\frac{1}{2^{h}})

and the
\phi^{\prime}
are simplicial maps induced by refinement. There is a continuous map

\displaystyle i^{\prime}_{[0,1]}:[0,1]\times[1,\infty)\rightarrow M_{[0,1]}

defined piecewise by partitions of unity subordinate to
\cup_{z\in A_{h}}B(z,\frac{1}{2^{h}})
and modified by coherent homotopies to ensure continuity. Therefore, there is a continuous map

\displaystyle\alpha:M_{X}\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbf{M}_{X\times[0,1]}

\displaystyle(x,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto(x,i^{\prime}_{[0,1]}(t,p(x)))

such that
\alpha
restricted to
M_{X}\times\{0,1\}
can be identified with the identity on
M_{X}
.

A set of vertices in
|\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{h}|
span a simplex exactly when their projections to
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
span a simplex, and their projections to
[0,2^{h}]
span a simplex. Therefore a simplex
\tau\subset\sigma\times I
, where
I
denotes an interval of length
1
in
\mathbf{M}_{X\times[0,1]}
, also exists in
M_{X\times[0,1]}
but as the face of a high-dimensional simplex that connects all the vertices of
\sigma\times I
. Moreover, the bonding maps
\psi_{h+1,h}
agree with those of
M_{X\times[0,1]}
. These facts are sufficient to show that
\mathbf{M}_{X\times[0,1]}
is a subcomplex of
M_{X\times[0,1]}
.

We take the restriction of
(i_{Y}s_{l}HR_{X\times[0,1]})^{\sim}
to the subcomplex
\mathbf{M}_{X\times[0,1]}
and observe that because a star in
\mathbf{M}_{X\times[0,1]}
is contained inside the star of
M_{X\times[0,1]}
, the restriction of
(i_{Y}s_{l}HR_{X\times[0,1]})^{\sim}
to
M_{X}\times\{0\}
and
M_{X}\times\{1\}
are simplicial approximations of
i_{Y}s_{l}\mathfrak{a}R_{X}
and
i_{Y}s_{l}\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}R_{X}
respectively (we also require the fact that
R_{X\times[0,1]}
restricted to the boundaries
M_{X\times\partial[0,1]}
can be chosen to agree with
R_{X}
). Therefore, we have a proper homotopy from
\widetilde{i_{Y}s_{l}\mathfrak{a}R_{X}}
and
\widetilde{i_{Y}s_{l}\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}R_{X}}
for sufficiently large
l
.
∎

Corollary 8.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}:cX\rightarrow cY
be a coarse map. There exists a
l
sufficiently large such that the geometric cellular approximation
\widetilde{i_{Y}s_{l}\mathfrak{a}R_{X}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\widetilde{i_{Y}s_{l}\mathfrak{a}R_{X}}\simeq\widetilde{i_{Y}s_{l^{\prime}}\mathfrak{a}R_{X}}

for all
l^{\prime}\geq l
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{H}^{1}
be the coarse homotopy from
\id:cY\rightarrow cY
to
s_{1}
, the shrinking map which divides heights by
2
. By Proposition
2.4
there is a coarse homotopy
I_{p\mathfrak{a}R_{X}}(cX)\rightarrow cY
between
\mathfrak{a}R_{X}
and
s_{1}\mathfrak{a}R_{X}
, obtained by composing
\mathbf{H}^{1}
with
\mathfrak{a}R_{X}
. By Lemma
2.31
we obtain a coarse homotopy
H:c(X\times[0,1])\rightarrow cY
between
\mathfrak{a}R_{X}
and
s_{1}\mathfrak{a}R_{X}
. Apply now Lemma
8.5
to
H
. There is a
l
large enough such that
\widetilde{i_{Y}s_{l}\mathfrak{a}R_{X}}\simeq\widetilde{i_{Y}s_{l+1}\mathfrak{a}R_{X}}
. Replace
l
with
l+1
, and inductively we have our claim.
∎

Theorem 8.7
.

Two objects
(W_{X},X),(W_{Y},Y)
are equivalent in
\mathbf{StrongShape}
if and only if their images
\mathcal{F}(W_{X},X),\mathcal{F}(W_{Y},Y)
are equivalent in
\mathbf{hCoarse}
.

Proof.

Suppose we have a coarse homotopy equivalence
\mathfrak{a}:cX\rightarrow cY
with inverse
\mathfrak{b}:cY\rightarrow cX
. We have to show that
M_{X}
and
M_{Y}
are properly homotopy equivalent. To that end, we take the pre-images in the proof of fullness of
\mathcal{F}
and show that they define a proper homotopy equivalence, ie. that
\psi:=\widetilde{i_{Y}s_{l}\mathfrak{a}R_{X}}:M_{X}\rightarrow M_{Y}
is a proper homotopy equivalence with inverse
\xi:=\widetilde{i_{X}s_{r}\mathfrak{b}R_{Y}}
. We compute both compositions.

\displaystyle\xi\psi=(i_{X}s_{r}\mathfrak{b}R_{Y})^{\sim}\circ({i_{Y}s_{l}\mathfrak{a}R_{X}})^{\sim}\simeq(i_{X}s_{r}\mathfrak{b}R_{Y}i_{Y}s_{l}\mathfrak{a}R_{X})^{\sim}\simeq(i_{X}s_{r}\mathfrak{b}s_{l}\mathfrak{a}R_{X})^{\sim}

\displaystyle\simeq(i_{X}s_{r}\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}R_{X})^{\sim}\simeq(i_{X}s_{r}R_{X})^{\sim}\simeq\id_{M_{X}}

Note that in this computation, we fix
l
, and can choose
r
large enough so that all the homotopies exist.

The computation for
\psi\xi
is exactly the same, this time fixing
r
and choosing a sufficiently large
l
. Therefore
M_{X}
and
M_{Y}
are properly homotopy equivalent. So
W_{X}\setminus X
and
W_{Y}\setminus Y
are also proper homotopy equivalent. The converse follows from the fact that
\mathcal{F}
is a functor so sends equivalences to equivalences.
∎

For the purpose of studying coarse homology theories, Bunke and Engel define a universal coarsification functor
\mathbf{P}:\mathbf{BornCoarse}\rightarrow\mathbf{Sp}\mathcal{B}
(Definition
5.1
in
[
9
]
), where
\mathbf{Sp}\mathcal{B}
denotes the stable
\infty
-category of closed motivic uniform bornological coarse spectra (Definition
4.14
in
[
9
]
).
\mathbf{P}
is u-continuous and invariant under coarse homotopies. Therefore, it is well-defined on
\mathbf{hCoarse}
. The composition

\displaystyle\mathbf{StrongShape}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{F}}\mathbf{hCoarse}\xrightarrow{\mathbf{P}}\mathbf{Sp}\mathcal{B}

is
\mathbf{Sp}\mathcal{B}
-valued local homology theory.

We now compare our
\mathcal{F}
to the universal cone functor
\mathcal{O}:\mathbf{UBC}\rightarrow\mathbf{BornCoarse}
, which takes a uniform, bornological coarse space
X
to its cone
\mathcal{O}(X)
and a uniformly continuous map
f:X\rightarrow Y
to the cone of
f
which is defined set-wise as
f\times\id
.
\mathcal{O}
restricts to the subcategory
\mathbf{UC}
, of uniform coarse spaces with bornology induced by the coarse structure, and descends to a well-defined functor
\mathcal{O}:\mathbf{hUC}\rightarrow\mathbf{hCoarse}
. However, this functor is not full. We have discovered that there are many more morphisms between
\mathcal{O}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(Y)
(coming from shape maps) than can be obtained from coning a uniformly continuous map
f:X\rightarrow Y
, even when
X,Y
are compact metric spaces.

Part III
Proper metric spaces

We wish to prove and generalise the following theorems in
[
3
]
:

Theorem 8.8
.

(Theorem
7.4
in
[
3
]
) Let
X
be a proper geodesic metric space. There exists a natural surjection
\pi^{c}_{0}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{E}nds(X)
.

Theorem 8.9
.

(Theorem
9.9
in
[
3
]
) Let
X
be a locally finite geometric tree. There exists a natural isomorphism
\pi^{c}_{0}(X)\cong\mathcal{E}nds(X)
.

9
\varprojlim^{1}
sequence

9.1
End homotopy groups

Let
X
be a connected proper metric space. We show
\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)\cong\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})
.

Recall that for
C>0
, a subset
Z\subset X
is
C
-separated if
d(x,y)>C
for all
x,y\in Z
. By Zorn’s lemma, there is a maximal
C
-separated subset for all
C
. Observe that if
X
is a simplicial complex equipped with the spherical metric, scaled so that edges have length
1
, the set of vertices
Z_{0}
of
X
form a maximal
\frac{1}{2}
-separated subset, and the open cover
\cup_{z\in Z_{0}}B(z,1)
corresponds to the open cover by stars
\cup_{z\in Z_{0}}\langle st(z)\rangle
.

Consider
X
a connected proper metric space. Let
\{Z_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a maximal
\frac{2^{h}}{2}
-separated subset, and take the open cover
\mathcal{U}_{h}=\cup_{z\in Z_{h}}B(z,2^{h})
. This open cover is locally finite, since
X
is proper: assume for a contradiction that there was a point
z\in Z_{h}
such that
B(z,2^{h})\cap B(w,2^{h})\neq\emptyset
for infinitely many
w
. Since
B(w,2^{h})\subset B(z,2^{h+2})
for all
w
and the closure of
B(z,2^{h+2})
is compact, we can construct a convergent sequence
\{w_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
. But this cannot possibly be Cauchy, since
Z_{h}
is a
\frac{2^{h}}{2}
-separated set. Let
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
denote the geometric realisation of
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
. We equip each simplex in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
with the spherical metric, scaled so that edges have length
1
, and take the induced path metric.
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
is a connected, proper geodesic metric space. As a CW complex, it is strongly locally finite (since it is regular and locally finite). There are proper, simplicial maps
\phi_{h,h+1}:|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|
induced by the refinement of open covers.

Let
\rho_{h}=\{\rho_{U}\}_{U\in\mathcal{U}_{h}}
be a partition of unity subordinate to the cover
\mathcal{U}_{h}
. Define
i_{h}:X\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
by sending a point
x\in X
to its image
\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}_{h}}\rho_{U}(x)[U]
in barycentric co-ordinates. Define
R_{h}:|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\rightarrow X
by sending vertices
[B(z,2^{h})]
to the point
z
, and by sending interiors of simplices
[B(z_{0},2^{h}),\dots,B(z_{m},2^{h})]
to a point contained in the intersection
\cap_{i=0}^{m}B(z_{i},2^{h})
.

Notation
.

From now on we identify points
z\in Z_{h}
with the vertices
[B(z,2^{h})]
in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
X
be a connected proper metric space and let
h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

1.

The map
R_{h}:|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\rightarrow X
is coarse.

2.

The map
i_{h}:X\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
is proper.

3.

The compositions
R_{h}\circ i_{h}:X\rightarrow X
and
i_{h}\circ R_{h}:|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
are close to the identity.

4.

\phi_{h,h+1}i_{h}
is close to
i_{h+1}
and
R_{h+1}\phi_{h,h+1}
is close to
R_{h}
.

Proof.

1.

The map
R_{h}:|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\rightarrow X
is controlled: consider the entourage

\displaystyle C_{h}:=\{(x,y)\in|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\,|\,x,y\text{ lie in a common simplex }\}\subset D_{1}

The coarse structure on
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
is generated by
C_{h}
. We repeat the argument from the Appendix, for emphasis, on why the spherical metric is important.

Lemma
.

(Lemma
13.16
) Let
Z
be a locally finite simplicial complex with a uniform spherical metric. Then the distance from a vertex
v
of
Z
to a simplex
\sigma
in the same component of
Z
is
\frac{\pi}{2}
times the length (ie. number of edges) of the shortest simplicial path from
v
to
\sigma
. In other words there are no shortcuts through the interior of a simplex.

We apply this lemma to the simplicial complex
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
. Our simplices are scaled so
\pi/2
is replaced with
1
. Let
x,y
be two points in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
with distance at most
S\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
x
be contained in the interior of
\tau_{1}
and
y
be contained in the interior of
\tau_{2}
. Let
x^{\prime}
be the closest vertex in
\tau_{1}
to
x
, and
y^{\prime}
the closest vertex in
\tau_{2}
to
y
. We have that

\displaystyle d(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\leq d(y^{\prime},x)+d(x,x^{\prime})\leq d(x,y)+d(y,y^{\prime})+d(x,x^{\prime})\leq S+1

d(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
is realised by a vertex path of distance at most
S+1
. Therefore, it travels through at most
S+1
simplices in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
. This shows there is a path between
x
and
y
which can be split up into
S+3
components, each lying within a single simplex. We conclude that
D_{S}\subset C^{\circ(S+3)}_{h}
.

Let
x,y\in|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
be points lying in a common simplex
\sigma=[z_{0},\dots,z_{m}]
.
R_{h}(x),R_{h}(y)
are contained in the union
\cup_{i=0}^{m}B(z_{i},2^{h})
, which is a set of diameter
2^{h+2}
, since the intersection
\cap_{i=0}^{m}B(z_{i},2^{h})
is non-empty. This shows that
R_{h}
is controlled.

Let
K\subset X
be a bounded set. Since
X
is proper, there exist
z_{0},\dots,z_{m}\in Z_{h}
such that
K\subset\cup_{i=0}^{m}B(z_{i},2^{h})
. We have that
R^{-1}_{h}(\cup_{i=0}^{m}B(z_{i},2^{h}))\subset\cup_{i=0}^{m}|st(z_{i})|
: for a point
x\notin\cup_{i=0}^{m}|st(z_{i})|
, consider the unique simplex
\tau=[y_{0},\dots,y_{n}]
such that
x\in int(\tau)
. We have that
R_{h}(x)\subset\cap_{j=0}^{n}B(y_{i},2^{h})
. If there is an
i
such that
R_{h}(x)\in B(z_{i},2^{h})
, then
[y_{0},\dots,y_{n},z_{i}]
is a simplex in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
, contradiction. The set
\cup_{i=0}^{m}|st(z_{i})|
has diameter
\leq\max_{i,j=0,\dots,m}\{d(z_{i},z_{j})\}+2
. This shows that
R_{h}
is proper.

2.

The map
i_{h}:X\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
is proper: the bornology of
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
is generated by the metric balls
B(z,S)
, where
z
is a vertex and
S\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose that
x\in B(z,S)
. There exists a unique simpliex
\sigma_{x}
whose interior contains
x
. The shortest simplicial path between
\sigma_{x}
and
z
consists of at most
S+1
edges. Therefore, there is a sequence of open sets
B(z_{0},2^{h}),\dots,B(z_{m},2^{h})
in
X
where
z_{0}=z
,
i_{h}^{-1}(x)\subset B(z_{m},2^{h})
,
m\leq S+1
, and
B(z_{i},2^{h})\cap B(z_{i+1},2^{h})\neq\emptyset
for all
0\leq i<m
. This means that
i_{h}^{-1}(x)
is at most distance
2^{h+1}(S+1)+2^{h}
from
z\in i_{h}^{-1}B(z,S)
. This shows that
i_{h}
is proper.

3.

The composition
R_{h}\circ i_{h}
is close to the identity: for a point
x\in B(z,2^{h})
we have
R_{h}\circ i_{h}(x)\subset R_{h}(\langle st(z)\rangle)\subset B(z,2^{h})
. The composition
i_{h}\circ R_{h}
is close to the identity. For a point
x\in\langle st(z)\rangle
we have
i_{h}\circ R_{h}(x)\subset i_{h}(B(z,2^{h}))\subset\langle st(z)\rangle
.

4.

\phi_{h,h+1}i_{h}
is close to
i_{h+1}
: let
x\in\cap_{i=0}^{m}B(z_{i},2^{h})\subset\cap_{i=0}^{m}B(\phi_{h,h+1}(z_{i}),2^{h+1})
. We have that
\phi_{h,h+1}i_{h}(x)\in\phi_{h,h+1}\cap_{i=0}^{m}\langle st(z_{i})\rangle\subset\cap_{i=0}^{m}\langle st(\phi_{h,h+1}(z_{i}))\rangle
and
i_{h+1}(x)\in\cap_{i=0}^{m}\langle st(\phi_{h,h+1}(z_{i}))\rangle
.

R_{h+1}\phi_{h,h+1}
is close to
R_{h}
: for
x\in int(\sigma)=[z_{0},\dots,z_{m}]
we have
R_{h+1}\phi_{h,h+1}(x)\in\cap_{i=0}^{m}B(\phi_{h,h+1}(z_{i}),2^{h+1})
and
R_{h}(x)\in\cap_{i=0}^{m}B(z_{i},2^{h})\subset\cap_{i=0}^{m}B(\phi_{h,h+1}(z_{i}),2^{h+1})
. The set
\cap_{i=0}^{m}B(\phi_{h,h+1}(z_{i}),2^{h+1})
has a diameter bounded by
2^{h+2}
.

∎

In the case that
X
is geodesic, we have the following additional properties.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
X
be a connected proper geodesic metric space. Let
h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
i_{h}
is a coarse equivalence (quasi-isometry) with inverse
R_{h}
.

\phi_{h,h+1}
is a quasi-isometry with inverse
i_{h}\circ R_{h+1}

Proof.

It suffices to prove that
i_{h}
is controlled. Since
X
is a path metric space, its coarse structure is generated by one entourage
D_{1}:=\{(x,y)\in X\,|\,d(x,y)<\frac{1}{2}\}
. Let
(x,y)\in D_{1}
. There exists a
z\in Z_{h}
such that
x,y\in B(z,2^{h})
. Therefore
i_{h}(x),i_{h}(y)\in\langle st(z)\rangle
, which is a set of diameter
2
.

To show that
\phi_{h,h+1}
is a quasi-isometry, we have

\displaystyle i_{h}R_{h+1}\phi_{h,h+1}\simeq_{c}i_{h}R_{h+1}\phi_{h,h+1}i_{h}R_{h}\simeq_{c}i_{h}R_{h+1}i_{h+1}R_{h}\simeq_{c}i_{h}R_{h}\simeq_{c}\id_{X}

\displaystyle\phi_{h,h+1}i_{h}R_{h+1}\simeq_{c}i_{h+1}R_{h+1}\simeq_{c}\id_{|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|}

The symbol
\simeq_{c}
here means that two maps are close.
∎

Before proving Theorem
9.3
, let us deal with some technicalities related to the base ray
\omega
. Give
[1,\infty)
the simplicial structure with edges of length
1
. Since
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow X
is coarse, there exists a
h_{0}
large enough such that
\omega[n-1,n+1]
has diameter less than
\frac{2^{h_{0}}}{2}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
h\geq h_{0}
. We have that
i_{h}\omega[n-1,n+1]\subset\langle st(z_{n,h})\rangle
where
z_{n,h}
denotes the closest vertex in
Z_{h}
to the point
\omega(n)
(if there are multiple, choose one arbitrarily). The star condition for
i_{h}\omega
is met and therefore the vertex map
\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}(n):=z_{n,h}
can be extended to a simplicial approximation of
i_{h}\omega
. We fix these simplicial approximations and denote them by
\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}
.

Assume for the rest of this section that
h\geq h_{0}
. The proper, simplicial (Lipschitz) maps
\phi_{h,h+1}:|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|
induce morphisms
(\phi_{h,h+1})_{\ast}:\pi_{n}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|,\phi_{h,h+1}\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})
. Since
\phi_{h,h+1}\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}
is a simplicial approximation of
i_{h+1}\omega
, there is a proper Lipschitz homotopy
J:c[0,1]\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|
between them. We define the bonding maps
\psi_{h,h+1}:\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|,\widetilde{i_{h+1}\omega})
as the composition
b^{J}_{\phi_{h,h+1}\widetilde{i_{h}\omega},\widetilde{i_{h+1}\omega}}(\phi_{h,h+1})_{\ast}
where
b^{J}_{\phi_{h,h+1}\widetilde{i_{h}\omega},\widetilde{i_{h+1}\omega}}
is the change of base ray homomorphism. So
\{\pi_{n}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}),\psi_{h,h+1};\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}\}
is a directed system with direct limit
\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})
. Since all the bonding maps and change of base ray homomorphisms are Lipschitz,
\{\pi_{n}^{L,e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}),\psi_{h,h+1};\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}\}
is also a directed system of groups with direct limit
\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi^{L,e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})
.

Theorem 9.3
.

Let
X
be a connected proper metric space,
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow X
a coarse base ray. There is an isomorphism

\displaystyle\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)\cong\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi^{L,e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})

Proof.

Let
[f]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)
. There exists a
S>0
such that
f\langle st(v)\rangle
has diameter
<S
for all vertices
v\in c[0,1]^{n}
. We choose
h
so that
\frac{2^{h}}{2}>S
. Then for every vertex
v
there exists a
z_{v}\in Z_{h}
such that
f\langle st(v)\rangle\subset B(z_{v},2^{h})
. Therefore
i_{h}f\langle st(v)\rangle\subset\langle st(z_{v})\rangle
and the vertex map
\widetilde{i_{h}f}(v)=z_{v}
can be extended linearly to obtain a simplicial approximation of
i_{h}f
. We can choose
\widetilde{i_{h}f}
such that it restricts to
\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}
on
c\partial[0,1]^{n}
. We define

\displaystyle\theta:\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi^{L,e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})

\displaystyle[f]

\displaystyle\mapsto[\widetilde{i_{h}f}]

Any two choices of simplicial approximation
\widetilde{i_{h}f}
are properly homotopic by a linear, Lipschitz homotopy.
\theta
respects the bonding maps
\psi_{h,h+1}
since
\phi_{h,h+1}\widetilde{i_{h}f}
and
\widetilde{i_{h+1}f}
are both simplicial approximations of the map
i_{h+1}f
: there exists a coarse-Lipschitz homotopy
G:c([0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|
such that
G_{|c([0,1]^{n}\times\{0\})}=\phi_{h,h+1}\widetilde{i_{h}f}
,
G_{|c([0,1]^{n}\times\{1\})}=\widetilde{i_{h+1}f}
and
G_{|c([0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])}=J
. Therefore
\psi_{h,h+1}[\widetilde{i_{h}f}]=b^{J}_{\phi_{h,h+1}\widetilde{i_{h}\omega},\widetilde{i_{h+1}\omega}}\phi_{h,h+1}[\widetilde{i_{h}f}]=[\widetilde{i_{h+1}f}]
.

Let
[f]=[g]
. There exists a coarse homotopy
H:c([0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])\rightarrow X
such that
c([0,1]^{n}\times\{0\})=f
,
([0,1]^{n}\times\{1\})=g
and
c(\partial[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])=\omega
. There exists a
h
large enough such that
i_{h}H
has a simplicial approximation. The homotopy
\widetilde{i_{h}H}
is relative to the base ray
\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}
and restricts to simplicial approximations of
i_{h}f
and
i_{h}g
on
c([0,1]^{n}\times\{0\})
and
c([0,1]^{n}\times\{1\})
respectively. We obtain
[\widetilde{i_{h}f}]=[\widetilde{i_{h}g}]
. This shows that
\theta
is well-defined.

We define the inverse
\lambda:\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi^{L,e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)
as follows. Let
[f]\in\pi^{L,e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})
. Observe that
R_{h}\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}
is close to
\omega
. We let
\lambda_{h}[f]=[(R_{h}f)^{\prime}]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)
where
(R_{h}f)^{\prime}
denotes the map
R_{h}f
with the boundary replaced by
\omega
. We have that
\lambda_{h+1}\psi_{h,h+1}[f]=\lambda_{h}[f]
because of the following:

\displaystyle(R_{h+1}(b_{\phi_{h,h+1}\widetilde{i_{h}\omega},\widetilde{i_{h+1}\omega}}\phi_{h,h+1}f))^{\prime}\simeq_{c}b^{\id}_{\omega,\omega}(R_{h}f)^{\prime}\simeq(R_{h}f)^{\prime}

where
\simeq
denotes coarsely homotopic. The first homotopy comes from the fact that
R_{h+1}\phi_{h,h+1}
is close to
R_{h}
,
R_{h}i_{h}
is close to
\id
, and
\phi_{h,h+1}\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}
is close to
