\widetilde{i_{h+1}\omega}
. Therefore
\lambda
is well-defined. It is a group homomorphism, since each
\lambda_{h}
is a composition of group homomorphisms:

\displaystyle\pi^{L,e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})\xrightarrow{\id_{*}}\pi^{c}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})\xrightarrow{R_{h}}\pi_{n}^{c}(X,R_{h}\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})\xrightarrow{(\cdot)^{\prime}}\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)

We have used here that
R_{h}
is coarse.

The composition
\lambda\circ\theta
: let
[f]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)
. We have that
\lambda\theta[f]=[(R_{h}\widetilde{i_{h}f})^{\prime}]
for some
h\geq h_{0}
. The map
R_{h}\widetilde{i_{h}f}
is close to
f
. Therefore
\lambda\circ\theta=\id
.

The composition
\theta\circ\lambda
: let
[f]\in\pi_{n}^{L,e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})
. Since the simplicial structure on
c[0,1]^{n}
is uniformly bounded, there exists a small enough barycentric subdivision
bs^{k}(c[0,1]^{n})
relative to the boundary
c\partial[0,1]^{n}
, and a Lipschitz map
\mathfrak{h}:c[0,1]^{n}\rightarrow c[0,1]^{n}
such that
f\mathfrak{h}
has a simplicial approximation, which we denote by
bs^{k}(\widetilde{f\mathfrak{h}})
.
f
is coarse-Lipschitz homotopic to
bs^{k}(\widetilde{f\mathfrak{h}})
relative to
c\partial[0,1]^{n}
.
\theta\circ\lambda[f]
is represented by the map

\displaystyle bs^{k}(i_{h}{(R_{h}bs^{k}(\widetilde{f\mathfrak{h}}))^{\prime}})^{\sim}

where
bs^{k}(\cdot)^{\sim}
denotes the simplicial approximation with respect to
bs^{k}(c[0,1]^{n})
. The reason why we can use
bs^{k}(c[0,1]^{n})
instead of the fixed simplicial structure on
c[0,1]^{n}
is because the homotopy class of a simplicial approximation is retained under barycentric subdivision relative to a subcomplex.

Observe that for a vertex
v
in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
we have that
i_{h}R_{h}\langle st(v)\rangle\subset\langle st(v)\rangle
. Therefore

bs^{k}(i_{h}{(R_{h}bs^{k}(\widetilde{f\mathfrak{h}}))^{\prime}})^{\sim}
is a simplicial approximation of
bs^{k}(\widetilde{f\mathfrak{h}})
. This shows that
\theta\circ\lambda=\id
. We conclude that
\theta
is a group isomorphism with inverse
\lambda
.

∎

Lemma 9.4
.

The identity induces an isomorphism

\displaystyle\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi^{L,e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})\cong\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})

Proof.

The forget map
\mathfrak{d}_{h}:\pi_{n}^{L,e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})
is an isomorphism for every
h\geq h_{0}
. The proof is analogous to Proposition
3.25
. The only additional points in the argument are as follows. To show surjectivity of
\mathfrak{d}_{h}
, for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the image of
c_{[2^{k},2^{k+1}]}[0,1]^{n}
under
f
lies in a finite subcomplex of
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
, so the ordinary relative simplicial approximation theorem (Theorem
13.4
) applies. To show that the homotopy
H
is proper, we consider a finite filtration
\{L_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
of
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
. Consider the double neighbourhood
N^{2}(L_{j}):=N(N(L_{j},|\mathcal{U}_{h}|),|\mathcal{U}_{h}|)
of
L_{j}
. Since
f
is proper, for every
j
, there exists a
l_{j}
such that
f(c_{2^{l_{j}}}[0,1]^{n})\subset(N^{2}(L_{j}))^{c}
. Therefore
H^{-1}(L_{j})\subset c_{[1,2^{l_{j}}]}([0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. For injectivity, we only need the fact that the cover of
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
by open stars has Lebesgue number
\frac{1}{4}
. Since
\mathfrak{d}_{h}
is induced by the identity map, it commutes with the bonding morphisms.
∎

Lemma 9.5
.

There is an isomorphism of directed systems

\displaystyle\{\pi_{n}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\phi_{h_{0},h}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega}),(\phi_{h,h+1})_{\ast};\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}\}\cong\{\pi_{n}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}),\psi_{h,h+1};\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}\}

Proof.

Let
\phi_{h_{0},h}:=\phi_{h-1,h}\circ\dots\circ\phi_{h_{0},h_{0}+1}
. It suffices to show that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\phi_{h_{0},h}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|,\phi_{h_{0},h+1}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|,\widetilde{i_{h+1}\omega})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{h,h+1})_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{H^{h}}_{\phi_{h_{0},h}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega},\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{H^{h+1}}_{\phi_{h_{0},h+1}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega},\widetilde{i_{h+1}\omega}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{J^{h,h+1}}_{\phi_{h,h+1}\widetilde{i_{h}\omega},\widetilde{i_{h+1}\omega}}(\phi_{h,h+1})_{\ast}}

commutes for all
h\geq h_{0}
. The homotopies
H^{h},H^{h+1},J^{h,h+1}
are obtained from the fact that two simplicial approximations are linearly homotopic.

To that end, it suffices to show that there exists a homotopy
G:[0,1]^{2}\times[1,\infty)\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|
such that

\displaystyle G_{|[0,1]\times\{0\}\times[1,\infty)}

\displaystyle=b^{H^{h+1}}_{\phi_{h_{0},h+1}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega},\widetilde{i_{h+1}\omega}}

\displaystyle G_{|[0,1]\times\{1\}\times[1,\infty)}

\displaystyle=b^{J^{h,h+1}}_{\phi_{h,h+1}\widetilde{i_{h}\omega},\widetilde{i_{h+1}\omega}}b^{\phi_{h,h+1}H^{h}}_{\phi_{h,h+1}\phi_{h_{0},h}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega},\phi_{h,h+1}\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}}

We give
[0,1]^{2}\times[1,\infty)
the following simplicial structure. The first copy of
[0,1]
has vertices
\{0,\frac{1}{2},1\}
(and two edges). The second copy of
[0,1]
has vertices
\{0,1\}
, and
[1,\infty)
has vertex set
\mathbb{N}
. Equip
[0,1]^{2}\times[1,\infty)
with the product simplicial structure. Let
\overline{G}:[0,1]^{2}\times[1,\infty)
be defined on vertices as

\displaystyle\overline{G}(0,0,m)

\displaystyle=\overline{G}(\frac{1}{2},0,m)=\overline{G}(0,1,m)=\phi_{h_{0},h+1}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega}(m)

\displaystyle\overline{G}(1,0,m)

\displaystyle=\overline{G}(1,1,m)=\widetilde{i_{h+1}\omega}(m)

\displaystyle\overline{G}(\frac{1}{2},1,m)

\displaystyle=\phi_{h,h+1}\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}(m)

for
m\in\mathbb{N}
. See Figure
46
.

Figure 46:
The map
\overline{G}
.

Observe that
\overline{G}
can be extended linearly to a simplicial map, which is a simplicial approximation of the composition

\displaystyle[0,1]^{2}\times[1,\infty)\rightarrow\{\ast\}\times[1,\infty)\xrightarrow{i_{h+1}\omega}|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|

Additionally, since the map
\phi_{h,h+1}
is simplicial,
\overline{G}
satisfies

\displaystyle\overline{G}_{|[0,\frac{1}{2}]\times\{0\}\times[1,\infty)}

\displaystyle=\id_{\phi_{h_{0},h+1}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega}}

\displaystyle\overline{G}_{|[\frac{1}{2},1]\times\{0\}\times[1,\infty)}

\displaystyle=H^{h+1}

\displaystyle\overline{G}_{|[0,\frac{1}{2}]\times\{1\}\times[1,\infty)}

\displaystyle=\phi_{h,h+1}H^{h}

\displaystyle\overline{G}_{|[\frac{1}{2},1]\times\{1\}\times[1,\infty)}

\displaystyle=J^{h,h+1}

\displaystyle\overline{G}_{|\{0\}\times[0,1]\times[1,\infty)}

\displaystyle=\id_{\phi_{h_{0},h+1}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega}}

\displaystyle\overline{G}_{|\{1\}\times[0,1]\times[1,\infty)}

\displaystyle=\id_{\widetilde{i_{h+1}\omega}}

Since
\id_{\phi_{h_{0},h+1}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega}}\ast H^{h+1}\simeq H^{h+1}
, we can rescale
\overline{G}
to obtain the desired homotopy
G
. Therefore, the diagram commutes.
∎

Assembling together Theorem
9.3
, Lemma
9.4
, and Lemma
9.5
we obtain

Theorem 9.6
.

There is a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle\Theta:\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\phi_{h_{0},h}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega})

\displaystyle[f]

\displaystyle\mapsto b_{\widetilde{i_{h}\omega},\phi_{h_{0},h}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega}}[\widetilde{i_{h}f}]

Proof.

It only remains to show that this isomorphism is natural. Let
Y
be a connected, proper metric space with associated sequence of open covers
\{\mathcal{V}_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. Let
\mathfrak{a}:(X,\omega)\rightarrow(Y,\mathfrak{a}\omega)
be a coarse map. Let
h^{\prime}_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
be large enough so that
\widetilde{i_{h^{\prime}_{0}}\mathfrak{a}\omega}
exists. We define a group homomorphism
\xi:\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})\rightarrow\varinjlim_{h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h^{\prime}_{0}}}\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{V}_{h^{\prime}}|,\widetilde{i_{h^{\prime}}\mathfrak{a}\omega})
which is induced by proper maps
\xi_{h,\rho(h)}:|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\rightarrow|\mathcal{V}_{\rho(h)}|
for an increasing function
\rho:\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{\geq h^{\prime}_{0}}
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{c}(Y,\mathfrak{a}\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h^{\prime}_{0}}}\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{V}_{h^{\prime}}|,\widetilde{i_{h^{\prime}}\mathfrak{a}\omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\phi_{h_{0},h}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h^{\prime}_{0}}}\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{V}_{h^{\prime}}|,\phi^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}_{0},h}\widetilde{i_{h^{\prime}_{0}}\mathfrak{a}\omega})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime-1}\xi b}

commutes.

Let
h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}
. Recall that for a vertex
v\in|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
, the set
R_{h}\langle st(v)\rangle\subset B(v,2^{h})
has diameter
<2^{h+1}
. Since
\mathfrak{a}
is coarse we have
d_{X}(x,y)<2^{h+1}\implies d_{Y}(\mathfrak{a}(x),\mathfrak{a}(y))<S_{h}
. We choose
\rho(h)\geq h^{\prime}_{0}
large enough such that
\frac{2^{\rho(h)}}{2}>S_{h}
. Therefore
i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}R_{h}
has a simplicial approximation. Additionally, this simplicial approximation can be chosen to send
\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}
to
\widetilde{i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}\omega}
: recall that
\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}(n)=z_{n}
corresponds to the closest
z_{n}\in Z_{h}
to
\omega(n)
. By construction, a valid simplicial approximation of
i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}R_{h}
sends the vertex
z_{n}\in|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
to the closest vertex
w_{y}\in Z^{\prime}_{\rho(h)}
to any point
y\in\mathfrak{a}B(z_{n},2^{h})
. Obviously the point
y=\mathfrak{a}\omega(n)
satisfies this property. Therefore, we can define
\xi_{h,\rho(h)}:(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})\rightarrow(|\mathcal{V}_{\rho(h)}|,\widetilde{i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}\omega})
as any choice of base ray-preserving simplicial approximation
(i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}R_{h})^{\sim}
. We can assume that
\rho:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
is a strictly increasing sequence.

We show now that the sequence of maps
\{\xi_{h,\rho(h)}\}_{h\geq h_{0}}
induces a map on the colimit. It suffices to show the commutativity of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{V}_{\rho(h)}|,\widetilde{i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}\omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h+1}\omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{V}_{\rho(h+1)}|,\widetilde{i_{\rho(h+1)}\mathfrak{a}\omega})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{h,\rho(h)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{h,h+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}_{\rho(h),\rho(h+1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{h+1,\rho(h+1)}}

We first show that the composition of bonding maps
\psi^{\prime}_{\rho(h),\rho(h+1)}:=\psi^{\prime}_{\rho(h+1)-1,\rho(h+1)}\circ\dots\circ\psi^{\prime}_{\rho(h),\rho(h)+1}
is the same as
b_{\phi^{\prime}_{\rho(h),\rho(h+1)}\widetilde{i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}\omega},\widetilde{i_{\rho(h+1)}\mathfrak{a}\omega}}(\phi^{\prime}_{\rho(h),\rho(h+1)})_{*}
. We prove this by induction. Assume that for
j\leq k
the composition of bonding maps
\psi^{\prime}_{j,k}
is the same as
b_{\phi^{\prime}_{j,k}\widetilde{i_{j}\mathfrak{a}\omega},\widetilde{i_{k}\mathfrak{a}\omega}}(\phi^{\prime}_{j,k})_{*}
. Let
[f]\in\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{V}_{j}|,\widetilde{i_{j}\mathfrak{a}\omega})
. The class
\psi^{\prime}_{j,k+1}[f]=\psi^{\prime}_{k,k+1}\circ\psi^{\prime}_{j,k}[f]
is

\displaystyle b_{\phi^{\prime}_{k,k+1}\widetilde{i_{k}\mathfrak{a}\omega},\widetilde{i_{k+1}\mathfrak{a}\omega}}\circ b_{\phi^{\prime}_{k,k+1}\phi^{\prime}_{j,k}\widetilde{i_{j}\mathfrak{a}\omega},\phi^{\prime}_{k,k+1}\widetilde{i_{k}\mathfrak{a}\omega}}[\phi^{\prime}_{k,k+1}\phi^{\prime}_{j,k}f]

since all maps
\phi^{\prime}_{k,k+1}\widetilde{i_{k}\mathfrak{a}\omega},\widetilde{i_{k+1}\mathfrak{a}\omega},\phi^{\prime}_{k,k+1}\phi^{\prime}_{j,k}\widetilde{i_{j}\mathfrak{a}\omega}
are simplicial approximations of
i_{k+1}\mathfrak{a}\omega
, there is a linear homotopy

\displaystyle b_{\phi^{\prime}_{k,k+1}\widetilde{i_{k}a\omega},\widetilde{i_{k+1}\mathfrak{a}\omega}}\circ b_{\phi^{\prime}_{k,k+1}\phi^{\prime}_{j,k}\widetilde{i_{j}\mathfrak{a}\omega},\phi^{\prime}_{k,k+1}\widetilde{i_{k}\mathfrak{a}\omega}}\simeq b_{\phi^{\prime}_{j,k+1}\widetilde{i_{j}\mathfrak{a}\omega},\widetilde{i_{k+1}\mathfrak{a}\omega}}

Therefore
\psi^{\prime}_{j,k+1}[f]=b_{\phi^{\prime}_{j,k+1}\widetilde{i_{j}\mathfrak{a}\omega},\widetilde{i_{k+1}\mathfrak{a}\omega}}(\phi^{\prime}_{j,k+1})_{*}[f]
, which proves the claim.

Let
[f]\in\pi_{n}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})
. The composition
\psi^{\prime}_{\rho(h),\rho(h+1)}\xi_{h,\rho(h)}[f]
is given by

\displaystyle b_{\phi_{\rho(h),\rho(h+1)}(i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}\omega)^{\sim},(i_{\rho(h+1)}\mathfrak{a}\omega)^{\sim}}[\phi_{\rho(h),\rho(h+1)}(i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}R_{h})^{\sim}f]

The composition
\xi_{h+1,\rho(h+1)}\psi_{h,h+1}[f]
is given by

\displaystyle b_{(i_{\rho(h+1)}\mathfrak{a}R_{h+1})^{\sim}\phi_{h,h+1}\widetilde{i_{h}\omega},(i_{\rho(h+1)}\mathfrak{a}\omega)^{\sim}}[(i_{\rho(h+1)}\mathfrak{a}R_{h+1})^{\sim}\phi_{h,h+1}f]

Let
\sigma=[z_{0},\dots,z_{m}]
be a simplex in
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
. We have that

\displaystyle\phi_{\rho(h),\rho(h+1)}(i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}R_{h})^{\sim}(z_{j})=\phi_{\rho(h),\rho(h+1)}(w_{j})=y_{j}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}B(z_{j},2^{h})\subset B(w_{j},2^{\rho(h)})\subset B(y_{j},2^{\rho(h+1)})

for
0\leq j\leq m
. Similarly, we have

\displaystyle(i_{\rho(h+1)}\mathfrak{a}R_{h+1})^{\sim}\phi_{h,h+1}(z_{j})=y^{\prime}_{j}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}B(\phi_{h,h+1}(z_{j}),2^{h+1})\subset B(y^{\prime}_{j},2^{\rho(h+1)})

By definition, the intersection
\cap_{j}B(z_{j},2^{h})\neq\emptyset
. Since
\cap_{j}B(z_{j},2^{h})\subset\cap_{j}B(\phi_{h,h+1}(z_{j}),2^{h+1})
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}(\cap_{j}B(z_{j},2^{h}))\subset\bigcap_{j}B(y_{j},2^{\rho(h+1)})\cap B(y^{\prime}_{j},2^{\rho(h+1)})

is non-empty. Therefore the maps
\phi_{\rho(h),\rho(h+1)}(i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}R_{h})^{\sim}
and
(i_{\rho(h+1)}\mathfrak{a}R_{h+1})^{\sim}\phi_{h,h+1}
are linearly homotopic by a homotopy
H:|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times[0,1]\rightarrow|\mathcal{V}_{\rho(h+1)}|
. The restriction of
H\circ f
to the set
c\partial[0,1]^{n}\times[0,1]
is a linear homotopy

\displaystyle\phi_{\rho(h),\rho(h+1)}(i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}\omega)^{\sim}\simeq(i_{\rho(h+1)}\mathfrak{a}R_{h+1})^{\sim}\phi_{h,h+1}\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}

For the base ray, recall that
\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}(n)=z_{n}
for
z_{n}
the closest vertex to
\omega(n)
. Consider a simplex
[n,n+1]\subset[1,\infty)
. We have that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}\omega(n,n+1)\subset\mathfrak{a}(\cap_{j}B(z_{j},2^{h}))\subset\bigcap_{j}(B(y_{j},2^{\rho(h+1)})\cap B(y^{\prime}_{j},2^{\rho(h+1)})\cap B(y^{\prime\prime}_{j},2^{\rho(h+1)}))

for
j=n,n+1
, where
(i_{\rho(h+1)}\mathfrak{a}\omega)^{\sim}(j)=y^{\prime\prime}_{j}
. This shows that there is a linear homotopy between the two change of base rays that restricts to
H\circ f
on the set
\partial U
. This shows that
\psi^{\prime}_{\rho(h),\rho(h+1)}\xi_{h,\rho(h)}=\xi_{h+1,\rho(h+1)}\psi_{h,h+1}
so
\xi
is well-defined.

To show that the top square commutes, suppose
[f]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)
. By construction
\theta[f]
is represented by
\widetilde{i_{h}f}
for a
h
large enough so that
f\langle st(v)\rangle\subset B(z_{v},2^{h})
for all vertices
v\in c[0,1]^{n}
. Since
R_{h}i_{h}B(z_{v},2^{h})\subset B(z_{v},2^{h})
, we have that

i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}R_{h}i_{h}f
has the same simplicial approximation as
i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}f
. We conclude that

\displaystyle(i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}R_{h})^{\sim}(i_{h}f)^{\sim}=(i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}R_{h}i_{h}f)^{\sim}=(i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}f)^{\sim}

This shows that
\theta[\mathfrak{a}f]=[\widetilde{i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}f}]=\xi_{h,\rho(h)}\theta[f]=\xi\theta[f]
.

Let us explain what the bottom square means. Recall that we have a sequence of morphisms
b_{h}:=b_{\phi_{h_{0},h}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega},\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}}:\pi_{n}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\phi_{h_{0},h}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega})\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})
(resp.
b^{\prime}_{h}
) which commute with the bonding maps so passes to a map on the colimit, which we denote by
b
(resp.
b^{\prime}
). The map
b^{\prime-1}\xi b
is defined levelwise as

\displaystyle(b^{\prime-1}\xi b)_{h}:=b^{\prime}_{\rho(h)}\xi_{h,\rho(h)}b_{h}=(b_{\phi^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}_{0},\rho(h)}\widetilde{i_{h^{\prime}_{0}}\mathfrak{a}\omega},\widetilde{i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}\omega}})^{-1}\xi_{h,\rho(h)}b_{\phi_{h_{0},h}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega},\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}}

A small 3D-diagram chase shows that
b^{\prime-1}\xi b
passes to the colimit. We have

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}_{\rho(h),\rho(k)}(b^{\prime-1}\xi b)_{h}=\phi^{\prime}_{\rho(h),\rho(k)}(b^{\prime}_{\rho(h)})^{-1}\xi_{h,\rho(h)}b_{h}=(b^{\prime}_{\rho(k)})^{-1}\psi^{\prime}_{\rho(h),\rho(k)}\xi_{h,\rho(h)}b_{h}

\displaystyle=(b^{\prime}_{\rho(k)})^{-1}\xi_{k,\rho(k)}\psi_{h,k}b_{h}=(b^{\prime}_{\rho(k)})^{-1}\xi_{k,\rho(k)}b_{k}\phi_{h,k}=(b^{\prime-1}\xi b)_{k}\phi_{h,k}

for
k\geq h
. The bottom square commutes by construction:

\displaystyle(b^{\prime-1}\xi b)_{h}(b_{h})^{-1}=(b^{\prime}_{\rho(h)})^{-1}\xi_{h,\rho(h)}b_{h}(b_{h})^{-1}=(b^{\prime}_{\rho(h)})^{-1}\xi_{h,\rho(h)}

∎

9.2
\pi_{0}
case

Let
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow X
be the fixed base ray from the previous subsection. We consider the pointed set
\pi_{0}^{c}(X,\omega)
of coarse homotopy classes of coarse base rays with base point
[\omega]
.

Let
h\geq h_{0}
. The proper, simplicial (Lipschitz) maps
\phi_{h,h+1}:|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|
induce morphisms
(\phi_{h,h+1})_{\ast}:\pi_{0}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})\rightarrow\pi_{0}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|,\phi_{h,h+1}\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})=\pi_{0}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|,\widetilde{i_{h+1}\omega})
. So
\{\pi_{0}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}),(\phi_{h,h+1})_{\ast};\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}\}
is a directed system with limit
\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi^{e}_{0}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})
. Since all the bonding maps are Lipschitz and
\phi_{h,h+1}\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}
is coarse-Lipschitz homotpic to
\widetilde{i_{h+1}\omega}
,
\{\pi_{n}^{L,e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}),(\phi_{h,h+1})_{\ast};\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}\}
is also a directed system of pointed sets with direct limit
\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi^{L,e}_{0}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})
.

Theorem 9.7
.

Let
X
be a connected proper metric space. Let
[\omega]\in\pi_{0}^{c}(X)
be fixed. There is an isomorphism of pointed sets

\displaystyle\pi_{0}^{c}(X,\omega)=\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi^{L,e}_{0}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})

Proof.

For
\theta:\pi_{0}^{c}(X,\omega)\rightarrow\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi^{L,e}_{0}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})
, choose the simplicial structure on
[1,\infty)
with vertices at integer heights. Let
[\tau]\in\pi_{0}^{c}(X)
. As before, there exists a
h\geq h_{0}
such that
[\tau]\in\pi_{0}^{c}(X)
has a simplicial approximation
\widetilde{i_{h}\tau}\in\pi_{0}^{L,e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|)
. The independence of choice of simplicial approximation, independence of barycentric subdivision, independence of representative in the coarse homotopy class of
\tau
, and commutativity with the bonding maps follow by previous discussion.

For the definition of
\lambda
, let
[\tau]\in\pi^{L,e}_{0}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|)
. We let
\lambda_{h}[\tau]=[R_{h}\tau]
. Since
R_{h+1}\phi_{h,h+1}
is close to
R_{h}
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{h+1}(\phi_{h,h+1})_{\ast}[\tau]=[R_{h+1}\phi_{h,h+1}\tau]=[R_{h}\tau]=\lambda_{h}[\tau]

Therefore
\lambda:=\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\lambda_{h}
is well-defined.

\lambda\circ\theta[\tau]
is represented by
R_{h}\widetilde{i_{h}\tau}
for some
h\geq h_{0}
. We have that
R_{h}\widetilde{i_{h}\tau}\simeq_{c}R_{h}i_{h}\tau\simeq_{c}\tau
. This shows that
\lambda\circ\theta=\id
.

For
\theta\circ\lambda
, let
[\tau]\in\pi_{0}^{L,e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})
. Choose a small enough barycentric subdivision of
[1,\infty)
such that
\tau\langle st(v,bs^{k}[1,\infty))\rangle\subset\langle st(z_{v})\rangle
. Recall that
i_{h}R_{h}\langle st(z_{v})\rangle\subset\langle st(z_{v})\rangle
. We obtain

\displaystyle bs^{k}(\widetilde{i_{h}R_{h}\tau})=bs^{k}(\widetilde{\tau})\simeq\tau

Hence
\theta\circ\lambda=\id
.

∎

Lemma 9.8
.

The identity induces an isomorphism

\displaystyle\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi_{0}^{L,e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})\cong\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi_{0}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})

Proof.

See Proposition
3.25
.
∎

Theorem 9.9
.

There is a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle\theta:\pi_{0}^{c}(X,\omega)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi^{e}_{0}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})

\displaystyle[\tau]

\displaystyle\mapsto[\widetilde{i_{h}\tau}]

Proof.

Let
Y,\mathfrak{a},\{\mathcal{V}_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
and
\xi_{h,\rho(h)}:|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\rightarrow|\mathcal{V}_{\rho(h)}|
be as before. For the well-definedness of
\xi
, observe that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{e}_{0}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{e}_{0}(|\mathcal{V}_{\rho(h)}|,\widetilde{i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}\omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{e}_{0}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h+1}\omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{V}_{\rho(h+1)}|,\widetilde{i_{\rho(h+1)}\mathfrak{a}\omega})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{h,\rho(h)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{h,h+1})_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi^{\prime}_{\rho(h),\rho(h+1)})_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{h+1,\rho(h+1)}}

commutes. This is because for
[\tau]\in\pi_{0}^{e}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})

\displaystyle(\phi_{\rho(h),\rho(h+1)})_{\ast}\xi_{h,\rho(h)}[\tau]=[\phi_{\rho(h),\rho(h+1)}(i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}R_{h})^{\sim}\tau]=[(i_{\rho(h+1)}\mathfrak{a}R_{h+1})^{\sim}\phi_{h,h+1}\tau]

\displaystyle=\xi_{h+1,\rho(h+1)}(\phi_{h,h+1})_{\ast}[\tau]

Additionally, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}^{c}(X,\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}^{c}(Y,\mathfrak{a}\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}}\pi^{e}_{0}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h^{\prime}_{0}}}\pi^{e}_{0}(|\mathcal{V}_{h^{\prime}}|,\widetilde{i_{h^{\prime}}\mathfrak{a}\omega})\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

commutes: for
[\tau]\in\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)
we have

\displaystyle\xi\theta[\tau]=[(i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}R_{h})^{\sim}\widetilde{i_{h}\tau}]=[(i_{\rho(h)}\mathfrak{a}\tau)^{\sim}]=\theta\mathfrak{a}[\tau]

∎

9.3
\varprojlim^{1}
sequence

We prove the following theorem in this subsection:

Theorem 9.10
.

Let
X
be a connected proper metric space and
[\omega]\in\pi_{0}^{c}(X)
a fixed base ray such that
\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}
exists for all
h\geq h_{0}
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{h}:=\{L_{j,h}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a finite filtration of
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
such that
\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}
is well-parametrised with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{h}
. Let
n\geq 1
. There is a short exact sequence of groups

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\varinjlim_{h}{\varprojlim_{j}}^{1}\pi_{n+1}(L_{j,h}^{c},\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}(j))\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)\rightarrow\varinjlim_{h}\varprojlim_{j}\pi_{n}(L_{j,h}^{c},\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}(j))\rightarrow 0

For
n=0
, there is a short exact sequence of pointed sets

\displaystyle\varinjlim_{h}{\varprojlim_{j}}^{1}\pi_{1}(L_{j,h}^{c},\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}(j))\hookrightarrow\pi^{e}_{0}(Y,\omega)\twoheadrightarrow\varinjlim_{h}\varprojlim_{j}\pi_{0}(L_{j,h}^{c},\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}(j))

We first prove Theorem
9.10
for
n\geq 1
. Fix
h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq h_{0}}
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{h}:=\{L_{j,h}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be a finite filtration of
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
such that
\phi_{h_{0},h}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega}
is well-parametrised with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{h}
. There is a natural
\varprojlim^{1}
sequence for each
h
(Theorem
5.10
), and we obtain the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varprojlim\pi_{n}(L_{j,h_{0}}^{c},\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega}(j))}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{h_{0},h_{0}+1})_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{h_{0},h_{0}+1})_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{h_{0},h_{0}+1})_{\ast}}

since direct limits preserve exactness, we have a short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\varinjlim_{h}{\varprojlim_{j}}^{1}\pi_{n+1}(L_{j,h}^{c},\phi_{h_{0},h}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega}(j))\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)\rightarrow\varinjlim_{h}\varprojlim_{j}\pi_{n}(L_{j,h}^{c},\phi_{h_{0},h}\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega}(j))\rightarrow 0

For
n=0
, Theorem
3.36
give us the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{e}_{0}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varprojlim\pi_{0}(L_{j,h}^{c},\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{e}_{0}(|\mathcal{U}_{h_{0}}|,\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varprojlim\pi_{0}(L_{j,h_{0}}^{c},\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega}(j))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{h,h+1})_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{h,h+1})_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{h,h+1})_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{h_{0},h_{0}+1})_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{h_{0},h_{0}+1})_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{h_{0},h_{0}+1})_{\ast}}

Injectivity, surjectivity, and exactness are preserved under taking the direct limit. Therefore, we obtain the short exact sequence

\displaystyle\varinjlim_{h}{\varprojlim_{j}}^{1}\pi_{1}(L_{j,h}^{c},\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}(j))\hookrightarrow\pi_{0}^{c}(X,\omega)\twoheadrightarrow\varinjlim_{h}\varprojlim_{j}\pi_{0}(L_{j,h}^{c},\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}(j))

where
\varinjlim_{h}\varprojlim_{j}\pi_{0}(L_{j,h}^{c},\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}(j))=\varinjlim_{h}\mathcal{E}nds(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\omega})
.

To recover Theorem
8.8
as a corollary, we restrict to the case where
X
is geodesic. Since the maps
\phi_{h,h+1}
are quasi-isometries, and the number of ends of a space is a quasi-isometry invariant, the direct limit on the right collapses to
h=h_{0}
. Moreover,
i_{h_{0}}
is also a quasi-isometry, so we obtain

\displaystyle\varinjlim_{h}\varprojlim_{j}\pi_{0}(L_{j,h}^{c},\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}(j))\cong\varprojlim_{j}\pi_{0}(L_{j,h_{0}}^{c},\widetilde{i_{h_{0}}\omega}(j))\cong\mathcal{E}nds(X,\omega)

where
\mathcal{E}nds(X,\omega)
denotes the pointed set
\mathcal{E}nds(X)
with the end defined by
\omega
as a base point. This gives us the surjection
\pi_{0}^{c}(X,\omega)\rightarrow\mathcal{E}nds(X,\omega)
as claimed.

10
Examples

We prove Theorem
8.9
now. It suffices to show that if
X
is a locally finite geometric tree, then
\varinjlim_{h}\varprojlim^{1}_{j}\pi_{1}(L_{j,h}^{c},\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}(j))
vanishes. In fact, we show quite explicitly that for each
h\geq h_{0}
and any finite filtration
\{L_{j,h}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
of
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
,
\pi_{1}(L_{j,h}^{c},\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}(j))=0
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

(of Theorem
8.9
)

The CW-complement of
L_{j,h}
is the subcomplex generated by the vertices not in
L_{j,h}
, ie. a simplex in
L^{c}_{j,h}
exists if and only if all its vertices are not in
L_{j,h}
. Consider
[\gamma]\in\pi_{1}(L_{j,h}^{c},\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}(j))
. By the CW approximation theorem, we can assume that after changing basepoints,
\gamma
is a closed edge path in
L_{j,h}^{c}
. Let
(z_{0},\dots,z_{m})
be the ordered tuple of vertices traversed by
\gamma
with
z_{0}=z_{m}
. So there exist metric balls
B(z_{0},2^{h}),\dots,B(z_{m},2^{h})
in
X
with
B(z_{i},2^{h})\cap B(z_{i+1},2^{h})\neq\emptyset
for
0\leq i\leq m-1
.

We show by induction that
\gamma
must be contractible in
L_{j,h}^{c}
. Assume that
m\leq 3
. The cases
m=1,2
are clear. For
m=3
, we have
B(z_{0},2^{h}),B(z_{1},2^{h}),B(z_{2},2^{h})
with

\displaystyle B(z_{0},2^{h})\cap B(z_{1},2^{h})\neq\emptyset

\displaystyle B(z_{1},2^{h})\cap B(z_{2},2^{h})\neq\emptyset

\displaystyle B(z_{2},2^{h})\cap B(z_{0},2^{h})\neq\emptyset

Choose
x_{0}\in B(z_{0},2^{h})\cap B(z_{1},2^{h}),x_{1}\in B(z_{1},2^{h})\cap B(z_{2},2^{h}),x_{2}\in B(z_{2},2^{h})\cap B(z_{0},2^{h})
. Let
[x_{0},x_{1}],[x_{1},x_{2}],[x_{2},x_{0}]
denote the unique geodesics in
X
betweeen the respective points. A geodesic triangle in a tree is a tripod with a unique midpoint

\displaystyle m([x_{0},x_{1}],[x_{1},x_{2}],[x_{2},x_{0}])

Since metric balls in a tree are convex, we have that
[x_{0},x_{1}]\subset B(z_{1},2^{h}),[x_{1},x_{2}]\subset B(z_{2},2^{h}),[x_{2},x_{0}]\subset B(z_{0},2^{h})
. Therefore
m\in B(z_{0},2^{h})\cap B(z_{1},2^{h})\cap B(z_{2},2^{h})
. This shows that the
2
-simplex
[z_{0},z_{1},z_{2}]
exists in
L^{c}_{j,h}\subset|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
. Therefore
\gamma
is a contractible loop.

Suppose that
m\geq 4
. Assume that all edge paths with length less than
m
are contractible. Metric balls in trees are subtrees. We apply Gavril’s chordal graph theorem (
[
21
]
) to the intersection graph
G
of
B(z_{0},2^{h}),\dots,B(z_{m},2^{h})
. The loop
\gamma
corresponds to a simple circuit in
G
with more than three vertices. This means that there is a chord connecting two non-consecutive vertices, ie. there exist
0\leq i<j\leq m-1
with
B(z_{i},2^{h})\cap B(z_{j},2^{h})\neq\emptyset
and
|i-j|>1
(we identify
m=0
).
\gamma
is homotopic to the concatenation of segments
\tau_{1}\ast\eta\ast\tau_{2}
where

\displaystyle\tau_{1}=(z_{0},\dots,z_{i})

\displaystyle\eta=(z_{i},z_{j},z_{j-1},\dots,z_{i})

\displaystyle\tau_{2}=(z_{i},z_{j},z_{j+1},\dots,z_{m})

\eta
is a loop of length
<m
, so it is contractible. The remaining segments
\tau_{1}\ast\tau_{2}
concatenate to form a loop of length
<m
as well. This shows that all edge paths of length
m
are contractible. By induction, the statement holds.
∎

This recovers the statement that
\pi_{0}^{c}(X,\omega)\cong\mathcal{E}nds(X,\omega)
for
X
a locally finite geometric tree. In fact, something even stronger is true:
L_{j,h}^{c}
is homotopy equivalent to a set of points for all
j,h\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
Z^{c}_{j,h}
denote the set of vertices that are not in
L_{j,h}
. Observe that
L_{j,h}^{c}
is the nerve of the open cover

\displaystyle\bigcup_{z\in Z^{c}_{j,h}}B(z,2^{h})

We show that this cover is good. Suppose that
\cap_{i=0}^{m}B(z_{i},2^{h})\neq\emptyset
for
z_{0},\dots,z_{m}\in Z^{c}_{j,h}
. Let
x,y\in\cap_{i=0}^{m}B(z_{i},2^{h})
. Since metric balls in a tree are convex, the geodesic
[x,y]\subset\cap_{i=0}^{m}B(z_{i},2^{h})
. Thus
\cap_{i=0}^{m}B(z_{i},2^{h})
is a connected subgraph of
X
, and therefore a tree, which is contractible. By the nerve theorem (see the proof of Corollary
3.2
or Chapter
4.
G of
[
25
]
),
L_{j,h}^{c}
is homotopy equivalent to
\cup_{z\in Z^{c}_{j,h}}B(z,2^{h})
.

\cup_{z\in Z^{c}_{j,h}}B(z,2^{h})
is a forest: any connected component is a subgraph of
X
, and therefore a tree. This shows that
L_{j,h}^{c}
is homotopy equivalent to the set of path components of
\cup_{z\in Z^{c}_{j,h}}B(z,2^{h})
. We conclude:

Lemma 10.1
.

Let
X
be a locally finite geometric tree,
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow X
a coarse base ray. The coarse homotopy groups of
X
are

\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)\cong\begin{cases}0&n\geq 1\\
\mathcal{E}nds(X,\omega)&n=0\\
\end{cases}

There is also an example constructed in
[
3
]
where
\pi^{c}_{0}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{E}nds(X)
is not injective. We attempt now to connect this example to our framework.

Example 10.2
.

Let
X
be the proper geodesic space defined by a pair of geodesic rays constructed by consecutive edges, connected by a distinguished vertex at one end, and
n^{2}
edges connecting the
n
-th vertex on each ray from the joined end, equipped with the usual path metric. We write points in
X
as
(l,x)
, where
l\in[0,\infty)
denotes the height and
x\in[0,l^{2}]
denotes the position along the rungs of the ladder.

Figure 47:
The simplicial complex
X
.

Let
\alpha
be the left ray in the graph rooted at the join-point, and similarly for
\alpha^{\prime}
the right ray.
X
has one end, so
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
define the same end. However,
[\alpha]\neq[\alpha^{\prime}]\in\pi^{c}_{0}(X)
.

It is proven in Theorem
7.7
of
[
3
]
that
[\alpha]\neq[\alpha^{\prime}]\in\pi^{c}_{0}(X)
by assuming the existence of a coarse homotopy between
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
and deriving a contradiction. Using our results, we have an exact algebraic characterisation of the kernel of
\pi^{c}_{0}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{E}nds(X)
, namely, it is

\displaystyle\varinjlim_{h}{\varprojlim_{j}}^{1}\pi_{1}(L_{j,h}^{c},\widetilde{i_{h}\omega}(j))

For now, let us focus only on the term
h=0
. In this case,
|\mathcal{U}_{0}|=X
, and a finite filtration is
\{X_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, where
X_{j}
is the subcomplex of
X
with height
\leq j-1
.
\widetilde{i_{0}\omega}
is just
\alpha
. We wish to compute

\displaystyle{\varprojlim_{j}}^{1}\pi_{1}(X^{c}_{j},\alpha(j))

where
X^{c}_{j}
denotes the subcomplex of
X
with height
\geq j
, which is the CW-complement of
X_{j}
in
X
(we shift the index
j
by
1
and consider rays
[0,\infty)\rightarrow X
). Let
\gamma_{j}:[0,1]\rightarrow X_{j}
be the loop based at
\alpha(j)
which traverses the horizontal steps
j
and
j+1
in an anti-clockwise fashion. The pre-image of
[\alpha^{\prime}]\in\pi^{e}_{0}(X,\alpha)
under the map
\varprojlim^{1}_{j}\pi_{1}(X^{c}_{j},\alpha(j))\rightarrow\pi_{0}^{e}(X,\alpha)
is the equivalence class of

\displaystyle(\gamma_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\pi_{1}(X^{c}_{j},\alpha(j))

This is because the horizontal components cancel out, and we are left with a copy of
\alpha^{\prime}
, and a copy of
\alpha
traversed in the opposite direction. It can be checked (Chapter
16.1
of
[
22
]
) that this exactly corresponds to
[\alpha^{\prime}]\in\pi_{0}^{e}(X,\alpha)
.

In fact, we can just compute
\varprojlim^{1}_{j}\pi_{1}(X^{c}_{j},\alpha(j))
. The fundamental group of
X^{c}_{j}
is the free group on the generating set
\eta_{k}:=\alpha_{[j,k]}\ast\gamma_{k}\ast(\alpha_{[j,k]})^{-1}
(traversing the
k
-th circle) which can be identified with the generating set
\mathbb{N}_{0}\setminus\{0,\dots,j-1\}=(\mathbb{N}_{0})_{\geq j}
. The bonding
\psi_{j+1,j}
maps are induced by the inclusion on spaces which can be identified with the inclusion

\displaystyle F_{(\mathbb{N}_{0})_{\geq j+1}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow F_{(\mathbb{N}_{0})_{\geq j}}

\varprojlim^{1}_{j}\pi_{1}(X^{c}_{j},\alpha(j))
is the cokernel of the group homomorphism

\displaystyle\varphi:\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}F_{(\mathbb{N}_{0})_{\geq j}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}F_{(\mathbb{N}_{0})_{\geq j}}

\displaystyle(a_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}

\displaystyle\mapsto(a_{j}(\psi_{j+1,j}(a_{j+1}))^{-1})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}

(\gamma_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is a nontrivial element in the cokernel: suppose not, ie.
\gamma_{j}=a_{j}(\psi_{j+1,j}(a_{j+1}))^{-1}
for some
(a_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. To derive a contradiction, we show that
a_{0}
must traverse each circle exactly once, which cannot happen because the interval is compact. To that end, fix
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. We have that
a_{k}
must traverse the
k
-th circle anti-clockwise exactly once, since
(\psi_{k+1,k}(a_{k+1}))^{-1}
is an element of
F_{\mathbb{N}_{\geq k+1}}\subset F_{\mathbb{N}_{\geq k}}
. From the equation
\gamma_{k-1}=a_{k-1}(\psi_{k,k-1}(a_{k}))^{-1}
we obtain that
a_{k-1}
traverses the
k
-th circle anti-clockwise exactly once. Going backwards inductively, we obtain that
a_{0}
traverses the
k
-th circle exactly once. This is true for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, so we are done.

It remains to show that
(\gamma_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is also a non-trivial element in the colimit. To do this, we prove that the bonding maps
\phi_{h,h+1}
induce an isomorphism for all
h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
X
be a strongly locally finite, path-connected CW complex,
K
a compact subcomplex. Let
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow K^{c}
be a base ray. The inclusion map induces an isomorphism

\displaystyle\iota:\pi_{n}^{e}(K^{c},\omega)\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{e}(X,\omega)

with inverse

\displaystyle\rho:\pi_{n}^{e}(X,\omega)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{e}(K^{c},\omega)

\displaystyle[g]

\displaystyle\mapsto b_{\omega_{j},\omega}[g_{j}]

where
g_{j}
is the restriction of
g
to
[0,1]^{n}\times[j,\infty)
for
j
sufficiently large, and
\omega_{j}(h)=\omega(j+h-1)
.

This lemma further justifies the use of the word "end" to describe
\pi_{n}^{e}
: we can delete any compact subcomplex without affecting the result.

Proof.

We show that
\rho
is well defined. Suppose we have
k>j
. Consider the homotopy

\displaystyle H:[0,1]^{n}\times[1,\infty)\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\rightarrow X

\displaystyle H(x,h,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto g(x,(1-t)j+tk+h-1)

This is a homotopy between
g_{j}
and
g_{k}
and restricted to the boundary it is
b_{\omega_{j},\omega_{k}}
. We obtain

\displaystyle b_{\omega_{j},\omega}[g_{j}]=b_{\omega_{j},\omega}b_{\omega_{k},\omega_{j}}[g_{k}]=b_{\omega_{k},\omega}[g_{k}]

It is obviously independent of the representative of
[g]
and is a group homomorphism. The composition
\rho\circ\iota
is the identity on representatives, and for a
[g]\in\pi_{n}^{e}(X,\omega)
we have
[g]=b_{\omega_{j},\omega}[g_{j}]
. Therefore
\iota\circ\rho=\id
.
∎

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
X,Y
be strongly locally finite, path-connected CW complexes. Let
\psi:X\rightarrow Y
be a proper map and let
K,L
be finite subcomplexes of
X,Y
such that
\psi(K^{c})\subset L^{c}
. Let
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow X
be a base ray and
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\omega[j,\infty)\subset K^{c}
. The following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{e}(K^{c},\omega_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{e}(L^{c},\psi\omega_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{e}(X,\omega_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{e}(Y,\psi\omega_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{e}(X,\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{e}(Y,\psi\omega)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{|K^{c}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{\omega_{j},\omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{\psi\omega_{j},\psi\omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

Proof.

The top square obviously commutes. The bottom square commutes since the change of base ray homomorphism is natural.
∎

Since the vertical arrows are isomorphisms, this shows that a proper map
\psi:X\rightarrow Y
induces an isomorphism on end homotopy groups if and only if
\psi_{|K^{c}}
is an isomorphism on end homotopy groups for any compact subcomplex
K
.

We apply the lemma to the bonding maps
\phi_{h,h+1}:|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|
. It suffices to show that there exist compact subcomplexes
K_{0}\subset|\mathcal{U}_{0}|=X
and
K_{h}\subset|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
such that
i_{h}(K^{c}_{0})=K^{c}_{h}
is a proper homotopy equivalence for all
h\in\mathbb{N}
. This is because
\phi_{h,h+1}i_{h}
and
i_{h+1}
have the same simplicial approximation and so are properly homotopic:

\displaystyle\phi_{h,h+1}i_{h}\simeq(\phi_{h,h+1}i_{h})^{\sim}\simeq\widetilde{i_{h+1}}\simeq i_{h+1}

Therefore if
i_{h}
induces isomorphisms on end homotopy groups for all
h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, so do the
\phi_{h,h+1}
.

To that end, fix a
h\in\mathbb{N}
. We construct a
\frac{2^{h}}{2}
-separated subset for
X
. Let
j_{h}=2^{h+1}
. We have that
5\cdot 2^{h}<(j_{h})^{2}
. Let

\displaystyle c_{l}=\min\{|l-2^{h}\lfloor\frac{l}{2^{h}}\rfloor|,|l-2^{h}\lceil\frac{l}{2^{h}}\rceil|\}

denote the distance to the closest multiple of
2^{h}
. Consider the vertices

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}_{h}:=L\bigcup R\bigcup\cup_{l\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq j_{h}}}L_{l}\bigcup\cup_{l\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq j_{h}}}R_{l}

\displaystyle:=\{(k2^{h},0)\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\cup\{(k2^{h},(k2^{h})^{2})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\cup\{(l,m2^{h}-c_{l})\}_{l\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq j_{h}}}\cup\{(l,l^{2}-m2^{h}+c_{l})\}_{l\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq j_{h}}}

where
m\in\{1,2\}
.
L,R
correspond to vertices on the rays
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}
respectively;
L_{l}
consists of two points on the rung of height
l
and analogously for
R_{l}
. Observe that
L_{l},R_{l}
are separated by a distance greater than
2^{h}
. Since
c_{l}\leq\frac{2^{h}}{2}
,
L_{l},L_{l+1}
(resp.
R_{l},R_{l+1}
) are separated by a distance greater than
2^{h}
for all
l\geq j_{h}
. Let
Z_{h}
be any maximal
\frac{2^{h}}{2}
-separated subset which contains
Z^{\prime}_{h}
. Let
K^{c}_{h}
be the subcomplex of
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
generated by vertices in
Z_{h}
with height
\geq{j_{h}}
.

It can be checked explicitly that
K^{c}_{h}
is properly homotopic to
X^{c}_{j_{h}}
: vertices in rungs at different heights do not share an edge, and a point in the interior of the rung at height
l
shares an edge with at at most one vertex in
L
(resp.
R
), unless we are in the specific scenario of
l=(k+\frac{1}{2})2^{h}
. In this case, the
2
-simplex defined by the three vertices at heights
l,k2^{h},(k+1)2^{h}
exists. The only other thing to consider is a triple of points in the interior of a rung with pairwise non-empty intersection. Here, a
2
-simplex also exists, because the triple intersection is an interval in the rung.

Therefore,
i_{h}:X^{c}_{j_{h}}\rightarrow K^{c}_{h}
induces an isomorphism on end homotopy groups; the colimit collapses to
h=0
and we obtain

\displaystyle\pi^{c}_{0}(X,\alpha)\cong\pi_{0}^{e}(X,\alpha)\cong(\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}F_{(\mathbb{N}_{0})_{\geq j}})_{/\image(\varphi)}

Additionally, is not difficult to show that all higher homotopy groups of
X
vanish.

We now generalise this computation to arbitrary ladders, addressing a question posed in
[
3
]
about how to define the "size" of a hole.

Lemma 10.5
.

Let
X
be a ladder: a
1
-dimensional simplicial complex defined by two rays
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}
joining at a distinguished vertex at height
0
, and rungs of length
l_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
joining the rays at heights
h_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
X
be given the path metric. Assume that
\{h_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is a strictly increasing sequence and that
\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}l_{m}=\infty
. Then the coarse homotopy groups of
X
are:

\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\alpha)\cong\begin{cases}0&n\geq 1\\
{\varprojlim}^{1}_{m}F_{(\mathbb{N}_{0})_{\geq m}}&n=0\\
\end{cases}

where the bonding maps
\psi_{m+1,m}:F_{(\mathbb{N}_{0})_{\geq m+1}}\rightarrow F_{(\mathbb{N}_{0})_{\geq m}}
are the canonical inclusion.

Proof.

Fix
h\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
j_{h}
be a power of
2
such that
5\cdot 2^{h}<l_{m}
for all
h_{m}\geq j_{h}
. Construct the sets
Z_{h}^{\prime}
as before and extend to a maximal
\frac{2^{h}}{2}
-separated subset
Z_{h}
of
X
. Let
K^{c}_{h}
be the subcomplex of
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
generated by vertices in
Z_{h}
with height
\geq j_{h}
.
i_{h}:X^{c}_{j_{h}}\rightarrow K^{c}_{h}
is a proper homotopy equivalence; the colimit collapses to
h=0
. We have

\displaystyle\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\alpha)\cong\pi_{n}^{e}(X,\alpha)

for all
n\geq 0
. We can choose a finite filtration
\{X_{h_{m}}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
of
X
corresponding to the rungs of the ladder. We have that

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(X^{c}_{h_{m}},\alpha(h_{m}))\cong F_{(\mathbb{N}_{0})_{\geq m}}

The bonding maps induced by the inclusion of spaces can be identified with the inclusion of groups
F_{(\mathbb{N}_{0})_{\geq m+1}}\rightarrow F_{(\mathbb{N}_{0})_{\geq m}}
. The higher homotopy groups of
X^{c}_{h_{m}}
vanish for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
X^{c}_{h_{m}}
is connected,
X
has one end. Applying the
{\varprojlim}^{1}
sequence gives us the claim.
∎

This lemma shows that the coarse homotopy groups cannot distinguish between different ladders that have rungs whose lengths grow unboundedly. We now show that there is a ladder
Y
and a coarse map
\mathfrak{a}:Y\rightarrow X
which induces an isomorphism on coarse homotopy groups, but is not a coarse homotopy equivalence.

Example 10.6
.

(Failure of general coarse Whitehead) Let
Y
be a ladder with rungs of length
2^{m}
joining the rays at heights
2^{m}
for
m\geq 10
. Give
Y
a simplicial structure with edges of length
1
. Let
X
be a ladder with rungs of length
m^{2}
joining the rays at heights
m
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mathfrak{a}:Y\rightarrow X
be the distinguished-vertex-preserving piecewise-linear map which sends rungs at height
2^{m}
to height
m
.

This map is
\frac{1}{4}
-Lipschitz so has a simplicial approximation: let
\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}(v)=x_{v}
for the vertices
v\in Y
. To apply Theorems
9.6
and
9.9
we only need to consider the map
\xi_{0,0}:Y\rightarrow X
which is the composition

\displaystyle\widetilde{i_{0}\mathfrak{a}R_{0}}

Observe that
i_{0}\mathfrak{a}R_{0}
satisfies the same star condition as
\id_{X}\mathfrak{a}\id_{Y}
: for a vertex
v\in Y
we have
i_{0}\mathfrak{a}R_{0}\langle st(v)\rangle\subset i_{0}\mathfrak{a}\langle st(v)\rangle\subset i_{0}\langle st(x_{v})\rangle\subset\langle st(x_{v})\rangle
. Therefore it suffices to consider the induced map

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{\ast}:\pi_{n}^{e}(Y,\alpha)\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{e}(X,\mathfrak{a}\alpha)

Since
\mathfrak{a}
restricted to height
\geq 2^{10}
is a proper homotopy equivalence, it induces an isomorphism on end homotopy groups for all
n\geq 0
.

\mathfrak{a}
does not have a coarse homotopy inverse. Suppose for a contradiction there existed a
\mathfrak{b}:X\rightarrow Y
such that
\mathfrak{b}\circ\mathfrak{a}
is coarsely homotopic to
\id_{Y}
via the homotopy
H:I_{p}(Y)\rightarrow Y
(as usual, we can assume that
p:Y\rightarrow[0,\infty)
is the projection to the height variable). There exists a
h
large enough such that
i_{h}\mathfrak{b}R_{0}
has a simplicial approximation. Then we have the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}^{c}(Y,\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}^{c}(X,\mathfrak{a}\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}^{c}(Y,\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{e}_{0}(Y,\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{e}_{0}(X,\mathfrak{a}\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{e}_{0}(|\mathcal{V}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\alpha})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{b}_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{i_{h}\mathfrak{b}R_{0}}_{\ast}}

By construction we have that
\widetilde{i_{h}\mathfrak{b}R_{0}}=\widetilde{i_{h}\mathfrak{b}}
, which is a proper, simplicial map.
(\mathfrak{b})_{\ast}(\mathfrak{a})_{\ast}
is the identity: this means that
(\widetilde{i_{h}\mathfrak{b}})_{\ast}\mathfrak{a}_{\ast}
on end homotopy groups has to be the identity too, after the identification of
\pi^{e}_{0}(|\mathcal{V}_{h}|,\widetilde{i_{h}\alpha})
with
\pi_{0}^{e}(Y,\alpha)
. We will show that actually
\widetilde{i_{h}\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{a}
is properly homotopic to
i_{h}:Y\rightarrow|\mathcal{V}_{h}|
.

To that end, consider the simplicial complex
I_{p}(Y):=\{(x,p(x)t)\in Y\times[0,\infty)\,|\,t\in[0,1]\}
with vertex set
Z
equal to the intersection of the integer lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
(in ladder coordinates) with
I_{p}(Y)
. We equip
I_{p}(Y)
with the metric

\displaystyle d_{\infty}((x,p(x)t),(y,p(y)l))=\max\{d(x,y),|p(x)t-p(y)l|\}

This is bi-Lipschitz equivalent to the path metric on
I_{p}(Y)
induced from the ambient
L^{1}
-product metric on
Y\times[0,\infty)
. It is clear that
Z
is a maximal
\frac{1}{2}
-separated set of
I_{p}(Y)
. We can denote a point in
Z
by
z_{mlt}:=((m,l),t)
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0},0\leq l\leq m
and
0\leq t\leq m
, where
(m,l)
is written in ladder coordinates.
I_{p}(Y)
is a subcomplex of the nerve
|\mathcal{W}|
of the open cover
\mathcal{W}:=\cup_{z\in Z}B(z,1)
. Let
i_{\mathcal{W}}:I_{p}(Y)\rightarrow|\mathcal{W}|
and
R_{\mathcal{W}}:|\mathcal{W}|\rightarrow I_{p}(Y)
be defined as usual. By construction we have that
\widetilde{{R_{\mathcal{W}}}_{|I_{p}(Y)}}=\id_{I_{p}(Y)}

We can assume that we have chosen
h
large enough so that
i_{h}HR_{\mathcal{W}}:|\mathcal{W}|\rightarrow|\mathcal{V}_{h}|
has a simplicial approximation
(i_{h}HR_{\mathcal{W}})^{\sim}
. We therefore obtain a proper homotopy

\displaystyle(i_{h}HR_{\mathcal{W}})^{\sim}_{|I_{p}(Y)}:I_{p}(Y)\rightarrow|\mathcal{V}_{h}|

between
(i_{h}\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a}(R_{\mathcal{W}})_{Y\times\{0\}})^{\sim}=(i_{h}\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{a})^{\sim}=(i_{h}\mathfrak{b})^{\sim}(\mathfrak{a})^{\sim}\simeq(i_{h}\mathfrak{b})^{\sim}\mathfrak{a}
and
(i_{h}R_{\mathcal{W}})^{\sim}=\widetilde{i_{h}}
. By rescaling, we obtain a proper homotopy
H:Y\times[0,1]\rightarrow|\mathcal{V}_{h}|
between
(i_{h}\mathfrak{b})^{\sim}\mathfrak{a}
and
\widetilde{i_{h}}
.

It can be checked explicitly (because of the nicely chosen separation of rungs and rung lengths) that
\widetilde{i_{h}}:Y\rightarrow|\mathcal{V}_{h}|
restricted to the subcomplex
Y^{c}_{h}
(generated by vertices with height
\geq 2^{h}
) is a homeomorphism, and the induced map on
\pi_{1}
can be canonically identified with the identity. We identify
Y^{c}_{h}
with its image
\widetilde{i_{h}}(Y^{c}_{h})\subset|\mathcal{V}_{h}|
. Choose
j
large enough such that
H(Y^{c}_{j}\times[0,1])\subset Y^{c}_{h}
. Consider the loop
\gamma_{j}
(traversing the
j
-th circle anti-clockwise) which is a generator of
\pi_{1}(Y^{c}_{h},\alpha(2^{j}))
. We have that

\displaystyle(i_{h}\mathfrak{b})^{\sim}_{\ast}\mathfrak{a}_{\ast}[\gamma_{j}]=b\widetilde{i_{h}}_{\ast}[\gamma_{j}]=b[\gamma_{j}]

where
b
is the change of base point homomorphism between
\alpha(2^{j})
and
(i_{h}\mathfrak{b})^{\sim}\mathfrak{a}\alpha(2^{j})
via the homotopy
H
. We know that
\mathfrak{a}
sends
\gamma_{j}
to the
j
-th loop in
X
, which has length
j^{2}+(j+1)^{2}+2
. Since
(i_{h}\mathfrak{b})^{\sim}
is simplicial, it is length-decreasing. But we know that
\gamma_{j}
has length
\frac{1}{2^{h}}(2^{j}+2^{j+1}+2\cdot 2^{j})
in
|\mathcal{V}_{h}|
, and any element in the homotopy class
b[\gamma_{j}]
must have at least this length: contradiction for
j
sufficiently large.

The example shows us the following:

Theorem 10.7
.

There exist
1
-dimensional simplicial complexes
Y,X
and a proper Lipschitz map
\mathfrak{a}:Y\rightarrow X
which induces an isomorphism on all coarse homotopy groups, but is not a coarse homotopy equivalence.

11
Relaxing the restrictions

We compare the coarse homotopy groups of
X
to the end homotopy groups of
X
for
X
a proper metric space.

Theorem 11.1
.

Let
X
be a proper metric space with a continuous coarse base ray
\omega:[1,\infty)\rightarrow X
. Let
n\geq 1
. There exists a group homomorphism

\displaystyle\lambda:\pi_{n}^{e}(X,\omega)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\omega)

\displaystyle[f]

\displaystyle\mapsto b_{\omega s,\omega}[f\circ s]

where
s:c[0,1]^{n}\rightarrow c[0,1]^{n}
is a shrinking map.

Proof.

Let
h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Consider the set
c_{[2^{h},2^{h+1}]}[0,1]^{n}=[0,1]^{n}\times[2^{h},h^{h+1}]
, the parts of the cone with heights in the interval
[2^{h},2^{h+1}]
. Let
[f]\in\pi_{n}^{e}(X,\omega)
. Since
f
is continuous,
f_{|c_{[2^{h},2^{h+1}]}[0,1]^{n}}
is uniformly continuous. There exists a
\varepsilon_{h}>0
such that

\displaystyle d(x,y)<\varepsilon_{h}\implies d(f(x),f(y))<1

We can assume that
\{\varepsilon_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is a decreasing sequence. Choose an increasing sequence of constants
\{m_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
such that
2^{m_{h}}>\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{h}}
. The projection

\displaystyle q_{h}:[0,1]^{n}\times[2^{h+m_{h}},2^{h+1+m_{h}}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow[0,1]^{n}\times[2^{h},2^{h+1}]

\displaystyle(tx,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\frac{t}{2^{m_{h}}}x,\frac{t}{2^{m_{h}}})

has Lipschitz constant
\frac{1}{2^{m_{h}}}
. We have that
q_{h+1}([0,1]^{n}\times\{2^{h+1+m_{h+1}}\})=q_{h}([0,1]^{n}\times\{2^{h+1+m_{h}}\})
so we can extend
q:=\{q_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
in the obvious way to a continuous map
s_{q}:c[0,1]^{n}\rightarrow c[0,1]^{n}
. The composition
f\circ s_{q}
satisfies

\displaystyle d(x,y)<1\implies d(fs_{q}(x),fs_{q}(y))<1

Since
c[0,1]^{n}
is a path metric space,
f\circ s_{q}
is controlled. It is proper because
f
and
s_{q}
are proper. Additionally, for any other choice
\{q^{\prime}_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
with
ps_{q^{\prime}}\leq ps_{q}
,
fs_{q}
is coarsely homotopic to
fs_{q^{\prime}}
. Since
\omega
is coarse,
\omega
is coarsely homotopic to
\omega s_{q}
and
\omega s_{q^{\prime}}
. Furthermore, there exists a coarse homotopy
G:c[0,1]^{2}\rightarrow X
such that

\displaystyle G_{|[0,1]\times\{0\}}=b_{\omega s_{q},\omega}

\displaystyle G_{|[0,1]\times\{1\}}=b_{\omega s_{q^{\prime}},\omega}

\displaystyle G_{|\{0\}\times[0,1]}=b_{\omega s_{q},\omega s_{q^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle G_{|[0,1]\times\{1\}}=\id_{\omega}

We obtain

\displaystyle b_{\omega s_{q},\omega}[f\circ s_{q}]=b_{\omega s_{q^{\prime}},\omega}[f\circ s_{q^{\prime}}]

which shows that
\lambda
is independent of the choice of
q
.

Suppose that
[f]=[g]\in\pi_{n}^{e}(X,\omega)
. Let
H:c([0,1]^{n}\times[0,1])\rightarrow X
be a continous, proper homotopy between
f
and
g
. As before, there exist a decreasing sequence of constants
\{\varepsilon_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, an increasing sequence of constants
\{m_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, and
\frac{1}{2^{m_{h}}}
-Lipschitz maps

\displaystyle q_{h}:[0,1]^{n+1}\times[2^{h+m_{h}},2^{h+1+m_{h}}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow[0,1]^{n+1}\times[2^{h},2^{h+1}]

\displaystyle(tx,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\frac{t}{2^{m_{h}}}x,\frac{t}{2^{m_{h}}})

such that
H\circ s_{q}
satisfies
d(x,y)<1\implies d(Hs_{q}(x),Hs_{q}(y))<1

∎

This is a coarse homotopy between
fs_{q}
and
gs_{q}
. After changing base rays, we obtian

\displaystyle b_{\omega s_{q},\omega}[fs_{q}]=b_{\omega s_{q},\omega}[gs_{q}]

It is clear that
\lambda
is a group homomorphism.

Part IV
Conclusion

12
Further research

12.1
Loose ends

We discuss some loose ends that have been left by the proofs in this thesis:

Connectedness of
X
:

We only considered connected spaces
X
in Theorem
7.9
. An analogous, but more complicated statement may be true for compact metric spaces
X
such that
M_{X}
has finitely many ends.

Suppose that
M_{X}
has
t\in\mathbb{N}
ends. Let
[\tau_{1}],\dots,[\tau_{t}]\in\pi_{0}^{c}(cX)
be such that
\{\theta[\tau_{1}],\dots,\theta[\tau_{t}]\}
are proper homotopy classes of proper rays in
M_{X}
which represent these ends. By post-composing with a shrinking map, we can assume that
\widetilde{i\tau}
exists for all
\tau\in\{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{t}\}
. I expect that by a similar proof to Proposition
7.6
, for each
\tau\in\{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{t}\}
there exists a
l\in\mathbb{N}
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\tau)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{c}(cY,\mathfrak{a}\tau)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{L,e}(M_{X},\widetilde{i\tau})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{L,e}(M_{Y},\widetilde{is_{l}\mathfrak{a}\tau})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{e}(M_{X},\widetilde{i\tau})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{e}(M_{Y},\widetilde{is_{l}\mathfrak{a}\tau})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta\circ s_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b\circ\psi}

commutes. The map
\psi
here is the geometric cellular approximation
(is_{l}\mathfrak{a}R)^{\sim}
, and
b
is the change of base ray homomorphism

\displaystyle b:\pi_{n}^{e}(M_{Y},\widetilde{is_{l}\mathfrak{a}R}\widetilde{i\tau})\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{e}(M_{Y},\widetilde{is_{l}\mathfrak{a}\tau})

To show that the diagram commutes would be technically more challenging, since
R
is not the identity now on
\widetilde{i\tau}
. If this works, we have the following statement:

Conjecture 12.1
.

Let
X,Y
be compact metric spaces with finite shape dimension. Let
\mathfrak{a}:cX\rightarrow cY
be a coarse map. Assume that
M_{X}
has
t\in\mathbb{N}
ends. Let
[\tau_{1}],\dots,[\tau_{t}]\in\pi_{0}^{c}(cX)
be such that
\{\theta[\tau_{1}],\dots,\theta[\tau_{t}]\}
are proper homotopy classes of proper rays in
M_{X}
which represent these ends. Assume that for all
\tau\in\{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{t}\}
, there is an isomorphism on coarse homotopy groups
\mathfrak{a}:\pi_{n}^{c}(cX,\tau)\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{c}(cY,\mathfrak{a}\tau)
for all
n\geq 0
. Then there exists a
l\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\psi:=(is_{l}\mathfrak{a}R)^{\sim}
induces an isomorphism

\displaystyle b\circ\psi:\pi_{n}^{e}(M_{X},\widetilde{i\tau})\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{e}(M_{Y},\widetilde{is_{l}\mathfrak{a}\tau})

for all
n\geq 0
and all
\tau
.

If
\psi
additionally induces an isomorphism on the ends of
M_{X},M_{Y}
, then
\mathfrak{a}
is a coarse homotopy equivalence.

Extending the result of coarse Whitehead to inifinitely many ends is significantly more problematic. First, in the proof of Theorem
5.15
, if
M_{Y}
has infinitely many ends, the set
K_{j}
may not be compact. The larger issue in the proof is that in the definition of
j:=\max_{\tau}j_{\tau}
we have taken the maximum over a finite set. I do not see a way to overcome these problems: the statement itself may be false.

Arbitrary ladders
:

Conjecture 12.2
.

Let
X
be a ladder: a
1
-dimensional simplicial complex defined by two rays
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}
joining at a distinguished vertex at height
0
, and rungs of length
l_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
joining the rays at heights
h_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\{h_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is a strictly increasing sequence. Let
X
be given the path metric. Assume that the sequence of perimeters of the
m
-th circle
p_{m}=2(h_{m+1}-h_{m})+l_{m}+l_{m+1}
satisfies
\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}p_{m}=\infty
. Then the coarse homotopy groups of
X
are:

\pi_{n}^{c}(X,\alpha)\cong\begin{cases}0&n\geq 1\\
{\varprojlim}^{1}_{m}F_{\mathbb{N}_{\geq m}}&n=0\\
\end{cases}

where the bonding maps
\psi_{m+1,m}:F_{\mathbb{N}_{\geq m+1}}\rightarrow F_{\mathbb{N}_{\geq m}}
are the canonical inclusion. If the perimeters
p_{m}
are bounded, then then
X
is coarsely homotopy equivalent to a ray.

We proved this statement via an explicit construction for
\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}l_{m}=\infty
. The proof of the general statement would probably involve the nerve theorem. The conjecture being true would mean that coarse homotopy groups of an arbitrary ladder depend only on whether the perimeters of circles grow to infinity.

12.2
Conjectures

We discuss some conjectures and possible generalisations of our results.

Coarse Hurewicz map
:

Let
(X,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B})
be a bornological coase space. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
we consider the abelian group
C\mathcal{X}_{n}
of functions
X^{n+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
which are locally finite and controlled. An element
c\in C\mathcal{X}_{n}
can be expressed as an infinite linear combination of
(n+1)
-tuples of points in
X
.
(C\mathcal{X},d)
is a chain complex with differential

\displaystyle d_{n}:C\mathcal{X}_{n}(X)

\displaystyle\rightarrow C\mathcal{X}_{n-1}(X)

\displaystyle d_{n}c(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}\sum_{x\in X}c(x_{0},\dots,x_{i-1},x,x_{i},\dots,x_{n})

The coarse homology of
X
, denoted by
H\mathcal{X}(X)
, is the homology of
(C\mathcal{X},d)
. Coarse homology is a functor
H\mathcal{X}:\mathbf{BornCoarse}\rightarrow\mathbf{Ab}^{\mathbb{Z}_{gr}}
which is excisive, invariant under coarse homotopies,
u
-continuous, strongly additive, and vanishes on flasques. See Chapter
5
of
[
11
]
.

I believe there exists a coarse Hurewicz map
\mathcal{H}:\pi^{c}_{n}(X)\rightarrow H\mathcal{X}_{n+1}(X)
which is an isomorphism for
n=0
and is the abelianisation map for
n=1
. The proof I have in mind relies on the following statement:

Conjecture 12.3
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. There is an isomorphism between
\pi^{c}_{n}(X,\omega)
and the group (or pointed set) of base ray preserving coarse homotopy classes of base ray preserving coarse maps
f:(c^{\prime}S^{n},c\{x_{0}\})\rightarrow(X,\omega)
, where

\displaystyle c^{\prime}S^{n}=\{(hx,h)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times[0,\infty)\,|\,x\in S^{n},h\in[0,\infty)\}

Assuming this is true, I can prove the following:

Conjecture 12.4
.

(The Hurewicz map) Let
(X,d)
be a proper metric space and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. There is a natural morphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}:\pi_{n}^{c}(X)\rightarrow H\mathcal{X}_{n+1}(X)

such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{c}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\mathcal{X}_{n+1}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}}\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{h\in\mathbb{N}}H^{lf}_{n+1}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

commutes.

Let us explain all the maps in the diagram.

\mathcal{H}:
We can choose a simplicial structure on
S^{n}
so that
c^{\prime}S^{n}
is a union of simplices of dimension
n+1
. Let
\Gamma
be the set of these simplices. For
[f]\in\pi^{c}_{n}(X)
we let

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}[f]=\sum_{\sigma\in\Gamma}(f(v^{\sigma}_{0}),\dots,f(v^{\sigma}_{n+1}))\in C\mathcal{X}_{n+1}(X)

which lies in the kernel of
d_{n+1}
so defines a coarse homology class.

\varphi:
The orientation class
\eta:=\sum_{\sigma\in\Gamma}\sigma
is a generator of
H^{lf}_{n+1}(c^{\prime}S^{n})
. As in ordinary topology, we let

\displaystyle\varphi_{h}:\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|)

\displaystyle\rightarrow H^{lf}_{n+1}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|)

\displaystyle\varphi_{h}[g]

\displaystyle=g_{*}[\eta]

\{\varphi_{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}}
commutes with the bonding maps so defines a group homomorphism
\varphi:\varinjlim_{h}\pi^{e}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|)\rightarrow\varinjlim_{h}H^{lf}_{n+1}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|)
.

\mathcal{A}:
Let
(C\mathcal{X}_{n}(X))_{h}
be the subgroup of
\frac{2^{h}}{2}
-controlled chains. We denote by
(H\mathcal{X}(X))_{h}
the homology of the chain complex
((C\mathcal{X}(X))_{h},d)
. It is a fact that

\displaystyle H\mathcal{X}_{n}(X)\cong\varinjlim_{h}(H\mathcal{X}_{n}(X))_{h}

For
c=\sum_{\tau\in X^{n+1}}c_{\tau}\tau\in(C\mathcal{X}_{n}(X))_{h}
, we consider each
\tau\in X^{n+1}
to be a vertex map from the standard simplex
\Delta^{n}
to
X
. Let
r_{h}:|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h}|
to be the map which sends a point to its closest vertex. We define

\displaystyle A_{h}(c)=\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{\tau}c_{\tau}[sp(r_{h}i_{h}\tau):e^{n}_{\alpha}]e^{n}_{\alpha}\in C^{\infty}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|)

where
C^{\infty}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|)
is the cellular chain complex of locally finite chains and
sp(r_{h}i_{h}\tau)
is the simplex spanned by the vertices of
r_{h}i_{h}\tau(\Delta^{n})
. The number
[r_{h}i_{h}\tau:e^{n}_{\alpha}]
is
\pm 1
if and only if this simplex is
e^{n}_{\alpha}
(up to orientation). The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H\mathcal{X}_{n}(X))_{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{lf}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H\mathcal{X}_{n}(X))_{h+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{lf}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h+1}|)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{h,h+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{h+1}}

commutes for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and so defines a group homomorphism
\mathcal{A}:H\mathcal{X}_{n}(X)\cong\varinjlim_{h}(H\mathcal{X}_{n}(X))_{h}\rightarrow\varinjlim_{h}H^{lf}_{n}(|\mathcal{U}_{h}|)
.

The diagram in the conjecture commutes on the level of chains.

There is a conjectural
\varprojlim^{1}
sequence for coarse homology:

Conjecture 12.5
.

Let
Y
be a compact Hausdroff space. Let
(Y_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a decreasing family of closed subspaces of
Y
. We consider the set-theoretic intersection
\cap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}Y_{i}
in
Y
with the subspace topology. We have an exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow{\lim_{i\in\mathbb{N}}}^{1}H\mathcal{X}_{n+1}\mathcal{O}(Y_{i})\rightarrow H\mathcal{X}_{n}\mathcal{O}(\cap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}Y_{i})\rightarrow\lim_{i\in\mathbb{N}}H\mathcal{X}_{n}\mathcal{O}(Y_{i})\rightarrow 0

for
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

This conjecture is stated as Lemma
7.12
in
[
11
]
, but the proof is incorrect. I believe I can prove it for
X=\cap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}Y_{i}
a compact metric space, written as an intersection of ANR neighbourhoods of
X
in
C_{b}(X)
, using Conjecture
12.4
. This would require showing that
\mathcal{A}
is an isomorphism (which should be true, since
\mathcal{A}
should coincide with the assembly map in coarse homology), and using some shape-theoretic machinery to prove that

\displaystyle\varinjlim_{h}H^{lf}_{n}(VR_{h}(cX))\cong H^{lf}_{n}(VR_{1}(cX))\cong H^{lf}_{n}(M_{X})

where
VR_{h}(cX)
is the nerve of the open cover of balls of radius
2^{h}
centred at a maximal
\frac{2^{h}}{2}
-separated set of
cX
.

Coarse assembly maps
:

Cones are an object of interest in studying coarse assembly.

Proposition 12.6
.

(Proposition
11.23
in
[
9
]
) Let
E:\mathbf{BornCoarse}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
be a coarse homology theory, and let
X
be a uniform bornological coarse space. Assume:

1.

\mathbf{C}
is complete.

2.

E
is additive.

3.

X
is homotopy equivalent in
\mathbf{UBC}
to a locally finite, finite-dimensional simplicial complex equipped with the metric structures.

Then we have an equivalence

\displaystyle(\Sigma E(\ast)\wedge\Sigma_{+}^{\infty})^{lf}(X)\simeq E\mathcal{O}^{\infty}(X)

The restriction to
X
homotopy equivalent to a locally finite, finite-dimensional simplicial complex is extremely strong, and is not satisfied for many spaces of interest (eg. arbitrary compact metric spaces or compact Hausdorff spaces). Note that
E
is applied to the cone of a space, but the locally finite homology theory is applied to the space
X
itself. This suggests that we are in some sort of degenerate case, and the
X
on the left hand side should be replaced with something one dimension higher. It is also suspicious that the functor
X\mapsto(\Sigma E(\ast)\wedge\Sigma_{+}^{\infty})^{lf}(X)
is defined on
\mathbf{TopBorn}
instead of
\mathbf{UBC}
, and is homotopy invariant with respect to proper homotopies with are not necessarily uniform. This strongly suggests that it should have something to do with (a uniform version of) shape theory.

Definition 12.7
.

An inverse ANR-system
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha,^{\prime}\alpha};\mathcal{A}\}
is said to be uniformly associated with a uniform space
X
if there exist homotopy classes of uniformly continuous maps
[p_{\alpha}]:X\rightarrow X_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
, satisfying the following conditions:

1.

[p_{\alpha}]=[\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}][p_{\alpha^{\prime}}]
for all
\alpha\leq\alpha^{\prime}
.

2.

For any ANR-space
P
and any homotopy class of uniformly continuous maps
[f]:X\rightarrow P
, there exists an index
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
and a homotopy class
[h_{\alpha}]:X_{\alpha}\rightarrow P
such that
[h_{\alpha}][p_{\alpha}]=[f]
.

3.

If
[h][p_{\alpha}]=[h^{\prime}][p_{\alpha}]
for two homotopy classes of uniformly continuous maps
[h],[h^{\prime}]:X_{\alpha}\rightarrow P
, then there exists an index
\alpha^{\prime}\geq\alpha
such that
[h][\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}]=[h^{\prime}][\phi_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}]
.

We call such a system a
uniform shape expansion
of
X
.

This definition just replaces all continuous maps and continuous homotopies with uniformly continuous maps and homotopies. In the compact metric case, the uniform condition is vacuous, and for a general proper metric space, the sequence of open covers

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{h}:=\bigcup_{z\in Z_{h}}B(z,\frac{1}{2^{h}})

for a maximal
\frac{1}{2^{h+1}}
separated subset of
X
, is a uniform shape expansion of
X
.

I have the following idea. Let
\{X_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha};\mathcal{A}\}
be a uniform shape expansion
X
, and let
M
be a model in topological spaces for

\displaystyle M:=\text{holim}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\{X_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\}

If
X
is a compact metric space,
M
corresponds to the inverse mapping telescope
M_{X}
. There are canonical inclusions

\displaystyle i_{\alpha}:\text{holim}_{\beta\leq\alpha}\{X_{\beta}\}\rightarrow M

where
M_{\alpha}:=\text{holim}_{\beta\leq\alpha}\{X_{\beta}\}
denotes everything "below" level
\alpha
. The cofibre of the inclusion, denoted
C(i_{\alpha})
, is modelled by the pair
(M,M_{\alpha})
. I believe the following may be true:

Conjecture 12.8
.

Let
E:\mathbf{BornCoarse}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
be a coarse homology theory, and let
X
be a uniform bornological coarse space. Assume:

1.

\mathbf{C}
is complete.

2.

E
is additive.

Then we have an equivalence

\displaystyle\colim_{\alpha}(E(\ast)\wedge\Sigma_{+}^{\infty})^{lf}(C(i_{\alpha}))\simeq E\mathcal{O}^{\infty}(X)

This reduces to the result of Proposition
12.6
in the case that
E=H\mathcal{X}
. If
X
is a locally finite, finite-dimensional simplicial complex, it is in particular a proper metric space. The locally finite homology theory constructed from
H\mathcal{X}
is ordinary locally finite homology
(E(\ast)\wedge\Sigma_{+}^{\infty})^{lf}=H^{lf}
. For a simplicial complex, there is a
h^{\prime}
large enough such that
|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\simeq X
for all
h\geq h^{\prime}
. We have that

\displaystyle\colim_{h}(E(\ast)\wedge\Sigma_{+}^{\infty})^{lf}_{n}(C(i_{h}))=H_{n}^{lf}(C(i_{h^{\prime}}))=H_{n}^{lf}(X\times\mathbb{R})=\Sigma H_{n}^{lf}(X)

\displaystyle=(\Sigma E(\ast)\wedge\Sigma_{+}^{\infty})^{lf}_{n}(X)

Assume the conjecture is true. If
E=H\mathcal{X}
and
X
is a compact metric space, we have

\displaystyle\colim_{h}(E(\ast)\wedge\Sigma_{+}^{\infty})^{lf}_{n}(C(i_{h}))=H_{n}^{lf}(M_{X})

Therefore, this case reduces to proving Conjecture
12.5
.

Coarse homotopy groups of compact Hausdorff spaces
:

Let
(X,\mathcal{T})
be a compact Hausdorff space. We begin with the theorem:

Theorem 12.9
.

(Chapter
5.2
, Theorem
7
in
[
27
]
) Every compact Hausdorff space
X
is the limit of an inverse system
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{\lambda},p_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda};\Gamma\}
of compact polyhedra
X_{\lambda}
with
PL
-bonding maps. One can always achieve that the cardinal
card(\Gamma)\leq w(X)
, the weight of
X
.

One proves this theorem by embedding
X
as a subset of the Hilbert cube
\prod_{\alpha\in A}I_{\alpha}
, where the cardinality of
A
is
w(X)
. This is the limit of a system of finite-dimensional cubes with projections as bonding maps, denoted

\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha\in A}I_{\alpha}=\lim_{\alpha}K_{\alpha}

let
p_{\alpha}:\prod_{\alpha\in A}I_{\alpha}\rightarrow K_{\alpha}
denote the projection onto
K_{\alpha}
and
p_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}:K_{\alpha^{\prime}}\rightarrow K_{\alpha}
denote the bonding maps. We consider the sets
p_{\alpha}(X)\subset K_{\alpha}
, and take polyhedral neighbourhoods
K_{\alpha i}
of
p_{\alpha}(X)
. Let
\Gamma=A\times\mathbb{N}
. We let
(\alpha,n)\leq(\alpha^{\prime},n^{\prime})
provided that
\alpha\leq\alpha^{\prime}
,
p_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}(K_{\alpha^{\prime}n^{\prime}})\subset K_{\alpha n}
and we define

\displaystyle q_{\alpha^{\prime}n^{\prime},\alpha n}={p_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}}_{|K_{\alpha^{\prime}n^{\prime}}}:K_{\alpha^{\prime}n^{\prime}}\rightarrow K_{\alpha n}

\displaystyle q_{\alpha n}=p_{\alpha}|_{X}:X\rightarrow K_{\alpha n}

It can be checked that
\mathcal{X}=\{K_{\alpha n},q_{\alpha^{\prime}n^{\prime},\alpha n};\Gamma\}
is a shape expansion of
X
.

It can be shown that any shape expansion
\mathcal{X}
is associated to a
\mathcal{T}
-admissible function
\phi_{\mathcal{X}}:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(X\times X)
. I expect that
\pi_{n}^{c}(\mathcal{O}_{\phi}(X))
is related to
\lim_{\alpha}\lim_{n}\pi_{n}(K_{\alpha n})
, possibly via a spectral sequence. Taking the colimit over all possible shape expansions, there should be a relationship between
\pi_{n}^{c}(\mathcal{O}(X))
and

\displaystyle\colim_{\mathcal{X}}\lim_{\alpha}\lim_{n}\pi_{n}(K_{\alpha n})

Part V
Appendix

13
Technical details

13.1
The metric on
M

This subsection proves some elementary statements about the path metric on
M
, which are used in many places in main section.

Let
M=\tilde{M}/_{\sim}
denote the mapping telescope as a quotient space, and let
\pi:\tilde{M}\rightarrow M
denote the quotient map. Recall

\displaystyle\tilde{M}=\coprod_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times[2^{h},2^{h+1}]=\coprod_{h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\tilde{M}^{h}

and that
\sim
is the equivalence relation obtained from the simplicial gluing maps
\phi_{h}:|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times\{2^{h}\}\rightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|\times\{2^{h}\}
. For technical purposes, we now define some projections. Let
h\in\mathbb{N}
. Define the projection to the
2^{h}
-th slice

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{2^{h}}:\big\{\bigcup_{l\geq h}|\mathcal{U}_{l}|\times[2^{l},2^{l+1}]\big\}\cup_{\phi_{h}}\big\{|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|\times(2^{h-1},2^{h}]\big\}\longrightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h-1}|\times\{2^{h}\}

as
\mathfrak{q}_{2^{h}}(x,t)=(\phi_{l,h-1}(x),2^{h})
for
t\in[2^{l},2^{l+1}],l\geq h
and as
\mathfrak{q}_{2^{h}}(x,t)=(x,2^{h})
for
t\in(2^{h-1},2^{h}]
. This is compatible with the gluing maps so passes to the quotient.
\mathfrak{q}_{1}
is defined similarly in the obvious way.

Let
s\in(2^{h},2^{h+1})
. Define the projection to the
s
-th slice

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{s}:\big\{\bigcup_{l>h}|\mathcal{U}_{l}|\times[2^{l},2^{l+1}]\big\}\cup_{\phi_{h+1}}\big\{|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times(2^{h},2^{h+1}]\big\}\longrightarrow|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times\{s\}

as
\mathfrak{q}_{s}(x,t)=(\phi_{l,h}(x),s)
for
t\in[2^{l},2^{l+1}]
,
l>h
and as
\mathfrak{q}_{s}(x,t)=(x,s)
for
t\in(2^{h},2^{h+1}]
.

Definition 13.1
.

The metric on
M
is defined as follows: for two points
x,y\in M
let

\displaystyle d(x,y):=\inf_{\gamma_{xy}}\inf_{\tilde{\gamma}_{xy}}l(\tilde{\gamma}_{xy})

where
\gamma_{xy}
is a path in
M
from
x
to
y
,
\tilde{\gamma}_{xy}
is a lift of
\gamma_{xy}
to
\tilde{M}
ie.
\pi\circ\tilde{\gamma}_{xy}=\gamma_{xy}
, and
l(\tilde{\gamma}_{xy})
is calculated as the sum of lengths of path components in each disjoint component
\tilde{M}^{h}
of
\tilde{M}
with product metric
d_{\tilde{M}^{h}}=d_{|\mathcal{U}_{h}|}+d_{[2^{h},2^{h+1}]}
, where
d_{|\mathcal{U}_{h}|}
is the path metric induced from the spherical metric on each simplex.

Let us describe what some geodesics between two points
x=(u,t)
and
y=(v,s)
in
M
look like. Let
\gamma
be a path between
x
and
y
. By "shorter", we mean "length less than or equal to".

1.

Any path segment
\eta
of
\gamma
with endpoints at height
l
and image at least height
l
can be replaced with a shorter segment
\eta^{\prime}
with the same endpoints.

\eta^{\prime}=\mathfrak{q}_{l}\circ\eta
clearly satisfies the desired properties:
\eta^{\prime}
is shorter than
\eta
because the maps
\mathfrak{q}
are
1
-Lipschitz.

2.

Any path segment
\eta
of
\gamma
beginning at
x=(u,t)
, ending at height
l<t
and with image between heights
l,t
can be replaced with a shorter segment
\eta^{\prime}=\sigma_{x}\ast\tau
with the same endpoints, where
\sigma_{x}
travels straight downwards, and
\tau
has image contained in the slice at height
l
.

Let
\tau=\mathfrak{q}_{l}\circ\eta
and let
\sigma_{x}
be the straight downward path from
x
to
\mathfrak{q}_{l}(x)
. It is clear that
\eta^{\prime}=\sigma_{x}\ast\tau
has the same endpoints as
\eta
and it is shorter, since the metric on
\tilde{M}
is the product metric, and the
\phi
used to define
\mathfrak{q}_{l}
are
1
-Lipschitz.

3.

The shortest path
\gamma
is a concatenation of
\sigma_{x}\ast\eta\ast\sigma_{y}^{-1}
where
\sigma_{x},\sigma_{y}
are straight downward paths beginning at
x
and
y
respectively, and
\eta
is a geodesic path with image contained in some height slice
l\leq\min\{t,s\}
.

By point
1
we can assume that the height variable of
\gamma
never has negative second derivative. Let
l
be the minimal height reached by
\gamma
. If
l\in\{t,s\}
we can replace
\gamma
by
\sigma_{x}\ast\tau
or
\tau\ast\sigma_{y}^{-1}
as in point
2
, then replace
\tau
by a geodesic path
\eta
with the same endpoints. If
l<\min\{t,s\}
then
\gamma
can be written as a concatenation
\gamma_{xz}\ast\gamma_{yz}^{-1}
where
z
is a point at height
l
,
\gamma_{xz}
is a path between
x
and
z
and
\gamma_{yz}
is a path between
y
and
z
. By point
2
we can replace
\gamma
with
\sigma_{x}\ast\tau_{\mathfrak{q}_{l}(x)z}\ast\tau_{\mathfrak{q}_{l}(y)z}^{-1}\ast\sigma_{y}^{-1}
. Finally we can replace
\tau_{\mathfrak{q}_{l}(x)z}\ast\tau_{\mathfrak{q}_{l}(y)z}^{-1}
with a geodesic path
\eta
between
\mathfrak{q}_{l}(x)
and
\mathfrak{q}_{l}(y)
.

4.

Let
r=\min\{t,s\}
. The distance between
x
and
y
is

\displaystyle\min\{\min_{2^{h}<r}l(\tilde{\gamma}^{2^{h}}),l(\tilde{\gamma}^{r})\}

\displaystyle=\min\{\min_{2^{h}<r}|t-2^{h}|+|s-2^{h}|+d_{\tilde{M}^{h-1}}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}_{2^{h}}(x)},\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}_{2^{h}}(y)}),|t-s|+d_{r}(\mathfrak{q}_{r}(x),\mathfrak{q}_{r}(y))\}

where
\gamma^{2^{h}}=\sigma_{x}\ast\eta_{2^{h}}\ast(\sigma_{y})^{-1}
is concatenation as described above with the image of
\eta_{2^{h}}
contained in the slice at height
2^{h}
, and similarly for
\gamma^{r}
.
d_{r}
refers to the restriction of the metric of
M
to height
r
, and the tilde denotes lifts to
\tilde{M}
with minimal distance.

By point
3
, any path can be replaced with one of the form
\sigma_{x}\ast\eta_{l}\ast\sigma_{y}^{-1}
where
\eta
lives on height
l
so it suffices to consider only paths of these form. If
l=r
then we are in the case of
\gamma^{r}
. Otherwise,
l<r
. If
l\neq 2^{h}
for some
2^{h}<r
,
\gamma
can be replaced with a strictly shorter path which is either of the form
\sigma_{x}\ast\eta_{r}\ast\sigma_{y}^{-1}
or by
\sigma_{x}\ast\eta_{2^{h}}\ast\sigma_{y}^{-1}
where
2^{h}
is the smallest power of
2
greater than
l
, by projecting the
\eta
segment upwards to the appropriate height and shortening
\sigma_{x},\sigma_{y}
.

The length of
\gamma^{r}
is a sum of the distance
|t-s|
to the lowest point
r=\min\{t,s\}
and the minimal distance
d_{r}(\mathfrak{q}_{r}(x),\mathfrak{q}_{r}(y))
between the endpoints of the path segment with image in slice
r
. Observe that
d_{r}=d_{|\mathcal{U}_{h}|\times\{r\}}
for
r\in(2^{h},2^{h+1})
